<meta>This is the title page of Robert Morison's seminal work on the classification of umbelliferous plants, marking a significant moment in the history of the University of Oxford's botanical studies.</meta>

### OF PLANTS

# UMBELLIFEROUS
<note>Plants with flower clusters that spread from a common center, like an umbrella; examples include carrots, parsley, and hemlock.</note>

## A new distribution,

### THROUGH TABLES

### OF KINSHIP AND AFFINITY
<note>Morison was one of the first to suggest that plants should be classified by their physical similarities and "bloodlines" rather than just their uses.</note>

->*F R O M*<-

# THE BOOK OF NATURE

->Observed & discovered.<-

### BY THE AUTHOR

->**ROBERT MORISON** *Royal Physician and Professor of Botany,*<-
->*as well as the first Professor of Botany of the renowned and most celebrated University of Oxford*<-
->*and the first Superintendent of its Botanical Garden.*<-

> I write for the learned (in the Art); the unlearned I shall teach.
<note>original: "Doctis ( in Arte ) scribo ; indoctos docebo."</note>

### AT OXFORD,

### From the SHELDONIAN Theatre.

---

->*In the Year of our Lord* **1672.**<-

<summary>The title page of Robert Morison's 1672 treatise on Umbelliferous plants, establishing a new system of botanical classification based on natural affinities and marking his role as Oxford's first Professor of Botany.</summary>

A detailed allegorical engraving commemorating a royal gift to the University of Cambridge. In the center is an oval shield bearing the arms of the University of Cambridge (a cross ermine between four lions passant gardant, with a closed book at the center of the cross). To the left, a standing male figure (Apollo, the god of music and truth) holds a lyre and stands upon a stack of books. To the right, a seated female figure (Minerva, the goddess of wisdom and war) wears a plumed helmet and holds a spear and shield, with an owl—her symbol of wisdom—nearby. 

Below the central shield is a decorative pedestal featuring a circular medallion with the profile of King George I. Above the medallion, a ribbon scroll bears a Latin inscription. The engraving is signed by the artist in the lower right corner.

## TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS PRINCE
# JAMES
## DUKE OF ORMONDE, &c.
### *Of the University of Oxford*
## CHANCELLOR
### MOST WORTHY.

->*Most Illustrious Prince,*<-

Since you preside over such a great choir of the most learned men by your authority and counsel, even if I am as yet scarcely known to you by face, there is no reason left for me to doubt that your countenance will be pleasing and favorable toward letters and literate men. For since you are the head of the Oxford Muses <note>The "Muses" here refers to the scholars and students of the University of Oxford</note>, and after our Most Serene Lord the King, their most kind patron, for that very name you are to be held in the highest veneration among all the Muses dwelling here. Your most ancient lineage, your most wealthy resources, the sincerity of your mind, the splendor of your actions, and above all your most upright loyalty toward the Father of the Country <note>original: "Patrem Patriæ"; a reference to King Charles II</note> (to whom you have always adhered like a faithful Achates <note>A reference to the loyal companion of Aeneas in Virgil's Aeneid, signifying a devoted friend</note> abroad and at home) praise you sufficiently: why then should the Oxford Muses not praise you as so worthy a Maecenas <note>A Roman patron of the arts; used here as a title for a generous supporter of scholarship</note>; especially since under your

<summary>This page contains a formal Latin dedication to James Butler, 1st Duke of Ormonde, celebrating his role as Chancellor of Oxford University and his loyalty to the Crown.</summary>

May the University flourish under your auspices, supported by the efforts, vigilance, and guidance of your most worthy Vice-Chancellor <note>The executive head of the university</note>, and of the other most prudent Rectors and Governors, who all obediently follow your command and will. They earnestly pray that you may long preside over them in safety. May your graceful Favor flourish, may the efforts of all the Rectors and Governors under your protection flourish, and may the Scholars flourish under their guidance.

->*Most Illustrious Prince*<-

->Your most humble and devoted<-
->Servant<-
->ROBERT MORISON.<- <note>Robert Morison (1620–1683) was a Scottish botanist and the first Professor of Botany at the University of Oxford.</note>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication of a botanical treatise, likely following a title page or preface. It establishes the author's gratitude toward the University of Oxford and his royal patrons.</meta>

# TO THE LORD
# VICE-CHANCELLOR
### Most Vigilant
## To the Heads of Colleges and Halls, to the Doctors,
## To the MASTERS,

*And to the other most worthy men of every rank or title, imbued with every kind of learning, his most munificent Benefactors in the University of Oxford.*

->Sends Fondest Greetings<- <note>original: "S. P. D." (Salutem Plurimam Dicit), a standard formal greeting in Latin correspondence.</note>

It is the mark of a **NOBLE** man to confess (most sincere and learned Gentlemen) those by whose help he has made progress. This treatise on Umbelliferous plants <note>Plants like carrots, parsley, or dill that produce flower clusters in an umbrella-like shape (umbels).</note>, once conceived under the protection and patronage of the Most Serene Duke of Orléans <note>Gaston, Duke of Orléans (1608–1660), a noted patron of botany.</note>, nurtured under the auspices of our Lord King Charles II, and finally brought forth under your protection, I now dare to make public property. Whatever this offspring may be, it would not have seen the light of day without your "midwifery" <note>The author uses a metaphor of childbirth to describe the publication process.</note>. For whatever light may come to the Botanical Literary Republic from this our new method of umbels, all those who take pleasure in botanical study must confess that it owes no less to your liberality than to our travels and midnight labors.

Called here by the favor and benevolence of certain among you, and admitted into your flock by the natural and ready inclination of some among you to benefit the Botanical Republic, I have no doubt that this occurred through the accompaniment of Divine Providence. We have encountered here men who are not only most learned, but also devoted to the public utility of the University; to them we inscribe and dedicate this new specimen, or treatise on umbels, constructed according to tables of kinship and affinity. It is indeed a slight gift, worthy neither of so great a Hero as the Chancellor <note>Likely referring to James Butler, 1st Duke of Ormonde, then Chancellor of Oxford.</note> nor of you, our patrons; nevertheless, we expect it to be pleasing to those skilled in the botanical art, and even to those being initiated into it, on account of its easy method.

We offer it to be surveyed and examined by everyone, both here among you and elsewhere in the world, to the end that those who are pleased by these efforts of ours may be made more inclined—by your example—to promote the remaining sections, which we promise to bring to light at the first opportunity, unless the labors of the designers or engravers <term>Chalcographus: a copperplate engraver</term> stand in the way; we ourselves are ready for action. In the meantime, let the doctrine of Umbels now go forth first under your auspices—restored, purged of the infinite errors and heavy delusions with which it has teemed until now, and reduced to nine primary genera. These have been established and composed by us based on the diverse form and figure of the seeds as observed from the book of nature, with their intermediate distributions noted and presented by the diverse appearance of the leaves, according to the rule prescribed by Nature herself, and not detected by any botanist before us until now.

If the Most Serene Royal Prince, the Duke of Orléans—the most sharp-sighted in botanical matters since Solomon—were alive today, this little work of ours would have needed no one's help, nor would our other forthcoming works have remained hidden for so long. But, alas! with so dear a Maecenas <note>A "Maecenas" is a generic term for a generous patron of the arts or sciences, named after the advisor to the Roman Emperor Augustus.</note> having passed away...

<summary>In this formal dedication, the author presents his pioneering botanical study of umbelliferous plants to the leadership of Oxford University, crediting their support and the patronage of King Charles II for the work's completion. He introduces a new classification system based on seed shape and leaf structure, claiming to have corrected numerous historical errors in the field.</summary>

<keywords>Oxford University, botany, umbelliferous plants, plant classification, Charles II, Duke of Orléans, scientific patronage, taxonomy</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface of Robert Morison's botanical work, following his lament over the loss of a patron and his decision to present his findings to the scholarly community.</meta>

With that light extinguished, with so firm an Atlas cast down, and so solid a foundation removed from us, and since no one endowed with a Herculean spirit <note>A reference to the myth of Hercules taking the weight of the heavens from Atlas; Morison is looking for a new powerful patron</note> has yet appeared to me who might deign to succeed his Highness, I have been compelled to flee to you as my final refuge. I do this so that this first preparation of the Umbellates <note>Plants of the family Apiaceae, such as carrots and parsley, characterized by umbrella-like flower clusters</note>, methodically arranged by genera and species, might be set before the public, and shared with those learned and skilled in the Botanical art both here and elsewhere. I do not shrink from their examination of this specimen of our umbellates; rather, I dare to undergo it most willingly and gladly.

Finally, lest I use a tedious heap of words (most Learned and Sincere Men), I heartily desire that all your labors be directed toward the greater Glory of God, the benefit of the Republic of Letters <note>original: "Reipub. literariæ" — the international community of intellectuals and scholars</note>, the safety and health of our most Serene Lord the King, and the holy and sound education of his subjects; indeed, I pray that God may turn all things to the good for you and your labors. Whatever this specimen of ours may be, it vows and dedicates itself to you.

->*The most humble Servant*<-
->*and Client of you all,*<-

->**ROBERT MORISON.**<-

<summary>In this concluding preface, Robert Morison dedicates his systematic study of umbellate plants to the scholarly community, seeking their patronage and peer review following the death of his previous benefactor.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Morison, botany, Umbelliferae, taxonomy, Republic of Letters, scientific patronage, plant classification</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to a botanical work, establishing the author's intent to reorganize plant classification based on direct observation rather than relying solely on previous authorities.</meta>

<sig>(o)</sig>

# PREFACE
### TO THE
## Reader.

As we are about to deliver that part of Natural History which treats of plants—namely, <term>Botany</term> <note>original: "Botanicen"; the study of plants</note>—and since it has been presented by authors in their written records far too wordily and confusedly until now; we have firmly resolved and determined in our mind to bring forth those things which we have learned from the book of nature through long experience, constant inspection, and proper examination. Indeed, we have decided to publish all plants according to a better method (than has been done by anyone hitherto), beginning with the <term>umbels</term> <note>original: "umbellis"; plants like carrots or parsley where flower stalks spring from a single center like umbrella ribs</note>, which are not yet reduced to subordinate genera or intermediate species by botanical writers. This is perhaps because it is extremely difficult to find a difference where there is too much similarity or closeness, such as is seen here in this section.

We have therefore proposed to ourselves for the present to set this specimen of umbelliferous plants before the public, and we have deemed it fair to offer the same to be weighed and examined by all who are learned or moderately skilled in the botanical art. If they approve, they will give us courage and incite us to publish the greater works we are meditating upon. Furthermore, if anything contained therein should displease botanists—at least those skilled in the art—once we are informed by them, relying on reason and experience, we shall wipe away the stains and correct those things. We shall strive to reach the goal we have set for ourselves and give our diligent labor so that all the sections joined together may form a complete botanical work (as far as lies within our power), according to the method proposed to us by nature. This we intend both here and elsewhere in the explanation of each individual section, yet without scorning the assiduous reading of authors.

We now have in our possession all the sections of all plants (at least those seen by us so far) sketched with a rough brush and described. We are not only publishing this specimen first, which constitutes the thirtieth part of the work we intend, but we are also giving the ninth section in order, containing the doctrine of all the umbels. We do this to the end that (as they say, "the lion is known by its claw" <note>original: "ex ungue leonem"; a proverb meaning one can judge the whole from a small part</note>) those skilled in botany may more easily judge the rest of the works to be sent forth by us. Furthermore, if this section is admitted to the stage by the applause of the learned in the art, the remaining sections, delivered in a fuller form, will follow. We admit all learned botanists everywhere into our counsel; their judgment and their esteem shall prevail, provided that reason and experience accompany them.

Nor do we (after the manner of other writers) use a long series of words in this our treatise on umbels, but briefly and succinctly (having assigned the <term>generic mark</term> <note>the defining characteristic of a group</note> first and before all else) we add the <term>specific marks</term> <note>the traits that distinguish one species from another</note> by which individual species, serving under their own proper genera as if under the same banner or password, can be distinguished from one another with easy or no trouble. For by this method, instructed by the book of nature, students of botanical matters can hereafter learn clearly, plainly, and methodically about plants, distinctly arranged through the highest and middle genera down to the lowest species, descending even to the varieties of individual species. Thus, without the great expense of books or the loss and waste of time, botanists may more quickly learn and more firmly retain in memory this our new doctrine by this certain scientific method, distinguished by infallible marks that remain constant, given by nature and first observed by us.

Moreover, at the request of certain friends skilled in the art, we have added the places of birth, the time of flowering, the temperaments and powers, and the better-known names. All these things we have demonstrated to the eye from the umbels themselves in the public garden of the University, in the same order in which they are presented in this treatise. The remaining sections, to be perceived and learned with equal ease (by the favor of the Divine Will), we shall deliver—

<summary>The author introduces a new, systematic method for classifying plants, specifically focusing on the complex "umbelliferous" family, based on direct observation in the University botanical garden. He argues that his method of using specific "marks" or traits is superior to the confusing and wordy texts of previous botanists.</summary>
<keywords>Botany, classification, umbelliferous plants, scientific method, University botanical garden, genus and species, plant characteristics</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>The catchword "tur" from the previous page suggests a continuation of a verb like "exhibentur" or "traduntur." This page serves as a programmatic preface or introduction to a botanical work, likely Robert Morison’s "Plantarum Umbelliferarum Distributio Nova" (1672), where he argues for a new system of classification based on seeds.</meta>

...we [intend to continue], by publishing one or two [volumes] each year, provided that ingenious men who desire the public good favor our undertakings. For without help (I speak candidly, plainly, and sincerely), it is impossible to provide illustrations drawn from life (as they say), engraved from the original plants, and not—as has been the case until now—taken from faulty and erroneous images, poorly drawn and engraved in the books of botanical writers. Provided our request is met, we shall in no way spare our labors so that we may benefit the public, and so that we may reduce plants, both native and exotic, to a <term>good order <note>original Greek: eutaxian</note></term> and an easy, optimal method. By this method, this part of natural history, <term>Botany <note>original: Botanice</note></term>, can be most easily learned and firmly retained, using identifying marks based on reason and experience and sought from the universal method of nature—which I claim for myself first, both here in this section on the <term>Umbel-bearing plants <note>original: Umbellarum; plants like carrots or parsley that have flower clusters spreading from a common center</note></term> and in others that I intend to produce.

We observe not a few in these latter centuries writing on herbal matters who have wasted paper uselessly rather than benefiting mortals. Indeed, if their labors are weighed more carefully, they bring forth nothing new or remarkable, but merely sell the writings of others as their own, compiled into huge volumes. These men are driven by a desire for empty little-glory, or for making a base profit with printers, thinking to themselves that it is sweet to make a gain from anything. Thus, as long as they produce plants presented with tedious descriptions—reciting parts common to the whole genus and applying them again and again to individual species, while assigning a generic or specific (or peculiar) mark in very few instances—they think they have performed the duty of botanists excellently. 

However, they are little concerned with the order of plants, since they have so far proposed no true or genuine method for themselves by which students of Botany might learn scientifically (that is, by genera and species). All methods proposed thus far by writers of botanical matters suit the plants only secondarily, as anyone can clearly see in the authors publishing General Histories. For some have taken a general method from the place where plants grow; others from their powers and properties; and others, finally, from the four seasons of the year. But none of these is to be admitted. The first might serve those practicing medicine, and is therefore in a way very necessary for physicians and healers; the second is worthy of observation by botanical gardeners, to whose care the cultivation of plants is entrusted. Yet, by these aforementioned methods, we see plants that are generically different "by a whole heaven" <note>a Latin idiom meaning "entirely different"</note> joined together, and conversely, we observe those that are generically similar separated by a long interval. 

Since, I say, they separate what should be joined and join what should be separated, all methods brought forward until now are to be observed only secondarily. Therefore, we reject them and propose a true and genuine method, serving primarily and of itself, communicated to vegetables by nature herself, perceptible by an infallible token or mark in all things contained under the same genus. And since method, or order, is the soul of all learning, we shall present the plants—both in this arrangement of the umbels and in the universal digestion of all plants which we promise—by arranging generic and essential marks sought from the seeds and their similarity through tables of kinship and affinity. 

We shall add specific differences taken from the less noble parts—namely the root, leaves, and stalks, as well as smell, taste, and color—assigning individual species to individual genera. Thus, species recognizable by a different appearance will march under intermediate genera, and intermediate genera under the highest ones, distinguished by their essential marks which always remain the same. This is the order given to plants by nature herself from the beginning, now observed by me first. For while I was constructing a <term>storehouse of seeds <note>original: promptuarium seminarium</note></term> of all plants, both those growing spontaneously in fields and countrysides and those raised for many years in the gardens of the <term>Most Serene Royal Duke of Orléans <note>Gaston, Duke of Orléans, a major patron of botany</note></term>, I noticed from frequent inspection and comparison of the seeds of each and every plant that authors had hallucinated seriously, both in naming plants and in arranging or disposing them. This will be apparent to anyone considering and looking more closely with me below in this treatise on the umbels. My <term>storehouse</term> of seeds is adorned with two thousand seeds of different species and more, with each species enclosed in its own individual small box. They are arranged in compartments in the same order...

<summary>The author critiques previous botanical classification systems based on habitat or medicinal use, proposing instead a "true" method based on the physical characteristics of seeds. He describes his extensive seed collection and his goal to create a scientific hierarchy of genera and species.</summary>
<keywords>botany, plant classification, seeds, Umbelliferae, Duke of Orléans, taxonomy, botanical illustration, scientific method</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface of Robert Morison's work on umbelliferous plants (the carrot and parsley family), establishing his methodology and acknowledging his royal patrons.</meta>

...as they are presented in the tables, and as the umbellifers <note>original: "umbellæ"; plants with flower clusters radiating from a central point like umbrella ribs</note> are currently arranged in the public Botanical Garden of the University. Throughout this following section, we provide three things which were neglected, let alone discovered, by others writing before us, and which have until now significantly hindered those desiring to learn about plants methodically. *First, we assign a generic or universal mark suitable to each genus, for those that are widely distributed as well as those that are intermediate. Second, we adapt a specific token <note>original: "tesseram"; a distinguishing sign or trait</note> proper or peculiar to each species. Third, we denote the duration of each and every species, having assigned appropriate symbols. For example:* ☉ *denotes long-lived or perennial plants, while* ☿ *indicates annual umbellifers. Furthermore, those to which the symbol* ♋ *is attached are either previously undescribed, or poorly named by authors, and consequently poorly arranged—that is, placed outside the class or series of their kin. Finally, at the request of certain botanists, as has been said, we assign the birthplaces <note>original: "natales"; the native habitats</note> of all the umbellifers, as well as their temperament, powers, and the various names given by at least the principal authors. We claim for ourselves alone not these same things, but a new and unheard-of method, which will perhaps please many highly learned botanists; even if it is difficult, indeed impossible, to please everyone. By this reasoning, when looking at our tables constructed by the said method, anyone will be able to perceive which umbellifers are perennial, which are annual, and finally which were collected and observed under the auspices of the Most Serene Royal Duke Gaston of Orléans <note>Gaston, Duke of Orléans (1608–1660), a major patron of botany</note> of France, and are now at last presented by us. Credit these same things to him and to his memory. We promise the rest under the auspices of our Most Serene and Powerful Lord, King Charles II, with the added goodwill of patrons and friends in our Illustrious and Most Celebrated University <note>Oxford University</note>, and of others elsewhere who are students of botany. Farewell.*

Given from our Study in the college
called University. 15th day before the Kalends
of February, 1672. <note>January 18, 1672</note>

->Yours, and serving your<-
->interests,<-
->**ROBERT MORISON.**<-

<summary>Robert Morison concludes his preface by detailing his new taxonomic system for umbelliferous plants, using specific symbols for lifespan and classification, while dedicating the work to his patrons Gaston of Orléans and King Charles II.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page follows the formal conclusion of the Latin preface dated June 15, 1672. Having signed off the scholarly introduction, Morison here presents a practical business proposal to potential investors and patrons to fund the massive undertaking of his illustrated herbal.</meta>

# THE PROPOSAL
->TO<-
## Noblemen, Gentlemen, and others, who are willing to Subscribe towards **Dr. Morison's New Universal Herbal**, ordering *Plants*, according to a new and true Method never mentioned heretofore.

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'W' featuring interlaced vines and floral patterns.</image-desc>WHEREAS Robert *Morison* Doctor of *Medicine* <term>Physick: An archaic term for the art of healing or the practice of medicine</term>, *Botanist* to *His Majesty*, and *Professor* of that *Science* in the *University* of *Oxford*, has, by his diligent and many years of observation, discovered a more easy and scientific method of identifying plants from the "Book of Nature"—based on the different forms of their flowers, seeds, and seed-pods—than has ever yet been published. He has lately (to the great satisfaction of the learned, both at home and abroad) provided a specimen or essay of this method in one of the most difficult classes or sections. Now, being desirous to advance and simplify that part of Natural History which has previously been so tedious and discouraging to students, for the honor of our famous island, and for the increase of learning (chiefly in the Universities of Oxford and Cambridge), he wishes to finish and publish the whole work with all possible speed. However, due to the excessive cost of designing, engraving, and printing, he is unable to do so without the assistance of such noblemen and gentlemen who wish to further and encourage this great work. He therefore pledges to every nobleman, gentleman, or other person who is pleased to favor him with a payment for one plate of **Five Pounds**, that an honorable memorial shall be made of them by engraving their Coat of **Arms** on their respective plates (as has already been done in the aforementioned essay, as well as in over one hundred plates of these current five sections). Furthermore, the subscriber shall receive a book consisting of five complete sections, making 116 plates, produced by copperplate engraving <term>Taille Douce: A high-quality method of intaglio engraving on copper plates that allowed for much finer detail than traditional woodcuts</term>, containing 2,450 plants along with the description of their principles according to his new and truly modeled method. Upon paying the five pounds, each subscriber shall receive one of his specimens of umbel-bearing plants <note>original: "specimens umbellarum"; plants like carrots or parsley that have umbrella-like flower clusters. Morison's work on this group was a landmark in botanical classification.</note>, etc., together with a receipt signed by his hand, obliging him to provide each subscriber or their representatives the aforementioned five sections complete with their descriptions. The subscribers are to provide him with their blazoned Coat of Arms, which shall be engraved under their respective plate. And we whose names are subscribed here do hereby commit ourselves to the said Robert Morison to pay, upon demand, the sum of five pounds for one plate toward this work.

The *Author*, finding that he has spent almost three years in engraving 108 plates—carrying on the work slowly during the first two years partly for want of good or excellent, and indeed more diligent, engravers—and now this last year having found some foreigners who are both diligent and capable, who have joined with some of our own, we now only lack the encouragement of subscribers to pay the painters and engravers to finish the entire work in as short a time as can possibly be allowed. Therefore, we earnestly wish that other noblemen and gentlemen who have not yet subscribed would follow the example of those who have already subscribed and paid; their respective Coats of Arms shall be engraved just as those of the others have already been done.

<summary>This document is a prospectus for Robert Morison's "Plantarum Historiae Universalis Oxoniensis," offering patrons the opportunity to fund botanical illustrations in exchange for having their coats of arms engraved on the plates.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Morison, botanical illustration, scientific patronage, Oxford University, botanical classification, copperplate engraving, 17th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded Robert Morison’s proposal for his universal herbal. This page continues the theme of academic publishing by subscription in 17th-century Oxford, but focuses on a different landmark work: Dr. Robert Plot’s "Natural History of Staffordshire." # THE PROPOSAL
->TO<-
## Noblemen, Gentlemen, and others, who are willing to Subscribe
### towards the Printing of <unclear>the Natural History of Staffordshire</unclear>
### in a new and <unclear>useful</unclear> Method.

WHEREAS Robert Plot, LL.D. <term>LL.D.: Doctor of Laws</term>, <term>Keeper: The head curator or director</term> of the Ashmolean Museum in Oxford, has <unclear>undertaken</unclear> to <unclear>publish</unclear> the Natural History of <unclear>Staffordshire</unclear>
<unclear>... several lines of mirrored text ...</unclear>
<unclear>... list of subscription conditions ...</unclear>

---

<note>The following paragraph is right-reading and constitutes the primary intended content of this side of the page, acting as an update to the subscribers.</note>

The Publisher, finding that his plans for faster progress have been frequently frustrated due to his own illness and the death of two of the workmen—who, though very slow, were highly skilled in this specialized trade—now finds himself, this autumn, working at a much more industrious pace. He has realized that the more workmen he employs, the sooner the task will be completed; therefore, he has now joined with him four of our own workmen and two others who are very expert in this type of business. Furthermore, he has arranged to print the entire work within his own house, so that he may personally oversee the production. Therefore, we earnestly desire that those who have already subscribed, and those who intend to subscribe, would follow the example of those who have already paid their respective costs, so that the work may be finished and published all the sooner. To the truth of this final statement, we who have witnessed the progress do hereby sign our names.

Robert Plot
<unclear>John</unclear> 
<unclear>Robert</unclear>

<meta>The previous page concluded with practical printing updates regarding Dr. Robert Plot’s "Natural History of Staffordshire." This page shifts focus entirely to the primary scholarly work of Robert Morison, the first Professor of Botany at Oxford. It marks the beginning of Section Nine of his massive botanical encyclopedia, "Plantarum Historiae Universalis Oxoniensis," which attempted the first truly systematic classification of plants based on their fruit and seeds.</meta>

# PLANTS
# UMBELLIFEROUS

## SECTION IX.
### CHAPTER I.

Having finished the eighth section, which established the *Corymbiferous* <note>original: "Corymbiferas"; plants with flat-topped flower clusters where the outer stalks are longer than the inner ones, such as daisies or tansy</note> plants, it is now worth our effort to arrange the *Umbels* <note>original: "Umbellas"; plants like carrots, parsley, and fennel, where the flower stalks spread out from a single point like the ribs of an umbrella</note>, which constitute Section Nine, using a more precise method than has been done by anyone before us. To more easily fulfill our promise, we will first define what an *Umbel* is.

An Umbel is the round top of a plant, resting on the peak of the stem, from which many rays arise from the same center (namely the tip of the stem). These form a surface that is mostly flat, though sometimes convex and occasionally concave, composed of flowers that are usually white, sometimes yellow, and very rarely purple. When these flowers fade, there follow—by a constant and invariable order of nature—two seeds joined together for every flower (each flower being composed of five petals or leaflets). Regardless of the shape or figure of the seed, they are concave on the inner side where they join, and convex on the outer side.

We shall arrange the Umbelliferous plants by taking into account the Shape of the Seeds. According to their form and figure, we will propose nine Classes, constituting the sub-genera of the *Umbels* properly so-called. Indeed, no order has yet been found for this vast and far-reaching Genus that provides more certain indicators than the identical form of the seed for establishing a sub-genus. Furthermore, observe that we have chosen this method based on the identity or similarity of the seed shape (from its way of growing), because seeds are the "nobler parts" of the plant, since all other parts are made for their sake; for by these, the lineages of every kind are perpetuated. Leaves, however, Roots, and Stems—since they serve the fruiting process partly by chance and partly as secondary supports—we will set aside, so that from them the specific or intermediate differences between plants may be taken, as we will teach more extensively in various places below.

---

## BALL-BEARING UMBELS.
### The First Genus of Umbels.

<image-desc>A hierarchical classification chart for "Cachrys, or Ball-bearing Libanotis". A bracket leads to "With a spongy seed", which then splits into "Smooth" (marked with a dotted circle symbol) and "Grooved". "Grooved" further branches into "Rough" and "Flat", both marked with the same dotted circle symbol.</image-desc>

---

### Explanation of the table above.

The first sub-genus of Umbels shall be the *Cachryophorum* <note>literally "pill-bearing" or "nut-bearing"</note>, or *Piliferum* <note>ball-bearing</note>, called *Cachrys* or *Cachrys-bearing Libanotis* <note>A group of plants in the carrot family with thick, corky seeds; "Libanotis" was an ancient name for several aromatic plants</note>, because it bears seeds covered in a spongy substance, oblong, joined in pairs, and compact. It has a shape like an oblong pill or a small nutmeg. Consult the third Plate of Illustrations provided by us.

<summary>Robert Morison introduces his systematic classification of Umbelliferous plants (the carrot family), arguing that seeds are the most reliable characteristic for botanical organization. He provides a technical definition of the "umbel" structure and begins with the "Ball-bearing" genus characterized by corky, nutmeg-like seeds.</summary>
<keywords>botany, Umbelliferae, Robert Morison, seed classification, plant taxonomy, Cachrys, Libanotis, 17th-century science, Oxford University</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of Umbelliferous plants, moving from general definitions to the first specific category: those with pill-shaped or globule-like seeds. The author, Robert Morison, references his own botanical plates and his personal network of international scholars and gardeners, including his observations at the famous Physic Garden in Westminster.</meta>

# UMBELLIFEROUS PLANTS

The **Pill-bearing** <note>original: "Piliferæ," likely referring to the Greek "pilla" or "pilula," meaning ball or pill-shaped seeds</note> **Umbels** known to us fall into two classes. The first is lower-growing and consists of leaves like those of **Giant Fennel** <note>original: "ferulaceis"</note>; it bears female fruit with a spongy, smooth, and uniform substance, covered in an oblong casing. While still green, these are so closely joined together that they appear as a single unit, and they do not separate until they are fully ripe. Finally, when dried, the individual seeds—which are striated (grooved) on the inside and contained within a smooth, spongy substance—hang from two small fibers that provide their nourishment. This feature is common to nearly all twin seeds of the **Umbels**.

Observe carefully the first figure of **Plate Three**, which is titled **Cachrys** <note>a genus of plants in the carrot family</note> with a smooth, spongy seed. You will see how skillfully the convex surface of the **Umbel** has been drawn and engraved, laden with seeds still tightly clinging to one another. In the same place, you can clearly see some dried and separated seeds hanging from their fibers; and after the two seeds have fallen, our diligent **engraver** <note>original: "Chalcographus," literally a copper-writer</note> has shown us with great skill the two remaining fibers. Just to the right, you also have a drawing of its **Fennel-like** leaf.

The second class, however, is taller and more stately, bearing leaves like **Hog’s Fennel** <note>original: "Peucedani"</note> but thinner. It likewise has oblong, striated seeds (like the one above) nestled within a spongy substance that is either channeled or furrowed. This class is twofold, based on whether the spongy seed is uniform or uneven: namely, it either has a furrowed, rough spongy seed, or a furrowed, flat one. Thus, there are two species endowed with furrowed seeds, distinguished by the uniformity or roughness of the ridges or furrows; both are gifted with leaves thinner than those of **Hog’s Fennel**. Therefore, from the difference in the leaves and the smoothness or roughness of the ridges on the spongy seeds, the specific difference between these three **Pill-bearing Umbels** is manifest to anyone. The generic trait, however, is revealed to all by the spongy substance covering the seed, which is striated within. Regarding the convex shape of all the seeds of these three **Umbels**, consult **Plate 3 of the Illustrations**.

### Location and Season.

*The first two species of **Libanotis** <note>literally "Frankincense-herb," so named for its scent</note> grow in the high mountains of Italy and France, also in Africa, and on the islands of Crete <note>original: "Candia"</note> and Sicily. We received the seeds of the **Cachrys** with the rough, furrowed, spongy seed from Rome, sent by the most learned and expert botanist, **Jacques Barrelier**, Doctor of Sacred Theology. Later, we saw the plant itself heavy with seed in Paris at the home of **Mr. Morin**, called "the Englishman." The seed of the **Cachrys** with the flat, furrowed, spongy seed was sent to me from Tangier <note>original: "Mauritaniâ Tingitanâ"</note> by the noble Englishman **Alexander Balam**, a great student of botany. As I write this, in the garden of **Edward Morgan**—located behind the Western monastery called **Westminster** [Abbey], the most expert of all botanical gardeners I have ever visited—the plant is laden with seeds. These seeds are three times smaller than those of the mature plant shown in our **Plate 3 of Illustrations**, because this plant bore seeds only in its third year after sowing; as it grows older, it will bear larger ones. They all produce flowers and perfect their seeds toward the end of summer.*

### Names

***Libanotis*** *is so called because it smells of frankincense <note>original: "thus libanum" or "olibanum"</note>. This is common to many other umbelliferous plants. The first is the **Cachrys-bearing Libanotis** of Camerarius. The **True Cachrys** of Lobel. It is so named because it has a burning quality like an iron pill <note>original: "pilulæ ferreæ"</note> which was used to brand the skin with cautery, which the Greeks called **cachrys** or **canchrys**. It is called **White Libanotis** by Cesalpino. The illustration of this plant is not well-presented by any author; therefore, I judged that the umbel and the leaf of this and the following two species should be drawn and engraved anew. The second set of leaves is shown by **Parkinson** in the appendix to his "Theater" under the title **Galen’s Genuine Libanotis Leaf**, but no seeds are depicted. The first is also shown by Parkinson under the name **Cachrys-bearing Libanotis**, or **True Cachrys**; an illustration of the same is also given by **J. Bauhin** with the seed. But since it is nowhere well-depicted, I thought it would not be out of place to present illustrations of all three of these **Libanotids**, with the leaves and seeds that establish their specific differences. Let whoever discovers more species of this genus add them. I do not speak of things I have not seen (as do the majority of botanists who merely transcribe from others). See **Plate Three of the Illustrations**, and consult the **General Table of Umbel Seeds** marked **Aa Bb Cc**.*

### Powers and Temperament.

*It is of a hot and dry temperament, and has the power to heat and dry vehemently, as do all the **frankincense-bearing Umbels**. It provokes the menses, draws out urine, and excites sweating. Regarding its powers, consult **Jean Bauhin**, page 38, part 1, volume three, column 1, who there describes at length the faculties or powers of **Libanotis**.*

<summary>This page describes the classification and physical characteristics of "Pill-bearing" umbelliferous plants, specifically the genus Cachrys and Libanotis. Morison emphasizes the importance of accurate botanical illustration and personal observation over merely copying previous authors.</summary>
<keywords>Cachrys, Libanotis, Umbelliferae, Robert Morison, Edward Morgan, Westminster Physic Garden, botanical classification, frankincense-scented plants, seed morphology, Jacques Barrelier</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of Umbelliferous plants (the carrot and parsley family) by Robert Morison. Having previously discussed those with "pill-shaped" seeds, he now moves to the second major group: those with "striated" or grooved seeds. He establishes a hierarchy based on leaf shape to help the reader distinguish between similar-looking plants.</meta>

UMBELLIFEROUS PLANTS. / UMBELS PROVIDED WITH STRIATED SEEDS. / The Second Genus of Umbels.

# CHAPTER II.

The second subordinate Genus of *Umbels* consists of those that produce striated <note>grooved or fluted</note> seeds, and these constitute the third part of this Section (namely the *Umbels*). We shall divide these into four parts according to the fourfold placement and arrangement of their leaves.

*Umbels* endowed with striated seeds either have giant-fennel-like or fennel-like leaves <note>original: "ferulaceis, fœniculaceisve"</note>, meaning the leaves are divided and dissected as finely as possible in the manner of giant fennel or fennel. Or, in the second place, they have leaves arranged in lobes, pinnately <note>feather-like</note>, or in pairs; that is, many leaf-lobes are placed on either side of the middle nerve or rachis <note>the main stem of a compound leaf</note>, either directly opposite or alternately, with a single lobe closing the tip. Or, in the third place, they hold a middle ground, being neither fennel-like nor lobed, but divided manifold in an intermediate fashion. In the fourth and last place, those *Umbels* endowed with striated seeds whose leaves possess some unique characteristic will be added. In the first place, we encounter the *Umbels* enriched with striated seeds and provided with fennel-like leaves; and these are of two kinds: those with thicker and longer seeds, or those with shorter and smaller striated seeds. We shall speak of the former now, and of the latter further below.

---

->## *UMBELS WITH FENNEL-LIKE LEAVES*<-
->### *Provided with larger striated seeds.*<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;{ Common ☉
*Fennel* {
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;{ Sweet ☉
*Cumin* ♀
*Spignel* <note>original: "Meum athamanticum," also known as Baldmoney</note> ☉

---

->### *Explanation of the preceding Table.*<-

**Fennel** is the first *Umbelliferous* plant of those provided with larger striated seeds and fennel-like leaves. Fennel is so well known to everyone that it is not worth the effort to dwell on many of the characteristics it shares in common with other giant-fennel-like plants. It is more easily known by its scent and taste than by description compared to others of its class. It is either the "common" kind, whose scent is milder; or the "sweet" kind, which resembles the common in all its parts, except for the sweeter taste alone and a more intense fragrant scent, found in both the leaves and the seeds.

->### *Place and time.*<-

*The common kind grows on the maritime cliffs of Brittany <note>original: "Armoricis"</note> and other warm and stony places; the sweet kind grows in Italy and warm regions. When brought to northern regions and propagated by cultivation from repeated sowing, it degenerates into the common kind. Both flower in summer and produce seed toward the end of autumn.*

->### *Names.*<-

*The first is the "common" kind of Gerard <note>John Gerard, author of the 1597 'Herball'</note>; the "common" of Gessner’s 'Gardens'; and the "garden" kind of Cesalpino. This differs from the wild kind or the 'Marathrum' <note>the ancient Greek name for fennel</note> of the ancients only by sowing, just as many other wild plants, when transferred to gardens through artifice <note>original: "mangonio," referring to the 'improving' or 'polishing' of plants through horticulture</note> and cultivation, become garden varieties. The second is the "sweet" kind of Mattioli, Lobel (in his 'Adversaria'), Gerard, and Dodoens. We also have the sweet Fennel of the Azores.*

<meta>This page continues the botanical descriptions of the second group of Umbellifers—those with striated (grooved) seeds and fennel-like leaves. Morison focuses here on Fennel, Cumin, and Meum (Spignel), providing their physical characteristics, medicinal uses (vires), and nomenclature according to historical authorities like Dioscorides, Bauhin, and Gerard.</meta>

# THE CARROT FAMILY (UMBELLIFERÆ)

...whose rays, or stalks, supporting the seeds are much thicker and longer, and form a concave surface; the seeds are likewise much larger.

### Properties and powers.

*Fennel is hot and dry. Its seed, boiled and drunk, is a help against the bites of serpents; it induces the monthly courses <note>menstruation</note>, and increases milk in nursing mothers, as Tragus <note>the Latinized name of Hieronymus Bock, a German botanist</note> witnesses. Consult Johannes Bauhin near the beginning of the second part of Volume 3, who teaches at quite some length the powers of fennel as handed down by various authors.*

Cumin with leaves and a larger striated seed very much resembles the former (namely, Fennel). It is the lowest plant of its whole Kind, being half a foot or barely a foot in height; at the tops of the stems the seeds are long and striated, rivaling the seeds of fennel in shape and size, but it is of a sharp and most unpleasant Taste, smelling of something quite foul, and even the seed itself is very disagreeable to the palate.

### Place and time.

*It grows abundantly in the Mediterranean islands of Malta, Crete, Cyprus, and various regions of Eastern Syria. It flowers in Summer, and brings its seed to completion toward the end of the same.*

### Names.

Cumin of the shops by Caesalpinus; the cultivated by Tragus, Matthiolus, and Dodoens; the common by Parkinson; that of Dioscorides by Gerard—which cannot be ours, since it is unpleasant and disagreeable to the mouth, whereas Dioscorides says of cultivated Cumin <note>original: "sativo Cumino"</note> that it is "pleasant to the mouth."

### Properties and powers.

*Cumin is hot and dry. Its seed dispels wind <note>original: "flatus", meaning intestinal gas</note> and heals colicky pains. Regarding the powers of Cumin, consult Johannes Bauhin, Page 23, Part 2, Volume 3, column 1.*

Meum Athamanticum <note>known as Spignel, Meu, or Bearwort</note> is much taller than Cumin, but lower than fennel, from which it differs in the divisions or notches of the leaves (which are indeed denser) and likewise in its very color; for it has leaves of a paler green, more finely dissected than the leaves of fennel itself, and much shorter. Above all, however, the taste and smell are completely different from fennel and cumin; the roots are sometimes single, sometimes several, longer than a span <note>original: "dodrante", a measurement of about nine inches</note>, sometimes thinner, sometimes thicker; from the sides of these arise other oblique roots, long enough, and they are not usually found growing attached to other roots in this way, but are mostly seen to stretch upwards.

### Place and time.

*It grows on the mountains of Auvergne <note>in France</note>, and other high places of Italy, Gaul, and Britain. It produces seed in summer, but not in great abundance, because the root is extensive, spreading itself here and there—not suddenly, but seeking the upper parts—therefore nature is not so intent upon its production through seeds.*

### Names.

Meum by Matthiolus, the History of Lyon <note>original: "Hist. Lugd."</note>, Dodoens, and Gerard; the more common by Parkinson. Common Meu or Bear-root <note>original: "radix ursina"</note> by J. Bauhin. Athamantic by Dioscorides; Cretan Carrot <note>original: "daucus Creticus"</note> by Fuchs, Tragus, and the illustrations of Lobelius.

### Properties and powers.

*Meum is hot and dry in the third degree; it stirs the urine and provokes the monthly courses. Its root, infused and steeped in beer <note>original: "cervisia"</note>, cures discharges of the chest and shortness of breath; see J. Bauhin, Page 2, Part 2, Volume 3, col. 1, who treats it more extensively there. Regarding the larger striated seeds, see the General Table of Illustrations of seeds Dd, Ee, Ff, and its explanation. Regarding the giant-fennel-like leaves <note>original: "foliis ferulaceis"</note>, see the Table of Illustrations, 4, where the leaf of fennel is depicted, after the fashion of the other Umbellifers gifted with larger striated seeds and giant-fennel-like leaves.*

It also attempts to reconcile conflicting botanical names used by classical and contemporary authorities like Dioscorides and Bauhin.</summary>

# THE UMBELLIFERS (THE CARROT FAMILY)
<note>The term Umbelliferae refers to plants whose flower stalks radiate from a single point like the ribs of an umbrella.</note>

## UMBELLIFERS WITH FENNEL-LIKE LEAVES
### Provided with smaller striated seeds.

| Name | Life Cycle |
| :--- | :--- |
| False or bastard Spignel <note>original: "Meum spurium seu adulterinum"</note> | ☉ <note>This symbol indicates an annual plant, completing its life cycle in one year.</note> |
| Pignut or Earth-nut <note>original: "Bulbocastanum"</note> | ☉ |
| Small mountain Milk-parsley, according to Clusius <note>original: "Selinum montanum pumilum"</note> | ♃ <note>This symbol indicates a perennial plant, which lives for many years.</note> |
| Pannonian Saxifrage, according to Clusius <note>original: "Saxifraga pannonica"</note> | ☉ |
| Spanish Picktooth <note>original: "Visnaga"</note> | ♃ |

---

->Explanation of the preceding Table.<-

**F**alse Spignel <note>original: "Meum spurium"</note>. This is the first of the Umbellifers with giant-fennel-like leaves <note>original: "Ferulacearum"</note> that possess smaller and shorter striated seeds. It has harder, thicker, and bushier leaves than the common kind, with a somewhat poisonous smell; in some way, it mimics the narrow-leaved French Rue. From the base of these leaves, slanted stems emerge, reaching a foot or sometimes a foot and a half in height, supporting the smaller grooved seeds. The root is larger, with the appearance and consistency of Hog’s Fennel, dark on the outside, and having a foul and unpleasant taste as well as smell. I do not know by what right authors have given it the name "False Spignel," since it has no affinity with Spignel <note>original: "Meo Athamantico"</note> in smell, taste, or other characteristics. It closer approaches the class of Meadow Saxifrage <note>original: "seselion"</note> (following Johannes Pona) in both smell and taste; but since it is an Umbelliferous plant with fennel-like leaves and smaller striated seeds, we have—being little concerned with names—reduced it to the group of its own likeness.

->Place and time.<-

*False Spignel grows everywhere on dry hills in France <note>original: "Gallia"</note>. It flowers toward the end of summer. It brings its seed to completion at the beginning of autumn.*

->Names.<-

*The Second Italian False Spignel by the Lyons History <note>original: "Lugd." refers to Dalechamps</note>; Italian False Spignel by Parkinson; the Second Italian False Kind by some, such as Johannes Bauhin, Lobelius, and the Lyons History.*

->Temperament and powers.<-

*It is of a hot and dry temperament, and it excels in the same powers as all the narrow-leaved Burnet-saxifrages <note>original: "Pimpinella Saxifraga"</note>.*

**P**ignut <note>original: "Bulbocastanum"</note>. This is a plant with giant-fennel-like leaves, also endowed with smaller and shorter striated seeds; it is very easy to distinguish from the others of its class because it is the only Umbellifer having a tuberous root consisting of bulb-shaped nodules. It produces these in the likeness of a truffle—black or brown on the outside, white within, tender, and with a quite pleasant and sweet taste. It is eaten both raw and cooked, like the fruit of the chestnut tree, called "marrons" <note>original: "marones"</note>.

->Place and time.<-

*It grows among crops, and in dry mountainous, clay-heavy, neglected places. Around Paris and London, the Pignut is quite common. It flowers and completes its seeding toward the end of summer.*

->Names.<-

*It is most often called "Bulbocastanum" <note>literally "Bulb-chestnut"</note> by authors. It is the Bunium <note>original Greek: Βούνιον</note> of Gesner and Dioscorides. It is the "Earth-nut of the Northerners" by Lobelius. Commonly called "Pancaseolus" <note>Bread-and-cheese</note> by the Italians, according to Caesalpinus—a name fashioned from "pane" (bread) and "caseo" (cheese), whose place it takes in meals when the tuberous root is dug from the earth, whether raw or cooked. The Pignut found its name from the shape of a bulb and the scent of a chestnut.*

<summary>This page describes False Spignel and Pignut, focusing on their physical characteristics, such as the Pignut's unique edible tuberous root, and correcting previous taxonomic misidentifications by other botanists.</summary>
<keywords>Meum spurium, False Spignel, Bulbocastanum, Pignut, Umbelliferae, botanical classification, medicinal plants, edible roots, Clusius, Parkinson, Dioscorides</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific descriptions of the Umbellifers provided with smaller striated seeds. It follows from the descriptions of False Spignel and begins with the medicinal properties of the Pignut, before moving on to Milk-parsley, Pannonian Saxifrage, and the Spanish Picktooth.</meta>

# THE UMBELLIFERS

### Qualities and medicinal properties.

The **Pignut** <note>original: "Bulbocastanum"</note> is of a hot and dry nature; it is moderately astringent, and its root is beneficial for those who spit or urinate blood. See more extensively in Jean Bauhin, Page 30, Volume 3, Part 2.

**Small mountain Milk-parsley** <note>original: "Selinum pumilum montanum"</note>. Of all the fennel-like plants <note>original: "Ferulacearum"</note> provided with smaller and shorter striated <note>grooved</note> seeds, its *Umbel* <note>the flower head</note> is the smallest; for it hardly exceeds a span <note>about 9 inches</note> in height. Furthermore, it splits itself into many small branches and is provided with short, fennel-like leaves.

### Habitat and season.

It grows on dry and gravelly hills around La Rochelle <note>original: "Rupellam"</note>, and Bourbon-l’Archambault <note>original: "caput Borbonum"</note>, and elsewhere at the edges of fields, and on the bare and grassy mountains of Austria and Pannonia <note>an ancient Roman province covering parts of modern-day Hungary and Austria</note>, according to Clusius. It flowers at the end of May. Toward mid-summer, it produces seeds that are thicker and more swollen than would be expected for the size of the plant; See the *General Table of Seed Illustrations, number 2.*

### Names.

*Mountain Carrot with multi-cleft leaves and Milk-parsley seeds, according to Caspar Bauhin. Small mountain Milk-parsley, according to Clusius’s History. Mountain Parsley, according to Tabernaemontanus.*

### Qualities and medicinal properties.

It is of a hot and dry temperament; therefore, it opens <note>relieves blockages</note>, thins the humors, and induces sweating.

**Pannonian Saxifrage, according to Clusius.** It has fennel-like leaves, much longer and thinner than the small Milk-parsley mentioned above. It sends out stems a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> or a cubit and a half high. Perhaps this plant is the same as the English Bur-parsley of Pena; but how poorly it would be called a *Caucalis* <note>Bur-parsley</note>, since it does not have bristly or prickly seeds, but rather smaller and shorter striated ones. The reader may see this below in our own system of the *Umbellifers*, arranged through Tables of kinship or affinity.

### Habitat and season.

*Pannonian Saxifrage* grows quite commonly in the royal forest of Fontainebleau, in the place called *Bois Bouronne*. It is of a hot and dry temperament, like the small mountain Milk-parsley, which is immediately apparent to anyone tasting the seeds of either. Consequently, its seed is no less beneficial for those suffering from bladder stones <note>original: "calculosis"</note> than the other species of *Saxifrage*.

### Names.

*Mountain Carrot with many-cleft and long leaves, according to our naming. Erroneously called "with short leaves" by Caspar Bauhin. Pannonian Saxifrage, according to Clusius’s History.*

### Qualities and medicinal properties.

It is endowed with the same powers as *all the umbelliferous Saxifrages*, as far as we have been able to gather from the chewing of the seeds, which are gifted with an equal heat and flavor.

**Spanish Picktooth or Gingidium** <note>original: "Visnaga seu Gingidium"</note>. This is a *Fennel-like Umbellifer* provided with smaller and shorter striated seeds. Its rays, or stalks, which arise from the top of the stem and support small white flowers, form a flat surface; however, the mature seeds form a concave surface, like a small bird's nest, in the manner of a Carrot, or the Syrian Hartwort with larger granulated seeds (which is called *Syrian Bur-parsley* by authors). This concave surface is common to a few other *Umbels*. The rays or stalks of the *Visnaga*, resting upon the upper top of the stem, are the stiffest of all the *Umbellifers* and are somewhat fragrant: for this reason, the Spanish use them in place of a toothpick <note>original: "dentiscalpii vice"</note> to clean away things that stick to the teeth from food, once the seeds are ripe and the stalks have finally turned hard. Its green leaves, when crushed, have a heavy scent in the manner of hemlock; moreover, both the leaves and roots are bitter.

### Habitat and season.

It grows of its own accord among the grain crops of the warm regions of Spain, Narbonnese Gaul, and Provence.

### Names.

*Gingidium, or Visnaga*, is the name commonly used by almost all authors. *Another kind of cultivated Cumin, according to Cesalpino.*

-># THE UMBELLIFERS. 7<-

### Qualities and medicinal properties.

The **Spanish Picktooth** <note>original: "Visnaga"</note>, according to the testimony of Galen <note>a prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note>, dries in the second degree <note>a classification in humoral medicine indicating its potency</note>. Inasmuch as it is bitter, it possesses a manifest heat and an astringent quality. Consult the *General Table of Seed Illustrations, Gg Hh Ii Kk Ll*, and the explanation of that same table regarding seeds. Regarding the smaller fennel-like <note>original: "Ferulacea"</note> leaves, see the *Table of Illustrations, 4*, where we have classified my "false" variety alongside the other Umbellifers provided with smaller fennel-like leaves, and we have seen to the engraving of its fennel-like leaf.

---

### Explanation of the preceding Table.

The second division of *Umbellifers* <note>plants with umbrella-like flower clusters</note> provided with striated <note>grooved</note> seeds shall be those that bear their leaves in a lobed, paired, or pinnate <note>feather-like</note> fashion along the mid-rib or rachis <note>the main axis of a compound leaf</note>. These leaves are positioned sometimes opposite one another and sometimes alternately, growing upon the rib, with one leaf always closing the tip. These are likewise divided into two types: the larger ones, provided with larger seeds; or the smaller ones, endowed with smaller seeds. We shall speak of the former first, and the latter afterwards.

**Lovage** <note>original: "Levisticum"</note>. Of all the lobed *Umbellifers* provided with larger striated seeds, this is the largest; so much so that its stem is round, thick, jointed, exceeding the height of a man, hollow, and grooved. From the root, it brings forth leaves on long, upright stalks, which are longer and paler—or a more dilute green—than the leaves of broad-leaved celery. These same stalks have a strongly medicinal scent, which is not without pleasantness if they are broken and held to the nostrils. It bears yellow, flat umbels. As the individual flowers fade, each with five yellow, reflexed <note>bent backward</note> petals joined together, the plump seeds succeed them in pairs; these are larger than fennel seeds, grooved, highly fragrant, and smelling of something almost resinous.

#### Habitat and season.

*Where it arises spontaneously is not yet certain to Botanists; it is observed only in gardens. It produces yellow, reflexed flowers in mid-summer, which are scarcely observed fully expanded because of nature's great haste toward the formation and perfection of the seeds. It perfects the seed as summer departs.*

#### Names.

*Lovage, according to Matthiolus, Dodonaeus, and Lobelius; Common Lovage, according to Parkinson; Common Libysticum, according to Fuchs.*

# THE UMBELLIFERS.
### Page 8

### Temperament and medicinal properties.

* **Lovage** <note>original: "Levisticum"</note> *belongs to the order of drying and heating plants; it is no less useful for driving out poisons than **Angelica**. The root of Lovage is beneficial for a "cold stomach" <note>in humoral medicine, a stomach lacking the "heat" necessary for digestion</note>; it aids digestion and consumes excess humors. When drunk in wine at the weight of half a drachm <note>original: ʒ ß; about 2 grams</note>, it expels poison and stimulates the flow of urine and menstruation. It is used in sitz-baths and standard baths to provoke urine, induce menses, and bring forth stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>. It warms all the internal organs. Women in childbirth use the seed in food and drink, and add it to sweat-baths so they may be properly cleansed after delivery. These details are from Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock, a 16th-century German botanist</note>. Whoever desires more concerning its powers should consult Johann Bauhin, page 123, Volume 3, Part 2, who brings to light in that place all the powers assigned to it by all botanical authors at quite some length.*

**Mountain Laserwort** <note>original: "Siler montanum"</note>. This plant closely resembles the former (namely, Lovage) in the height of its stem. However, the leaves themselves are divided into opposite "wings" <note>pinnate leaflets</note> on stalks the length of a palm or a palm and a half <note>about 3 to 5 inches</note>. The outer segments are almost always in groups of three, oblong, somewhat wide, with a slightly blunt tip, similar to the leaves of the **Laburnum** <note>original: "cithyfi"</note> described by Tragus. The seed is large, oblong, and striated <note>ribbed</note>, with an unpleasant taste very similar to **Cumin**. The lowest part of the stem, near the surface of the earth, is adorned with a fibrous and wrinkled "beard" <note>original: "coma"</note>, as one may see in **Sulphur-wort** <note>original: "Peucedanum"</note> and various other fragrant Umbellifers.

### Place and time.

***Ligusticum** <note>another name for Lovage or its relatives</note> is so named from the Ligurian or Apennine mountains, because it grows there. It is found not far from Genoa on rugged mountains. It also grows on the mountain overlooking the thermal baths of Baden, where it grows more copiously and abundantly than anywhere else. Carolus Clusius <note>a highly influential 16th-century botanist</note> observed it growing throughout all of Austria. It flowers in the month of June and perfects its seed in July and August.*

### Names.

**Mountain Laserwort**: according to Tragus, Lobel, and Dodonaeus. **Common Mountain Seseli or Laserwort**: according to Johann Bauhin. **Mountain Laserwort, commonly called Seseli**: according to Parkinson. **Ligusticum**: according to Matthiolus and the "History of Plants of Lyon" <note>original: "Lugd." or Historia Plantarum Lugdunensis</note>.

### Temperament and medicinal properties.

*The power of this seed is heating and drying in the third degree <note>a high level of potency in the four-degree scale of Galenic medicine</note>; it draws out urine and menses when drunk. It promotes digestion. See Johann Bauhin, pages 169 and 170, Volume 3, Part 2.*

**Columbine-leaved Laserwort** <note>original: "Siler aquilegiæ foliis"</note>, so named by us. It is called this because it corresponds more closely with the **Laserworts** in the scent and taste of the seed than with any species of **Moonwort** <note>original: "Libanotidum"; literally "incense-herbs"</note>—which we properly defined at the beginning of this treatise on Umbellifers as those having seeds enclosed in a spongy substance. It was inadvertently called *Libanotis* by Caspar Bauhin, except for the fact that it pours out a juice from its pith that smells like something between a spice and **Birthwort** <note>original: "Aristolochiam"</note> in flavor; yet it happens that many different species of Umbellifers pour out fragrant juices. Based on the scent, it is more aptly reduced to the **Laserworts** than to the **Lovages** (as Johann Bauhin does) or to the **Seselis** (as Camerarius does). Whether it be called *Siler*, *Ligusticum*, or *Seseli*, it has a larger striated seed; therefore, I do not admit it to be a species of *Libanotis*, which has a spongy seed. Otherwise, if all incense-bearing Umbellifers served under one and the same name without any regard for the shape of the seeds, but only for the incense-like scent they breathe out, the same confusion would remain that has existed in these recent centuries. Among our more general observations, we shall provide a nomenclature of the Umbellifers, including those that offer pleasing and delightful liquids, as well as those that are unpleasant and foul-smelling. Among all the Umbellifers, this species of Laserwort is recognized at first glance because the leaves are thick, rigid, smooth, green, and shining above, while becoming white underneath. They are divided like the leaves of the **Columbine** <note>original: "aquilegiæ"</note>; the lower ones on a more vigorous plant usually have five lobes from a triple division of the stalk, while elsewhere they have three lobes sitting on three stalks. It has a single stem, rarely exceeding two or three cubits <note>about 3 to 4.5 feet</note>, distributed into several branches, bearing a large umbel with white flowers—

# THE UMBELLIFERS. 9

...bearing a small composite [umbel]. It produces seeds in pairs that are long, compressed, and ribbed, with a somewhat bitter taste imitating cumin and smelling of something aromatic; if these are chewed, they warm the mouth moderately and stimulate the flow of saliva.

### Location & time.

->*It grows on Mount Gargano in Apulia <note>a region in Southern Italy</note>. It flowers in summer.*<-

### Names.

We call it **Laserwort with Columbine leaves** <note>original: "Siler aquilegiæ foliis"</note>. It is listed as **Broad-leaved Frankincense-herb with Columbine leaves** <note>original: "Libanotis latifolia aquilegiæ folio"</note> in Caspar Bauhin’s *Prodromus* and in Parkinson. It is the **Ethiopian Sesame** <note>original: "Seseli Æthiopicum"</note> of Camerarius; and the **Lovage with Rauwolf’s Columbine leaves** <note>original: "ligusticum, Rawolffii foliis aquilegiæ"</note> of Johann Bauhin, who provides an incomplete illustration of it.

### Temperament & powers.

->*It heats and dries in the second degree; it opens [blockages], cuts [thick humors], and thins [the fluids]; it appears bitter and sharp upon chewing.*<-

**Angelica.** It has large leaves that are lobed at the base and divided into three parts (in the manner of the plants previously described), sometimes more and sometimes less divided, attached to small branches with one leaf always closing the tip. If the stalks or leaves are broken, they pour out a scent pleasing to the nostrils. The seeds are much shorter than those of the preceding plants and more fragrant; they are gifted with deeper ridges or furrows—that is, the "wings" or chaffy, membrane-like ridges are raised higher than in any other **Umbellifers** endowed with large, ribbed seeds.

1. The first is the **cultivated** or **apothecary’s Angelica**, so called from its cultivation, scent, taste, and the saffron-colored juice that flows when it is cut. It is known even to "the bleary-eyed and the barbers" <note>original: "lippis & tonsoribus," a Latin idiom meaning "everyone knows it," even those with poor sight or common folk at the barbershop</note>; therefore, we shall not linger long in assigning its common characteristics (which it shares with others); you may see the plant cultivated in gardens.

2. The second is the **wild Angelica**, because it flourishes in damp woods or comes forth on the banks of streams and in marshy places. It is similar to the cultivated kind in every way, except that its scent and taste are not so powerful, and it yields much to the cultivated kind in fragrance. In its leaves, seeds, and all other parts (excepting color and smallness), it is a rival to the cultivated type.

3. The third is the **Mountain Wild Angelica**, or **Archangelica**, according to Clusius. This plant surpasses the cultivated one in the size of its leaves and the frequent number of individual leaflets. The small branches of this plant provide a great many leaves. The seed is broad, longer, and thicker; the root is deep. The leaves and stems possess a faint and obscure scent. If anyone wishes to make this and the "Wild Mountain" variety into the same species, differing only by birth-place, I shall not object. However, if someone brings forward a plant with a yellow **Umbel** (as Lobelius maintains), it will undoubtedly be a distinct species.

4. The fourth is the **Shining Canadian Angelica** of Cornut. It is very easily distinguished from the others at first sight because it bears shining leaves (its stem barely reaches a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> in height); they are so glossy that they appear to anyone looking at them to be coated in birdlime or some other shining liquid.

5. The fifth is called **Goutweed** <note>original: "podagraria"</note> from its effect [against gout]. It is easily distinguished from the aforementioned by its root, which spreads itself through the earth, and its black seed, which is smaller than the seeds of the others in its class and wrinkled, with ridges visible only when a **Microscope** is applied. Indeed, among the **Umbellifers** (excepting Bishop's Weed, Masterwort, and a few others), a creeping root is a unique characteristic. Its seed is black and oblong, half the size of the preceding ones, perhaps due to a lack of nourishment, since nature is so intent on its multiplication via the roots creeping underground.

### Location & time.

*The first, **Cultivated Angelica**, grows spontaneously in Scandinavia, Norway, and the high Alps covered in perpetual snow. The second, the **Wild** kind, grows in damp woods and in the marshy margins of brooks and the banks of rivers. The third in the Alpine regions...*
