<meta>This page continues the discussion of the various species of the Angelica genus and introduces Masterwort (Imperatoria) and Master-wort (Astrantia), comparing their botanical features and medicinal virtues.</meta>

# THE UMBELLIFERS. 10

...and is found on other mountains. The 4th species is from the American Islands, brought to us from Canada. The 5th, or Goutweed <term>podagraria: Aegopodium podagraria, a plant often used in the Middle Ages to treat gout</term>, grows in damp places and along the base of hedges. They all flower at the beginning of summer and produce seed shortly after. They do not complete their seeds until the second, or sometimes the third year after sowing; however, after the seed has matured, all of them perish completely except for the long-lived Goutweed.

### Names.

The 1st is called **Cultivated Angelica** <note>original: "Angelica sativa"</note> by Caspar Bauhin, Mattioli, Tragus, and Parkinson; **Scented Angelica** <note>original: "odorata"</note> by Camerarius; and **Greater Angelica** <note>original: "major"</note> by Lobel and Dodoens. The 2nd is the **Greater Wild Angelica** <note>original: "sylvestris major"</note> of Caspar Bauhin, and simply **Wild Angelica** <note>original: "sylvestris"</note> by Tragus, Mattioli, Fuchs, and Dodoens. The 3rd is the **Mountain Wild Angelica or Archangelica** <note>original: "Angelica montana, sylvestris, seu Archangelica"</note> of Clusius and Dodoens. The 4th is the **Shining Canadian Angelica** <note>original: "Canadensis, lucida"</note> of Cornut. The 5th is the **Lesser Wild or Erratic Angelica** <note>original: "sylvestris minor, seu erratica"</note> of Caspar Bauhin, the **Goutweed** <note>original: "podagraria"</note> of Lobel and Pena, and the **Erratic Angelica** <note>original: "erratica"</note> of Thal.

### Temperament & powers.

Angelica is hot and dry; it draws out poison, stirs the blood <note>original: "sanguinem dejicit," likely referring to stimulating menstrual flow or purging humors</note>, and warms the body. If someone is seized by a sudden plague or immoderate sweating, they should take half a dram <note>represented by the symbol ʒ β</note> of Angelica root reduced to powder, mixed with one dram <note>ʒ j</note> of **Theriac** <term>Theriaca: a famous ancient treacle or "universal antidote" made of dozens of ingredients, used against poisons and infectious diseases</term> in three or four spoonfuls of distilled Angelica root water; they should drink this, then retire to bed and cover themselves well, fasting for at least three hours, and they shall find relief. The root, macerated in vinegar and smelled during a time of plague—or even occasionally drinking the vinegar with the root on an empty stomach—preserves one from the plague. Its distilled water and powdered root are beneficial for all internal ailments; finally, its seed is of the highest efficacy along with the root: see Tragus, and consult Johann Bauhin, page 142 of Part 2, Volume 3, and page 143 of the same volume. The Cultivated, Wild, Canadian, and Mountain Archangelica, after they have finished flowering and brought their seed to maturity—which, as I said above, usually happens in the second or third year—rot away into the earth and perish totally; that is, the stalks, roots, and all parts die completely, renewing themselves from the seed falling anew into the ground as the year turns.

**Masterwort** <note>original: "Imperatoria"</note>. So named because of the "imperial" powers with which it is gifted. It has leaves much larger than those of Angelica, usually joined in threes, wrinkled and somewhat rough; each of these is again distributed into three serrated segments or lobes, sometimes more lightly and other times more deeply divided. The stalks reach a height of barely two or at most three feet. They bear small white flowers in an **umbel** <term>umbel: a flower cluster where stalks spring from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</term> (the petals of which fall off easily). The seed is white, somewhat broad, compressed, with sunken streaks, and almost like chaff, often not well-nourished; this happens because of the plant's excessive capacity for spreading through its roots. Indeed, the roots are wrinkled and gifted with creeping lateral fibers by which they propagate themselves. When pulled from the top of the earth, the flesh is white and aromatic, nipping the tongue and warming the mouth. Therefore, it must be concluded that the true and genuine Masterwort is a species of Angelica, based on its scent, flavor, and consequently, its powers.

### Location & time.

Masterwort grows in the mountains of Italy, France, and Germany. It completes its seeds toward the end of summer, but they are rarely well-nourished or filled with pulp. Consequently, they are sterile for the reason mentioned above here, and in the deduction regarding Goutweed.

### Names.

It is called **Greater Masterwort** <note>original: "Imperatoria major"</note> by Caspar Bauhin; **Masterwort** <note>original: "Imperatoria"</note> by Mattioli, Tabernaemontanus, and Gerard; **Common Masterwort or Astrantia** <note>original: "imperatoria seu astrantia vulgaris"</note> by Parkinson; **Magistrantia** by Camerarius; and **Herba Rena** by Cesalpino.

### Temperament & powers.

Masterwort is hot and dry in the third degree; it provokes the menses and urine, and expels stillborn fetuses. It wonderfully disperses gas in the uterus and intestines, as well as in the belly; for this reason, it is extremely useful for the pains of colic and griping of the stomach. For other matters, consult Johann Bauhin, pages 138 and 139, Volume 3, Part 2.

**Master-wort** <note>original: "Astrantia"</note>. This is an Umbelliferous plant and possesses all the conditions of a properly-defined Umbel; therefore, it was carelessly placed with the Hellebores by the most learned Caspar Bauhin in his *Pinax* (with other authors writing before him agreeing). See the hallucinations of Caspar Bauhin in his *Pinax*, pages 377 and 370, and our own *Botanical Preludes*. The leaves of this plant are commonly divided into five...

<meta>The text cuts off here at the catchword "gariter" which likely begins the word "regulariter" (regularly) or a similar description of the leaf segments on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page details the varieties, medicinal uses, and life cycles of Angelica and Masterwort, emphasizing their effectiveness against the plague and digestive issues. The author also critizes the taxonomic placement of Astrantia by earlier botanists like Caspar Bauhin.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Umbelliferae family, moving from the Angelica genus discussed on the previous page to the Black Masterwort (Astrantia) and Alexanders (Smyrnium).</meta>

->**THE UMBELLIFERS. 11**<-

...and are sometimes divided into 7 lobes, closely joined to one another toward the stalk; in the manner of the common mountain sanicle, but of a darker green; from their center emerge stalks a foot and a half high, supporting at their tops many small, broadish, short, greenish leaves, which surround in a star-like fashion the base of the half-inch rays. These rays bear very small flowers, composed of five petals (in the manner of other Umbellifers), the color of peach blossoms, adorned with as many small stamens with reddish tips. As each flower falls away, it is succeeded by two seeds, which are striated, rough, or wrinkled. There is a smaller species of this plant, a span high <note>A "span" (spithama) is an ancient unit of measurement, about 9 inches or the width of an outstretched hand.</note>, which is smaller in all the aforementioned parts and bears seed half as large, as we have observed in the Royal Garden at Blois <note>The Hortus Regius Blæsensis was a significant 17th-century botanical garden in France.</note>.

### Place & time.

*Both species grow in the Alps; they flower in May and June, and shortly thereafter they bring their seeds to maturity. See the general Table of seeds, etc., and Table of Illustrations 4, where we have taken care to have the plant itself newly drawn and engraved, laden with seeds, so that learned Botanists might observe the carelessness and negligence of the aforementioned modern authors. These authors cling tooth and nail to the ancients, calling it black hellebore or veratrum following Dioscorides, simply because* (*if it pleases the gods*) *of its black roots and its ability to purge.*

### Names.

**Black Astrantia** by Lobel. **Female Sanicle** by Gesner and Fuchs. **Black Masterwort** and **Female Sanicle** by Tabernaemontanus. **Black Hellebore with a larger sanicle leaf** by Caspar Bauhin. **Mountain Masterwort** <note>original: "ostertitium montanum"</note> in the descriptions of Tragus. The other species is named **Black Hellebore with a smaller sanicle leaf** by Caspar Bauhin. To us, however, it is more fittingly named with Tabernaemontanus as the **greater black masterwort**, and the other the **lesser black masterwort**, placing and naming them among their own kind.

### Temperament & powers.

*This entire plant has a hot and sharp nature. The root and seed have more sharpness than the stalks and leaves, which—it is worth noting—is a trait common to all Umbellifers; that is, they work more effectively through the seed and root than the leaves. This is because the seeds of all of them are hot, and I exclude neither hemlock nor coriander. Even though the leaves of both of these, while they are still green and full of nourishing sap, give off a foul odor and spread a sort of poisonous quality that is unpleasant and harmful to the sense of smell, their dried seed is nevertheless pleasant in odor and taste. Indeed, the seed shows itself to be hot and dry, even if that quality lay hidden while it was still green and full of sap. Regarding the power of the roots of this Black Masterwort to purge black bile and other humors, Germany provides several very experienced physicians as witnesses, who have successfully restored the melancholic to health by purging them with these roots. Gesner also testifies to this purging faculty in a letter to Occon, in which he reports that he first experienced the powers of Black Astrantia to be almost similar to white hellebore, but milder, purging through the bowels. This confirms the opinion of Dodoens—namely, that it is the black veratrum of Dioscorides. For this reason, Dodoens and Fabius Hildanus prescribe it in a decoction for the treatment of a hardened spleen* <term>schirrhi lienis: a "scirrhus" is a hard tumor or swelling of the spleen</term>. *Fuchs affirms that his "female sanicle" closely approaches the true sanicle in form and faculty, and that in it, as in the male, there is bitterness and astringency. The flowers are white with a dilute color, somewhat purplish, or as Dioscorides says,* "white tinged with purple" <note>original Greek: λευκοὶ ἐπιπορφυροὶ</note>. *Though it may have the faculties of one or the other veratrum, or Hellebore, and be more effective for all purposes, it by no means approaches veratrum, at least regarding its principal parts—namely the flowers and seeds (as is evident from the book of nature). These entirely resemble the umbellifer called Masterwort* <note>original: "ostertitium"</note> *by Tragus. In consideration of its purgative faculty, which I do not deny despite Gesner’s experience, it can be arranged among the purgatives, just like Thapsia (from the root of which they gave the name Turbith) and many other umbellifers. But by reason of the parts required to constitute a perfect umbellifer, I contend by best right that it is indeed an umbellifer, and that in taste, smell, and the form of its other essential parts, it entirely approaches the Black Masterwort—so named aptly enough from the blackness of its roots. See Illustration Table 4, where you have the plant itself, that is, the leaves, stalks, and the umbel laden with striated, rough seeds.*

**ALEXANDERS** <note>original: "Smyrnium"</note>. It received its name from its place of birth. There are two species given; the first and larger one rests upon a thick root, white inside and out, fragrant, and sharp with a certain bitterness. It produces solid stalks, more than a cubit <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches, the length of a forearm.</note> high. The leaves are larger than those of marsh celery, dark green, spread over the ground near the base; they grow in three-parted sets on their own stalks or petioles before the plant itself begins to produce a main stem. The flowers on the cubit-high stalks (more or less, depending on the soil in which it is planted) sit at the very tops, composed of 5 white petals. The seeds are thick, black, striated or angular and slightly wrinkled, solid, twice as thick and larger than the seeds of all the aforementioned plants of its class. They cling to each other in pairs, curved at the part where they join; by that very characteristic, Alexanders is easily recognized from others of its race...

<meta>catchword: nium (the continuation of Smyrnium)</meta>

<summary>This page describes Black Masterwort (Astrantia nigra), arguing for its classification as an Umbellifer rather than a Hellebore, and begins the description of Alexanders (Smyrnium).</summary>
<keywords>Astrantia nigra, Masterwort, Black Hellebore, Sanicula, Smyrnium, Alexanders, botanical classification, purging, melancholy, Dioscorides, Gesner</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Umbelliferæ (carrot family), moving from Black Masterwort to the genus Smyrnium (Alexanders), and then providing a taxonomic classification of smaller lobed species like Burnet-saxifrage and Water-parsnip.</meta>

->## THE UMBELLIFERS. 12<-

The second species of this genus <note>original: "Smyrnium"</note> is very easily distinguished from the first because of the root it sends down into the earth, which is turnip-shaped <term>napiformem: shaped like a turnip or "napus"</term> in the first year after germination, and sometimes in the second. Furthermore, it should be noted that nature plays a wondrous game in the arrangement of this plant's leaves: for the first leaves it produces, which are spread across the ground, are deeply incised in many ways in the manner of marsh parsley <note>original: "apii palustris"</note>; those which grow immediately from the root are saw-toothed <note>serrated</note> around their edges; but those which sit below the branching of the small stalks are of a circular roundness, like perfoliate leaves <term>perfoliatæ: where the stem appears to pass through the center of the leaf</term>, being pierced or bored through by the stalk and branches. It bears seed that is dark or brownish-black, somewhat round, similar to the seed of the great cabbage. Among all the Umbellifers, one cannot find another similar to this because of the marvelous variation of the lower leaves spread on the ground and the upper leaves, or "wings," which embrace the stems below in the shape of a saucer, as seen in the Honeysuckle <note>original: "Periclymeno"</note>. The seeds of both the larger species and this one are marked by three prominent ridges <note>original: "striis, seu carinis"</note> and deeper grooves; they are black, wrinkled, warm, and highly aromatic.

### Place & time.

*The first grows in the islands adjacent to that most powerful and flourishing island where we first drew breath, Great Britain. The second was brought to us from the island of Crete.*

### Names.

*The first is called **Smyrnium** by Mattioli, Dalechamps, and Camerarius; **Alexandrian Parsley** <note>original: "Petroselinum Alexandrinum"</note> by Tragus; **Horse-parsley** <note>original: "Hipposelinum"</note> by Fuchs and Dodoens; **Common Horse-parsley** or **Smyrnium** by Parkinson; and **Smyrnium with large black seed** or **Macerone** by Jean Bauhin. The second is **Cretan Smyrnium** by Mattioli, Dalechamps, Camerarius, Gerard, and Parkinson; **Smyrnium of Mount Amanus** by Dodoens; and **Another perfoliate plant** by Dalechamps.*

### Temperament & virtues.

*The first is of a warm and dry temperament. It excels Angelica and Lovage in pungency and heat, and in its power to expel; in other respects, they have similar properties. See Jean Bauhin, page 124, Volume 3, Part 2, Column 1, who treats there at length the use of Smyrnium among the ancients. Regarding the larger ridged seeds of the aforementioned Umbellifers, consult the general Table of seed illustrations Mm Nn Oo Pp Qq Rr Sf. For the lobed leaves of the Umbellifers in this group, see Table of Illustrations 4, where Lovage and Masterwort are offered as examples of all other lobed Umbellifers of this branch.*

<meta>This page continues the classification of the Umbelliferæ (carrot family), transitioning from the Alexanders (Smyrnium) discussed on the previous page to Skirret (Sifarum) and the Burnet-saxifrages (Pimpinella).</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

## Explanation of the Plate Above.

Skirret <term>Sifarum: also known as Water Parsnip or Crummock; a sweet-rooted vegetable popular in the Renaissance</term> claims the first place among the lobed umbels provided with smaller striated <note>grooved or striped</note> seeds. What is most worthy of note, and what distinguishes it from others of its family, is that it has fleshy roots—several growing from a single head—hanging toward the surface of the earth. These roots are tender, brittle, and covered with a thin, pale skin; the inner flesh is white, about the thickness of a finger and a foot long (though sometimes shorter), with a sweet, aromatic, and quite pleasant flavor. They are frequently sought after in the kitchen throughout the winter; when fried with butter, vinegar, and pepper and served at the table, they are eaten as a delicacy during the season of Lent <note>original: "quadragesimali tempore"</note>. Indeed, they are preferred over the broad-leaved German parsnip and are more highly valued among sweetmeats because of their superior sweetness. Skirret is easily discerned from other umbels of this sub-group by its smaller, smooth, lobed leaves which attach to the midrib in opposite pairs, its smaller striated seed, and especially by the aforementioned roots.

->**Place & time.**<-

*Skirret grows wild in Narbonne Gaul <note>Southern France</note>, according to the testimony of Lobelius. It flowers and perfects its seed in mid-summer.*

->**Names.**<-

*Sifarum, according to Dodonaeus, Tragus, and Lobelius. Siser, according to Matthiolus, Cordus, Gesner, and Camerarius. Common Siser, according to Parkinson. Sifarum of many, according to J. Bauhin. Skirret of the Germans, according to C. Bauhin’s Pinax.*

->**Temperament & virtues.**<-

*Skirret is temperately warm and moist. The roots are easily digested in the stomach and provide moderate nourishment; they excite the passions <note>original: "excitant ad venerem" — a common Renaissance claim for sweet root vegetables</note>, and they do not produce "bad juices," though they possess a certain flatulence. They also promote the flow of urine.*

Burnet-saxifrage <term>Pimpinella saxifraga: literally "the little pepper-like stone-breaker," so named for its supposed ability to break up kidney stones</term>. It is so named by authors because of its effects; however, we call it an Umbellifer because of its essential character, to distinguish it from the Salad Burnet <note>original: "pimpinella sanguisorba"</note>. The latter is named by authors for its effect <note>stopping bleeding</note>, but we call it "spiked," and we shall speak of it at the end of the Umbellifers. While the Salad Burnet is an Umbellifer in the arrangement of its lobed leaves, it departs from the true rule of the Umbellifers in its spike, as established at the beginning of this treatise, since it possesses neither flowers nor seeds in pairs in the manner of the Umbellifers properly so called by us; we shall discuss Salad Burnet more at length later in its proper place.

The Greater Burnet-saxifrage is the first of the lobed Umbellifers gifted with smaller striated seeds. It possesses a midrib one foot or half a foot long, depending on the fertility or barrenness of the soil; to this rib many lobes are attached in opposite pairs, with one lobe closing the end of the rib, which is a common and familiar trait of all this sort. From the center of the stalks rises a stem two feet, or sometimes three feet high. At its summit, after the flowers have vanished, two smaller, striated, short seeds grow on each ray (in the manner of all other Umbellifers). These seeds, when compared with others of their class, are larger; indeed, the plant itself is larger in all its parts, and possesses larger and more saw-toothed leaves, except in the degenerate variety. There is a second "greater" kind, which is a degenerate form from the seeding of the first; it resembles the first in everything, except that it has leaves more finely divided or laciniated <note>fringed or slashed</note>; hence it is aptly called by us the "other degenerate greater" kind. The third is the "lesser," whose leaves are arranged in pairs or lobes along the midrib or central nerve, with one similarly attached at the end. The leaves of this one are so similar to the leaves of the Lesser Salad Burnet that they can scarcely—if at all—be distinguished from one another before they grow a stem and expand their flowers...

<keywords>Skirret, Sifarum, Burnet-saxifrage, Pimpinella saxifraga, Salad Burnet, root vegetables, Renaissance botany, medicinal plants, taxonomy, Lenten foods</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Umbellifer family, specifically focusing on the different varieties of Burnet-saxifrage (Pimpinella) and Stone Parsley (Sifon), following the description of Skirret.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

can be distinguished <note>This sentence continues from the previous page's discussion of different varieties</note> except by those most skilled in Botany; hence the ancient verse used among botanists: *Burnet-saxifrage has no hairs, Saxifrage has none at all.* <note>original: "Pimpinella pilos Saxifraga non habet ullos." This mnemonic helped distinguish Pimpinella from other similar-looking plants.</note> Of this smaller kind, another variety is also found, whose leaves are dissected in many ways and are more hoary, as if covered in ash. Near the base, these leaves attach to the midrib <note>the central stalk of the leaf</note> in lobes or feathers, though as the plant grows a stem, they become more finely cut. For this reason, it is aptly called *Tragofelinum petreum* <term>Tragofelinum: literally "Goat-parsley," an old name for certain aromatic umbellifers</term> or "curled" by Theodorus Tabernaemontanus. Whether someone calls this smaller curled variety a divergent form of the previous smaller one (as we said of the larger one above) will not be disputed by me; since these two—whether they be distinct species or merely varieties makes little difference—grow on dry, parched hills where little grass grows. We have observed a variety of the first small kind whose lobes were more deeply serrated than the common one we previously cited. This leads me to the conclusion that all these plants occasionally vary from the same fallen seed. You will observe, moreover, that although the leaves of both the larger divergent form and this smaller curled one are dissected and fringed—nature thus playing <note>original: "ludente sic natura," a common phrase referring to natural variations as "nature at play"</note>—they nevertheless attach to the leaf-vein in opposite pairs upon the stalks. Regarding the leaves and their shape, consult Plate 5 of the Illustrations, where the leaves of all four aforementioned *Umbels* are presented individually.

### Place & time.

*The greater Burnet-saxifrage grows everywhere in damp and shady woods. The second, another larger kind, arises as a divergent form in cultivated gardens from the sowing of the seeds of the common lobed variety. The smaller and the other curled smaller variety grow everywhere together on arid and dry hills. They all flower in mid-summer and perfect their seeds toward the end of the same season. Note that we have intentionally omitted showing or engraving the seeds in the General Plate of related seeds for this group of umbels because of their identical shape (being grooved) and equal size.*

### Names.

*The first is the Greater Burnet-saxifrage with a white umbel <note>C. B. P. refers to Caspar Bauhin’s Pinax</note>. Great Saxifrage <note>Dod. refers to Rembert Dodoens</note>. Greater Goat-saxifrage <note>F. B. refers to Jean Bauhin; Park. to John Parkinson</note>. The second, another larger divergent variety, is so named by us. The third is our Lesser Burnet-saxifrage; it is also called Middle Goat-saxifrage by J. Bauhin, in whose work a better illustration of it may be seen. Lesser Pimpinella <note>Fuch. refers to Leonhart Fuchs</note>. Lesser Goat-parsley <note>Tab. refers to Tabernaemontanus</note>. The fourth is our Lesser Curled variety, also called Lesser Goat-saxifrage of the hills by Parkinson. The Smallest Goat-saxifrage, Curled Pimpinella of Tragus, Small Saxifrage, and Stone Goat-parsley. In its root, stem, flower, seed, scent, and flavor, it is clearly similar to the common smaller one mentioned before, differing only in name (as stated) because it has leaves more minutely cut into segments and serrated; thus it is easily distinguished from the third or Lesser Pimpinella. It is likewise distinguished from Burnet <term>Sanguisorba: Salad Burnet, which has similar leaves but lacks the aromatic root and umbel flowers</term> by tasting the root or leaves. Despite these playful variations of nature, it will be very easy to conclude from the flavor that these two divergent forms are indeed Burnet-saxifrages.*

### Temperament & virtues.

*Burnet-saxifrage is of a hot and dry temperament. It is frequently used to induce sweating. It settles griping pains of the belly <note>colic</note> and treats stones and the retention of menses. It is an excellent defense against jaundice and dropsy <note>edema or swelling</note>. It greatly assists those who feel a tightness or oppression of the chest. Regarding its other faculties, consult Jean Bauhin, Volume 3, Part 2, in the chapter on Goat Burnet-saxifrage.*

Stone Parsley <term>Sifon: likely Sison amomum, known as Stone Parsley; its seeds were used as a substitute for the expensive spice Amomum</term> shall claim the third place among the umbels contained in the aforementioned Plate. Its lobes or leaflets are serrated around the edges, like those of Skirret, and are attached in opposite pairs to the midribs which spread across the ground. Between the leaf stalks rises a branched stem, one to one-and-a-half cubits high <note>about 18 to 27 inches</note>, at the tops of which grow many small white flowers in the manner of other umbels. When these fall, they are succeeded by pairs of small, short, grooved seeds joined together, which are the most pungent of all the umbels (except for Pellitory-of-Spain). For this reason, it is called *amomum* in the apothecary shops of Germany. By this mark it is easily distinguished from others of its kind: namely, by its sharp and aromatic seed.

# UMBELLIFERS.

### Location and season.

*It grows naturally at the base of hedges and the edges of ditches, and in damp places on the banks of ditches around Blois, Paris, and London, and widely elsewhere. It flowers toward the end of summer and ripens its seed shortly thereafter.*

### Names.

*Sifon <note>original: "Sifon, Tab. Cam. Lugd." refers to the works of Tabernaemontanus, Camerarius, and the Historia Plantarum Lugdunensis</note>, Sinon or Sifon by Cordus in his commentary on Dioscorides; Macedonian parsley by Fuchs; common Sifon or German amomum by Parkinson; Sifon or the amomum of the apothecary shops by Bauhin; German amomum by Tragus.* <note>The name "Amomum" originally referred to an exotic Eastern spice, but Northern European botanists often applied the name "German Amomum" to local aromatic seeds like Stone Parsley.</note>

### Qualities and medicinal virtues.

*Stone Parsley is hot and dry in the third degree. <note>In the Galenic system of medicine, "degrees" measured the intensity of a plant's effect, with the fourth being the strongest.</note> It removes obstructions of the spleen, provokes menstruation, promotes the flow of urine, and aids digestion.*

**Sium.** <note>Commonly known today as Water-parsnip.</note> This plant comes very close to the previous one in the arrangement of its leaves. It is of two kinds based on its habitat: one is terrestrial or "of the cornfields," and the other is aquatic. The terrestrial kind consists of lobes or leaflets of a pale green color, somewhat resembling (near the base) Cumin with its rounded head. It is a two-foot-tall plant found in damp fields sown with grain and along the edges of hedges and damp ditches; indeed, it maintains the same leaf arrangement as the aquatic Sium. Terrestrial Sium is most aptly named by Johnson in his edition of Gerard <note>Thomas Johnson updated John Gerard’s famous "Herball" in 1633</note>, since it is never found in watery places. In its entire appearance, smell, and taste, the plant declares itself to be a *Sium*; therefore, it has been less accurately called *selinum* <term>Selinum: a Greek-derived name for Parsley</term> by authors until now, since it possesses neither the leaf arrangement, taste, nor smell suitable to parsley or celery, but rather to *Sium*. For this reason, we have judged it more appropriate to call it "Terrestrial Sium" in our Tables of kinship and affinity.

### Location and season.

*We have indicated its origins above. It flowers toward the end of August and completes its seed shortly thereafter.*

### Names.

*It is appropriately enough named "Terrestrial Sium" by Johnson in Gerard. Parsley with Sium-leaves by Gerard (in the figures) and by Parkinson.*

### Qualities and medicinal virtues.

*It is of a hot and dry temperament, possessing the same powers as the aquatic variety.*

**Aquatic Sium.** This is distinguished from the previous terrestrial kind without any difficulty because of the places in which it grows; it is fivefold. There are four river-dwelling kinds: the first is the Great Broad-leaved variety, two or three cubits high <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches, making this plant 3 to 4.5 feet tall</note>, adorned with wider and longer saw-toothed leaflets. It bears large white umbels <term>Umbels: flower clusters where stalks spring from a single center like umbrella ribs</term> at the top and larger ribbed seeds. The second is the Narrow-leaved variety with smaller, narrower leaves, also arranged in pairs like feathers and saw-toothed; it is perhaps the same as the first, differing from it only in the size of its parts. The third bears umbels of white flowers at the leaf axils <note>original: "ad alas florum," the angle between the leaf and the stem</note>; it is much shorter than the first two, and along the individual axils of the stems (which lie prostrate and float in the water), it bears white flowers (almost without stalks, as in common celery). When these fall away, they are succeeded by short, ribbed seeds, smaller than the seeds of the aforementioned kinds. The fourth is the Dwarf variety with "ferulaceous" leaves <term>Ferulaceous: finely divided into hair-like segments, like Giant Fennel (Ferula)</term> very thinly dissected. This is the smallest of all, barely exceeding a palm's breadth in height. It has thick leaves like fennel or ferula, two or three inches long. It produces little stems barely or not at all exceeding a palm's height, at the tips of which grow small white flowers; as each of these falls, two tiny, ribbed seeds follow, in the manner of other umbellifers of this genus. There is also a fifth kind, not found in rivers...

# UMBELLIFERS.

...growing not in paths, springs, or streams, but in stagnant or swampy places. Its leaves are wrinkled, oblong, saw-toothed, divided into several lobes, and notched at the edges; they hang from their own stalks, which are attached to stems two or three feet tall. At the very tips of the stems, the flowers and seeds are similar in all respects to the flowers and seeds of the previously mentioned varieties.

->Location and season.<-

The first two varieties grow in rivers and small streams. The third, which flowers at the leaf-axils, <note>The "axil" is the angle between the upper side of a leaf or stem and the supporting stem or branch.</note> is found in the same places, as well as in springs and fountain waters. The fourth, a very small or puny variety, was observed by us at the banks of the river called the Cosson (within the royal grounds of Chambord) in the year 1659; we have not seen it anywhere else until now. The last, or fifth variety, is commonly found in swampy and stagnant waters; however, we have never been able to see it in flowing waters. All of them flower toward the end of summer; at the beginning of autumn, or shortly thereafter, they bring their seeds to maturity.

->Names.<-

The first is called the Great Broad-leaved Sium by J.B. <note>original: "Sium maximum latifolium, J. B." refers to Jean Bauhin's "Historia Plantarum Universalis"</note>; the Sium of Dioscorides, or the Great Water-parsnip, by Parkinson <note>original: "Sium Dioscoridis, seu pastinaca aquatica major, Park."</note>. The second is the Sium of Dodoens <note>original: "Sion Dod."</note>; the Middle Sium of Jean Bauhin; and the second Smaller Sium of Parkinson. The third is the Sium flowering at the axils, or the creeping Sium with umbels arranged at the joints—this is what we call it. The fourth we call the Dwarf Sium with fennel-like leaves. The fifth we call the Sium with wrinkled, three-cleft or many-cleft, notched leaves. This is the other Great Narrow-leaved Sium of Parkinson; the other Sium of Dodoens; the other Sium with the appearance of Alexander's <note>An "Alexander" (Smyrnium olusatrum) is a celery-like pot herb.</note> by Lobel; and the Water Hemlock of Gesner and Jean Bauhin.

->Temperament and medicinal powers.<-

Sium possesses a warming faculty and is fragrant to the taste. Whether raw or cooked, it breaks down and expels kidney stones—according to the testimony of Dioscorides. When eaten, it is also useful for those suffering from dysentery. For the lobed leaves of the three "umbel-bearing" Siums described above, consult the fifth Table of Illustrations.

<meta>This page continues the botanical classification of the Umbelliferae family. The previous page concluded the description of the Water-parsnip (Sium) genus, specifically its fifth variety and its preferred swampy habitats. # U M B E L L I F E R S.

## Explanation of the preceding Plate.

**I**n the third place follows an enumeration and explanation of the *Umbellifers* provided with leaves divided in many ways and a larger striated <note>Striated: marked with parallel grooves or ridges.</note> seed. Their leaves are neither fennel-like nor lobed, but divided in many parts; that is, the small leaflets adhering to the stalks are not as delicately divided or cut as the *Umbellifers* equipped with fennel-like leaves, yet they are much more incised and divided than those arranged in a feather-like or lobed fashion; therefore, they hold a middle position between the two. These are of two kinds, according to the size of the seeds and other parts: namely, those provided with either a longer and thicker seed, or those gifted with a shorter and thinner seed. We shall speak of the former first, and the latter second.

**S**eseli <note>Also known as Moon-carrot.</note>. This is either a shrub or an herb. This shrub, or rather sub-shrub <term>sub-shrub: a plant that is woody at the base but has soft, green stems at the top</term>, has a woody consistency. Although it does not have leaves divided in many parts (in the manner of the other *Umbellifers* of this sub-category), we have nevertheless placed it among its kindred plants because it produces flowers and seeds arranged in an umbel <term>umbel: a flower cluster where the stalks spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</term> which are striated, larger, and of the same shape, scent, and taste as other species of Seseli. We are forced to do this here and there in several other cases, as in the division above. For example, the Sium with fennel-like leaves was joined to the others because of its complete conformity in scent and taste with all its relatives that bear lobed leaves; you will observe the same in other instances. Moreover, the aforementioned Seseli is easily distinguished from the others that follow by two main names: first, because it is a shrub, and second, because it remains green with leaves all year round. It produces a whole leaf, not cut in any part nor saw-toothed, which nonetheless occurs in all other fennel-like or *Umbelliferous* plants. The leaf stalk encircles the joints of the stem, which is like a long, hollow navel, as in the honeysuckle <note>original: "pereclymeno", referring to Lonicera periclymenum</note>. Indeed, because the stem seems to pass through the leaves, some commonly call it "perfoliate" <term>perfoliate: a leaf that completely surrounds the stem so that the stem appears to grow through the center of the leaf</term>.

**S**eseli, the herb. This is either the meadow variety or the mountain variety. 1st: The meadow variety. This is an *Umbelliferous* plant adorned with a larger striated seed, whose leaves are divided in many parts; if these are handled, they give off a heavy scent. It is common enough in all damp meadows. 2nd: The First Mountain Seseli of Pannonia <note>Pannonia: a historical region covering parts of modern-day Hungary and surrounding countries</note> described by Clusius. The leaves of this are cut in many ways after the manner of hemlock <note>original: "cicutæ ritu"</note>; its seeds are shorter and thicker than the seeds of the aforementioned variety. There is also another species or variety of it whose leaves are cut much more finely still. The small flowers of all Seselis are yellow, or a grassy-yellow, provided with petals that are turned back.

### Location and season.

*The Ethiopian Shrub Seseli grows in dry and neglected places around Montpellier <note>original: "Monspessulum", a city in southern France famous for its botanical garden</note>, reaching a height of a foot or a foot and a half, but when moved into gardens, it rises to a height of two or three cubits <term>cubit: an ancient measurement of about 18 inches (45 cm)</term>. The Meadow Seseli herb grows abundantly in meadows and at the edges of vineyards, damp woods, and in other moist places. The Mountain Seseli of Pannonia described by Clusius, and its other species with thinner leaves, grows in the mountains of Hungary. They flower in summer and perfect their seeds in Autumn—all these species of Seseli.*

### Names.

*The Shrub Seseli of Ethiopia, as named by Dodoens, Gerard, and Parkinson <note>original: "Dod. Ger. Park." refer to famous 16th and 17th-century botanists Rembert Dodoens, John Gerard, and John Parkinson</note>. The Ethiopian shrubby Seseli with a honeysuckle leaf, according to Jean Bauhin <note>original: "J. B."</note>. 2nd: The Meadow Seseli herb of the people of Montpellier, according to de l'Obel <note>original: "ad. Lob."</note>, Lugdunensis, and Camerarius. The meadow variety, perhaps the 'Silaus' of Pliny, according to Caspar Bauhin's Pinax <note>original: "C. B. P."</note>. 3rd: The First Mountain Seseli of Pannonia in Clusius's History; the smooth mountain Seseli with a hemlock leaf, according to Caspar Bauhin and Parkinson.*

<sig>E</sig>
<meta>catchword: Tempe-</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Umbelliferae (carrot family), specifically focusing on the medicinal properties of Moon-carrot (Seseli) and providing a detailed classification of various types of Hemlock (Cicuta) and their physical characteristics, habitats, and naming conventions.</meta>

-># U M B E L L I F E R S.<-

### Temperament and virtues.

*Moon-carrot <note>original: "Seseli"</note> is hot and dry. It assists with strangury <note>Strangury: a condition characterized by painful, frequent, and slow urination.</note>, relieves difficulty in breathing, and promotes the flow of menses, the afterbirth <note>original: "secundinas"</note>, and the delivery of the fetus. Both the root and the seed, when drunk in wine, dispel gas and soothe the pains of the intestines, especially if they are ground and reduced to a powder, mixed with wine, and consumed.*

Hemlock <note>original: "Cicuta"</note>. As a generic trait (by which it is distinguished from others of its kind contained in this table), it possesses a poisonous <note>original: "virosum," meaning sickly or venomous</note> smell. It is either the greatest variety, or the very foul-smelling broad-leaved kind, or the fine-leaved kind. The leaves of the first are broader, divided into many parts, and saw-toothed, adhering in a feathered <term>feathered: arranged like a bird's feather, also known as pinnate</term> manner to longer stalks; other smaller leaf-stalks with lobes are placed alternately on shorter stalks. It produces stems 4 feet high, which are pipe-like or hollow and marked by nodes or joints. At the tops of these are umbels <term>umbel: a flower cluster where the stalks spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</term> laden with yellow flowers; as each of these falls away, they are succeeded by two curved seeds (a trait it shares only with Alexanders <note>original: "Smyrnio"</note> among the Umbellifers) which are ribbed and twice as large as the seeds of others of its kind, and in each of them, higher ridges and deeper furrows can be seen. The second variety is the greater common hemlock, growing everywhere along the embankments and ditches of the suburbs; its stem is two or three cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> high, sometimes much taller, and is sprinkled with dark brown spots. Its leaves, when handled, give off a poisonous and most unpleasant smell that strikes the head. The flowers in the umbel are white. The third, a smaller variety, resembles garden parsley <note>original: "apium hortense seu sativum"</note> so closely that it is gathered by unskilled servants in gardens (where it often grows spontaneously) in place of the real thing and served for eating; for this reason, it is not ineptly called the "fault of parsley" <note>original: "petroselini vitium"</note> by Hieronymus Bock <note>original: "Trago"</note>. The fourth is the marsh variety, occurring in swampy places in both stagnant and flowing waters, never on dry land. Its leaves are finely divided, and its stems are zig-zagging, two to three feet high, jointed, and spread into many small branches. At the tips are many small five-petaled white flowers, which, when they wither, are succeeded by ribbed, oblong seeds, much thinner and longer than the seeds of the others in this order. They are joined in pairs (after the manner of other Umbellifers) and adhere so tightly to one another that even after being dried for many years, they cannot be easily separated except with the help of a very sharp needle; nor could they be parted from each other by rubbing between the fingers in the palm of the hand, although we have kept the seeds of this plant enclosed in a box in our seed-store for many years. This caused us to err in our *Botanical Preludes* <note>original: "præludiorum nostrorum Botanicorum," referring to the author's previous work</note>, page 371. We now sing a recantation <note>original: "Palinodiam canimus"</note>. When we desired to count the number of seeds, ridges, and furrows with the help of a needle, by driving a very sharp point into the center of the seed where the germ is located, the two distinct seeds appeared of their own accord, hollow at the part where they are joined. This may be observed in all seeds of this plant by using a needle, as we have advised above.

### Place and Time.

*The first is the very foul-smelling broad-leaved hemlock-parsley, which grows in the Peloponnese. The second is the Common Hemlock, found everywhere along the embankments and ditches of villages, towns, and suburbs. The third grows in damp woods and gardens, or pleasure-grounds, occurring spontaneously without sowing. The fourth is found everywhere in marshes and stagnant waters. They all bloom toward the end of summer; the seeds can be gathered at the beginning of Autumn.*

### Names.

*The first: Very foul-smelling broad-leaved hemlock-parsley, Lobel: History of Lyon. Broad-leaved hemlock, Gerard's images. Foul hemlock-parsley, Tabernaemontanus. Greatest, Parkinson's description. The Moon-carrot of the Peloponnese of the moderns, Parkinson. The Peloponnesian [Moon-carrot] of Mattioli, or the hemlock-parsley of some, John Bauhin. The second: Greater hemlock, Caspar Bauhin's Pinax. True hemlock, Gesner's Gardens. Common hemlock-parsley, Clusius. Greater common, Parkinson. The third: Lesser hemlock similar to parsley, Caspar Bauhin's Pinax. The fault of parsley, Hieronymus Bock. Lesser or foolish hemlock-parsley, Parkinson. The fourth: Fine-leaved marsh hemlock-parsley, Caspar Bauhin's Pinax and Parkinson. Marsh hemlock-parsley, Lobel, Tabernaemontanus, and Gerard. Water-fennel <note>original: "Phellandryum"</note>, Dodoens and History of Lyon. Water-fennel, or the water hemlock-parsley of some, John Bauhin.*

The author includes a personal correction regarding the seed structure of Water-fennel based on his own microscopic observations with a needle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Umbelliferae (parsley family), moving from the discussion of Moon-carrot to the dangerous properties of Hemlock and a detailed classification of Water Dropwort (OEnanthe).</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

<meta>catchword: Location</meta>

### Temperament and virtues.

*Hemlock <note>original: "Cicuta"</note> is considered to be of a cold temperament, and its leaves are not administered internally without the present danger of death. For this reason, the Athenians saw to it that Socrates was removed from their midst (by the administration of its juice) because he held improper thoughts regarding their Gods. If anyone should eat the leaves in place of parsley <note>original: "selini"</note> leaves, or the root in place of a parsnip <note>original: "pastinacæ"</note> root, let him drink vinegar and he will be cured. So says Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), a German botanist</note>. See more in the works of Johann Bauhin, who treats the powers of hemlock more at length on pages 178 and 179.*

-> Vol. 3. Part. 2. <-

**Water Dropwort** <note>original: "OEnanthe"</note>. Take this as the generic mark by which it is distinguished from all other Umbellifers—which are gifted with leaves divided in many ways and larger ribbed seeds—namely, that its root is divided into many parts like the root of an **asphodel** <note>The Asphodel was known in antiquity for its bunch of tuberous, sausage-shaped roots</note>. Many roots hang closely from a single head, and they spread out more widely (namely, beneath the surface of the earth) than they grow deeply; they are covered in a blackish skin and are white inside, so much so that each individual root resembles a fleshy spindle. This is found in no other whole class of Umbellifers except in this one alone.

**The Greatest Water Dropwort**, with poisonous juice and a hemlock-like appearance. This is the largest of all. It produces its stems to a height of three or four feet, and sometimes higher; it bears fleshy roots equal to those of a peony, sharp and unpleasant to the taste, full of a juice that is first milky, then turns yellowish, and is poisonous and blistering. It grows in muddy and watery places where **marsh parsley** <note>original: "paludapium," likely Apium graveolens or Wild Celery</note> occurs, which it resembles in its leaves; however, it is more similar to **meadow-rue** <note>original: "rutæ pratensi"</note>, which is why it is poorly named by Lobelius as having a "hemlock-like appearance." It is very easily distinguished from the others to be mentioned below by the marks previously described. 

The **2nd** variety has leaves like celery or chervil; this type is twofold. One is a larger, two-foot-tall plant with a straight, firm stem, sometimes rising to the height of a cubit or a cubit and a half <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches</note>. The roots are glandular, hanging by long threads, extending further out than deep; by these marks it is discerned from the previous and the following variety. The other variety consists of a winding, pipe-like or hollow stem, somewhat similar to rushes; instead of leaves, there are a few thin, empty-like stalks, <unclear>pānges</unclear>, or ribs, from which some small and narrow leaflets are produced. When planted in gardens or dry places, it is shorter and produces leaves closest to the ground similar to chervil but thinner; however, the upper leaves are like those of the kind that grows in water. It has thick roots, similar to small wild turnips, but smaller and shorter, from which creeping fibers emerge, and from these, little roots sprout that send forth leaves. 

The **3rd** is the narrow-leaved variety with longer narrow leaves, like **samphire** <note>original: "crithmi"</note> or **hog's fennel** <note>original: "peucedani"</note>. It has oblong roots, two or three inches long, many from one head, which are more slender near the origin and thicken further down, with fibers lowering themselves from the thicker and lower part, by which juice is drawn from the earth for the nourishment of the whole plant. 

The **4th** and last has leaves like **water-yarrow** <note>original: "millefolii aquatici"</note>, which was erroneously called "meadow carrot with marsh-yarrow leaves" first by Dalechamps and later by others following him. In fact, it has nothing in common regarding its essential mark, smell, or taste with a true carrot <note>original: "dauco"</note>, whose seed ought to be shaggy or hairy. It declares itself to be a Water Dropwort by its universal structure, as well as by its taste and smell; therefore, we have rightly referred this Umbellifer to the class of Water Dropworts, as stated by us on page 366 of our *Botanical Preludes*. Furthermore, by its root which rivals the asphodel, and its seed being prickly at the top (as are all the species of the aforementioned Water Dropworts), it manifests itself to be no carrot. Moreover, various species of Umbellifers are confused by authors under the name of "carrot" (as we have taught in the place cited, and as we shall teach further here below).

<summary>This page discusses the toxic properties of Hemlock, famously used to execute Socrates, and provides a detailed botanical classification of four varieties of Water Dropwort (OEnanthe) based on their root structures and leaf shapes.</summary>
<keywords>Hemlock, Water Dropwort, OEnanthe, Socrates, botanical classification, asphodel-like roots, medicinal temperament, poisonous plants, Umbelliferae</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical classification of the Umbelliferae (parsley family), specifically focusing on the species of OEnanthe (Water Dropwort) and moving into the description of Crithmum (Sea Samphire). It follows the anatomical description of the root systems provided on the previous page.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

### Habitat & Season.

The **Greatest Water Dropwort** <note>original: "OEnanthe maxima"</note> grows along the sea coast at the mouth of the **Loire** <note>The Ligeris is the Loire river in France</note>, in wet ditches that receive the high tide of the sea; there we found it exceeding five or six feet in height. It also grows on the banks of the **Thames**, and elsewhere here and there in the wet ditches of the North near rivers, though it is shorter there. The second kind grows in the wet woods planted within the Royal enclosures of **Chambord** <note>The Château de Chambord in the Loire Valley</note>. The third grows in meadows frequently flooded and moistened by water. The fourth in higher meadows, which sometimes even descends into the valley meadows. The last kind is found everywhere in France, in both wet meadows and woods. They all bloom toward the end of summer, and shortly after they perfect their seed, which is ribbed <note>original: "striatum"</note>, slightly compressed, and endowed with tiny prickles on the upper part, usually in pairs. This is also common to certain other species of umbellifers, but in all these seeds, the prickles are seen individually, falling off after the seeds have been nourished.

### Names.

1. The first is the **Water Dropwort with poisonous juice and the appearance of hemlock** according to Lobelius and Johann Bauhin <note>Jean Bauhin (1541–1613), a Swiss botanist</note>, but to us it is rather "parsley-leaved." **Hemlock-faced** according to Lobel's *Adversaria* and Parkinson. The **third Dropwort** or **cat-leafed Dropwort** of Tabernaemontanus. 2. The second and 3. third **Dropworts**, as displayed by us above in the Table, are the **parsley-leaved** kind, with a firmer, upright stem, and one with a winding, trailing stem. See below the "hallucinations" <note>The author uses "hallucinationes" to refer to botanical misidentifications or errors in classification</note> of **Caspar Bauhin** and other authors concerning the chervil-leaved, narrow-leaved, and parsley-leaved Dropworts, where I show that he has passed down the synonyms of all these to us in a very confused manner. 4. The **narrow-leaved** kind of Lobel. 5. The fifth is the **Water Dropwort with marsh-yarrow leaves**, according to us. It is wrongly called the **meadow carrot with marsh-yarrow leaves** by Caspar Bauhin in his *Pinax*, and the **meadow carrot** by Daléchamps in the *History of Lyon*; see page 366 of our *Botanical Preludes*.

### Qualities & Virtues.

**Water Dropwort** is temperately hot and dry, and is therefore opening and cleansing. It has a somewhat bitter taste with a slight astringency; consequently, the roots—whether used internally or externally—move the urine and small stones <note>original: "arenulas," referring to kidney or bladder gravel</note>, open obstructions, and clear out the urinary passages. The root of the Dropwort, when ground into a powder, steeped, and drunk with wine, is suitable for a "dripping of blood" <note>original: "sanguinis stillicidio," likely referring to hematuria or bloody urine</note>; according to **Dioscorides** <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note>, it heals those who are sighing and gasping for breath.

**Sea Samphire** <note>original: "Crithmum maritimum"</note>. Since it possesses all the conditions of the Umbellifers, it ought by right and merit to be counted among them, nor can it be more fitly placed than here. Indeed, it has leaves divided in many ways and is gifted with larger seeds. It is easily recognized by its thick and fleshy leaves, commonly divided into three parts and clinging to a stalk, which grow on sea cliffs or rocks and smell of the saltiness of the sea (these are usually prepared with vinegar and salt and served as a salad <note>original: "acetario"</note> during the winter months). Regarding its poor classification by **Caspar Bauhin**, see page 402 of our *Botanical Preludes*, and regarding the hallucinations of Caspar Bauhin and other authors observed by us below. Another kind, the **Prickly Sea Samphire**, has trailing stems that are very much jointed. The leaves are divided into thinner, thicker, and sharper segments; it has a ribbed, larger seed, joined in pairs, covered in an oblong cartilaginous casing that is prickly at the top. When I had not yet opened this capsule, I thought it consisted of a single seed, but once it was cut open, I found two large, ribbed seeds hiding there. We asserted the contrary in our *Botanical Preludes*, but now being more "sharp-eyed" <note>original: "oculatiores," meaning having observed more closely</note>, we retract what was said there concerning the seed of the prickly samphire. See below in the hallucinations of Caspar Bauhin and others regarding Samphire.

### Habitat & Season.

It grows abundantly on the sea cliffs facing the **Armorican sea** <note>The coast of Brittany, France</note>, likewise toward the Western sea coast of **England**, and it grows elsewhere on the sea cliffs of **Scotland**, **Ireland**, and **Norway**. It blooms toward the end of summer and perfects its seed a little later. The other, the **Prickly Sea Samphire**, grows on the sea coast of **Narbonne France** <note>The Mediterranean coast of southern France</note>. The common folk there are known to eat the dug-up roots.

### Names.

1. The first is called the **Smaller Sea Samphire or Sea Fennel** by Caspar Bauhin; **Sea Samphire** by Dodoens and Gerard; **Sea Samphire or Sea Fennel** by Johann Bauhin; the **Commoner Sea Samphire** by Parkinson; **Samphire and Saint Peter’s Herb** <note>original: "herba sancti Petri," the root of the French name 'Saint-Pierre' which became 'Samphire'</note> by Dodoens in French; **Sea Chalk** by Lonicer; and **Baticula** (as if a small *Batis*) by Caesalpinus. 2. The second is called **Prickly Sea Samphire** by Dodoens and Caspar Bauhin.

<meta>catchword: maritimum</meta>

<meta>This page continues the classification of the Umbelliferae, specifically concluding the discussion of Crithmum (Sea Samphire) and its various species, then transitioning to a new taxonomic table covering plants like Ammi (Bishop's Weed), Apium (Celery/Parsley), Caraway, and Anise.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

[Cri]thmum <note>The text begins mid-word; "Crithmum" is Sea Samphire</note> **spiny**, or **Sea Parsnip** <note>original: "pastinaca marina"</note>, according to Parkinson. Called **Sea Parsnip Secacul** <note>Secacul refers to a root traditionally used in Middle Eastern medicine, often identified as a type of parsnip or eryngo</note> by some, and **Spiny Samphire** by J. B. <note>Jean Bauhin</note>. This seems to me to be a species distinct from the aforementioned common spiny sea samphire. Even if it possessed a spiny seed—on account of the unequal arrangement of its leaves, which approach those of the thin-leaved parsnip and differ from the leaves of the other two—anyone could conclude from the leaves alone that it is a different species. But if it were endowed with a spiny seed (which is not apparent to us, neither from the description of Rauwolf <note>Leonhard Rauwolf (1535–1596), German physician and botanist</note>, nor from the appendix of Daléchamps to the *History of Lyon*), perhaps it would be the same as the **Apulian Prickly Parsnip** of Colonna <note>Fabio Colonna (1567–1640), an Italian botanist</note>, which it very closely approaches in all its parts, according to the figure shown in the appendix of the *History of Lyon*. However, since neither Rauwolf nor Daléchamps make any mention of the seed in the description, nor is it shown in the figures, we leave it in doubt as to where it should be classified. See the errors <note>original: "hallucinationes"</note> of Caspar Bauhin observed by us below in the chapter on *Gingidium* <note>Toothpick-weed</note>.

### Temperament and Virtues.

**Samphire** <note>original: "Crithmum"</note> is hot and dry. It removes obstructions of the liver and spleen, provokes menstruation, and expels calculous matter <note>stones or gravel in the urinary tract</note>. The seed and root of samphire, boiled in wine and drunk, aid difficulty of urination and the royal disease <note>original: "morbo regio"; a historical term for jaundice</note>. It is eaten raw and cooked, in the manner of a vegetable; its leaves are preserved in brine, and are of primary use for strangury <note>painful or difficult urination</note>. If the leaves are cooked and seasoned with oil and vinegar like capers, and eaten as a salad, they are a pleasant seasoning for foods. They benefit the stomach, free the internal organs from obstructions, and excite the appetite for food. Concerning the seeds of the umbellifers contained in the aforesaid Table, consult the general Table of seed illustrations <note>original: "icon. seminum"</note> *Tt Vu Xx Yy Zz && †† AAA B. CCCD. E. F. G. H.* For the explanation regarding leaves divided into many parts, see Table of illustrations 6, where the leaf of the **Greater Hemlock** and the **Greatest Foul-smelling Hemlock** are represented, and the **Double Water-Dropwort** is engraved.

### UMBELLIFERS WITH LEAVES DIVIDED INTO MANY PARTS,
->endowed with a smaller and shorter ribbed seed.<-

<image-desc>A taxonomic branching diagram classifying various plants:
- **Bishop's Weed** [Ammi] branches into: Common Annual (according to Dodoens) and Perennial (according to our classification).
- **Parsley/Celery** [Apium] branches into four main categories: 
    1. **Cultivated or Common Garden**, which further splits into Broad-leaved (with flat and curled sub-types) and Narrow-leaved.
    2. **Broad-leaved or Greatest Garden**, which includes types described by Bauhin, the Yellow Umbel, and a degenerate type with long dissected leaves.
    3. **Marsh types**, including what is called **Celery** [Seleri] and our local variety.
    4. **With hairy or hoary seed**, including **Macedonian** and **Foreign** types.
- Followed by single entries for: **Caraway** [Carum], **Anise** [Anisum], and **Pellitory of Spain** [Pyrethrum].</image-desc>

### Explanation of the Preceding Table.

**Annual Bishop’s Weed** <note>original: "Ammi annuum"</note>. It has longer leaves, saw-toothed or notched, with a white flower in the umbels; with a ribbed seed, small, bitter, emulating the seeds of marsh parsley (as far as the shape is concerned),

<meta>This page continues the botanical classification of the Umbelliferae family. It concludes a comparison of plant leaf structures before moving into a detailed description of Ammi (Bishop’s-weed) and Apium (Celery/Parsley), including their habitats, various names given by previous botanists, and their medicinal properties.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

[resembling] <note>The text continues from the previous page's comparison</note> it, except that it is a little thicker; therefore, it is quite appropriately called *ammioselinum* <note>original: "ammioselinum," literally "Bishop's-weed parsley"</note> by certain authors. The leaves are divided into lobes in many ways, and into more segments than the leaves of the following plant, whose lobes are fewer—usually three—and more deeply cut in the manner of a saw.

**Perennial Bishop’s-weed** <note>original: "Ammi perenne"</note>. *The flower cluster* <note>original: "Umbella"</note> is bothersome in gardens because of the way its roots creep far and wide. The leaves are divided into three (for the most part) serrated, narrow segments, and sometimes more. The *umbels* <term>umbel: a flower cluster where the stalks spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</term> are quite large and white in color. The seed is slender, oblong, and striped. I do not see by what reasoning certain authors, along with Caspar Bauhin <note>A Swiss botanist (1560–1624) known for his pinax of plant names</note>, classify this with the *eryngos* <note>Sea-holly</note>, since it is a perfect *umbel*, as is clearly apparent even to those moderately skilled in botany. See page 421 of our *Botanical Preludes*.

## Habitat & Season.

*Annual Bishop’s-weed grows abundantly in the grain fields of Poitou <note>original: "Pictonum"</note>: and even near Salamanca <note>original: "Salmanticum"</note>, according to Charles de l'Écluse <note>original: "Carolo Clusio," a pioneering 16th-century botanist</note>. We also found Perennial Bishop’s-weed growing wild and in great numbers among the crops around Poitiers.*

## Names.

1. **Annual or common Bishop’s-weed, according to us. The Greater Ammi of C. Bauhin. The Common Ammi of Dodoens, Camerarius, and Gerard. The more common Ammi of L'Obel and Parkinson. The Greater Common Ammi with broader leaves and less fragrant seed, according to Jean Bauhin.** 2. **Perennial Bishop’s-weed, according to us. It is called the Ammi of certain people by Fuchs and in the "History of Lyon." The Eryngo with saw-like leaves by C. Bauhin. The 4th Eryngo of Dodoens. The 4th Samphire of Mattioli, Tabernaemontanus, and Camerarius. The 4th Umbel-bearing Samphire of Jean Bauhin.**

## Properties & Virtues.

*Bishop’s-weed heats and dries in the 3rd degree. It promotes urine and menstruation, eases abdominal griping, and, if mixed with honey, it removes bruises or skin marks.*

**Celery or Parsley** <note>original: "Apium"</note>. It is distinguished from the other *umbels* of this class by its odor and taste, its smaller striped seed, and its leaves divided in many ways. First, there is the cultivated or more common garden variety, which is either broad-leaved or narrow-leaved. The leaves of the former are either flat or curled, as nature plays with the form. The narrow-leaved mountain or thin-leaved variety—compared to the two previously mentioned garden-cultivated types—even if it grows wild in the mountains, becomes a garden plant through cultivation and is sought after for its root, which is served as food in winter. Since it has a herbaceous or pale yellow flower, it is easily distinguished from the others by its narrow leaves and its blossom. There is also a very large broad-leaved garden variety, all of whose dimensions are larger; specifically, its seed is twice as large as the seeds of the others, according to Bauhin in his *Prodromus*. We also have another with a yellow *umbel*, very much resembling the last-mentioned variety, except for the smallness of the seeds and the greenish color of the *umbel*. From the seed of the previously mentioned broad-leaved garden type, a degenerate form is sometimes found whose leaves are oblong, wavy, and divided into many segments—nature playing in this way, just as we described above regarding the Greater Burnet-saxifrage and many others in the immense ocean of vegetation. There is another named for the marshy places in which it grows, called Marsh-parsley or *Paludapium*; it is of two kinds: the Italian, called **celery** <note>original: "seleri"</note>, and our common variety. It becomes sweeter and blanched <note>original: "dealbatum"</note> when the leaves are wrapped in straw and earth and protected from the air. This is also achieved by compressing and tightly tying the leaves of the same plant to keep the air from them, which occurs in many plants through the "fattening" <note>original: "mangonio," a term for artificial enhancement or "grooming" of plants</note> and industry of gardeners. One can see this in the leaves of the artichoke, beet, endive, Roman lettuce, Alexander’s, Myrrh, and chicory, etc. Indeed, nature itself shows this to us in Cabbage and Head Lettuce: for the internal leaves, embracing one another tightly like scales, prevent the cold air from entering—

<meta>catchword: pro- (completing "prohibent" meaning "prevent")</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Umbellifers, specifically moving from the physical characteristics of Bishop’s-weed into a broader discussion of how environmental factors like air and light affect plant color (blanching), before returning to the classification of Celery (Apium) and its relatives.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

[Those things] which prevent [access to air] cause plants to turn white naturally, while the outer leaves that are not yet folded in on themselves (such as Cabbage, lettuce, and others of that sort) are green; this is because they enjoy free access to the air, which is the cause of greenness in all plants. This is evident in all grain-bearing plants growing in pits and seaside caves frequented by pigeons feeding their young. In our own Scotland, while penetrating caves that extend several hundred feet underground by nature’s design, the pigeons (which are called "rock pigeons") build and arrange their nests on rocky ledges. They feed their young pigeons <note>original: "pipiones," young squabs</note> with oats, barley, and peas, and gorge their tender young with these kinds of grains and many other diverse things. Some of these seeds slip past and fall to the ground, where they sprout; and due to the lack of air, the leaves of all of them are seen to be white as they hide in those dark places. Indeed, they could not even be seen without lanterns in those underworld-like caves; for we were forced to use these both to guide ourselves and direct our steps through the various twists and turns there—the path being quite rugged and the cavern hidden—as well as to look for the nests and extract the tender young pigeons from them. I remember, when I was still a youth, penetrating such caves for several hundred feet, where it was always black night, both day and night, and there I commonly trod underfoot these kinds of grain-leaves, which were white and stripped of all greenness.

I observed the same thing in mountain forests toward October and November, where there were certain low-lying spots. By the agitation of the wind, the fallen leaves from the trees were driven there, and by these, the grasses and other plants were covered indiscriminately. Once the overlying leaves were removed, they all appeared whitened; nor did they take on their greenness or original color until they had penetrated through the dry, densely packed leaves lying upon them so as to catch the air.

But lest this become too long a digression, let us return to our marsh celery, the Italian *selleri*—perhaps a name corrupted by the Italians and French from *selinon* <note>original: "selino," from the Greek σέλινον (selinon), the classical word for celery or parsley</note>, which is the proper and genuine name used by both Greeks and Latins. While this is still growing, it has leaves of a paler green than our common marsh celery, which in my opinion happens because of its cultivation and sowing in gardens. Our native kind, or marsh celery, called *paludapium* <term>paludapium: literally "marsh-parsley," a common historical name for wild celery</term>, is endowed with a greater bitterness and is gifted with darker green leaves. Whoever decides that these two are one and the same, differing only by cultivation and horticultural "doctoring" <note>original: "mangonio," referring to the manipulation or "faking" of a plant's natural state through intense gardening</note>, and constitutes them as merely a variety of one and the same *Umbel*, to him I will yield the point <note>original: "illi dabo manus," literally "to give one's hands," a Latin idiom meaning to concede or admit defeat in an argument</note>.

Next follows the Parsley with hairy seeds <note>original: "Apium sem. villoso"</note>, which is either the Macedonian kind (so called from its place of birth) or the foreign kind. This one produces leaves that are more finely jagged at the margin and arranged in lobes, unlike common garden parsley; the leaves are also ringed with denser "teeth" and appear as if covered in ash or somewhat hoary. Moreover, the stalks and the twin seeds are seen to be striped, smaller, hairy, and hoary—by which marks it is easily distinguished specifically from all those mentioned before and those that follow. 

The last, called the "First Foreign Selinum" by Charles de l'Écluse <note>original: "Carolo Clusio," the influential 16th-century botanist</note>, has somewhat roundish leaves arranged in lobes or wings at intervals along the stalk or midrib, and is serrated at the margin. It also produces seeds that are striped, smaller, and hairy on each ridge as in the Macedonian kind, but brown. They agree in the hairiness of the seeds, and for this reason these last two are distinguished from all those previously described: but they differ from each other, first, by the dissimilarity of the leaves in both structure and color; and second, by the color of the hairy seeds—one being white or hoary, and the other brown. In smell and taste they agree with the *selinum* <note>celery/parsley</note>, even if they differ in the color of the seed. This latter plant is placed here with its relatives because of its smell and taste, as I have said, even though the lower leaves are arranged like wings and cling to the midrib, with one larger, round, serrated leaf closing the end of the rib. Furthermore, the leaves are not divided into many parts except in the upper part of the stalks; nevertheless, I have placed it here with its likes, even if it contradicts the title assigned by us at the beginning of this section. This happens in other sub-categories, as we noted above concerning the Ethiopian *Seseli* shrub, and stone-parsley <note>original: "pimpinella saxifraga"</note>, and the *Sium* <note>water-parsnip</note> endowed with fennel-like leaves.

<meta>This page continues the botanical classification of Umbellifers (the parsley family), specifically applying the principles of "blanching" mentioned on the previous page to the cultivation of Celery (Apium), and introducing Caraway (Carum).</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

### Habitat & Season.

The origins of cultivated celery <note>original: "Apii sativi"</note> are hidden from us. The second or "Crisped" variety was brought to us from Sardinia. The third is found in the mountains and rocky mounds toward Grenoble <note>original: "Gratianopolin"</note> according to the witness of Lobelius. The fourth and fifth varieties came to England from Virginia <note>The English colonies in North America</note> and the islands adjacent to it. The sixth is merely a variety that degenerates from sowing, whose leaves become long and wavy. The seventh is called "selleri" <note>Celery</note> (perhaps a corruption of *selini*) by the Italians and French, and this name has also been brought into England. Let it be called *selini* in England hereafter, as this name fits it more aptly than the depraved and corrupt word of the French and Italians, *selleri* or *seleri*. It grows when sown in the gardens of Italy, France, and England. It is a degenerate form produced by sowing common marsh celery <note>Smallage</note>, and in all the aforementioned places, it is whitened <note>blanched by burying it or blocking light, as discussed on the previous page</note>; throughout the winter season, it is sought after in salads by people of the better sort. Macedonian parsley, as the name itself suggests, was first brought from Macedonia. The foreign variety grows wild in the countryside of Salamanca <note>original: "Salmanticensi"</note> according to Carolus Clusius. The flowers and seeds of all these reach maturity toward the end of summer and autumn.

### Names.

1. Garden celery, or commonly "rock-parsley" <note>original: "petroselinum"</note>, according to Caspar Bauhin; common or cultivated parsley according to Lonicer; garden celery according to Matthiolus, Dodonaeus, Lobelius, Gerard, and Fuchs; common parsley according to Parkinson; common flat parsley according to Jean Bauhin.
2. Celery or "Crisped" parsley according to Caspar Bauhin; curled garden celery according to Lobelius and the *History of Lyon*; curled or many-leaved parsley according to Gerard and Tabernaemontanus; crisped parsley according to Camerarius, Jean Bauhin, and Parkinson.
3. Mountain celery with finer leaves, according to Caspar Bauhin; Dale’s mountain celery according to the *History of Lyon*.
4. Broad-leaved garden celery, or "The Greatest," according to Caspar Bauhin in his *Prodromus*. Columna's English cultivated celery; Parkinson’s Great or Virginian parsley; called Great Garden or English parsley by some, according to Jean Bauhin.
5. Celery with a yellow umbel <note>the umbrella-like flower cluster</note>, according to our classification.
6. Degenerate celery with long, divided leaves, according to our classification.
7. Marsh celery, called *selleri* or *seleni*.
8. Our local marsh celery, or "of the shops" <note>term: "officinarum" indicates it was the standard variety used by apothecaries</note>, according to Caspar Bauhin; marsh celery according to Matthiolus, Fuchs, and the *History of Lyon*; *Eleoselinum* according to Dodonaeus; marsh-parsley according to Tabernaemontanus.
9. Macedonian celery, according to Caspar Bauhin; Macedonian parsley according to Matthiolus, Dodonaeus, the *History of Lyon*, Tabernaemontanus, and Gerard; many call it Macedonian celery or parsley, according to Jean Bauhin. It is the second *Daucus* <note>Wild Carrot</note> of Dioscorides according to Columna. Finally, the celery with somewhat round leaves according to Caspar Bauhin in his *Prodromus*; Clusius’s first foreign variety with hairy seeds, according to Jean Bauhin.

### Temperament & Virtues.

Celery warms and dries in a moderate degree; it provokes the menses, promotes urine, and loosens the belly. Its root is opening <note>deobstruent; it clears blockages</note>, and its seed is hot, effective for provoking urine, eases inflammations of the stomach, frees one from obstructions, and is beneficial for those with dropsy <note>edema</note> or jaundice. See Tragus for other details. Regarding the virtues of celery, consult Jean Bauhin, page 96, Volume 3, part 2; and page 99 of the same volume; also page 101, where he treats the use and virtues of celery quite extensively from page 95 all the way to page 166.

---

**C**arum, or Caraway. It is known even to a blind man by the odor and flavor of its seeds. It produces a single root, long, a thumb's breadth in thickness or more, white, and with an aromatic flavor. It produces many stalks, a cubit high <note>about 18 inches</note>, furrowed and jointed, divided into small branches. It bears leaves resting on a long stalk, narrower than those of a carrot <note>original: "staphilini"</note>. The umbels consist of white flowers made of five heart-shaped petals, with a not unpleasant odor. Each flower is followed by two seeds that are oblong, thin, and furrowed. When chewed, they have a sharp and aromatic taste.

### Habitat & Season.

Caraway is found growing wild everywhere in the fields of Germany, according to the report of Tragus. It flowers in June and July, and shortly thereafter brings its seed to perfection.

### Names.

Caraway according to Matthiolus, Dodonaeus, the *History of Lyon*, Tabernaemontanus, and Gerard; *Carui* according to Lobelius and Caesalpinus; Meadow Cumin or the "Caraway of the shops" according to Caspar Bauhin; *Caros* according to Jean Bauhin.

### Temperament & Virtues.

Caraway dispels wind <note>it is carminative</note>, aids digestion, promotes urine, and is pleasant to the stomach and the mouth. For the virtues and use of caraway, see Jean Bauhin, pages 69 and 70, Volume 3, part 2.
