<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Umbelliferae family. Following the discussion on celery and parsley varieties from the previous page, the author now transitions to Anise and Pellitory, detailing their physical characteristics, medicinal uses, and the conflicting descriptions found in the works of Renaissance botanists like Bauhin, Cesalpino, and Lobelius.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS <note>The parsley or carrot family, known for flower clusters that spread like an umbrella.</note>

### Anise.
The lower leaves of this plant cling to long stalks, usually appearing in pairs; they are somewhat round yet serrated, fragrant, and whitish in color. The upper leaves are finely divided. The stem is round, hollow, hairy, and ribbed. At its top, many small branches grow, and at their ends sit the "umbels" <note>flower clusters</note> composed of tiny white flowers. It also produces small, ribbed seeds, almost ash-colored, with a pleasing scent and taste, being sweet to the palate.

->Habitat & Season.<-

*Anise grows successfully in Crete, Syria, Egypt, and other regions of the Orient. It flowers in July and August, and shortly thereafter completes its seeding. However, it is less full-bodied in Northern regions, for its seed there is more pungent <note>original: "acrosum"</note> and less suitable for extracting oil than in Eastern regions.*

->Names.<-

*It is called Anisum <note>Anise</note> by all authors.*

->Temperament & Powers.<-

*It warms and dries, and it dispels gas <note>original: "flatus discutit"</note>. Its seed increases milk in nursing mothers; when boiled with licorice, figs, and barley water <note>original: "Ptysæ"</note>, it is beneficial for the chest, etc. Consult Johann Bauhin, Volume 3, Part 2, pages 92 and 93, where he teaches at length the opinions of both ancient and modern authors on the powers of anise. See the general table of seed illustrations, 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th, and 5th; and regarding the leaves, consult Table 7 on the three diversities of celery, and Table 8 on caraway and anise.*

---

**Pellitory** <note>original: "Pyrethrum"</note> consists of a black, short root, divided into fibers like those of Spignel <note>original: "Mei specie"</note>; it is fragrant and bitter, not burning the tongue immediately unless it has been chewed for a little while. It bears leaves thinner than those of the wild carrot <note>original: "staphylyni sylvestris"</note>. It sends up a stem a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> high, which is straight and smooth, with several umbels at the top. It bears reddish flowers and flat, round seeds shaped like a lentil, which are black and stinging. These details are from Cesalpino. Melchior Guillandinus used to grow *Pellitory* in the garden at Padua; it had roots and leaves like Stinking Chamomile <note>original: "cotulæ foetidæ"</note>, and umbels supported by many stalks as if from a center, in the manner of dill or Shepherd's Needle <note>original: "pectinis veneris"</note>, with small white flowers and a stinging, bitter taste; these details are from the *Adversaria* of de l'Obel. Pellitory has leaves like wild chervil, with a stinging and almost burning quality similar to Dittander <note>original: "lepidii"</note>. It bears white flowers in an *umbel*. It has a root the thickness of a thumb, ending in many fibers. It produces a flower in clusters <note>original: "muscariis"</note> that is entirely white like Hemlock, with a seed larger than anise; these details are from Tragus <note>The botanist Hieronymus Bock</note>. 

From the descriptions cited above, it appears there are two types of Pellitory. One has leaves like the fine-leaved parsnip or wild carrot, with a reddish flower and a flat seed like a lentil; this is referred to Chapter 7 among those with flat, round, membrane-like seeds and many-times-divided leaves. The other has leaves like chervil and a smaller, ribbed seed (though larger than anise), which is the one we place here. There is also another mentioned by Gesner, whose leaves rival those of dill; he suggests that in the copies of Dioscorides one should read "of a round canopy" <note>original Greek: "σκιάδου τροχώ-δες" (skiadou trochodes)</note> rather than "hair-like umbrella" <note>original Greek: "περικοειδές" (perikoeides - likely a corruption of trichoeides)</note>. No mention of the seeds of this variety is made by authors, and therefore we leave it in doubt. If it is found to have a smaller, ribbed seed, let it be referred to the related plants contained at the beginning of this treatise; if it is found with a roundish, membrane-like seed, let it be placed among the related plants like dill, masterwort <note>original: "panax"</note>, or asclepias at the beginning of Chapter 7.

->Habitat & Season.<-

*Pellitory is found in abundance in the Apennine Mountains. It flowers toward the end of summer and completes its seeding shortly thereafter.*

G

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog of the Umbelliferae (parsley family). It moves from the discussion of Anise to Pyrethrum (Pellitory-of-Spain) and then transitions into a new taxonomic group: plants with "pierced" leaves like Perfoliata (Thorough-wax) and Bupleurum (Hare’s Ear). The author explicitly challenges the classification systems of his contemporary, Caspar Bauhin.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS <note>Plants characterized by flower clusters that radiate from a single point, like the ribs of an umbrella.</note>

### Names.

Pellitory-of-Spain <note>original: "Pyrethrum umbelliferum"</note>, according to Caspar Bauhin; the "true" version described by Mattioli, the Lyon Herbal, and Johann Bauhin; the *pyrethrum* of Dioscorides and Camerarius.

### Temperament and Powers.

The seed of Pellitory is hot and dry even to the third degree <note>In the Galenic system of medicine, the 'third degree' indicates a potent effect, strong enough to cause significant physiological change.</note>. Its root, taken with honey, is effective against epilepsy, apoplexy, and all long-standing "cold" diseases of the brain <note>In early modern medicine, "cold" ailments were thought to be caused by an excess of phlegm or a lack of vital heat.</note>. When chewed, it draws out a great deal of phlegm from the brain, which is why the Latins called it the "saliva plant" <note>original: "salivaris"</note>. It calms terrible toothaches if the root is boiled in vinegar and the strained liquid is used to rinse the mouth. The oil extracted from the fresh root is wonderfully helpful for tooth pain, especially if the crushed seeds of Stavesacre <note>original: "staphidis agriae"; a type of larkspur used topically to treat pests and pain</note> are mixed with equally crushed Pellitory roots, placed in a small bag, and held between the teeth for some time; boiling them in vinegar (and holding the liquid in the mouth while still warm) has the same effect.

The fourth section will deal with those Umbels provided with ribbed seeds which have leaves that are neither fennel-like <note>fine and feathery</note>, nor lobed, nor divided into many parts, but rather imitate something unique. Because of their ribbed seeds, joined in pairs—a feature enjoyed by *Perfoliata* and *Bupleurum*—we have placed them here at the end of their similar relatives. They reveal themselves to be true Umbels by the characteristics required for that family. As for how incorrectly they are separated from the Umbels by Caspar Bauhin and other authors, let the judgment be left to those botanists skilled in the art who read and weigh our *Botanical Preludes* <note>original: "praeludia Botanica"</note>, page 399.

---

->UMBELS WITH UNIQUE LEAVES<-
->bearing ribbed seeds.<-

**Perfoliata** <note>Thorough-wax; named because the stem appears to grow through the leaf</note>
- **Perennial:** Alpine narrow-leaved, the larger (C. B. P.) ☉ <note>perennial symbol</note>
- **Annual:** 
    - Most common type ✠ <note>annual symbol</note>
    - With longer leaves (J. B.) ✠
    - Smallest, with Hare's-ear leaf (Col.) ✠

**Bupleurum** <note>Hare's Ear</note>
- **Perennial:**
    - Broad-leaved (Tab.) ☉
    - Narrow-leaved (Dod.) ☉
- **Annual:** Smallest annual (Col.) ✠

---

### Explanation of the preceding Table.

**Perfoliata.** This is an Umbel provided with smaller, ribbed seeds; it is so named because its stem is so surrounded by leaves that they not only seem to be pierced by it, but truly are pierced. The first is the Alpine perennial, whose leaves are oblong and a palm's breadth <note>about 3 inches</note> wide; indeed, in size they approach the leaves of the pod-bearing *perfoliata*. The flowers in yellow Umbels are surrounded below by small, round leaves <note>bracts</note>. The seeds are oblong, ribbed, black, and larger than the seeds of the common *perfoliata*. The roots are thick and perennial. The second is the most common annual; it has a stem a cubit <note>roughly 18 inches</note> or a cubit and a half high, smooth, round, ribbed, jointed, hollow, and divided into many branches. It produces smooth, green, somewhat round leaves, through which the stems or branches pass; these support small yellow flowers at the top, arranged in Umbel fashion. As each of these falls away, they are succeeded by two seeds joined together, which are dark or blackish...

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog of the Umbelliferae (the parsley or carrot family). The previous page concluded the description of Pellitory-of-Spain and transitioned into plants with "pierced" leaves. This page describes several species of Perfoliata (Thorough-wax) and Bupleurum (Hare’s Ear), detailing their physical characteristics, habitats, and medicinal uses according to major Renaissance botanists.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS <note>Plants that produce flowers in "umbels"—clusters where all the flower stalks radiate from a central point like the ribs of an umbrella.</note>

...decorated with many more stripes and flat furrows than the larger species that follows, which we shall now discuss. Of this common kind, there is another species whose leaves are much longer and wider; the stems divide into many more bent, spreading branches, and the seeds are twice as large as the aforementioned common Thorough-wax <note>original: "perfoliata"</note>, being wrinkled and very rough, not black but brownish; it is decorated with three also wrinkled ridges on the back, or the outer part of the seed; the concave part is also wrinkled, and by this name it is most easily distinguished from the common kind. The 3rd is the smallest annual Thorough-wax with a *Bupleurum* leaf <note>original: "Bupleuri folio"</note>, according to Columna. This little plant relies on a thin, tiny root, from which oblong, narrow, veined leaves arise in the manner of the narrow-leaved *Bupleurum*, in pairs and arranged irregularly. At the top of the stem it bears a yellow *Umbel*, with small leaves radiating around it like a star, as can be seen in the aforementioned species of Thorough-wax. Each yellow flower is followed by two small, striped, blackish, smooth seeds.

### Place and Time.

*The first Thorough-wax grows in the Alps and high mountains. The most common annual kind, and its other species, thrive everywhere in grain fields. The smallest with a Bupleurum leaf, described by Columna <note>Fabio Columna (1567–1640), an influential Italian botanist.</note>, grows on dry, sparsely grassy hills around Blasas <note>Likely referring to the area around the Abbey of St. Blasien in the Black Forest.</note>, near the Hermits' cell not far from the town. They flower and ripen their seed in the months of August and September.*

### Names.

*The first perennial kind is called the Greater Narrow-leaved Alpine Thorough-wax by Caspar Bauhin <note>original: "C. B. P." refers to Bauhin’s Pinax Theatri Botanici (1623), a standard reference of the time.</note>. The second, the most common annual or field variety, is named the Greater Thorough-wax by Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), known as Tragus.</note>, the Common Thorough-wax by Gerard and Parkinson, and the Common Annual by Johann Bauhin; another species of this is called the Annual with longer leaves by Johann Bauhin. The sixth, or smaller variety with bent branches, is listed in Bauhin’s Prodromus and Pinax. The third is the annual, smaller narrow-leaved Thorough-wax with a Bupleurum leaf of Bauhin; it is the Narrow-leaved Mountain Thorough-wax of Columna, illustrated on page 247, and the Smallest Thorough-wax with a Bupleurum leaf by the same author on page 84 of his "Less Known Plants" <note>original: "minus cognitarum".</note>.*

### Temperament and Powers.

*Thorough-wax is hot and dry; therefore, it is suitable for drying out wounds and ulcers; it is most fit for ailments of the navel and deep-seated ruptures <note>A term often used for hernias.</note>: for internal ruptures, it is praised when decocted in wine and drunk. Consult Tragus. See more in Johann Bauhin, Volume 3, part 2, page 198.*

---

**B**upleurum <note>Hare's Ear.</note>. It is so named from the shape of its leaves, which resemble an ox’s rib <note>Derived from the Greek "bous" (ox) and "pleuron" (rib).</note>. It is commonly called Hare’s Ear <note>original: "Auricula leporis"</note> because it bears stiffer and more veined leaves (at least the larger ones), ending slightly in a point, somewhat resembling the upright ear of a hare. The first kind is perennial and of two types: the broad-leaved, which is larger in all its parts, or the narrow-leaved, which is half the size of the former. This one has a thin, rounded, smooth, striped stem, provided with frequent joints, a cubit <note>Approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> or a cubit and a half tall, to which cling somewhat long, narrow, veined leaves. The flowers are yellow in a less-spreading *Umbel*: each is followed by two seeds, stinging and similar to parsley. The 3rd is an annual, provided with a very narrow and somewhat long leaf, whose stems are trailing, nine inches <note>original: "dodrantales"</note> or a little more in length; it bears yellowish florets likewise in a less-spreading *Umbel*; the seeds are similarly tiny, striped, black, and joined in pairs.

### Place and Time.

*The first grows in dry places around Montpellier <note>original: "Monspessulum", a city in France famous for its ancient botanical garden and medical school.</note>. The second along the edges of hedges and on dry, wooded hills. The third in damp places around Blasas.*

### Names.

1. *Rigid-leaved Bupleurum of Bauhin; the Greater of Gesner "in gardens"; the Broad-leaved of Tabernaemontanus and Parkinson; the Broad-leaved of Montpellier of Gerard; the other Hare’s Ear of Johann Bauhin. 2. Narrow-leaved Bupleurum of Dodoens and Tabernaemontanus; the Narrow-leaved of Montpellier of Gerard; the Narrow-leaved of the herbalists of Lobel; the Hare’s Ear with a yellow umbel of Johann Bauhin. 3. The very narrow-leaved kind of Bauhin; the smallest of Columna; a relative of Hare’s Ear, the yellow Tooth-wort <note>original: "odontitis lutea"</note> of Valerandus and Dalecampius, according to Johann Bauhin.*

### *Temperament and virtues.*

*Hare’s Ear <note>original: "Bupleurum"</note> is moderately hot and dry; it heals wounds and ulcers. Regarding the seeds of Thorough-wax and Hare’s Ear, consult the General Table, seed illustrations 66 and 77. Regarding the leaves, see the first figure of the 7th Table of illustrations, and the last figure of that same Table. These two figures ought to have been placed together at the bottom of the Table, but through the carelessness of the engraver, they were separated.*

->UMBELS ENDOWED WITH SEEDS SURROUNDED BY LEAFY WINGS,<-
->Constituting the 3rd Genus of Umbels.<-

## CHAPTER III.

<image-desc>A botanical classification chart for Laserwort (Laserpitium).
Laserwort leaves:
1. Broader and lobed:
   - Ethiopian Seseli <note>original: "Seseli Æthiopicum"</note>, herb of Dodoens ☉
   - Similar to Libanotis <note>original: "Libanotidi Simile"</note>, with curled seeds, according to Jean Bauhin ☉
2. Narrower:
   - Deep green and shining ☉
   - Pale green:
     - Divided in many ways ☉
     - Arranged in pairs ☉
The sun symbol (☉) typically indicates a perennial plant or a specific medicinal status in early botanical texts.</image-desc>

### *Explanation of the Table above.*

Laserwort <note>original: "Laserpitium"</note>. Take it as a generic marker that two seeds joined together, surrounded by leafy or membranous wings, resemble the paddles of a river watermill wheel. It is twofold in the nature of its leaves: it is either endowed with broader leaves arranged in wide lobes, or with smaller and narrower lobes, more finely cut and clinging to their stalks. Those that are characterized by broader lobes are also twofold. The first of these is incorrectly called "Ethiopian Seseli" by authors; but since its seed is surrounded by several leafy wings (in the manner of other Laserworts), it can be placed nowhere more aptly than among its fellow plants; as is also the case with the second one following, which is likewise wrongly considered by authors to be a kind of Libanotis <note>A name used historically for several aromatic plants, often "Moon-carrot."</note>. The first, called Ethiopian Seseli, has a thick, very long root, which is tufted where it emerges from the earth. (This is very common to all incense-bearing <note>original: "thuriferis"</note> *Umbellifers* that abound in a milky or yellow sap.) It produces a knotty stem of two or three cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>. The individual leaves are larger, somewhat round, serrated, glaucous <note>original: "glauca"; a pale sea-green or bluish-grey color</note> underneath and greenish above, clinging to a long stalk; these are subdivided again into leaflets positioned opposite one another. The *Umbels* consist of many white flowers; and from each flower, composed of five petals, there follow two seeds provided with several leafy wings, possessing a certain sharpness and bitterness. The 2nd is the "Bitter Costus with curled seeds" of Jean Bauhin. The leaves of this plant are much longer, wider, and thicker than those of the former, whereby it is very easily distinguished from others of its kind at first glance by these marks—and especially by the seeds, which have curled and wrinkled leafy wings. Alternatively, the plant has narrower leaves, of which three types are presented by us according to the three-fold appearance of the leaves. It either has shining, deep green leaves divided in many directions, or it has leaves of a much paler green compared to the former, and also a little wider. The last type is endowed with leaves much narrower than the previous ones, with somewhat long, narrow lobes arranged opposite one another and clinging to the rachis <note>The main axis or "spine" of a compound leaf.</note>, or central rib. They differ little among themselves in the height of their stems or the size of their seeds, except that the seed of all three of these has wings lightly adorned with curls and waves. All three of these last species of Laserwort have a very large root, white inside and ash-colored on the outside, full of juice, soft, fatty, and fragrant—

<meta>catchword: fragrant-</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal virtues of Hare’s Ear and provides a detailed classification and morphological description of various species of Laserwort (Laserpitium), focusing on their leaf structures and winged seeds.</summary>
<keywords>Umbellifers, Hare’s Ear, Laserwort, Seseli, botanical classification, medicinal plants, seed morphology, leaf structure</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Laserwort (Laserpitium), specifically detailing how its seeds appear different when fresh versus when dried. It bridges the transition from the third genus of umbellifers to the fourth, Thapsia.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>UMBELLIFERS.</header>

...fragrant. This characteristic is shared by many *Umbels* <note>Plants in the parsley family (Apiaceae)</note> serving under different genera, as we shall teach more clearly below in the more general observations on the Umbels. Likewise, the leafy wings of the seeds of all these three Laserworts <note>original: "laserpitiorum"</note>, just like the wings of the other broad-leaved types, number eight. Specifically, there are two erect wings on each seed, here and there, and two others at the base where the seed is joined in pairs; so that on individual seeds there always appear two on the back, or convex part of the seed, and just as many toward the base. While they are considered together, and while they are still green and joined, six are counted, with the two wings at the base being closely joined together, which can be counted as two on each side; whereas in individual seeds that are dried and fallen, four appear.

---

->Habitat and season.<-

*The first grows on Mount Baldo, and other mountains of Italy and France. We found this species of Libanotis (but more rightly called Laserwort) growing abundantly at the southern edges of vineyards on the hills or small mountains near the village of Sancerre, situated on a mountain along the Loire. It is also frequently seen in the mountains of Auvergne. The second grows on Mount Summano in Italy. The following three grow in the mountains of Italy, Narbonnese Gaul, and Provence.*

->Names.<-

*The first is called by us the broader-leaved lobed Laserwort. By other authors it is called Seseli, and Ethiopian Seseli; Dodoens <note>original: "Dod."</note> gave many the name of Libanotis. The second is a Laserwort similar to Libanotis with a curled seed; in our tables it is called Bitter Costus similar to Libanotis with a curled seed, according to Jean Bauhin <note>original: "F. B."</note>, by whom it is well illustrated. The lesser broad-leaved Libanotis with a curled seed, according to Caspar Bauhin’s Prodromus <note>original: "C. B. Prod."</note>. The third is Laserwort with narrower, shining, and deep green leaves, according to us. The fourth is Laserwort with narrower, pale green leaves, according to us. French Laserwort, from Caspar Bauhin’s Pinax <note>original: "C. B. P."</note>. The Marseille type, according to Tabernaemontanus <note>original: "Tab."</note>, brought from the region of Marseille, according to Jean Bauhin. The last, as shown by us in the table, is explained above: it is the Laserwort with leaves placed in pairs and a curled seed.*

->Temperament and virtues.<-

*Laserwort is hot and dry; therefore it promotes urine and also the menstrual flow of women; it soothes colicky pains if eaten with myrrh and pepper. Concerning the laser <note>The resin or juice</note> extracted from Laserwort, consult Jean Bauhin, Volume 3, Part 2, page 136, where he deals at length with its powers and use in his usual manner. Regarding the seeds, consult the general table of seed illustrations 8, 8, 9, 9, 10, 10. We did not care to depict the leaves because they can be easily understood from the description and explanation provided above.*

---

### UMBELS ENDOWED WITH A SEED SURROUNDED BY A LEAFY WING.
#### The Fourth Genus of umbels.

# CHAPTER IV.

<image-desc>A large curly bracket groups the following three botanical names under the genus "Thapsia" (Deadly Carrot) listed to the left.</image-desc>

> Thapsia { Great, with a very broad leaf, Caspar Bauhin's Pinax 31. Clusius ☉
> Thapsia { Broad-leaved and shaggy, Caspar Bauhin's Pinax 1. Clusius ☉
> Thapsia { Lesser, in the form of a carrot, Lobelius ☉

---

->Explanation of the preceding Table.<-

THapsia constitutes the 4th subordinate genus of *Umbels*. Let its generic mark be a seed that is almost circular or orbicular, surrounded by a leafy wing that is nonetheless split at the end; by this name it is especially distinguished from Laserwort, whose seeds are surrounded by several smaller and more flattened leafy wings. The seeds of Angelica have much chaffiness...

<meta>catchword: keel*</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the study of Laserwort (Laserpitium), describing its seed morphology, habitat in France and Italy, and its medicinal uses for colic and digestion. It then introduces the fourth genus, Thapsia, defined by its circular, winged seeds.</summary>
<keywords>Laserwort, Thapsia, seed anatomy, Mount Baldo, Sancerre, Jean Bauhin, Caspar Bauhin, medicinal herbs, diuretics</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the genus Thapsia (Deadly Carrot) within the Umbellifer family, detailing three specific varieties, their habitats, and their medicinal properties. It follows the previous discussion on Laserwort seeds.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>UMBELLIFERS.</header>

...exceeding the membranous ridges. There are three types: the first is the largest, with a very broad leaf and the largest seed of its entire family. This plant has dark green leaves, covered with a sort of ash-colored or shaggy down, spread upon the ground and divided in many ways, with a very unpleasant taste; from among these emerges a stem one or two cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> high, thick, round, knotted, and hollow, as is the case in the majority of the **Umbels** <note>Plants with flower clusters that spread from a common point like umbrella ribs.</note>. At the very top, it supports a large **Umbel** laden with yellow flowers; when pregnant with seed, it forms a surface weighed down by seeds joined in pairs and compressed, encircled by a leafy wing in a rotating or circular fashion. The root is thick and long (the upper part is hairy or "crested," as are most Umbellifers clothed in a thicker bark), dark or dusky on the outside and white within, full of a milky sap. Its taste is extremely bitter, sharp, and unpleasant, so much so that it even induces nausea. The second type is larger in respect to the one following it; its leaves are broader than those of the Great Mountain Hemlock, which is foul-smelling; they are a pale green, covered with a light hairiness. Its seeds are half the size of the first. The third is the smallest of all, and in its entire appearance (if one considers the arrangement of the leaves, which have the likeness of a carrot, or leaves divided in many directions) it is different from the preceding two: indeed, its compressed seeds, encircled by a leafy wing and joined in pairs, are three times smaller than the seeds of the previous ones.

### Habitat and Season.

->*The first and second grow on the stony hills of Spain, Portugal, and many other high and uncultivated places; the third grows in the mountains of Narbonnese Gaul <note>Modern-day Southern France.</note>. They flower and perfect their seeds toward Autumn.*<-

### Names.

*The first is the **Great Thapsia with a very broad leaf**, Caspar Bauhin <note>C. B. P.</note> 3, Clusius in his History. The second is the **Broad-leaved shaggy Thapsia**, Caspar Bauhin, the **Broad-leaved Thapsia** of Tabernaemontanus 1, Clusius in his History, the **Broad-leaved Spanish Thapsia** of Parkinson, the **First Thapsia** of Clusius, the **Broad-leaved yellow-flowered Thapsia with broad seed**, similar to the Peloponnesian Seseli of Lobelius, and Jean Bauhin. The **Peloponnesian Seseli** of Tabernaemontanus. The third is the **Thapsia with the appearance of a carrot** of Lobelius, with a **carrot leaf** according to Caspar Bauhin, with the **appearance of a common carrot** according to Parkinson. We would desire that both the leaves and seeds of all three of these be described and engraved anew, along with many other images poorly provided by authors and by the compilers of this latter century, who present poorly drawn and painted images and descriptions of the same; this has done no small harm to the Botanical Republic of Letters. The third has a leafy seed, smaller than the second, and leaves more closely approaching the leaves of Giant Fennel.*

### Temperament and Virtues.

*Thapsia is hot and dry. Its root was wrongly sold by impostors in Spain in place of **turbith** <note>A valuable purgative root usually imported from the East.</note>, to the great detriment of the public. The old women of Salamanca used the root of this plant to provoke menstruation and to bring about other evacuations according to the testimony of Clusius, but it purges with such force <term>upward</term> <note>original Greek: ἄνω (ano), meaning to vomit</note> and <term>downward</term> <note>original Greek: κάτω (kato), meaning through the bowels</note> that it puts those taking it in the greatest danger. Therefore, one must entirely abstain from such <term>violent purgatives</term> <note>original: "mochlicis," derived from the Greek word for a lever, used for drugs that "move" the body with extreme force</note>, and I would advise always, and on every given occasion, to use gentler and safer remedies. Concerning the seeds, consult the General Table of seed images II. 11. since the leaves easily distinguish the species according to the explanation of the preceding Table. It is sufficient to have shown the seeds of the second Thapsia.*

# UMBELS PROVIDED WITH HAIRY OR SHAGGY SEEDS.
### *The Fifth Genus of Umbels.*

# CHAPTER V.

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| | **Cultivated** | White ☉ <note>The symbol ☉ often denotes an annual plant.</note> |
| | | Yellow ☉ |
| | | Red ☉ |
| | | |
| **Fine-leaved Parsnip, Daucus, or Carrot.** <note>In historical botany, the terms for Parsnip (*Pastinaca*) and Carrot (*Daucus*) were often used interchangeably or as synonyms.</note> | **Wild** | Genuine of Dioscorides <note>Referring to the 1st-century Greek physician Dioscorides, whose work was the standard botanical reference for centuries.</note> ☉ |
| | | Shining ☉ |
| | | With yellow root and umbel ♃ <note>The symbol ♃ often denotes a perennial plant.</note> |
| | | |
| | **Cretan** | With rays of the umbel longer than those of the *Gingidium* <note>A type of wild chervil or shepherd's needle.</note> ☉ |
| | | With spherical heads. Wild spherical Cumin of Tabernaemontanus. <note>original: "Cuminum sylvestre globosum, Tab." Jacobus Theodorus Tabernaemontanus was a famous 16th-century herbalist.</note> ☉ |

---

->*Explanation of the preceding Table.*<-

The **fine-leaved Parsnip**, **Daucus**, or **Carrot** has hairy leaves, divided in many directions like wings. It sends up stems two cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> high, which are shaggy, grooved, hollow, and branched; at the tips of these, very small white flowers appear, forming a convex surface that is round at the edge. As the flowers fade, they are succeeded by an **Umbel** <note>The umbrella-like flower cluster characteristic of this family.</note> pregnant with hairy seed, which becomes concave, **like a bird's nest.** <note>original: "nidi avis instar." This is still a common name for the wild carrot today.</note> See the second plate of the illustrations we have provided. Today it is sown among all Europeans and is cultivated for its root, which is a foot long, pear-shaped (or an inverted cone), consisting of a pithy substance inside and a fleshy, crumbly substance outside—which may be white, yellow, or red—and it is greatly sought after in the kitchen. In the second year, stems rise from the same root supporting a white **Umbel**; in the wild type (or the genuine one of Dioscorides), a purple spot is seen in the very center.

The fine-leaved Parsnip is of three kinds. The 1st is the **cultivated** or garden variety, in which the care of the roots is the primary concern; for this reason, it is brought up in gardens through cultivation and careful tending. It is provided with a larger and thicker top-shaped root, which is white, yellow, or red in color, as nature thus plays with the sowing. The second is the **wild** variety, which is also threefold: the first is called the "genuine wild" by Dioscorides; it is sought after for its seed, which spreads a pleasant and very agreeable odor to the nostrils and is adapted for various uses in medicine. Except for the purple spot in the middle of the **Umbel** and the smallness of the root (which changes only due to more fertile soil), they differ in species not at all. There is another wild type whose leaves are not shaggy, but a little broader, thicker, more finely cut, and shining; the seed of this is much larger than the seeds of the previous ones. There is also a 3rd wild type, whose root and **Umbel** are a pale yellow. It is distinguished from the aforementioned ones by the color of the **Umbel**, insofar as the aforementioned **Umbels** are all consistently seen to be white. The **Cretan Daucus**, or Cretan Carrot, is also observed to be twofold; the first is known especially by this mark: that it produces rays of the **Umbels** five inches long...

<vocab>Pastinaca tenuifolia (Fine-leaved Parsnip/Carrot), Daucus (Carrot), Umbelliferæ (Umbellifers), Umbella (Umbel), Dioscoridis (Of Dioscorides), Sativa (Cultivated), Sylvestris (Wild), Cretica (Cretan)</vocab>

<summary>This page initiates Chapter V, focusing on carrots (Daucus) and their hairy-seeded relatives, providing a taxonomic breakdown into cultivated, wild, and Cretan varieties. It describes the physical characteristics of the carrot, including its "bird's nest" shaped seed head and its culinary and medicinal importance in early modern Europe.</summary>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the carrot family (Daucus), specifically focusing on the structure of the umbel and the geographical origins of various specimens, including those grown in the author's own contemporary gardens.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>WHORL-FLOWERED PLANTS.</header>

...inches <note>original: "cias," likely the end of "uncias" (inches) from the previous page.</note> long, thicker, and stiffer than the rays of the **toothpick-weed** <note>original: "visnagæ" (*Ammi visnaga*), a plant whose dried umbel rays were used as toothpicks.</note>; these form a concave surface like a bird’s nest while the seeds are still maturing. They produce hairy, shaggy seeds, similar to the common carrot but much larger and thicker. The **carrot**, or second **Cretan Daucus**, is characterized by spherical heads; it is easily recognized by this mark alone from all **Umbels** <term>Umbels: umbrella-like flower clusters where all stems radiate from a single point.</term> properly so called. It has leaves growing from the central stalk in a wing-like pattern, trailing on the ground, smooth, and jagged-edged, somewhat mimicking the leaves of the **earth-nut** or **corn-parsley** described by Parkinson <note>original: "sii terrestris seu segetalis Park." referring to the English herbalist John Parkinson.</note>. From the center of these trailing leaves rises a small stem half a foot high, supporting at its peak little spheres composed of many individual, oblong seeds, which are hairy on the upper and thinner part (not joined in pairs). Due to this lack of the usual characteristics, I conclude it is not a **true Umbel**; nevertheless, we have classified it here because of its hairy seed and aromatic scent, which resembles carrots. If anyone chooses to place it among the "improper" **Umbels** at the end of the section, I shall not object. Furthermore, it has no kinship with cumin, as its scent is closer to a carrot. As I said, it produces single seeds, gifted with many hairs or downy tufts at the tip, and the seed itself is surrounded by a sort of net, elegantly constructed by nature. Perhaps it might not be inappropriately placed after the **valerian** (which also has downy seeds) among the **Umbels** so-called "improperly."

### Place & Time.

*The fine-leaved cultivated parsnip is grown in gardens; the wild variety grows everywhere in meadows. The "shining" wild variety is native to the damp and rich meadows of the Brittany coast <note>original: "oram maritimam Armoricam".</note>; its seed, when sown in the Royal Garden at Blois <note>original: "horto regio Blasensi".</note>, produced a parsnip with shining and more deeply divided leaves. From the seeds of this plant given to me last year (along with a thousand others shared with me by the noble and gentleman Patrick Murray of Livingston <note>Sir Patrick Murray (1632–1671) was a Scottish botanist whose collection formed the basis of the Edinburgh Botanic Garden.</note>), the plant itself currently flourishes in the public University garden, as well as in Edward Morgan's garden <note>Edward Morgan was a famous 17th-century gardener who maintained a celebrated "physic garden" near Westminster Abbey.</note> behind Westminster Cathedral. Even now, despite the winter, one can see the leaves of this blue-green carrot—shining and without any hairs or bristles. Therefore, it is not merely a chance variety, because it does not revert to the wild form, which is usually characterized by hairy leaves and stems. A carrot with a yellow root and yellow umbel-seeds was sent to us from the Paduan fields by Master Alexander Balam, as were many other rare seeds from Crete and Ragusa <note>Modern-day Dubrovnik, Croatia.</note>. Seeds of the carrot with umbel-rays longer than those of the **Gingidium** <note>A plant similar to a wild carrot, sometimes called "Shepherd’s Needle".</note> were brought to England and shared with me from Crete and, last year, from Tangier in Mauritania <note>original: "Mauritania Tingitana".</note> by the same English gentleman, Master Alexander Balam. The last Cretan parsnip, or Cretan **Daucus** with spherical heads, is named by us more fittingly than "Cumin," with which it agrees neither in leaves, nor scent, nor taste, and much less in seeds. It is an umbel-bearing plant, "improperly" speaking. It is called Wild Cumin by Parkinson, Dodonaeus, and L'Obel, and the "first highly aromatic, spherical wild cumin" by Bauhin.*

### Names.

*The first fine-leaved cultivated parsnip with a white, yellow, or dark red root: Caspar Bauhin. Fine-leaved cultivated: Dodonaeus. Our carrot: Camerarius. Common yellow-rooted: L’Obel. Yellow **Staphilinus** <note>A Greek-derived name for the carrot.</note>: Dodonaeus's French edition. The second is the Wild Parsnip of Dioscorides: L’Obel. Wild **Staphilinus**: Tragus. Common **Daucus**: Clusius. The third and fourth have been observed by no one before us. The fifth is as presented by us above under the name **Daucus**, or Cretan parsnip, with umbel-rays longer than those of the **Gingidium**. The last is the Cretan **Daucus** with spherical heads. On the island of Lemnos, it is called **Lagochymeni**, which means "the hare’s lair" <note>original: "Lagochymeni, id est leporis cubile", from the Greek lagos (hare) and koite (bed).</note>: Belon. See above for other synonyms. Concerning the seeds of the **Daucus**, consult the General Table of Illustrations of seeds 12, 12, 13, 13. For the leaves, the concave umbel, and the seeds, see the second Table of Illustrations.*

### Temperament & Powers.

*The fine-leaved cultivated parsnip is of a hot and moist temperament (combined with sweetness); it generates gas and stirs up passion <note>original: "ad venerem excitat" — historically believed to be an aphrodisiac.</note>. The wild variety stimulates urine and menstruation, and dispels fluid under the skin <note>Edema or dropsy.</note>. It assists with colic pain and "suffocations of the womb" <note>A historical diagnosis for various female ailments, often related to what was then called "hysteria."</note>. See the properties of Carrots in Johann Bauhin, Volume 3, part 2, page 66.*

It highlights the author's botanical network, mentioning specimens received from Patrick Murray and Alexander Balam currently growing in London gardens.</summary>

## UMBELS PROVIDED WITH PRICKLY OR THORNY SEEDS.
### The Sixth Genus of Umbels.

# CHAPTER VI

**The Genus Bur-Parsley** <note>original: "Caucalis"</note>
*   **Greater**
    *   **Broad-leaved** <note>original: "Platyphyllon"</note>
        *   Purple <note>original: "purpurea"</note> (Annual plant)
        *   White <note>original: "alba"</note> (Annual plant)
    *   **Fine-leaved** <note>original: "Leptophyllon"</note>
        *   Montpellier variety with large prickly fruit, known as the Burdock-parsley <note>original: "C. B. P. Lappa"</note>
        *   Pliny’s cattle-fodder <note>original: "Boaria Plinii Hist. Lugd."</note> (Annual plant)
        *   Prickly large-flowered <note>original: "Echinata magno flore"</note> (Ornamental)
    *   **Tangier Carrot-like Bur-parsley** <note>original: "Daucoïdes Tingitana"</note> (Annual, Ornamental)
*   **Lesser**
    *   Field variety with small flowers & fruit (Annual plant)
    *   Knotted prickly-seeded variety <note>original: "nodoso echinato semine"</note> (Ornamental)

---

### Explanation of the preceding Table.

**B**ur-parsley <note>original: "Caucalis"</note> is an **Umbel** <term>Umbel: a flower cluster where all stalks spring from a single point, like the ribs of an umbrella.</term> that (in regards to the shape of the seed) is very closely related to the previous genus, namely the **carrot**, and for that reason, we have placed it immediately following. Its seed is prickly <note>original: "echinato," meaning covered in spines like a hedgehog</note> or thorny, much stiffer than the seed of the preceding carrot, and provided with longer and more solid prickles. All species of bur-parsley have a fibrous root; by this mark, especially when combined with the stiffer prickles, they are distinguished from the carrot; additionally, the seeds are much larger and thicker. Furthermore, besides **Dioscorides** <note>A first-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*.</note>, **Galen** <note>An influential Roman physician and philosopher of the second century.</note> also writes that the bur-parsley is similar to the wild carrot in flavor and medicinal power, specifically that it warms and dries the body. However, we have drawn this correspondence from the "book of nature" based on the similarity in the powers and appearances of the seeds and other parts. 

There are two types: the **broad-leaved** <note>original: "$\pi\lambda\alpha\tau\acute{u}\phi\upsilon\lambda\lambda o\varsigma$" (platyphyllos)</note>, whose leaves are twice as large as the others, with larger flowers and larger parts overall, which is why it is divided by **branching classification** <note>original: "$\delta\iota\chi\omicron\tau\omicron\mu\acute{\iota}\alpha\nu$" (dichotomian)</note> into the "greater" or "lesser." The **greater** is either the **broad-leaved** variety, with flowers that are more often purple and rarely white; or the **fine-leaved** <note>original: "$\lambda\epsilon\pi\tau\acute{o}\phi\upsilon\lambda\lambda o\varsigma$" (leptophyllos)</note> variety, whose flower is white. This latter type is two-fold: one with large fruit and small flowers (which is twice the size of those described below) and another with large flowers and seeds that are also large but flatter. 

Next follows the **Tangier Carrot-like bur-parsley**. Upon its first emergence from the earth, and before it develops a stalk, its leaves so closely mimic those of the **carrot** (or common carrot) that it can be mistaken for one. It produces stalks one or two feet high, at the tops of which appear white flowers that turn purple before they open. As each of these falls away, they are succeeded by two seeds, oblong and compact, provided with very long and very fine prickles. See the 1st general table of seed illustrations, numbers 17 and 17. 

The **Lesser** bur-parsley is either the kind with small flowers and small, shaggy, or bristly fruit, whose stalk is one or two feet high, supporting tiny white (rarely purple) florets on the highest branches; or it is the **trailing** <note>original: "procumbens"</note> kind, which flowers at every node of the stalk and bears many shaggy, wrinkled seeds clustered like grapes at the nodes or "little knees" <note>original: "geniculos"</note> of the stem. From this, it takes the name **knotted prickly-seeded bur-parsley**.

### Location & Time.

All these bur-parsleys grow among crops (except for the **knotted** variety, which is more often found along embankments, ditches, and at the bases of walls,

<header>U M B E L L I F E R S.</header>

...they spring up naturally in the suburbs). They produce flowers and ripen their seeds at the same time as the wheat harvest in France, occurring partly in the Northern and partly in the Southern regions. For the most part, they are also seen growing wild in England, as much in the grain fields as in the hedges. The **fourth** species of seeds were sent to me from Tangier <note>original: "Mauritania Tingitana"</note> by my friend Robert Spotswod, a resident surgeon and apothecary there. The **fifth** species is found everywhere in grain fields and the ditches of hedges; the last species frequently grows at the base of walls and the margins of ditches.

->Names.<-

The first of the larger types is the *broad-leaved prickly field bur-parsley* <note>original: "arvensis echinata, latifolia, C. B. B."</note>, also called the *greater purple broad-leaved hedgehog-parsley* <note>original: "echinophora major platyphyllos purpurea, Col."</note>. It is abundant among crops around Blois; the same plant is very rarely found with a white flower. The **second** is the *Montpellier prickly type with large fruit* <note>original: "Monspeliaca echinata magno fructu, C. B. P."</note>, also known as Pliny’s "ox-bur" <note>original: "lappa boaria Plinii"</note>. The names were transposed by Fabio Colonna <note>original: "Columna"</note> on page 94 of his work on lesser-known plants; for the term **dense-fruited** <note>original: "pyknocarpos"</note> ought to be inscribed over the figure on the right, and **thick-fruited** <note>original: "pachycarpos"</note> is the figure to be inscribed on the left. Thus, Bauhin’s *Montpellier bur-parsley with large fruit* is actually the **dense-fruited** hedgehog-parsley, and the other—the *prickly field bur-parsley with large flowers*—is the **thick-fruited** hedgehog-parsley. This is the same plant we named in the kinship table above as the *greater Tangier carrot-like bur-parsley*. The **fifth** is the *field bur-parsley with small flowers and fruit*. The **sixth** is the *knotted prickly-seeded bur-parsley*, related to the *Bristly Chervil* <note>original: "anthrisco Hispido"</note>, if not a variety of it.

->Temperament and medicinal powers.<-

Bur-parsley <note>original: "Caucalis"</note> is of a **hot and dry temperament** <term>Temperament: In early medicine, plants were classified as hot, cold, moist, or dry to balance the body's "humors."</term>; it promotes the flow of urine and removes obstructions of the spleen and liver. See the various authors regarding the medicinal powers of bur-parsley. For the figures or illustrations of the seeds, consult the general table of seed images, numbers 14 through 19, and the explanation of that table. The leaves of all these species are divided in many ways, similar to a carrot.

---

# CHAPTER VII

**T**he seventh genus of **Umbels** will consist of the **Umbelliferous** plants provided with a **membranous** <term>Membranous: thin, dry, and flexible, like a parchment skin.</term>, somewhat round, and compressed seed. According to the threefold arrangement and position of the leaves, these are to be divided by us into three parts. They are either: firstly, those with **ferulaceous** <term>Ferulaceous: leaf structures resembling the Giant Fennel, often very fine and thread-like.</term> or fennel-like leaves; secondly, those with **lobed** leaves; or finally, thirdly, those which bear leaves that are neither fennel-like nor lobed, but an intermediate type divided in many ways. First, we shall speak of those with fennel-like leaves and a leaf-like or membranous, roundish, compressed seed. Although **Hog's Fennel** <note>original: "Peucedanum"</note> does not bear leaves that are entirely fennel-like—since it consists of long, grass-like leaves very finely divided into threes and sets of nine—it is nonetheless adorned with a membranous, roundish seed, and thus shall serve under the same banner as the aforementioned plants.

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>UMBELLIFERS.</header>

## ->Explanation of the preceding Plate.<-

**The larger Ferula, or female.** <note>original: "Ferula major, seu fœmina"</note> This plant holds the first place among the *Umbellifers* <note>Plants characterized by umbrella-like flower clusters</note> that possess leafy, compressed, round or oblong seeds and fennel-like leaves. It is the tallest of its entire class, for it produces a stalk sometimes five or six cubits high <note>About 7.5 to 9 feet tall</note>, which is thick, filled with a spongy substance, and branched. It has fennel-like leaves, though they are gifted with wider and shorter segments and a paler green color. The *Umbels* are wide and round with yellow, reflexed <note>bent backward</note> flowers; once these fall away, they are succeeded by large, leafy, round-oblong seeds which are blackish when ripe. These seeds are always paired; the outer part is marked by three sunken lines, while the inner part has two small fibers by which they are joined and nourished while still green. After the seeds are mature and have fallen, these two dry fibers remain, standing apart at the upper tip. This can be seen in the paired combination of almost all *Umbellifers* after the seeds have dried. Look at and consider the *Umbels* of the three types of "Cachrys" <note>A genus of umbellifers with corky seeds</note> shown in our 3rd Plate of illustrations; you will observe the seeds joined together while still green, some already dried and gaping open, and finally some fallen away at maturity, leaving the two dry, open fibers behind. The root of this plant, like that of the three Cachrys, pours out a milky "tear" <note>sap or resin</note> when wounded. Its scent is pleasant and its taste is sharp. The **second** type is the **lesser Ferula with thinner leaves** <note>original: "ferula minor tenuiori folio"</note>: it has a striated fennel-like stalk which is hollow inside. Its leaves are also like fennel but wider and fuller—though smaller than in the larger Ferula—emerging at the base of the branches, which occurs in all fennel-like plants, alternating now from this side, now from that. Its flower is yellow and reflexed in the *Umbels*; the seed is also oblong, leafy, and turns somewhat yellowish upon maturity, marked with sunken streaks.

**Asclepius’s Heal-all.** <note>original: "Panax asclepium"</note> This grows in the mountains and has shorter leaves of a paler green than fennel. It bears a round, hollow stalk, one or two cubits high, with thick joints. It bears yellow flowers and fuller, yellowish seeds that are quite compressed, with three sunken streaks on the outside. When the root of this plant is cut, a saffron-colored juice flows out; whether this juice, once thickened, is the resin known as **opopanax**, let those who are diligent and curious in botanical matters examine, and let them consult Andrea Cesalpino <note>A famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> on pages 272 and 280.

---

->Location and Season.<-

*The larger Ferula grows on cliffs accustomed to being scorched by the sun, as much in Southern France <note>original: "Gallia Narbonensi"</note> as in the fields of Apulia or Florence. The thin-leaved Ferula and Asclepius’s Heal-all are found growing wild in the mountains of Italy and France, and in the islands of the Mediterranean Sea, such as Crete, Sicily, etc. They bloom around the end of July; all of them finish their seeds around the end of summer.*

->Names.<-

*The first is the Ferula of Mattioli, Cordus, Dioscorides, Dodoens, L'Obel, Daléchamps, Camerarius, and Tabernaemontanus. The second is the Ferula with thinner leaves, or Ferulago, according to Caspar Bauhin; the Ferulago of Tabernaemontanus. The third is the Panax asclepium of Dodoens, Cesalpino, and Tabernaemontanus; the Ferula-leaved and seeded Libanotis <note>Frankincense-plant</note> of Caspar Bauhin. I would prefer to call it a Ferula that pours out the scent and juice of Frankincense.*

->Temperament and Powers.<-

*Ferula is hot and dry, and its gum—Sagapenum and Galbanum <note>Medicinal resins</note>—distilling from the root is also hot and dry in the third degree. When dissolved in a broth of rue, it stimulates urine and menstruation, and draws out a dead fetus. See more concerning all three of these (which possess the same powers to a greater or lesser degree) in the works of Jean Bauhin, and consult Andrea Cesalpino regarding the use of Asclepius’s Heal-all.*

<meta>This page continues the botanical classification of the Umbellifer family, moving from the giant fennels (Ferula) described on page 35 to Dill (Anethum) and Hog’s Fennel (Peucedanum). The author continues to emphasize seed structure as the primary means of identification.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS.

**Dill.** <note>original: "Anethum"</note> The seed is likewise leafy <note>meaning thin-edged or winged</note>, somewhat round, flattened, quite small, and dark green, marked with three sunken lines on the outer side. It has leaves so similar to the leaves of fennel that it could not easily be distinguished from fennel before the production of the seed, except for its darker green color, its different scent, and the overall smallness of its parts. However, once the seeds appear, it is easily separated from fennel by the shape of the seed and its specific scent and flavor, and it is categorized here with its relatives. Furthermore, Dill is the only annual plant that possesses a leafy, somewhat round seed and fennel-like, or finely divided, leaves.

### Location and Season.

*Dill grows of its own accord in vegetable gardens; it is also found among the grain crops in southern regions. It produces yellowish or greenish-yellow flowers and greenish or dark green seeds, which it ripens in the same summer. The engraving of Dill at the top of Plate 8 should have closed that Plate; and immediately before it, Thorough-wax <note>Perfoliata</note> and Hare's Ear <note>Bupleurum</note> should have been placed, and before those, Caraway and Anise. However, while I was away, after the drawings of the individual umbels <note>umbrella-like flower clusters</note> contained in this Plate were properly finished, the engraver arranged them in a very confused manner. One must follow the order I have provided here in the text and inserted into the individual Plates.*

### Names.

*Garden Dill, according to Caspar Bauhin <note>C. B. P.</note>. Dill, according to Mattioli, Dodoens, the Lyon group <note>Lugd.</note>, Tabernaemontanus, Gerard, and Jean Bauhin.*

### Temperament and Virtues.

*It is hot in the third degree and dry in the second <note>This refers to the Galenic system of medicine, which classified plants by their effects on the body's humors</note>. According to Dioscorides, it increases breast milk, and a decoction of it aids in childbirth. The seed dispels gas and intestinal cramps, and it dries out abscesses and ulcers. See more on its capabilities in Jean Bauhin, pages 6 and 7 of Volume 3, Part 2.*

**Hog's Fennel.** <note>original: "Peucedanum," also known as Sulphur-root</note> This is a giant-fennel-like plant, bearing grass-like leaves that are thrice-divided into three parts. Since it bears leafy, somewhat round, flattened seeds—marked with three light lines on the outer part of the seed—in the manner of other giant-fennel or fennel-like umbels on the tops of two-foot or sometimes three-foot stalks (depending on the soil where it is planted), we have categorized it here; indeed, we believe it cannot be more aptly placed elsewhere. There are three types: the first is the **Italian**, the largest of all; this has a very long, juicy, and bitter root sunk deep into the earth, which has a "beard" or grey tuft above the ground similar to pig bristles. It has a smooth, round stalk and leaves more like a pine than a fennel; for according to Tragus, it has more similarity to pine than to fennel. It has a yellow umbel in its clusters, of which the individual flowers, standing on individual little stalks or rays, are composed of five reflexed <note>bent backward</note> petals (a feature found in the majority of giant-fennel-like plants gifted with yellow leaflets). As these individual flowers fade, they are succeeded by two seeds that are leafy, flattened, oblong, and somewhat swollen or rounded in an oval shape on the outer part with sunken lines. The **second type** is the smaller **German** variety, which is smaller in size than the first but equal in all other respects. There is also a **third, lower-growing type** with leaves arranged in pairs; the parts of its leaf-divisions are almost equal in width and length to the leaf of Toadflax <note>Linaria</note>. This appears on the plant in early spring. The leaves are arranged more densely on the central stem and are divided into lobes or pairs near the ground (not in three parts like the previous types); however, as the plant grows its stalk, the leaves are more finely split. If the root of any of these is wounded, it emits a yellow sap with a pitch-like, foul smell.

# UMBELLIFERS.

### Location and Season.

The first variety is found in Italy and Sicily, from where its seed was first brought to the Italians; the second grows in the Hercynian Forest <note>A vast, ancient forest region in south-central Germany, including the modern Black Forest</note> in Germany, and indeed in all the aged forests I have ever visited; it also occurs throughout France, especially in shady and humid places. The third variety is found in the mountains of Auvergne <note>original: "Arvernia"</note>, from where it was sent to us by that noble man and diligent seeker of plants, Monsieur de Fontain, a most worthy Abbot, and a man of integrity and learning. Indeed, many other plants were shared (by sending seeds) by the aforementioned gentleman with my colleague Monsieur Marchant, residing at Blois, who is an intimate and familiar friend of the said Abbot. They bloom toward the end of summer; at the start of autumn, they produce seeds that are membrane-like, oblong, needle-shaped, and at least three times narrower, thinner, smoother, and smaller than the seeds of the Giant Fennel <note>Ferula</note>.

### Names.

Greater Italian Hog's Fennel <note>original: "Peucedanum majus Italicum"</note>, according to the illustrations of Lobel, Caspar Bauhin, John Bauhin, and Parkinson. The second is German Hog's Fennel, according to Caspar Bauhin; or Lesser German Hog's Fennel, according to John Bauhin; or "Swine-Fennel" <note>original: "foeniculum porcinum"</note> and "Pig-tail" <note>original: "cauda porcina"</note> according to Tabernaemontanus. Common Hog's Fennel, described by Parkinson. The third is called by us "Hog's Fennel with leaves arranged in pairs" <note>original: "Peucedanum foliis conjugatim dispositis"</note>, or the Lesser variety with narrow lobed leaves. See the description on page 293 of our *Botanical Prelude*.

### Temperament and Virtues.

The root of Hog's Fennel <note>term: Peucedanum (Hog's Fennel), likely Peucedanum officinale. Its name derives from the Greek "peuke" (pine), referring to its resinous scent.</note> is hot in the second degree and dry in the third degree <note>These "degrees" refer to the Galenic system of medicine, which classified plant properties based on their perceived effect on the body's humors</note>. Its root, macerated in wine with cypress seeds and drunk, cures "suffocation of the womb" <note>A historical medical term for hysteria or fainting fits believed to be caused by uterine displacement</note>; its juice is beneficial for an umbilical hernia <note>original: "exomphalo"</note>. See the properties described by John Bauhin in Volume 3, Part 2, page 37, where he teaches them quite extensively. Regarding the seeds of the <term>umbels</term> <note>umbrella-shaped flower clusters</note> provided with leaf-like, roundish, compressed seeds and fennel-like leaves, consult the General Table of Seed Illustrations, numbers 20, 21, 22, 23, and 24. For the leaves, see the table of illustrations number 8 for Dill and number 9 for Italian Hog's Fennel.

---

The second member of the seventh subordinate genus of Umbellifers is composed of those Umbels with a leaf-like, roundish seed, and <term>lobed leaves</term> <note>leaves divided into distinct parts or "lobes"</note>—that is, leaves arranged in pairs <note>original: "pinnatim, seu conjugatim"</note> emerging from each side of the central rib, always ending with a single leaf at the tip. In this member, the leaves are for the most part hairy. Just as we taught in the distribution of that large subordinate genus of Umbellifers endowed with striated <note>grooved</note> seeds, we shall observe here the same method, drawn from the three-fold appearance of the leaves: those that are fennel-like, those that are lobed, or those that are divided in many ways. We have already spoken of the fennel-like varieties; it remains for us to speak of those adorned with lobed leaves and a compressed, membrane-like, roundish seed.

->provided with lobed leaves.<-

<meta>This page continues the botanical classification of the Umbellifer family, moving from the narrow-leaved "Pig-tail" varieties (Peucedanum) described on the previous page to the broader-leaved members of the family, specifically Hogweed and Parsnips. # UMBELLIFERS

### Explanation of the Above Plate.

**Hogweed** <note>original: "Sphondylium"</note>. This is the first of the Umbellifers characterized by leaf-like, somewhat round, and flattened seeds, and endowed with lobed leaves. It has a striated, straight, hairy stem, about three feet <note>original: "bicubitalis," or two cubits</note> tall, jointed and hollow inside. The leaves spread across the ground like those of the Bear’s Breech <note>original: "acanthi"</note>; they are hairy, deeply jagged, and composed of several individual lobes arranged like wings or feathers, attached to the midrib without individual stalks. The white flowers form and encircle the disk of a rather wide *Umbel*; each individual flower consists of five heart-shaped petals or leaflets, which are split in two at the middle. See the flowering Hogweed shown by us in the second Plate of Illustrations: there you will clearly observe the five heart-shaped petals throughout, drawn and engraved with great skill and ingenuity by our University’s copper-plate engraver. Each flower is followed by two membrane-like, round seeds, marked on the convex side with three black, oval stripes, and two on the inside. It drives its root deep into the earth; it is thick, wrinkled, and emits a somewhat saffron-colored juice with a burning, slightly bitter taste that stings the tongue. The second variety differs from the larger Hogweed only by the division of its leaves; for while the leaf of the former is composed of five broad, rounded parts, the leaves of this one divide into oblong, narrow, and pointed segments, and these are further divided irregularly and notched along the edge. In all its other parts, it agrees with the former, of which it is merely a variety—nature thus playing <note>original: "ludente sic natura," a common Renaissance phrase suggesting natural variation is a form of divine playfulness</note>.

### Habitat and Season.

*Common Hogweed grows everywhere of its own accord in damp meadows and on the banks of wet ditches. The second variety was found in the meadows of the Alps <note>original: "pratis Rhæticis"</note> and Lusatia <note>a region in eastern Germany and western Poland</note>, and was reported in the County of Shropshire in England by Mr. Bowles, as cited by Parkinson. I withhold my belief in his reliability and agree with the opinion of Mr. John Ray <note>the famous English naturalist, 1627–1705</note>, who has faithfully presented to us the origins of all plants in England and its adjacent islands; but since I observe it marked with a specific characteristic, I am left with room to doubt whether it grows spontaneously in England. They bloom in June and July; they finish their seeds in September.*

### Names.

*The first is Common Hairy Hogweed, according to Caspar Bauhin’s Pinax. It is also called Sphondylium by Dodoens and Lobel. Tragus and Valerius Cordus (in his commentary on Dioscorides) call it Bear’s Breech <note>original: "Branca ursina"</note>; Fuchs calls it Common or German Acanthus. The second is another larger variety with jagged leaves, according to Parkinson; and the hairy variety with narrower leaves, according to Caspar Bauhin’s Prodromus.*

### Qualities and Virtues.

*Hogweed is of a hot and "cutting" <note>original: "incisivi," referring to the ability to break up phlegm</note> temperament; therefore its seeds are a remedy for coughs and asthma. Consult John Bauhin, Volume 3, part 2, page 161, who will provide more information.*

**Broad-leaved Parsnip** <note>original: "Pastinaca latifolia"</note>. Among the other members of its family, it is easily recognized by its fleshy, top-shaped, oblong root, which is greatly sought after in all European kitchens during the time of Lent <note>original: "quadragesimali tempore"</note>; at least the cultivated variety is grown for this use in all the aforementioned places. Its stem is three feet tall, striated, and hollow. It bears leaf-stalks spreading on the ground a foot or more in length, to which are attached leaves three inches long and an inch and a half wide, more or less saw-toothed, with an odd leaf closing the end, and a pair of smaller leaflets joined near the base. From the middle of the leaves springs the aforementioned stem, supporting *Umbels* adorned with yellow, reflexed flowers; when these fall away, they are succeeded by chaff-like seeds, somewhat round and flattened, much smaller than the seeds of the Hogweed...

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics, habitats, and medicinal uses of Hogweed (Sphondylium) and the Broad-leaved Parsnip (Pastinaca). It highlights the botanical debate regarding plant native status in England, citing experts like John Ray and Parkinson.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Parsnip family. The previous page ended while describing the second variety of Hogweed or Parsnip; this page completes that description and transitions into the classification of Tordylium (Hartwort). The catchword "mina" from the previous page completes the word "minora" (smaller) at the start of this text.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS

...smaller. The second, wild variety is smaller in all its parts. When the seed of the wild variety is sown, and then sown again in richer and more fertile soil, it becomes the cultivated or common variety grown in gardens—just as the cultivated carrot arises from the wild bird’s-nest or wild carrot <note>original: "staphylino, seu carota sylvestri"</note>. Both varieties complete their seed in the second year after sowing, propagating themselves anew. Both also dry up and die once the seed is mature. This is common to many biennials and triennials, which perish totally (that is, completely and from the root) once the seed is perfected and dried, leaving no lasting root in the earth as seen in hardy or perennial plants. By the skill and care of gardeners, it is sown in the springtime so that the root of the cultivated variety may be displayed for sale in the public vegetable market throughout the following winter; then, in the following summer, it completes its seed.

### Location and Season.

*The broad-leaved cultivated Parsnip, as has been said, is grown in kitchen gardens. The wild variety is found everywhere in sown fields and meadows. Both complete their flowers and seeds in the month of August—sometimes earlier, sometimes later, depending on the timing of the sowing, the fertility of the field, or its barrenness; the same is to be said of all others.*

### Names.

*The first is the broad-leaved cultivated Parsnip of Parkinson, Gerard, Dodonaeus, and Caspar Bauhin (in his Pinax). The broad-leaved cultivated German variety with a yellow flower of Jean Bauhin. (It is called the common broad-leaved domestic variety to distinguish it from the carrot, which is called the narrow-leaved parsnip). The common domestic variety of Lobelius. The large cultivated parsnip of Fuchs. The parsnip of Dioscorides and Columella. The cultivated "Deer-fodder" <note>original: "Elaphoboscum"</note> of Tragus. The second is the wild broad-leaved parsnip of Dodonaeus, Gerard, and Parkinson. The "Deer-fodder" or Lion’s Paw <note>original: "branca Leonina"</note> of Tabernaemontanus. The wild parsnip of Lobelius. The wild German parsnip, called by some "Deer-fodder," of Jean Bauhin.*

### Qualities and Medicinal Virtues.

*The Parsnip is of a moderately warm and moist temperament. It provides much nourishment, but it generates gas <note>original: "flatus"</note>, stimulates desire, and promotes the flow of urine, especially the wild variety. Dioscorides writes that deer use it as fodder against the poisonous bites of serpents, which is why the name "Deer-fodder" <note>Greek: Elaphoboscum</note> was given to it by authors.*

**Hartwort** <note>original: "Tordylium"</note>. This takes the third place among the lobed Umbellifers endowed with leaf-like, somewhat round, compressed seeds. Two species of this have become known to us. One is the larger, which puts forth many stalks two or three cubits high <note>about 3 to 4.5 feet</note>, sometimes more; these are striated, hollow inside, broken by many joints at intervals, and divided into several branches. At the beginning of summer, it puts forth foot-long stalks from the base, supporting many somewhat round, hairy leaves in lobes, attached to a central rib with one leaf closing the end, much like the broad-leaved cultivated Parsnip. At the tops of the branches, white flowers appear in an Umbel, which lean toward a flesh-red color before they open, especially on the underside. As each flower falls, it is succeeded by pairs of membrane-like, round seeds, which are somewhat hairy on the outer and middle parts, and are split and prickly at the very tip. Clusius names this the "greater bur-parsley" <note>original: "caucalidem majorem"</note>; but Jacobo Plateau, considering the "book of nature" and the ultimate goal more correctly, felt it should be referred to the genus of *Tordylium* because of its twin, round, cartilage-like seed. See page 268 of our *Botanical Preludes*. The other species of Hartwort is smaller, endowed with a round leafy or membrane-like seed with a granulated border (that is, the outer edge is encircled by a ring or circle of bead-like grains linked together). It is two-fold: first, the Syrian Hartwort, which has a larger, thicker, white, hairy seed with a granulated margin. Its tiny flowers are white. It has many narrow leaflets, two inches long, growing just below the base or the starting point of the rays that support the seeds; these leaflets exceed the rays themselves in length by an inch, and together with the rays, they form a concave surface like a bird's nest. The plant is scarcely a span high <note>about 9 inches</note>, and within a three-month space, the seeds...

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Umbelliferae family. The previous page discussed the parsnip; this page completes the description of its seeds and transitions into the classification of Hartwort (Tordylium) and Foreign All-heal (Panax/Opopanax).</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS

...completes its seeds; which, if observed under a microscope, are hairy on the outside, larger, white, and decorated with a granulated border. The second is the Apulian variety, also provided with a granulated seed, consisting of a two-foot stem, likewise with a leaf-like or membranous seed, endowed with a golden color inside, and surrounded on the outer border by many white granules: the inner circle of all these three species of *Tordylium* <term>Tordylium: Hartwort, a genus of the parsley family known for its distinct flat, fringed seeds</term> abounds with downy or bristly hairs. Why the Bauhin Brothers <note>Caspar and Jean Bauhin, famous 16th-17th century botanists</note> and other authors, following each other stubbornly, have called these *Umbels* <note>The flat-topped flower clusters characteristic of this family</note> by the names *Seseli* <term>Seseli: Moon-carrot</term>, *Caucalis* <term>Caucalis: Bur-parsley</term>, or *Gingidium* <term>Gingidium: A type of wild carrot or bishop's weed</term>, I do not understand; consult the errors of Caspar Bauhin in our *Botanical Preludes* <note>original: "prælud. nostr. Botan." referring to the author's own published work</note>, pages 368, 371, and 372.

### Location & time.

*The smaller Tordylium with a less hairy seed and a smooth or slightly granulated border (wrongly called the Great Caucalis by Clusius) grows naturally here and there around Battersea* <note>original: "Blasas," likely referring to the marshes of Battersea near London</note>*, London, and elsewhere on the edges of ditches and hedge banks. The second was sent to us from Syria, says Lobelius, who says it was sent from Aleppo in Syria to Venice by the effort of Seguin Martinelli. The third grows in the warm regions of Italy and France, such as in Apulia according to Columna. They flower and complete their seeds at the beginning of and during the summer, and perish very quickly thereafter.*

### Names.

*1. The Greatest Tordylium with prickly Hogweed-like seed, according to us. The Great Caucalis of Clusius's History. The Great Caucalis with less beautiful, hairy seed of Jean Bauhin. How ineptly it was first named Caucalis by Clusius, and by other dim-sighted authors following him in a flock, let the discerning* <note>original: "emunctæ naris," literally "of a wiped nose," a Latin idiom for having good taste or a sharp mind</note> *Botanists see and weigh what was said by us above. 2. The Syrian Tordylium, according to us. Wrongly called Seseli or Gingidium by certain authors, and Caucalis by others. Since it is manifest to anyone looking at the book of nature that all the characteristics of Tordylium fit it perfectly, it shall more rightfully be named Tordylium with us. 3. The smallest Apulian Tordylium of Columna. This same plant is also ineptly called Cretan Seseli by authors. Consult our Botanical Preludes, pages 371 and 372.*

### Temperament & powers.

*Tordylium seems to me to be of the same temperament as the Broad-leaved Parsnip, and to possess the same virtues, but to a lesser degree. Because of their shared character and the identical arrangement of all parts (except for the fleshy, top-shaped, brittle, thick root which the Broad-leaved Parsnip possesses), it ought to be joined to the same group.*

The Panax, or Foreign All-heal <term>Panax: Here referring to Opopanax, a plant used for its medicinal resin, not modern Ginseng</term>. This shall constitute the last species of this intermediate genus. Its leaves are entirely imitative of the cultivated Broad-leaved Parsnip (regarding the attachment and shape of the leaves along the midrib), yet they are smoother, of a paler green, and long and pointed: they somewhat resemble the leaves of Dock <note>original: "Lapathi"</note>, yet are much smaller. The stem is jointed and four or five cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches or 45 cm</note> high. The flowers in the *Umbels* are yellow, large, and bent backward. The seed consists of a pair joined together, membranous, flattened, and larger than that of the cultivated Broad-leaved Parsnip. From its wounded root, a juice flows during the summer months, thickening of its own accord; it has a faint odor but is warming to the taste. It is easy to notice that this plant lasts longer than others of its kind, for by its thick root descending deep into the earth, it propagates itself for many years.

### Location & time.

*Foreign All-heal grows naturally in Italy and Sicily. It flowers toward the end of summer and brings its seed to maturity shortly after, unless the weather or soil are unfavorable; for in this and similar plants, nature is so intent on production or multiplication through the root that its seed rarely reaches perfection, at least in Northern regions.*
