# UMBELLIFERS

<meta>catchword: THA-</meta>

it cures all diseases stirred up by a cold cause <note>In Galenic medicine, diseases were classified by "humors" and qualities; a "cold" cause might include sluggishness or poor circulation</note>. The powder of the root, drunk with wine, heals stomach pains and also assists with the iliac passion <note>original: iliacam; a painful intestinal obstruction</note> arising from a cold cause and from gas. Andrea Caesalpinus <note>A famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> attributes the same powers—and indeed even more effective ones—to this dropwort than to the umbelliferous dropwort (which is also called dropwort <note>original: filipendula</note>), on account of its bitterness.

Goat’s Beard <note>original: Barba capræ; likely referring here to Aruncus dioicus</note> has leaves that rival the chestnut; they are oblong, pointed, saw-toothed, with many attached to the rib opposite one another, with no smaller leaflets coming between them, as occurs in Meadowsweet <note>original: ulmaria</note>, from which it differs quite clearly in this regard, as well as in its leaves and flowers. It has stems two or three cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</note> high, endowed with many wings clustered at the top. It is granted a moderately thick, fibrous root, covered with a somewhat thick bark that inclines toward a reddish color. It bears very many tiny flowers, or yellowish tufts <note>original: muscarios</note>. And it is of two kinds: the first is the **greater mountain variety**, which exceeds four feet in height and is adorned at the tips with oblong, less fragrant flowers or tufts; the second is the **smaller meadow variety** with compact, yellowish flowers that breathe out a most sweet scent, because of which the name **Queen of the Meadow** <note>original: regina prati; modern Meadowsweet</note> was bestowed upon it. The leaves shine with a bright green and are connected to the rib in groups of five or seven, with several smaller ones coming between them; the individual leaves are saw-toothed, yet sometimes they are folded with a curled texture, approaching the appearance of elm leaves. In taste, they dry the palate vigorously without a sharp bite. The sweet-smelling flowers are arranged in a spike-like fashion and have the form of the common dropwort; they are whitish, consisting of five petals that are white, or rather turning toward yellow. Oblong seeds follow both kinds of Goat’s Beard, which are twisted in pointed capsules arranged in a circle, resting upon the top of the stem as if on a base.

### Location & Time.

*The first grows in the mountain forests of Hungary, Austria, and Styria, as well as in the mountains of Auvergne and other high regions of France. The second occurs everywhere in damp meadows. They flower and perfect their seeds toward the end of summer.*

### Names.

*The first: Goat’s Beard with oblong flowers (Caspar Bauhin). Goat’s Beard (Fuchs, Gerard, Jean Bauhin). Goat’s Beard (Lyon edition, Camerarius). Great Meadowsweet, or the other (Parkinson). The second: Goat’s Beard with compact flowers (Caspar Bauhin). Meadowsweet, see also the Illustration (Lobel, Jean Bauhin). Queen of the Meadow (Dodoens, Gerard). Common Meadowsweet (Parkinson).*

### Temperament & Powers.

*Goat’s Beard is of a hot and dry temperament; it stops hemorrhaging and excessive flows of blood, as well as the "white flow" <note>leucorrhea</note>. Johannes Bauhinus judges these plants to be rather cold, contrary to the opinion of Tragus. See Johannes Bauhin, page 489. Regarding the seed of the dropwort, consult the General Table of Seed Illustrations, number 67.*

# UMBELLIFERS

### MEADOW-RUE
->The Sixth Genus of the "Improperly Named" Umbels.<- <note>In the 17th century, botanists were still refining classification; plants like Meadow-rue were grouped with "umbels" (the carrot family) because their leaves looked similar, even if their flower shapes differed.</note>

## CHAPTER VI

**Meadow-rue (Thalictrum)**
*   **With a striated pod** <note>original: siliqua striata; referring to the grooved seed-vessel</note>
    *   **Greater**
        *   With flowers, or tassels:
            *   **Purple** ☉ <note>The solar symbol likely denotes a perennial plant or a sun-loving species</note>
            *   **Yellow**, which is twofold:
                *   **With smooth leaves** ☉
                *   **With wrinkled, three-cleft leaves** ☉ ♂ <note>The male symbol here may refer to a specific variant or a biennial growth habit</note>
    *   **Lesser**
        *   **Dodoens' variety** ☉ <note>referring to Rembert Dodoens, a famous 16th-century Flemish physician and botanist</note>
        *   **Most foul-smelling** (Bauhin’s *Prodromus*) ☉ <note>referring to the botanical work by Caspar Bauhin</note>
*   **With a three-sided pod, Canadian** (Cornut) ☉ <note>referring to Jacques-Philippe Cornut, who described North American plants in 1635</note>

---

->Explanation of the preceding Table.<-

We have categorized **Meadow-rue** <term>Thalictrum; a member of the buttercup family with foam-like flowers and foliage similar to columbine</term> here because of its leaves, which are arranged in many ways and attached to a single central nerve in the manner of many Umbellifers. It is of two types: either having a seed-case (or pod) that is striated, or one that is triangular. The seed of the first kind is of a flat, cylindrical shape, ending in a point on both sides. The **greater** variety has flowers—or rather "tassels" <note>original: muscariis; a term for flowers consisting mostly of visible stamens</note>—that are either purple (composed of purely purple stamens) or yellow. This yellow type is also twofold: the first has broader, smooth leaves that appear blackish when they first sprout, emerging in damp meadows; the other has rough or wrinkled, three-cleft <note>divided into three lobes</note>, and longer leaves, with stems that are taller than the others and are striated or angular. A **smaller species** of this kind is seen with much narrower leaves, likewise rough, wrinkled, and three-cleft, ending in much finer points. I observed this species in the year 1644 among many other plants at the home of the most learned physician **François Blondel**, a most worthy Professor of the Medical Schools of Paris, highly skilled in botany, and my most esteemed teacher.

Then there is the **lesser** variety in respect to the former, whose leaves are also divided in many ways, though they are considered "broader" only when compared to the following kind. Or there is the **smallest and most foul-smelling** of all, whose leaf-lobes are divided into three, spread across the ground, and are much narrower than the preceding kind, hanging from their stalks.

The last type is the one with a **three-sided pod** or seed-husk, somewhat like that of dock or sorrel. It is endowed with violet or dark-purple stems, which are jointed at intervals where several smaller stalks grow out here and there. This Meadow-rue has leaves similar to **Columbine** <note>original: aquilegiæ</note> both in their arrangement and shape, though they differ in their sea-green <note>original: glauco; a pale, bluish-gray color</note> color. The flowers or "tassels" at the top of the stems are composed of many pale-purple globes; when these open, they reveal a thousand white filaments, each topped with a yellow tip. When those filaments fall away, an equal number of capsules or pods succeed them, triangular in shape, with a certain parchment-like ridge standing out along each corner; the seeds contained therein are flat and smooth,

# UMBELLIFERS

...cylindrical, as are the individual seeds of all the aforementioned Meadow-rues, which are covered by a striated <note>original: "siliquâ striatâ"; meaning a seed pod marked with parallel grooves or ridges</note> pod. See the General Table of illustrations of seeds presented by us, numbers 68, 69, 70, and 71. Note further that the creeping roots of many of the aforementioned Meadow-rues are yellow.

### Place & Time.

*The first Meadow-rue grows in the mountains of Savoy and on Mount Salève. The second in damp meadows throughout France, Germany, and England. The third in Spain, Hungary <note>original: "Pannonia"; the Roman name for the region covering modern-day Hungary and surrounding areas</note>, and Austria. The fourth is quite common on the hills around Blois. The fifth is found in the Royal Madrid wood—called the Wood of Boulogne <note>the Bois de Boulogne in Paris was once associated with a "Château de Madrid"</note>—and is encountered throughout the forest of Fontainebleau. The sixth is from Canada. They flower and complete their seeding at the end of summer.*

### Names.

*The first is the **Greater Meadow-rue with purple flower-tufts or tassels**, according to Caspar Bauhin. The **Fourth Meadow-rue** of Dodoens; the **Greater with angular seeds and a smooth stem** of Jean Bauhin. The second is the **Greater Meadow-rue with an angular or striated pod**, according to Caspar Bauhin; the **Common Greater** of Parkinson; the **Blacker Meadow-rue with a striated stem and seed** of Jean Bauhin; and the **Meadow-rue of the herbalists** according to de l'Obel. The third is the **Greater Yellow Meadow-rue with yellow stamens or a sea-green leaf**, according to Caspar Bauhin; the **Most beautiful sea-green Meadow-rue with a striated seed and stem** of Jean Bauhin; and named by us the **Meadow-rue with rough and three-cleft leaves**. The fourth is the **Lesser Meadow-rue**, according to Caspar Bauhin, Dodoens, and Gerard; the **Narrow-leaved meadow-rue** of de l'Obel; the **Lesser Meadow-rue** of Dodoens. The fifth is the **Smallest, most foul-smelling Meadow-rue**, according to Caspar Bauhin’s Pinax and Prodromus. Note that the leaves of all Meadow-rues have three-cleft lobes <term>trifid; divided into three parts or lobes</term>, whether they are smooth, rough, or wrinkled, and they sit upon their stems in the manner of the lobed Umbellifers; for this reason, we have brought them here to the end of the Umbellifers, although they differ greatly from them in their flowers and seeds. The last is the **Canadian Meadow-rue** of Cornut; named by us the **Canadian Meadow-rue with a three-sided seed pod, reddish stems, and leaves like columbine**, and by that name it is easily distinguished from its other companions.*

### Qualities & Powers.

*Meadow-rue is moderately warm and dry; the leaves, when mixed with pottage <note>original: "oleribus"; refers to leafy vegetables or garden herbs used in cooking</note>, loosen the bowels. A decoction of its root performs this even more powerfully, according to the testimony of Dioscorides <note>a Greek physician and the father of pharmacology</note>. Crushed leaves of Meadow-rue lead old ulcers to scarring. Regarding the seed vessels and seeds of the Meadow-rues, consult the General Table of seed illustrations; numbers 68, 69, 70, and 71 indicate the striated capsule or pod and the triangular pod. Consult the explanation of the aforementioned Table. See also Plate 12 of the illustrations, where the **Greater Meadow-rue with wrinkled, three-cleft leaves** is engraved. A smaller species of this currently flourishes in the garden of Edward Morgan <note>a celebrated 17th-century botanist who maintained a famous "physic garden" in London</note> behind the Westminster Cloister, along with many other rarer plants which, having been raised and nourished by him from seed I shared, are preserved with the greatest vigilance, care, and industry.*

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of Meadow-rues (Thalictrum) to a new section focusing on the Umbelliferae family. It begins a detailed catalog of plants that the author believes were either previously undocumented or incorrectly classified by earlier botanists, starting with the genus Cachrys and Azorean Fennel.</meta>

# UMBELLIFERS
->OR<-
## UMBELLIFEROUS PLANTS
### NOT YET DESCRIBED,
->or incorrectly classified.<-

*The Umbels <note>plants with flower clusters that spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</note> contained in this section were either first described by us, or have been wrongly named and placed by other authors. For the reasons provided throughout our teachings, we have changed their names and assigned new ones, returning each to its proper and genuine class according to the characteristics <note>original: "tesseram aut symbolum"; literally a token or symbol used for identification</note> they bear.*

❦ **Cachrys** <note>a genus in the carrot family characterized by its thick, corky seed-vessels</note> with a smooth spongy seed and giant-fennel-like leaves. This plant is distinguished at first glance from common fennel by its shorter and thicker leaves, which resemble fennel but are a paler green, and by a stem that reaches a cubit <note>roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</note> or sometimes a cubit and a half in height. However, it differs from the following two species of its class primarily by the thinness of its leaves and by its smooth seed of a spongy substance. This seed is enclosed in a spongy fruit-case <note>original: "pericarpio"</note> that resembles a small nutmeg (as we mentioned at the beginning of this treatise). Indeed, these plants differ from all other umbellifers by this seed enclosed in a spongy substance. All four of the aforementioned species also share a common characteristic: they all have an aromatic, resinous, and sharp taste.

❦ **Cachrys** with a spongy, furrowed, and rough seed, and somewhat broad hog’s-fennel leaves. This second variety is easily distinguished from the first (which has giant-fennel-like leaves) by the roughness of its seeds and its leaves, which are divided in the manner of hog's-fennel <note>original: "peucedani"</note>. It differs entirely from the third or following species (with which it almost agrees in leaf shape) because of the inequality or roughness of its seeds.

**Cachrys** with a spongy, furrowed, flat, and larger seed, and narrow hog’s-fennel leaves. This differs from the previous one in its leaves and its flat, furrowed seed. While the seeds of the second variety are furrowed and rough, both are gifted with hog's-fennel leaves—one being somewhat broad, the other narrow.

❦ **Cachrys** with a spongy, furrowed, flat, and smaller seed, and hog’s-fennel leaves. This is distinguished from the preceding one by no other characteristic than the smallness of its seeds; in all other respects it agrees. If a more mature plant were to acquire the same seed size, it would undoubtedly not be distinguished as a separate species.

**Sweet Azorean Fennel** differs from our common fennel, first because it is sweet like the Italian variety (agreeing with it in sweetness, though it is more pleasant); but the Azorean differs from both the Italian and common varieties because it has much thicker and shorter stalks.

<summary>This page introduces the classification of Umbellifers, specifically focusing on the genus Cachrys, noting its unique spongy seeds, and describing the physical differences between its species and Azorean fennel.</summary>

# UMBELLIFERS

...more spongy, and at the top of the stem it produces stalks or rays supporting larger seeds. These are very pleasant and far exceed the degree of sweetness of the common seed popularly called "Italian Sweet Fennel." I say the Azorean seeds of the common type are twice as thick, even if they are of nearly the same length, and when chewed in the mouth they appear more fragrant in both scent and taste; as those who taste them starting to ripen in the Oxford Botanic Garden can perceive. The seed of this excellent fennel was sent to us from France by the noble and most studious man of botany, Patrick Murray <note>A Scottish botanist and collector who helped found the Edinburgh Physic Garden</note>, and was sent there from the southern islands of the Mediterranean Sea to Mr. Nicholas Marchant <note>A noted French botanist and first director of the Jardin des Plantes</note> (once our most dear colleague staying at Blois, now living in Paris, and likewise a most worthy Professor of Botany to his most Christian Gallic Majesty); we hope for more seeds from there daily.

❦ **Pimpinella** <note>Burnet-saxifrage</note> *the greater degenerate saxifrage.* This Burnet-saxifrage is a variety that deviates <note>original: "degenerans"; in this period, it refers to a natural variation or mutation from a known type</note> from the sowing of the common greater seed, which it resembles in all essential parts—namely the flower, seed, scent, and taste—except that it has leaves that are merely more dissected or laciniated <note>deeply and irregularly slashed into narrow segments</note>. Nevertheless, it is not improperly placed with its parent plant, which is endowed with lobed leaves, despite the arrangement of the leaves being thus "played with" by nature. This happens in many other umbels—namely, that the leaves vary from sowing (as said above in the doctrine of umbels). We did not think it right to separate these from their congeners <note>members of the same genus</note> simply because of a dissimilar arrangement of leaves; rather, because of the conformity of the essential parts, and furthermore of the scent and taste, we have associated them with their own kind.

❦ **Sium** <note>Water Parsnip</note> *flowering at the axils.* The variety of this Sium is easily perceived from the rest: first, because it thrives by creeping and floating in the waters (while others ascend straight up); second, because it bears its umbels sitting on short stalks or rays at the axils <note>the "armpit" where the leaf joins the stem</note> after the manner of celery, rather than at the very tops of the stems as others do. In its essential parts, however, as well as in scent and taste, it is equal to its congeners.

❦ **Sium** *aquaticum with wrinkled, three-parted, toothed leaves.* The leaves of this one are oblong, saw-toothed, and wrinkled on both the top and bottom sides; they are mostly divided in threes, and often three (sometimes more) hang from the leaf-stalks, coming from stems two or three feet high. Flowers and seeds come forth on the highest branches of the stems, similar in all respects to the flowers and seeds of the previously mentioned ones. It is not well named by Lobelius <note>Matthias de l'Obel, a Flemish physician and botanist</note> as "Sium with the appearance of Alexanders" <note>original: "sium olusatri facie"</note>, nor by others as "with a Rocket leaf"; therefore we have changed the name and adapted a more convenient one for it. Let judicious botanists examine this name of ours, and they will perceive it fits this plant better for correctly and specifically distinguishing it from others of its kind than the aforementioned names.

❦ **Sium** *pumilum with fennel-like leaves.* This is distinguished from the others of its family with lobed leaves solely by the arrangement and cutting of the leaves, combined with the smallness of its parts. See the explanation above in this treatise, and in our *Botanical Prelude*, page 309.

❦ **OEnanthe** <note>Water Dropwort</note> *with the leaf of Marsh Yarrow.* This umbel is wrongly called by authors "Meadow Carrot" and "Carrot with Marsh Yarrow leaves." The Bauhin brothers <note>Jean and Gaspard Bauhin, influential Swiss botanists</note> and Par-

<meta>This page continues the catalog of umbelliferous plants (the carrot and parsley family), specifically focusing on variants of Celery (Apium) and the classification of Laserworts (Laserpitium). The author continues his argument that plants should be classified by their "essential parts"—seeds and flowers—rather than just leaf shape, correcting earlier botanists like Bauhin and Mattioli.</meta>

# UMBELLIFEROUS PLANTS

Parkinson and Dalechamps, following the *History of Lyon* <note>original: "Historia Lugdunensi", a massive botanical encyclopedia published in 1586 by Jacques Daléchamps</note>, named them thus. See earlier in this treatise of ours the chapter on *oenanthe* <note>Water Dropwort</note>.

❧ **Degenerate cultivated celery, or that with long, dissected leaves.** We have this variant <note>original: "degenerans"; in this period, it refers to a natural mutation or variation from a known type</note> in gardens, arising from the seed of the larger cultivated or garden celery. Its leaves are oblong and deeply lobed, or cut into many long, pointed segments; nature plays in this way, just as in many other species—not only of umbellifers, but also of other classes or tribes. We noted this above in the **greater and smaller curled degenerate Burnet-saxifrage** <note>original: "pimpinella saxifraga"</note>, and in **thin-leaved garden celery**, and perhaps in **Sium** <note>original: "Sio"; Water Parsnip</note> with fennel-like leaves, and other similar ones. Consult Plate 7 of the illustrations, where one reads "degenerate celery with long dissected leaves," and Plate 5, where the **greater degenerate Burnet-saxifrage and the smaller curled** are shown.

❧ **Greater garden or cultivated celery, broad-leaved, with a yellow umbel.** Regarding its essential parts, it differs little or not at all from the common garden type, except for the yellowish-green color of the umbels; by this mark, it is distinguished with little or no trouble from the others, which are gifted with white flowers.

❧ **The Bishop's Weed of certain authors** <note>original: "Ammi"</note> in Dalechamps' *History of Lyon*, or the **fourth Samphire of Mattioli** <note>original: "Crithmum 4. Matth."</note>. How ineptly this is assigned by authors to the Sea Hollies <note>original: "eryngiis"</note>, and placed under that name among the Sea Hollies by Caspar Bauhin, we leave to be discussed by judicious botanists; since it has the complete structure of the Bishop's Weeds, we have referred it to *Ammi*. See our *Botanical Preludes* <note>original: "praelud. nostra Botanica", a 1669 work by Robert Morison</note>, page 421, and the explanation of the Plate on *Ammi* earlier in this treatise.

❧ **Greater Laserwort with broader lobes**, according to us. The **Ethiopian Seseli plant of Dodoens** <note>Rembert Dodoens, a 16th-century Flemish physician and botanist</note>. Since its green seeds (after the manner of other Laserworts) are composed of six leafy wings, it is more appropriately reduced here to its kindred umbellifers than to the *Seseli* or the *Libanotis* <note>Moonwort or mountain-parsley types</note> as other authors have done. See Chapter 3 on Laserwort above.

❧ **Greater or broad-leaved Laserwort (that is, with broader lobes) with a curled and wrinkled seed**, according to us. The **bitter Costus similar to Libanotis with a curled seed of Jean Bauhin** <note>original: "J. B."; a Swiss botanist</note>. I have placed both this and the previous species of *Libanotis* among the Laserworts, not because they are undescribed umbellifers, but because they were poorly named and arranged by authors among the *Libanotides*. For since they are true and genuine Laserworts based on their seeds, scent, and taste alike, they must be arranged among the Laserworts according to the rule prescribed by nature; consult our *Botanical Preludes*, pages 370 and 443.

❧ **Laserwort with narrower, pale green lobes, divided into many parts.** It is distinguished from the French or Marseilles Laserwort of de l'Obel <note>original: "Lob."; Matthias de l'Obel, after whom the Lobelia is named</note> solely by the paler green color of the leaves.

❧ **Laserwort with narrower, longer, and pale green lobes placed in pairs.** It is distinguished from the others endowed with narrower leaves, first, by the position of the leaves alone. Secondly, it produces seeds with other leafy, wavy, or wrinkled parts, but not warty like the bitter Costus of Jean Bauhin. See the General Plate of seed illustrations 8, 9, and 10 concerning the seeds of Laserwort.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Umbelliferae family, moving from types of celery to detailed descriptions of parsnips (Pastinaca), wild carrots (Daucus), and Bur-parsleys (Caucalis), emphasizing seed structure as the primary means of classification.</meta>

# UMBELLIFEROUS PLANTS

**Shiny, thin-leaved wild Parsnip.** <note>original: Pastinaca tenuifolia sylvestris lucida</note> This plant has a thicker, shiny, smooth leaf, and is a darker green than the common wild variety, which is covered in hairs on both the leaves and stems. It also has larger, thicker seeds that are more bristly or hairy; by these traits, it is easily distinguished from the cultivated carrot or the common wild carrot.

**Thin-leaved Parsnip, or carrot with a yellow root and umbel.** This is distinguished at first sight when flowering from other carrot species solely by the pale yellow or <term>pale yellow-white <note>original Greek: ὠχρολεύκῳ (ochroleukō)</note></term> color of its <term>umbel <note>the umbrella-like flower cluster characteristic of this plant family</note></term>. Before it flowers, even the most keen-eyed botanist cannot distinguish it from the common carrot.

**Cretan Parsnip with longer rays like Gingidium.** This species of carrot produces <term>rays <note>the individual stalks that make up the flower cluster</note></term> five inches long, much thicker and stiffer than the rays of *gingidium* <note>a type of wild chervil</note> or *visnaga* <note>Toothpick weed</note>. Like *visnaga* and the common carrot, it forms a concave surface resembling a bird's nest, especially when the umbel is weighted down with seeds. It bears hairy, bristly seeds in the manner of a carrot, but much larger and thicker; because of the shape of the umbel and seed, we have classified it with the wild carrot or *Daucus*. Furthermore, a specific difference arises from the length and thickness of the rays bearing seeds at the tops of the stems, by which it is very easily distinguished from all other species of wild carrot.

**Great carrot-like Bur-parsley from Tangier.** <note>original: Caucalis major Daucoïdes Tingitana</note> When this umbel first emerges from the ground, and while it still carries its leaves spread across the earth, it is so similar to the common wild carrot that any skilled botanist would conclude it was the same, before it grows a stem and produces seeds. It produces stems a foot and a half to two feet high if planted in fertile soil. At the tops of these grow white flowers (which are purplish before they open) composed of five split petals. As each flower falls, two oblong, slightly compressed seeds follow, gifted with very long and thin prickles. Each seed is marked by two <term>keels <note>ridges or raised lines on the seed surface</note></term> on the back and two below at the base where they attach. Thus, when two seeds are still closely joined together, eight keels appear, endowed with longer prickles. Because of the wider distance at the point where they join, the two keels at the base on either side do not merge into one, as can be seen in the seeds of <term>Laserworts <note>Laserpitium</note></term>. Furthermore, this umbel forms a concave surface like the common carrot. This plant has the largest seeds of all the Bur-parsleys, endowed with longer prickles and higher cartilaginous membranes. When Edward Morgan <note>a prominent 17th-century botanist who maintained a famous garden in Westminster</note> sowed its seed, received from Tangier in Morocco three or four years ago, it produced a foot-long stem in early spring, and seeds half the size of the seeds shown here in the General Plate, illustration 17. However, after a year had passed, it sprouted again in autumn (since it had not yet perished thanks to the mildness of the winter), and this year, as I commit these words to the press, it grew twice as large and produced seeds twice as thick. Thus, it surpasses all its related species of Bur-parsley in the length of its prickles and the width of its seeds.

**Lower Hog's Fennel with leaves placed in pairs.** <note>original: Peucedanum humilius</note> This species of Hog's Fennel produces longer leaves, growing opposite each other from the <term>rachis <note>the central stem of a compound leaf</note></term> or mid-nerve, and five...

...six, sometimes more or fewer <note>original: "senave" (likely *senasve*), referring to the number of leaf segments</note>, which it bears in opposite pairs, with a single, longer leaf at the tip. From the upper part of the root, a "beard" or tuft of dense fibers emerges, much like the other <term>Sulphur-worts <note>original: "peucedanorum"; plants of the genus *Peucedanum*</note></term> and other frankincense-bearing umbels contained under various genera. From the middle of these fibers rises a two-foot-tall stem, supporting small yellow flowers that are <term>reflexed <note>bent or curved backward</note></term> in the umbels. It bears seeds that are membrane-like, flattened, and oblong, similar to common Sulphur-wort (consult the General Plate of Seed Illustrations, figures 24, 24). It differs from that plant primarily in the arrangement of the leaves, which are placed in pairs along long <term>pinnae <note>the primary divisions of a compound leaf</note></term> or lobes, rather than being divided into three parts as in the common Italian and German varieties, as we clearly indicated above. It likewise produces a smaller seed.

---

### ☘ Common Large Hartwort, with a nearly smooth seed border, according to us. *The Great Bur-parsley of Clusius’s History.*

This is the largest species of this entire genus in terms of height. From the root, it produces many straight <term>stolons <note>here referring to upright stems or shoots</note></term>, two or three cubits high—sometimes reaching a height of four <term>cubits <note>a cubit is roughly 18 inches; thus, the plant grows 3 to 6 feet tall</note></term>. These are <term>striated <note>marked with parallel grooves</note></term>, hollow inside, interrupted by joints, and divided into several branches higher up. Beneath these branches, large winged leaves grow in opposite pairs from a wide base; they are notched or serrated around the edges like the <term>Broad-leaved Parsnip <note>Pastinaca latifolia</note></term> (indeed, this plant and two other varieties of Hartwort appear to be species of wild parsnip), and are also somewhat hairy. 

At the tops of the branches grow umbels of white flowers, which are slightly purplish underneath and, as they say, approaching a flesh-colored tint. Each flower is succeeded by two broad seeds, almost like those of common <term>Cow Parsnip <note>Sphondylium vulgare</note></term>, but thicker at the margin. They are slightly hairy on the convex, outer part—a feature also seen in the seeds of the other <term>Hartwort <note>Tordylium</note></term> species we have displayed. This plant’s seed has a slightly protruding border that is nearly smooth and less visibly granular than in the Syrian and Apulian Hartworts described in our 7th Table. Consult the Plate of Seed Illustrations, figures 27, 27, 28, 28, 29, 29, where the seeds of these three Hartworts are seen excellently engraved, being <term>bifid <note>split into two parts</note></term> or prickly at the very tip. 

This plant has a white, hard root provided with many woody fibers. From this description, which is brought forth almost entirely from <note>Charles de l'Écluse (1526–1609), a pioneering physician and botanist</note> Clusius, it clearly appears to be a perfect and genuine species of Hartwort (*Tordylium*). Therefore, it is wrongly called "Great Bur-parsley" (*Caucalis major*) to this day by <note>Gaspard Bauhin (1560–1624), a famous Swiss botanist who attempted to clarify plant names</note> Caspar Bauhin and other authors who transcribe from and name plants after Clusius. Since it is manifest from the "book of nature" that the seeds of all Bur-parsleys are prickly or <term>echinated <note>covered in spines like a hedgehog</note></term>, whereas the seed of this plant is membrane-like, compressed, and round or orbicular, it should be classified by us among its kin as a species of Hartwort—a title more justly held alongside Jacobus Plateau, who sent its seed to Clusius and distinguished it by the name of *Tordylium*.

---

### ☘ Small White Perennial Myrrh, with hairy leaves and golden seeds.

This species of Mountain Myrrh is easily distinguished from its other perennial relatives because it produces lightly hairy leaves and a <term>rostrate <note>beaked or having a snout-like tip</note></term>, striated, shorter seed of a golden color. There are two other varieties of this plant which are distinguished from the preceding by this mark: the leaves of one are much more hairy than the first, and the striated seeds are much longer than the golden seeds of the first. The last variety has very hairy leaves and long, somewhat dark-brown seeds.

After discussing Hartworts on the previous page, the author moves into the genus Myrrhis (Myrrh or Chervil-like plants), emphasizing seed texture, stem nodes, and root shape as primary diagnostic features to correct errors made by earlier botanists like Caspar Bauhin.</meta>

-># THE UMBELLIFEROUS FAMILY<-

**Perennial Myrrh, yellow and carrot-like.** <note>original: "Myrrhis perennis, lutea Daucoides"</note> Among all the other perennial species of myrrh, this one is easily recognized because it produces leaves that completely mimic those of the common wild carrot, and it bears a yellow <term>umbel <note>a flower cluster where the stalks spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</note></term>, whereas the umbels of all the others consist of white flowers. Furthermore, its seeds are <term>striated <note>marked with narrow parallel grooves or lines</note></term> and longer than the seeds of the other myrrhs (excepting the Great Scented Myrrh and the Annual Knotted Myrrh with rough seeds), yet they are less swollen than the "golden seeds" of the Mountain Myrrh described previously.

**Annual Knotted Myrrh, with a beaked, rough, and oblong seed.** The stems of this plant thicken at the joints <note>original: "geniculos"</note>, or bear <term>turbinate <note>shaped like an inverted cone or a spinning top</note></term> or <term>pyriform <note>pear-shaped</note></term> nodes, which are the largest among all the myrrhs. It bears white flowers at the tops of two-foot-tall stems; as these fade one by one, they are succeeded by pairs of seeds that are striated, rough or wrinkled, and reddish or rather yellowish on the <term>gibbous <note>the rounded, convex, or "hunched" side of the seed</note></term> part. These seeds are much larger and thicker than those of the other myrrhs, with the exception of the Great Scented Myrrh. (See the General Plate of Seed Illustrations, figures 38, 38).

**Annual Myrrh with a smooth, striated seed.** This plant has a hairy stem, two <term>cubits <note>an ancient measurement; two cubits is approximately three feet</note></term> high, which is also knotted at every joint, top-shaped, or thick in the middle, and hollow inside. Its leaves are dissected in the manner of <term>apium <note>the genus containing celery and parsley</note></term>, hairy, and sprinkled with reddish or dark spots (the stem is also somewhat bluish). The seeds at the very tips of the small branches are beaked and lightly covered with <term>striae <note>fine grooves</note></term>, appearing as if each seed were marked with three lines of ink. Consequently, since the "keels" and furrows are barely visible, it is called "smooth-seeded" by us, as is the following species.

**Tuberous, Knotted, Hemlock-leaved Myrrh, according to us.** <note>original: "Myrrhis tuberosa, nodosa, coniphyllon"</note> This plant is distinguished from all others by the following characteristics: first, by the tuberous nature of the root (a trait it shares only with the Cretan Alexanders <note>original: "Smyrnio Cretico"</note> among the annual umbels, and with the Earthnut <note>original: "Bulbocastano"</note> among the perennials); second, by the knottiness of the stem at the joints, which it has in common with the two previously mentioned species of myrrh. However, its leaves entirely mimic those of <term>hemlock <note>original: "cicuta"</note></term>, a noteworthy fact that distinguishes it from the others. It has two or three <term>internodes <note>the parts of the stem between the joints</note></term> near the ground that are covered with hairy, grayish-white down; the remaining parts further up are smooth and marked with dark spots, in the manner of common hemlock. I leave it to those botanists learned in the art to discuss how foolishly this plant is called "hemlock-chervil" <note>original: "cicutaria"</note> by other authors; I refer them to the hallucinations of Caspar Bauhin and other authors regarding this umbel, which we have noted in the chapter below on hemlock-chervil.

**Annual Myrrh with a striated, shaggy, and grayish-white seed.** In a word or two, this is a "myrrh" based on its seed, scent, and flavor; therefore, it is wrongly called "Cretan Carrot" <note>original: "daucus creticus"</note> by other authors. See more below among the hallucinations of Caspar Bauhin and other authors in the chapter we have provided regarding the Cretan Carrot and Spignel <note>original: "Meo"</note>.

It maintains the author’s focus on seed morphology as the primary diagnostic tool.</meta>

->68<-

**Wild perennial chervil with smooth black seeds, according to our classification.** <note>original: "Cerefolium sylvestre perenne feminibus lævibus nigris, Nobis"</note> This plant differs from the cultivated garden chervil first by its lifespan <note>it is perennial rather than annual</note>, and second because it grows wild in meadows and ditches in every province I have ever visited. Specifically, it is found around the regions of Bourges, Poitiers, Blois, Orléans, and the capital of all these, Paris <note>original: "Lutetiam Parisiorum"</note>. Likewise, it grows wild throughout England in the ditches and meadows of the suburbs and villages adjacent to London, Oxford, and other towns. 

This chervil is much larger than the cultivated or garden variety; it produces much longer and tougher leaves, and the stems are larger, more solid, and more deeply <term>striated <note>marked with parallel grooves</note></term>. These stems, which are filled with a tougher pith, grow to a height of two <term>cubits <note>an ancient measurement; two cubits is approximately three feet</note></term> or more. At each of the stem's joints <note>original: "genicula"</note>, knotty swellings or nodules appear. The seeds are smooth, black, and nearly twice as thick as those of garden chervil. As mentioned, the plant lives for many years. By these marks, it is easily distinguished from common or garden chervil. However, they agree in this: the seeds of both are smooth, black (or nearly black), and lack the ridges, keels, or furrows found in other plants of this family. This characteristic of the seeds is shared by this plant and the <term>Tordylium <note>Hartwort</note></term> among the umbellifers. 

This plant cannot be called a "Myrrh" (as all other authors would have it), since it does not have seeds endowed with ridges. It is rather a "Chervil," since its seed is smooth and black in the manner of a chervil. It is by these characteristics that Myrrh and Chervil are distinguished from one another. See our *Botanical Preludes* <note>original: "prælud. nostr. Botanicorum"</note>, page 288, and see below for the errors <note>original: "hallucinationes"</note> of Caspar Bauhin <note>a famous Swiss botanist (1560–1624)</note> and other authors regarding chervil and myrrh. Consult also the Table of Seed Illustrations, numbers 34 through 39b, which designate the seeds of the Myrrhs, and numbers 40 and 41, which indicate the seeds of the cultivated and wild perennial chervils.

**Great wild valerian with broader leaves.** <note>original: "Valeriana major sylvestris foliis latioribus"</note> This is merely a variety of the great wild valerian, whose leaves are simply broader, less hairy, and a paler green; the lobes are attached to the midrib just as in the common variety. Whether this should be considered a distinct species or merely a variety matters little; however, they appeared distinct to us when seen in the wild and in gardens.

**Small valerian with a navel-like, naked, and oblong seed.** <note>original: "Valerianella semine umbilicato, nudo, oblongo"</note> The seed of this plant is oblong and "naked" <note>lacking a fuzzy coating</note>; it is hollowed out on the front side <note>original: "prona parte"</note> and hunched or convex on the back <note>original: "supina"</note>, just as one can see in the small valerian with the round navel-like seed. Insofar as the seed of this variety is stretched out in length and hollow, it is easily distinguished from its relative, the small valerian with a round, naked, navel-like seed. Furthermore, the round-seeded variety is seen to be reddish in color, whereas this one is endowed with a whitish color, as has been said elsewhere: see the General Table of Illustrations, 50, 51, 52, 53.

**Small valerian with a hairy navel-like seed.** <note>original: "Valerianella semine umbilicato hirsuto"</note> This is of two kinds: the "larger hairy" type, which is hollow in the middle and at the edge of the front surface, or the "smaller hairy" type, which is hollow only in the middle of the front surface. They are distinguished primarily by these marks, as they can hardly be told apart by their leaves (which differ very little in color or texture). Regarding their seeds, consult the General Table of Seed Illustrations, 54, 55, 56, 57, and the explanation thereof, which points out the unique and singular seeds of all the aforementioned and following small valerians (except for the Lamb's Lettuce, or "white pot-herb" of Dodoens <note>Rembert Dodoens was a major 16th-century Flemish physician and botanist</note>).

**Taller late-flowering field valerian, with a more swollen seed.** <note>original: "Valerianella Arvensis serotina altior, semine turgidiori"</note> This plant has a stem of one and a half to two feet, which bears at the top white flowers arranged in an umbel that appear later than all the others; for it flowers when the grain is ripe, near the end... <note>The sentence breaks off at the end of the page.</note>

->69<-

<meta>catchword: non</meta>

...then in July, or around the beginning of August—sometimes earlier, sometimes later—it is found pregnant with a single seed, quite thick and swollen. The early variety <note>original: "Præcox"</note>, however, is lower to the ground and is marked by compressed, slightly convex seeds joined in pairs, which we have not been able to observe in any other species of cornsalad <note>original: "valerianellarum"</note>. It produces small, five-petaled flowers, or flowers divided into five segments at the edge, bluish, sometimes white, and almost fleeing the sight <note>meaning they are so tiny they are hard to see clearly</note>; it blooms earlier than the others at the end of winter, and shortly after, it perfects its seeds joined in pairs, in the manner of other true umbellifers <note>plants that bear flowers in an umbrella-like cluster</note>. This was also observed and noted by us above in our teaching on cornsalads, so that botanists might more diligently observe the difference between this early field or vegetable variety (called lamb’s lettuce, white pot-herb, or *locusta* <note>original: "lactuca agnina, olus album, aut locusta"</note>) and our late-blooming one, which possesses a single, more swollen seed.

Although this early variety is described by nearly all modern authors, we have described it anew and seen to it that its seed was drawn both joined and separate, because the difference between them was by no means well-observed by other authors writing before me. Already from a distance, I catch the scent of some compiler of plant names—someone who merely transcribes from "Theaters," "Compendiums," and "General Histories"—who will object to me that this early cornsalad (since it is a true umbellifer) is poorly placed and not arranged according to the method given by nature. For now, it will suffice to have taught him what is what; on another occasion, we will not be reluctant to bring forth our reasons. I add this one thing for the present: that as far as leaf arrangement, smell, and taste are concerned, it agrees with the aforementioned plants, but departs from them regarding the double seed. This happens with many other species—that they differ in a few ways while agreeing greatly in many others. I assert that this plant can be placed better and more fittingly with no other subordinate genus of true umbellifers. If those transcribers, whoever they may be, should add a better reason, I would say they have brought forth something from their own brains.

**Cornsalad** with scabious-like or starry seeds, the larger Portuguese variety <note>original: "Valerianella scabiosæ semine, seu stellato, major Lusitanica"</note>. It differs from the cornsalad with smaller scabious-like seeds, described and illustrated by the most learned Fabio Columna <note>Fabio Colonna (1567–1640), a pioneering Italian botanist</note>, primarily by the size of the seeds alone: the starry seeds of the larger Portuguese variety are twice as large as the seeds of that cornsalad described by Fabio Columna. We have seen to it that the seeds of all these cornsalads were drawn and engraved, since the primary difference of all of them consists in the seeds, which are fashioned by nature in diverse ways; the leaves of nearly all of them are constructed in the same manner. Although some distinction can likewise be drawn from the leaves, the seeds give the most certain indications.

**Burnet**, the great Canadian bloodwort, with a longer red spike <note>original: "Pimpinella sanguisorba major, Canadensis, spicâ longiori rubrâ"</note>. This differs from the common great bloodwort <term>Sanguisorba <note>literally "blood-absorber," historically used to stop bleeding</note></term>, or *forbestrella*, in that it bears a flower spike twice or thrice as long, sometimes consisting of three or four inches. It also has larger and broader leaves; in other respects, it is equal.

**Great Burnet**, agrimony-like <note>original: "Pimpinella major agrimonoides"</note>. It has rough and hairy leaves, like agrimony. At first glance, or upon first appearance, it presents the look of agrimony, since it entirely approaches agrimony in both leaves and scent. I say it has wrinkled, or rough and hairy leaves, attached opposite one another to the rib or mid-vein (in the manner of other burnets), which, when handled, give off a most pleasant scent, very closely approaching that of the fragrant Italian agrimony. The stems are a foot high, and in fertile soil a foot and a half...

### UMBELS PROVIDED WITH A CREEPING ROOT.

*Wild Angelica <note>as described by Johannes Thal, a 16th-century German physician and botanist</note>. Great Masterwort <note>as listed in Caspar Bauhin’s "Pinax theatri botanici" (C. B. P.)</note>. Perennial Bishop’s Weed, according to us. All other umbels properly so called either possess a fleshy root—top-shaped <term>turbinate<gloss>shaped like an inverted cone or a spinning top</gloss></term>, long, either single or several joined together, many-cleft, forked, or double-forked—or a fibrous root. Very few are gifted with a tuberous root, such as the knotted or tuberous Sweet Cicely, hemlock-leaved parsley <note>original: "coniophyllon"</note>, and pignut <note>original: "bulbocastanum"</note>.*

*Note that the seeds of all umbels are hot and dry <note>In the medical theory of the time, plants were classified by "qualities" (hot, cold, moist, dry) to determine how they affected the body's humors.</note> in a milder or more intense degree; let the proof of this claim lie with the palates of those who taste them. Among the umbels improperly so called <note>Plants that have umbel-like flowers but belong to different families</note> are found certain creeping species of Valerian and Meadow-rue <note>original: "Thalictri"</note>.*

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic reorganization of the Umbelliferae family. Having established his own system on the previous pages based on the physical structure of seeds, he now begins a critical review of the "hallucinations" (errors or oversights) of previous authorities, most notably Caspar Bauhin, whose 1623 "Pinax" was the standard botanical reference of the era.</meta>

# THE ERRORS
## OF CASPAR BAUHIN,
### AND OF OTHER AUTHORS.

**HAVING ESTABLISHED** this our new and unheard-of doctrine of umbels <note>Plants with umbrella-like flower clusters, such as carrots or parsley</note>, observed from the book of nature—in which most umbels that are treated in a confused manner by Caspar Bauhin and other authors are restored by genus and species, and arranged according to tables of kinship and affinity—we thought it would not be out of place to point out the errors of the same author admitted in individual chapters. We shall also offer at the beginning of each chapter the names used by the ancients, both Greek and Latin, taken from Theophrastus, Dioscorides, Pliny, and other authors used by Bauhin.

---

## CHAPTER I.
### Concerning Libanotis.

Libanotis <note>original: Λιβανωτὶς</note>, so named because its root smells of frankincense <note>original: ὄζα λιβάνω</note>, is of two kinds: the broad-leaved and the ferula-leaved <note>A plant with giant fennel-like leaves</note>. Theophrastus (Book 9, Chapter 12) identified two types: one sterile and the other fertile; one useful for both its fruit and leaf, the other only for its root. The fruit-bearing type has a leaf similar to marsh celery but larger; the sterile type consists of a leaf like bitter lettuce. Pliny (Book 19, Chapter 12) treated Libanotis confusedly, and in Book 20, Chapter 16, he mixed it up with *conyca*.

<margin>
Bauhin, *Pinax* page 157, column 1, plant 1. Great broad-leaved Libanotis of Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax* (C.B.P.); the Great Libanotis of Theophrastus, according to the *History of Lyon*. Also plant 2: the other broad-leaved or common Libanotis of C.B.P.; the Ethiopian *Seseli* of Dodoens.
</margin>
These two are true and genuine species of *Laserpitium* <note>Laserwort, prized for its medicinal resin</note>, because the seeds of both consist of several leafy wings joined together like the paddles of a river water-mill. They differ from one another in size, color, and the cutting or divisions of the leaves.

<margin>
Same page, column 2, plant 3. Lesser broad-leaved Libanotis with curled seed (C.B.P. and *Prodromus*). A bitter *Costus*-like plant resembling Libanotis with curled seed (Jean Bauhin).
</margin>
This umbel, likewise for the reason mentioned above, ought to be counted as a *Laserpitium*; indeed, it is easily distinguished from the previous ones by the width of its leaves (which are very large) and by the wrinkled and curled nature of its seeds. It produces two seeds following each white flower that are thick, curled, and warty on their leafy wings, resembling the waves of the sea. Chiefly for this reason, we have sent this and the two preceding umbels to their proper relatives—since they bear the mark of the Laserworts and possess a scent and flavor very similar to *Laserpitium*—having been taught by the book of nature, regardless of the naming and arrangement of authors under the name of *Libanotis*. For they should not be arranged with the true and genuine *Libanotis* (the berry-bearing type) proposed by us at the start of this little treatise, simply because they produce frankincense, as authors claim. This property is secondary for them, just as it is for many other umbels that serve under entirely different signs <note>i.e., belong to different classifications</note>; as we demonstrated above in the more general observations on umbels that pour out a white, yellow, or reddish sap smelling of frankincense.

<margin>
Same page, column 2, plant 4. Lesser Libanotis with celery leaves (C.B.P.). The Libanotis of Theophrastus, according to Matthias de l'Obel’s *Adversaria*.
</margin>
Since the seed of this plant is white like dill (according to the cited passage of L'Obel) and broad, approaching somewhat the seed of the broad-leaved parsnip (according to Jean Bauhin), it must be relegated by the book of nature to the seventh genus of this preceding treatise and placed there among its relatives.

<margin>
Same page, column 2, plant 5. Libanotis with celery leaves and rough seed (C.B.P.). Rosemary with leaves like celery, or Cretan *Siler* (Honorio Belli, Epistle 1 to Clusius <note>Charles de l'Écluse, a famous 16th-century botanist</note>).
</margin>
Whose words are these: "The leaves of this rosemary are like celery, serrated, and the flowers in the umbel are white; when these fade, they are succeeded by oblong, rough seeds; it rests on a thick root, black on the outside,

<column-break/>

white on the inside, smelling strongly of frankincense." From this, anyone can gather that this umbel is a species of *Laserpitium*. No one even moderately skilled in the botanical art will deny that it should be placed with its preceding relatives based on the seed and the entire appearance of the leaves.

<margin>
Same page, column 2, plant 6. Broad-leaved Libanotis with columbine leaves (C.B.P. and *Prodromus*).
</margin>
In a few words, we shall show that this belongs neither to the genus of *Libanotis* nor *Laserpitium*. For it has oblong, flattened, and striated <note>grooved</note> seeds; therefore, it is more aptly referred to *Siler* <note>Laserwort or Masterwort</note>, with which the umbel is entirely consistent in the structure of the seeds. For *Siler* has this unique feature: its individual seeds are gifted with seven ridges or stripes (that is, three larger and four smaller ones). This is not seen in the aforementioned genera or species of *Laserpitium*, whose seeds are gifted with two upright leafy wings on the back of each seed, and one on each side at the base; in two seeds still green and not yet dried, these are closely joined to each other and form six wings in a circle, like the paddles of a river water-mill, as we said above in the chapter on *Laserpitium*. Therefore, since this *Libanotis* (so-called by authors, but called by us *Siler* with columbine leaves) has complete conformity with the greater mountain *Siler* in its seeds, we have placed it with *Siler* in our tables. Nor are any others endowed with this mark on the seeds seen in the entire arrangement of umbels with striated seeds, except for the species of *Siler*. Thus, the conformity in the seeds revealed to me the conformity of the genus in this *Siler* with columbine leaves (as in all other umbels). Add to this that its seed represents cinnamon in scent and flavor, and when chewed smells of something aromatic, just like the seed of the larger and smaller *Siler*. Thus, the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, and 5th of these species of *Libanotis* ought to be sent back to the family of *Laserpitium*, and consequently to Chapter 3 of the system above. The 4th species, however, belongs to the 7th chapter of our new system. The 6th and 7th belong to our second chapter, regardless (I say) of the "tears" <note>resin</note> of frankincense that flow from the roots of all of them, since this is common to these and many others to be mentioned below, which must nevertheless be arranged under different genera when one considers the final shape of the seeds—including both the broad-leaved Libanotis with columbine leaves and the fertile Libanotis of Theophrastus, or common Lovage.

<sig>T</sig>
<meta>catchword: LIBA-</meta>

<summary>The author critiques Caspar Bauhin’s classification of the genus Libanotis, arguing that plants should be grouped by seed structure—specifically the presence of "wings" or "ridges"—rather than by the frankincense-like scent of their resin.</summary>
<keywords>Caspar Bauhin, Libanotis, Laserpitium, Siler, botanical classification, seed morphology, frankincense, Theophrastus, Pliny the Elder, Laserwort</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Caspar Bauhin’s classification of the *Umbelliferae* family. Having addressed broad-leaved species on the previous page, he now turns to the fine-leaved "Libanotis" and "Panax" varieties, arguing that their seed structures require them to be moved to different categories in his own system. He then begins Chapter II on Fennel.</meta>

<header>THE ERRORS OF CASPAR BAUHIN.</header>

# LIBANOTIS WITH THE LEAF OF FERULA,
->& the all-heal of Aesculapius.<-

Libanotis <note>original: Λιβανωτίς</note> according to Dioscorides (Book 3, Chapter 87) is of two kinds; one is fertile, whose seed is round, angular, pungent, burning the tongue, and is called *narthex* <note>original: νάρθηξ; a term for the giant fennel stalk</note>, having leaves like fennel; there is another similar to it, but with broad seeds that do not burn at all. He mentions a sterile version that reflects their likeness but produces neither stalk, nor flower, nor seed. I greatly doubt the truth of this latter assertion.

---

Page 158, column 1, plant 1: Libanotis with the leaf of Ferula <note>Giant fennel</note>, with angular seeds, in Bauhin’s *Pinax* (C.B.P.). By this is understood our true and genuine *libanotis cachyrophoros* <note>Libanotis bearing "cachyros," a term for a corky or fungous seed-pod</note>, with the seed enclosed in a smooth, fungous fruit-case, and having the leaf of a ferula. From the descriptions of all the authors cited in the index by Caspar Bauhin, I observe no mention made of furrows or streaks in the seeds, except in the works of Andrea Cesalpino, that most learned philosopher and sharpest botanical writer, whose words are these: *White Libanotis bears a thick, round, and striated seed, provided with a white bony shell, in which the seed appears inside like a kernel, much larger than in cumin.* Even if distinct species of the genuine Libanotis (or *cachyros*) have not been found among authors until now, but were handed down in a completely confused manner, one may clearly see at the beginning of this treatise the "generic mark" of the genuine Libanotis. Its species are recognizable by their own particular marks sought from the "book of nature."

*On the same page 158, Col. 1, plant 2.* Libanotis with the leaf and seed of Ferula, C.B.P. *The all-heal of Aesculapius* <note>original: panax Asclepium</note>, according to Dodoens. Since the seed of this plant is compressed, membrane-like, oblong, and decorated with shallow streaks, and the leaves are likewise like those of ferula, it ought to be banished to its own kindred umbels—namely, the ferula and the dill.

*Ibidem, plant 3.* Libanotis with the leaf of fennel and a leaf-like seed, C.B.P. This, for the reason stated above, ought to be sent to the same place, since Dodoens and Cesalpino report that it has leaf-like <note>meaning flattened or winged</note>, depressed, oblong, black, pungent, and fragrant seeds. It grows in Crete, according to Camerarius, where it is commonly called *seseli*, and in Sicily, where it is called *peucedanum*; these names more properly belong to other plants, as we have said elsewhere.

*Ibidem, Col. 2, plant 4.* Similar to the 2nd Libanotis, or the 1st all-heal of Aesculapius, C.B.P. *The all-heal of Aesculapius* according to Matthiolus and the *History of Lyon*. It is similar in appearance to the ferula, but much smaller; the seed is not like fennel, but broad like ferula; and it is not unlike it in leaves and flowers. Therefore, according to the "book of nature," the 2nd, 3rd, and 4th species of Libanotis contained here ought to be banished to the seventh sub-genus, or Chapter 7, of our doctrine. However they may be named, provided they are correctly arranged, this does not worry me. They are related to the ferula in their more noble and important parts—consequently in seed, smell, and taste. They yield a juice which can be called *opopanax* <note>A medicinal resin</note>, just as the ferula pours forth *Galbanum*.

*Ibidem, col. 2, plant 5. All-heal of Aesculapius with a yellow umbel, C.B.P.*

<column-break/>

*The other Aesculapius, History of Lyon.* No mention of this seed is made by Dalechamps in the *History of Lyon*; therefore, it is still uncertain to us where this umbel should be certainly reduced.

*Ibidem, col. 2, plant 6. All-heal of Aesculapius with a leaf-like seed, C.B.P. The Apulian all-heal of Aesculapius, Colonna.* The seeds of this (says Fabio Colonna) are leaf-like, double, winged, and provided with two cartilages, as in *Thapsia* <note>The "deadly carrot"</note>, etc. This umbel is gifted with a seed surrounded laterally by a leaf-like wing; therefore, it should be referred to our 4th sub-genus, namely *Thapsia*, according to the "symbol" or token under which kindred plants ought to be placed, and added to similar ones from the "book of nature," both in our 4th table and all the others. Hence, it is poorly separated from its relatives—namely *Thapsia*—by Caspar Bauhin, and is placed here among dissimilar plants.

*Ibidem, col. 2, plant 7.* Lesser Libanotis with a white umbel, C.B.P. *The Libanotis of the northern herbalists, Lobelius's Adversaria.* Since, from Lobelius's own description, the seed of this is tiny, ripening toward winter, less fleshy and round, almost equal to the *ammi* of the shops <note>Bishop’s weed</note>, it ought to be referred to the umbels with smaller striated seeds and lobed leaves, contained in our 2nd sub-genus and its 2nd member.

*Ibidem, col. 2, plant 8.* Fine-leaved Libanotis, the greater and lesser German varieties, C.B.P. The greater and lesser *parchynides* <note>original: παρχυνίδης</note> of Thalius. Thalius speaks much too shortly and succinctly here in his *Harz Forest* (especially since he makes no mention of these seeds), so that we are forced to hesitate in doubt as to where they should be referred. If anyone were to place the seeds of these and many other umbels into our hands, they would easily be reduced to their proper classes. Let others provide what I could not; the way is now open. From what has been said, there are three umbels with compressed, membranous, oblong seeds (whether under the name of libanotis or panax), one gifted with a seed surrounded by a leaf-like wing, and one with a smaller striated seed; the latter is affected by the name of libanotis, the former by panax. The first is the true and genuine Libanotis, properly so called; enough and more has been said about it and its division above at the beginning of our treatise. If more genuine species of *cachyros* <note>the corky-seeded type</note> are discovered—provided with a seed enclosed in a fungous fruit-case, differing from these by some particular or specific mark—let them be added by those who detect and observe them; we have given and explained those we have seen so far; nothing should be rashly asserted about those not yet seen.

---

## CHAPTER II.

### UMBELS WITH A LARGER STRIATED SEED,
->gifted with ferula-like leaves.<-

# FENNEL.

*Marathron* <note>original: Μάραθρον</note> according to Dioscorides (Book 3, Chapter 81), is named from the Greek "to wither" <note>original: ἀπὸ τοῦ μαραίνεσθαι</note>, because when it has dried out, it has much use for seasoning many things. Pliny (Book 19, Chapter 9) says fennel is most pleasing to snakes, used for seasoning many things when it has dried; and in Book 20, Chapter 23: snakes made fennel famous by tasting it to cast off old age, and by refreshing the sharpness of their sight with its juice. To the Latins it is *foeniculum*, because when sown it returns seed with great interest <note>original: fænore; a play on words between "interest/usury" and "fennel"</note>; or rather, in imitation of the Greeks, because it is stored away like hay <note>original: fœnum</note> that is dried and withered for the winter.

*Kinds:* According to Dioscorides, there is the cultivated and the wild, which is *hippomarathron* <note>literally "horse-fennel"</note>, and it is of two types: one with a large seed, similar to *cachrys*, the other with a seed like Coriander. Pliny says the same in Book 20, Chapter 23. To more recent authors, it is the common or cultivated, the sweet, the round, and the wandering or wild fennel.

Page 147, columns 1 and 2: fennel varies in the color of its leaves, the size of its parts, its smell, and its taste. It seems to me that in this chapter on fennel, Caspar Bauhin (along with other authors) multiplies entities without necessity; for common German fennel (1st in C.B.P.), common Italian fennel (2nd), and wild

<column-break/>

fennel (5th), or the wandering kind growing naturally in the fields of Narbonne, appear to differ only by their place of birth. For the seeds of all of them are provided with three streaks, or ridges on the back (the convex part of each seed), and 4 furrows, and they are larger and longer, or shorter, according to the fertility or sterility of the soil.

<summary>The author systematically reassigns several Libanotis and Panax species to different categories based on their seed morphology, often correcting Caspar Bauhin's "errors." He then begins a discussion on Fennel, arguing that many supposed species are actually just environmental variations of the same plant.</summary>

<keywords>Libanotis, Panax, Ferula, Caspar Bauhin, plant classification, seed morphology, Fennel, Foeniculum, botanical errors, Andrea Cesalpino, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s detailed critique of Caspar Bauhin’s botanical classifications. <header>OF BAUHIN, AND OTHER AUTHORS.</header>

shorter and rounder. In Northern regions, they have a sharp, pungent taste; in southern regions, they are sweeter and much more pleasing to the palate.

Caspar Bauhin incorrectly identifies the common fennel of Gerard’s *Herball* <note>original: "Ger. non emac." refers to the 1597 edition of John Gerard’s *The Herball*, which had not yet been "emended" by Thomas Johnson</note> as the same as the second fennel of Tabernaemontanus. Although the illustrations in both Gerard and Tabernaemontanus are the same, by Gerard's common fennel one ought to understand a fennel with a striated, oblong seed and yellow, reflexed flower petals; whereas by Tabernaemontanus's round fennel, a fennel is understood with a striated, round seed and white or snowy-white flowers, as Caspar Bauhin himself claims in his *Pinax* <note>original: *Pinax Theatri Botanici*, Bauhin’s 1623 index of 6,000 plants</note>. In my opinion, this cannot be seen in any species of fennel; or if it does exist, it differs from the common kind in both flavor and scent, no less than it differs in form from the smaller-seeded caraway, and it should be classified elsewhere.

Furthermore, <term>horse-fennel</term><note>original: *hippomarathrum*, or wild fennel</note> and the sweet fennel of Gerard are shown with the same illustration; yet the illustration for both represents the meadow saxifrage of Montpellier. From this, it is easy to infer that Caspar Bauhin incorrectly assigned the names of various different fennels cited above to the image of the Montpellier meadow saxifrage. See the distinct species of fennel at the beginning of the second chapter of our system shown above; these have a sharp, pungent, or sweet scent—that is the "Italian" or "Azorean" fennel for us, which is perhaps the sweet fennel with a large white seed described by Jean Bauhin, whose illustration represents our Azorean fennel well enough.

Page 147, Plant 6: Cretan horse-fennel. Caspar Bauhin (in his *Pinax* and *Prodromos*) and Honorius Bellus (in his first letter to Clusius) say it bears a yellow flower in an umbel, leaves like dill, and large seeds similar to *cachrys* <note>a term for seeds with a corky, fungous covering</note>; it is called <term>platykyminon</term> <note>original: πλατυκύμινον</note> which means "broad cumin." From this bare and quite sterile description, nothing certain can be determined; yet perhaps by the leaves, it is the Azorean fennel, if it has a larger striated seed in the manner of fennel. If it has a seed enclosed in a fungous fruit-case, it could be conveniently reduced to the smallest species of the aforementioned and described *cachrys* with a flat, furrowed seed.

Page 147, column 2, Plant 7: Chà, a herb from Japan. I cannot grasp from which author Caspar Bauhin drew the idea that this plant should be placed among the species of fennel; nor do I perceive that he excerpted any note, even the slightest, from any author writing before him that matches this plant with fennel. When the leaves of the plant called Chà and Te <note>Tea</note> were brought to us while we were living in Paris and Blois, we took care to soak them in water and afterward spread them out upon a copper plate (which women living there use for drying their linens). They appeared to us to be wider and longer than the leaves of the ox-eye daisy, or at least

<column-break/>

more entire; moreover, the scent and flavor of the aforementioned leaves—whether chewed by themselves or soaked in water—receded far from the scent and flavor of fennel. Therefore, from the shape of the leaves, we concluded in our *Botanical Preludes* that this is not an <term>umbellifer</term> <note>a plant in the carrot family, characterized by umbrella-like flower clusters</note> endowed with fennel-like or giant-fennel-like leaves, and consequently, it was incorrectly placed here among the fennel species by Caspar Bauhin. Much less can it be admitted that the leaves of Chà or Te are the leaves of Brabant Myrtle <note>original: *Myrtus Brabantica*, also known as Sweet Gale or Bog Myrtle</note>, or that they have a similar effect, as some botanists have claimed recently. These men are not at all bad, but they are poorly practiced in botany, since the leaves of the Brabant Myrtle emit a most fragrant scent, which indicates they are hot and dry in the third degree; the leaves of Chà (which are of a more temperate nature) do not approach the aforementioned leaves in either scent or flavor, and consequently not in effect. For the seeds of the Brabant Myrtle are intensely hot and dry, reaching even the third degree. The fruit and leaves are troublesome when added to beer during brewing; they make it very heady and bring on drunkenness. The whole shrub, along with its fruit, protects dry clothes from moths and worms when placed among them. Let it suffice to have said these things about what Chà is *not*. Let us see what it is (from modern authors who have seen the little tree growing). Peter Martini, author of the *Atlas Sinicus*, describes Chà or Te thus: its small leaf is similar to the leaf of the shrub called Sumach; for it is a shrub that spreads itself into various twigs or branches. In summer, it first puts forth a flower fragrant with a light scent; after this fades, there follows a berry, first green, then soon turning black. For this reason, Chà is not Brabant Myrtle, as the flowers of the latter are small, spiked, and yellowish, emerging from the leaf axils, and are followed by rough seeds with stinging tips, clinging together in the form of a small, oblong branch, and overflowing with an oily juice upon maturity. The whole shrub has a scent that is somewhat heavy, especially the seed and its oily fatness. From these points (without a long series of words), it is evidently clear that Chà is not Brabant Myrtle. From the words of Peter Martini, what it is appears clearly enough. Whoever desires more on Chà, Cià, or Te, should consult Peter Martini, Athanasius Kircher, Nicolaas Tulp’s *Medical Observations* (page 400 and following to the end), Jacobus Bontius (Dialogue 7 on Indian Medicine), and several others cited by Tulp toward the end of his work. Regarding its powers, see Athanasius Kircher (page 180) and others cited. The method of preparation among the Chinese is also described there, but by now it is <term>known even to the blind and the barbers</term> <note>original: *lippis & tonsoribus*; a Latin idiom meaning "everyone knows this," famously used by the poet Horace</note> here as well.

---

### C U M I N

<term>Kyminon</term> <note>original: Κύμινον</note> according to Dioscorides (Book 3, Chapter 68), Theophrastus (History of Plants, Book 7, Chapter 4), and Pliny (Book 19, Chapter 8, and Book 20, Chapters 14 & 15). The types: for Dioscorides, there is the cultivated kind, to which he prefers the Ethiopian (which Hippocrates called Royal). The Egyptian holds the second place, and the rest follow. There is also a wild kind, one with round heads at the top, and another which sends forth "horns" <note>seed pods</note> raised high from individual flowers. But this plant must be excluded entirely from the umbellifers, as it is of its own kind and irregular. For Theophrastus, there are more types: according to Pliny (Book 20, Chapter 15), there is cultivated and wild, Ethiopian and African; but in Book 19, Chapter 8, he calls another wild kind "rustic," and others call it Thebaic. The prize goes to the Ethiopian and African; some prefer the Egyptian to these.

---

Page 146, column 1, Plant 2: Cumin with a rounder and smaller seed, called "sweet" by the Maltese, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. This is a perfect species of anise in all its parts, judged by the shape of the seeds and their scent and flavor; moreover, it has leaves emulating those of anise. Indeed, it consists of the seed of common anise, though thicker and shorter, with a certain sweetness (also like anise) if chewed. It does not produce the oblong, chestnut-colored, striated, foul-smelling seeds of the Cumin of the shops; therefore, it is incorrectly placed here and separated from anise by Caspar Bauhin.

Ibid, column 2, Plant 3: Wild cumin with globular heads, in Bauhin's *Pinax*; wild cumin of Dodoens. This plant is not a true and genuine species of umbellifer (much less is it Cumin) because it lacks the principal condition required to constitute

<column-break/>

a true umbellifer. For it bears seeds singly that are hairy on the upper part, not in pairs in the manner of other umbellifers. They are enclosed in a net of admirable structure, woven from four feathered or plumed ridges and many angular lobes, as if composed of thin plates, wrapping around each seed. Moreover, it does not approach Cumin in scent, flavor, or the structure and composition of its parts; therefore, it is incorrectly placed here by Caspar Bauhin and other authors among the species of Cumin, following the example of Dioscorides. We have placed it with Cretan carrot (a genuine species of carrot) in our previous system. See the explanation of Tabernaemontanus regarding the fine-leaved parsnip or carrot above, in Chapter 2 of our new system.

---

#### SPIGNEL <note>original: *Meu* or *Meum*</note> <term>adamantikon or meion</term> <note>original: ἀδαμαντικὸν ἢ μεῖον</note>, the Spignel of Athamas in Dioscorides (Book 1, Chapter 3). It is similarly named by Pliny (Book 20, Chapter 23), who writes: "In Italy, *Meu* is sown only by physicians, and by very few of them." There are two types: the Macedonian and the Spanish (though these are not actually two different types; rather, it grows most abundantly in those places, as Dalechamp adds from Dioscorides in his notes). The nobler kind is called *Athamanticum*; some say it was discovered by King Athamas, while others say it is because the most praised variety is found on Mount Athamas (in Phthiotis, Thessaly).

<meta>The catchword at the bottom is "UMBEL-" indicating the next page will likely continue with "Umbellifers."</meta>

<summary>This page critiques Caspar Bauhin’s classification of fennel and cumin, arguing that he misidentified several species and mistakenly included Tea (Chà) and Bog Myrtle in the fennel family. The author uses personal observation and various historical authorities to reclassify these plants based on seed structure and sensory characteristics.</summary>
