<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic critique of Caspar Bauhin’s botanical classifications. Following the discussion of fennel species, the author now examines the genus Meum (Spignel), Bulbocastanum (Earthnut), and Gingidium, arguing that Bauhin often misidentified these plants due to a lack of focus on seed morphology and reliance on inaccurate illustrations from older sources.</meta>

<header>THE ERRORS OF CASPAR BAUHIN.</header>

# UMBELLIFEROUS PLANTS WITH SMALL STRIATED SEEDS,
# provided with fennel-like leaves.

->**M E U M.**<-
<note>Commonly known as Spignel, Meu, or Baldmoney.</note>

<margin>Page 148, column 2. *Meum* with the appearance of Cretan carrot, from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. Another *Meum*, from the *History of Plants of Lyon*.</margin> This small umbelliferous plant, so far as can be gathered from the description provided by Dalechamps in his *History of Lyon*, seems to be a species of fine-leaved saxifrage; and from its very tiny, bitter seed and minute white flowers, it is more closely related to that plant than to the Cretan carrot <note>original: *daucus creticus*</note>, the seed of which is oblong, pointed, whitish, and hairy. Nor does it agree in the shape of its parts, its scent, or its taste with *Meum Athamanticum* <note>Spignel</note>, or the common fine-leaved variety.

<margin>Same place, plant 3. Alpine *Meum* with a purplish umbel, from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. Cretan carrot or *mutelina*, from Camerarius’s *Epitome of Mattioli*.</margin> Since this umbelliferous plant has an oblong, pointed, whitish, hairy,

<column-break/>

and fragrant seed, with an aromatic taste when chewed—according to the words of Camerarius in the cited passage, and reasoning from <term>personal observation</term> <note>original: *αὐτοψία* (autopsia)</note> of the seed, the image of which can be seen in Camerarius—it will be very easy for any botanist skilled in the art to conclude with us that this plant is a species of myrrh-plant <note>original: *myrrhis*</note>; therefore, it is poorly placed here.

In the same place, plant 4: false *Meum*. This (as we said above in the explanation of the umbelliferous plants provided with fennel-like leaves and smaller striated seeds) is closer to the fine-leaved saxifrage, both in the smallness of the seed and in taste and smell, than to *Meum Athamanticum*, with which it is joined by Caspar Bauhin and other modern authors.

---

## EARTHNUT.
<note>original: *Bulbocastanum*, literally "Bulb-chestnut."</note>

<term>Bulbocastanon</term> <note>original: *Βουλβοκάστανον*</note>, which Trallianus mentions in Book 7, Chapter 2, takes its name from the bulbous shape of the root and the taste which mimics chestnuts. Some think it is the <term>Bunion</term> <note>original: *Βούνιον*</note> of Dioscorides (Book 4, Chapter 124), to which he adds <term>false Bunion</term> <note>original: *ψευδεβούνιον*</note> in the following chapter.

<margin>Page 162, column 2, plant 2. Smaller Earthnut, from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. Another from Camerarius’s *Hortus*.</margin> This plant with smaller bulbs, frequent in the Rhine region, is by no means a different species; rather, from young bulbs, or rather new tubers, it produces thinner leaves in the third or fourth year. For, depending on the location and long duration of growth, the tuberous roots grow larger, and consequently so do the stalks, leaves, and other parts. This is very common in many aged and long-lived plants, whether they are

<column-break/>

provided with tuberous, fleshy, or fibrous roots. Accordingly, Caspar Bauhin, along with the other authors he cites in this passage and others writing after him, inadvertently established two species of Earthnut, when there is only one and the same, differing only in age and therefore in greatness or smallness. Anyone will be able to observe this in many other plants, both those cultivated in gardens and those growing wild in the fields.

---

## GINGIDIUM.
<note>A term historically used for several plants, including Syrian Carrot or types of Chervil.</note>

Dioscorides (Book 2, Chapter 167) says <term>Gingidion</term> <note>original: *Γιγγίδιον*</note> is an herb similar to the wild carrot. Pliny (Book 20, Chapter 5) says *Gingidium* is very similar to the carrot <note>original: *staphylinus*</note>.

<margin>Page 151, column 1, plant 2. *Gingidium* with fennel leaves, from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. Syrian *Gingidium* from Camerarius’s *Epitome* and the *Gingidium* of Dioscorides from Rauwolf, in the Appendix to the *History of Lyon*.</margin> Authors have described this plant in a very confused manner, as anyone skilled in the botanical art will observe, following the testimony of John Bauhin. The figures of Mattioli and Camerarius seem to have much in common with the greater bur-parsley <note>original: *lappula majore* or *caucalide majore*</note>. The description is taken partly from Dioscorides, partly from the Canary Island bur-parsley, and partly from Spanish carrot <note>original: *visnaga*</note>; so much so that one may clearly gather from this the fictitious and false descriptions of certain authors, and an even greater distortion in the image by Tabernaemontanus—something quite familiar for this compiler. Let us proceed to the true description, taken from the seed-bearing plant itself as presented by John Bauhin, and afterwards we shall reduce it to its own kindred with no trouble.

This little plant is very similar to our dill or *Gingidium*, with a broad, jagged seed; it has a small whitish root and fennel-like leaves, thinner than in the wild parsnip and more bitter in taste. In the middle of the umbels, they have a small blue head with thin leaves placed beneath, like Nigella <note>original: *melanthio*</note>. The seeds are round, gathered together like leather into a single head. Two seeds usually follow each flower, where they are mutually joined; they are rough, wrinkled, oblong, striated on the outside, and fringed at the edge, as in certain umbelliferous plants shown by us under the name of "granulated Hartwort" <note>original: *tordylii granulati*</note>. The whole seed is a little smaller than a pinky fingernail or a small coin. Leonhard Rauwolf, who shared the seed with John Bauhin, writes that it grows on Mount Lebanon and in the rugged places of Syria. From these words, partly from Rauwolf and partly from John Bauhin, it appears to have a membranous, compressed, round seed, joined in pairs and fringed or granulated; such are certain species of Hartwort <note>original: *tordylium*</note>, which we discussed above in the theory of Chapter 7 of this treatise. Since it has dill-like leaves and a membranous, compressed, oblong seed that is granulated or fringed at the margin, in my opinion, it will be more fitly restored to its kindred contained in that same chapter and gathered by us; by calling it "dill with a membranous, compressed, oblong, fringed seed" with John Bauhin, rather than *Gingidium*, with which it in no way agrees in its essential parts: for the seeds of *Gingidium* are smaller and striated. Since Rauwolf mentioned neither the form nor figure of the seeds, nor did others transcribing from him in their descriptions, and since they attached fictitious representations of seeds to their icons, it must be concluded that this plant was better described and depicted by John Bauhin, who says he had the plant cultivated and observed in the garden of the Prince at Montbéliard.

<column-break/>

<margin>Same page, column 2, plant 3. *Gingidium* with chervil leaves, from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. *Gingidium* from Mattioli and the *History of Lyon*.</margin> None of the authors cited by Caspar Bauhin makes mention of this seed, and therefore it is by no means certain to determine whether it should be reduced to the *Gingidium* with striated seeds lacking hair, or to the fine-leaved parsnip or carrot provided with hairy seeds. The umbel is (as Dalechamps says in the *History of Lyon*) white at the top, with leaves surrounding it everywhere and being jagged; the seed comes from the flowers (but he forgets the form and figure, in his usual manner shared by others), and as it ripens, the umbels contract into themselves, as in the carrot or parsnip, and feel sticky to the touch. But since the seeds are neither described nor illustrated—which occurs quite frequently among authors—we therefore leave the classification of this plant in ambiguity here, as elsewhere. It could be a carrot (because of its concave surface) no less than a Spanish carrot; for this is common to certain plants contained under different genera, as we said above in the general observations.

<margin>Same page, column 2, plant 4. *Gingidium* with broad parsnip leaves, from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. First *Gingidium* of Dodoens. Syrian Bur-Parsley of John Bauhin.</margin> Since this umbelliferous plant does not have a striated seed, it is not to be considered a species of *Gingidium* or Spanish carrot; nor is it aptly called Bur-Parsley <note>original: *caucalis*</note> by some, since it does not have a prickly or thorny seed in the manner of Bur-Parsleys. Therefore (as we have said elsewhere) it must be concluded that, given the arrangement and composition of its membranous, round, compressed seeds which are fringed at the margin (after the fashion of the Smallest Apulian Hartwort of Columna), and the position of the lobed leaves—which adhere to the central stalk or petiole in lobes or leaflets—it ought to serve under the banner of Hartwort <note>original: *tordylium*</note>. For this same reason, it is excluded from the class of *Seseli* <note>Meadow-saxifrage</note>, as stated by us in our *Botanical Prelude*, page 272. See Table 5 of kinship and affinity, and Icon Table 9, in which are seen the cow-parsnip and the cultivated broad-leaved parsnip; where also the Syrian Hartwort is represented with its stalks, leaves, and umbels, laden with membranous, compressed, round, and granulated seeds. See the General Table of icons, seeds 28 and 18. This greater Syrian Hartwort, the smallest Apulian, and the common greater variety with an almost smooth edge (so named by us), are nothing other than illegitimate or "bastard" species of broad-leaved parsnip, all of whose seeds represent a buckler or shield in their round or oblong shape. Accordingly, we have adapted a name for them in the English idiom: <term>Bucklerwort</term> in the list for Table 5 of kinship and affinity.

<summary>In this text, the author disputes the botanical classifications of Caspar Bauhin regarding Spignel, Earthnut, and Syrian carrot-like plants. He argues that many "different" species are actually just the same plants at different ages, and reclassifies several specimens into the Hartwort (Tordylium) family based on their flat, shield-like seeds.</summary>
<keywords>Meum, Spignel, Bulbocastanum, Earthnut, Gingidium, Caspar Bauhin, Tordylium, Hartwort, Bucklerwort, seed morphology, botanical classification, plant age variation</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's detailed examination of umbelliferous plants (the parsley family), focusing on their seed characteristics to correct what he perceives as errors in Caspar Bauhin’s famous botanical index, the *Pinax*. Having previously discussed small-seeded varieties like Meum, he now moves into dwarf mountain species and larger-seeded plants like Angelica and Masterwort.</meta>

<header>OF BAUHIN, and other Authors.</header>

## DWARF MOUNTAIN PARSLEY, according to Clusius.

*Dwarf Mountain Parsley* <note>original: *Selinum pumilum montanum*</note>, as described by Clusius. This is the smallest of all the umbelliferous plants provided with small striated <note>Term for seeds marked with parallel grooves or ridges.</note> seeds; it is an annual plant and has finely dissected leaves. Indeed, it is the same plant called "the tiny plant with the appearance of hog's fennel" by Lobelius. Although it is placed under hog's fennel <note>original: *peucedanum*</note> by Caspar Bauhin (as will be shown below), it is nonetheless categorized under different genera and different names, even though it is actually a species of fine-leaved saxifrage. This is easily seen from reading Clusius and Lobelius, and from the description of the seeds, as well as by comparing them to one another.

## PANNONIAN SAXIFRAGE, from the *History* of Clusius.

*Pannonian Saxifrage* <note>original: *Saxifraga pannonica*</note>, from the *History* of Clusius. This plant, along with its relatives contained in the chapter on carrots <note>original: *daucus*</note>, should be placed here; most of these consist of ferulaceous <note>Resembling the giant fennel (*Ferula*), with thin, feathery leaves.</note> leaves and a smaller striated seed. See the examination below, regarding the Carrot after the fine-leaved parsnip in the chapter on Carrots.

---

## UMBELLIFERS WITH LARGER STRIATED SEEDS,
### provided with lobed leaves.

## SILER.

*Siler montanum* <note>Now known as *Laserpitium siler* or Mountain Laserwort.</note>, from the *Histories* of Clusius and Dodonaeus. The *Ligusticum* <note>Lovage</note> which is the *Seseli* of the pharmacies, according to Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. Pliny, in Book 19, chapter 8, and Book 20, chapter 15, says they now call it *Siler*, a name derived from the river Sile, because they use it in place of *Seseli* seed; yet the ancients also called *Seseli* itself *sili* and *seli*. *Ligustikon* <note>original: *λιγυστικὸν* (ligustikon); Galen calls it *λιβυστικόν* (libystikon)</note> grows mostly in Liguria, which is why it took its name from Liguria, specifically on the mountain called the Apennine: the locals call it <term>marans</term> <note>A local folk name for the plant.</note>. Dioscorides, in Book 3, chapter 58, says wild *ligusticum* is found in the mountains of Liguria: some call it *panax* <note>A "heal-all" plant.</note>.

## ANGELICA.

*Angelica*, which some refer to the genus *Smyrnium*, and others to *Laserpitium*, or the *Silphium* of Cyrene: it is also called the "root of the Holy Spirit," either because of the root's most sweet scent, or because of its singular power against poisons.

<columns>2</columns>
<margin>Pages 155 and 156.</margin> Cultivated *Angelica* <note>original: *Angelica sativa*</note> is distinguished from all wild varieties by its more intense smell and taste, its paler green leaves, and its grass-colored flower; add to this that the cultivated female plant produces thicker and whiter seeds. All wild species of *Angelica* are affected by the same smell and taste, but to a lesser degree.
<column-break/>
They have deeper green leaves, white flowers, and seeds provided with shallower grooves, not white, but tinged with a wan or dark color. If someone were to reduce the greater wild *Angelica*, the mountain wild *Angelica*, and even the aquatic *Angelica* (all from Bauhin’s *Pinax*) to the same group of wild plants—as so many varieties differing only by climate, soil, and consequently by size—I would not argue against them.

## MASTERWORT.

*Masterwort* <note>original: *Imperatoria*</note>, which was so named because of its rare and excellent powers, seems to be an intermediate between *laserpitium* and *panax*.

<columns>2</columns>
<margin>Page 156, column 1, plant 1.</margin> *Masterwort*. In its smell and taste, it declares itself entirely to be a true and genuine species of *Angelica*. From the wider clefts of the leaves, the deeper serrations, and the wrinkled, 
<column-break/>
uneven nature of the two veins that mark the leaves, it is joined by an equivalent medicinal power. Because of its seed, which is endowed with shallower grooves, it is easy to recognize among the others of its kind.

## ASTRANTIA, OR BLACK MASTERWORT.

<columns>2</columns>
<margin>Page 186, column 1, plant 5.</margin> *Black Astrantia*, or *Black Masterwort*. Since the aforementioned plant has all the requirements to constitute a perfect umbellifer—namely, it produces five-petaled flowers in an umbel, and as each flower fades, it is succeeded by two striated, rough, and oblong seeds—these things appear clearly to those looking closely and examining the plant while it is flowering and producing seeds. Therefore, it is clearly established that this is a true and genuine species of umbellifer. Let all curious Botanists look at the book from which I have drawn these things (that is, the "Book of Nature," as we often call it) and they will see more clearly than the midday sun that I have, with the greatest right, rebuked the carelessness and supine negligence of the Botanists writing before me. This is true not only in this place, but everywhere in these observations of mine, published against Caspar Bauhin and other Botanists. This plant has been assigned various names by modern authors. First, because of the small leaflets at the very top of the stems—which are reddish-grey, green above, and surround the base of the rays in a star-like pattern supporting the tiny flowers—the name *Astrantia* <note>From the Latin *astrum*, meaning star.</note> 
<column-break/>
was given to it. However, because its individual leaves, resting on their stalks, are usually divided into five dark green lobes in the manner of common fennel, they adapted the name of fennel to it. By many others, it is called black hellebore, or black *veratrum*, because of the blackness of the roots, a feature it shares with many other plants (which are not species of hellebore). But whether it is more aptly named by us, following Theodor Tabernaemontanus, as "Black Masterwort called Astrantia," let the judgment rest with those reading this and weighing the "Book of Nature." From what has been said, we conclude that Caspar Bauhin inadvertently, or at least not with enough diligence, unfolded the "Book of Nature," even if he surveyed the books of authors accurately enough and collected the synonyms of all plants into a *Pinax* <note>A "Table" or index; referring to Bauhin's *Pinax Theatri Botanici* (1623).</note>—certainly a work most worthy of such a great man, given the many and great labors he endured. Consult the 4th Illustration of Tabernaemontanus, where we have purposefully presented an umbel laden with wrinkled, striated, oblong seeds joined in pairs. See the general Table of Illustrations for seeds 81, 85, and 86, and the explanation thereof.

## SMYRNIUM.

<margin>Page 154, column 1, plant 13.</margin> *Smyrnium* <note>original: *σμύρνιον* (smyrnion); commonly known as Alexanders.</note> of Dioscorides; *Horse-parsley* <note>original: *ιπποσέλινον* (hipposelinon)</note> of Theophrastus, growing in Sicily.

<header>ERRORS OF CASPAR BAUHIN.</header>

# SMALLER LOBED UMBELLIFERS,
## provided with smaller striated <note>original: *striato* - marked with parallel grooves or ridges</note> seeds.

### S K I R R E T.

*Sisaron* <note>original: Σισαρον</note> in Dioscorides, Book 2, Chapter 139, is commonly known; its boiled root is pleasant to the palate. It is called *Siser* by Pliny in Book 19, Chapter 5, which he notes was once sown in Germany for food; for this reason, Tiberius Caesar (according to Pliny’s writing) demanded it from the Germans every year.

Page 155, column 2, plant 2. *Syrian Skirret*, according to Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. This is the *secacul* of the Arabs or Moors, or the *sea parsnip* of Rauwolf in the appendix to the *History of Lyon*. Neither Rauwolf (who first discovered and described this plant) nor the one who transcribed the same from Rauwolf and transferred it word-for-word into the *History of Lyon*, made any mention of its seeds; and therefore it is difficult to determine to which genus it should be reduced. This is an all-too-familiar habit of authors: to describe the umbrellas <note>The flower clusters (umbels) characteristic of this plant family.</note> briefly, and either forget their

<column-break/>

seeds, or at least see that they are depicted carelessly. If it happens that the seed is covered by a prickly, cartilaginous husk <note>original: *pericarpio*</note>, it could be joined to the *prickly sea samphire* and the *thorny parsnip* of Columna. From these, a tenth sub-genus of umbellifers could emerge, characterized by seeds wrapped in a prickly, chaff-like, cartilaginous husk; see the explanation of Table 2, in which the *prickly sea samphire* is arranged.

### B U R N E T  S A X I F R A G E,

Called *Pimpinella* and *Bipenella* by more recent authors because of the two rows of leaves arranged like feathers or plumage <note>original: *pinnatim seu plumatim* - referring to pinnate leaf structures</note>; these names are thought to have been unknown to the ancients. They are also called *Bipennula* and *pampinella*. This plant is of two kinds: the *Burnet Saxifrage* <note>original: *saxifraga*</note>, named for its ability to expel stones from the kidneys; and the other is the *Great Burnet* <note>original: *pimpinella sanguisorba* or *sorbastrella*</note>, so named because it stops the flow of blood. This is called *epipinella* by Myrepsus, and is considered by some (such as Fabius Columna) to be the *sideritis 2* of Dioscorides.

Page 160, column 1, plant 6. *Great Burnet Saxifrage* and the three following plants in column 2, and however many others of this sort have been added by us, or are to be discovered and added by others hereafter: since these are not "umbellifers" properly speaking, they ought not to be placed here. Instead, they belong at the "heel" <note>At the very end of the section.</note> of the umbellifers among those "improperly so called," as we have shown elsewhere. Because of the similarity in the arrangement of the leaves, they agree with

<column-break/>

*Burnet Saxifrage*, but they disagree because of their longer spike, and their different flowers and seeds. Therefore, unless I am greatly mistaken, they are more aptly relegated by us to the related plants "improperly called umbellifers," than being placed here among the "true umbellifers" by Caspar Bauhin.

->S I S O N.<-
<note>Commonly known as Stone Parsley.</note>

*Sison* <note>original: Σισων</note> in Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 64.

->S I U M.<-
<note>Commonly known as Water Parsnip.</note>

*Sion* <note>original: Σιον</note> in Dioscorides, Book 2, Chapter 154. It is named from *seiein* <note>original: ἀπὸ το σειειν - "to shake"</note>, because it is shaken by the waters passing by as it wavers within them; or rather, from "shaking out" kidney stones. Pliny, Book 22, Chapter 22. Perhaps the *Sion* of his Book 26, Chapter 8, is the *laver* <note>A type of water plant.</note>.

---

# UMBELLIFERS WITH LEAVES DIVIDED IN MANY WAYS,
## provided with larger striated seeds.

*Seseli* <note>original: Σεσeli</note> in Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 60, is of four kinds: *Massalian*, with leaves like fennel; *Ethiopian*, with leaves like ivy; *Peloponnesian*, with broader leaves like hemlock; and *Cretan*, or *tordylion*. Pliny, Book 8, Chapter 32, writes (from Aristotle's *History of Animals*, Book 9) that female deer are purged before giving birth by a certain herb called *seseli*. And in Book 25, Chapter 8: "Deer having given birth showed the use of *Seseli* after labor." And in Book 20, Chapter 22: "*Tordylion* is the seed from *seseli*."

Page 161, column 1, plant 3. *Greater Cretan Seseli* of Bauhin. *Tordylium*, or *Greater Seseli*, in the *History of Lyon*. In the same place, plant 3: *Lesser Cretan Seseli* of Bauhin; *Cretan Seseli* of Lobelius’s *Adversaria*. Lobelius presents two images of *Cretan Seseli* in his *Observations*, which are labeled *Greater Cretan Seseli* and *Cretan Seseli*. It seems to us that Lobelius has there displayed or depicted the seeds of the *Greater Bastard-Parsley* <note>original: *caucalidis majoris*</note> of Clusius, which is described and depicted by us under the name of *Greater Tordylium*, with a nearly smooth border. Since he omitted the fringes or granules in both figures, yet described the fringes in his *Adversaria* with these words: "the seeds are white, abundant, circular, as if crafted by a lathe <note>original: *toreumatis*</note>, orbicular, compressed, joined in pairs, resembling the appearance of a small shield." Either Lobelius, in his *Observations* and images, means one and the same plant by his two figures (namely, our common *Greater Tordylium* without granules), or by the description provided in his *Adversaria* he means *Cre-

<column-break/>

tan Seseli*, or the *Lesser Syrian Tordylium*, or our *Smallest Apulian Tordylium*, the figure of which he did not show. Furthermore, it should be noted that *Greater* and *Lesser Cretan Seseli*, and the *Large-fruited Cretan*, and the *Smallest Cretan* (plants 3, 4, 5, and 6 in that same place), are wrongly called *Seseli* by Bauhin and others. According to the book of Nature, they do not have striated seeds, but rather produce seeds that are membranous, compressed, and round. Therefore, they ought to be referred to the related umbellifers (whose mark they bear), namely *Cow Parsnip* <note>original: *sphondylium*</note>, *Broad-leaved Parsnip*, *Hercules' All-heal*, and the rest—both those already discovered and those yet to be—under the name of *Tordylium*. This name is rightly and deservedly attributed to them by Pliny, from the form of the seed which resembles a buckler or a shield. Regarding the seeds of the *Tordyliums*, consult the General Table of seed images, numbers 27, 27, 28, 28, 29, 29. And regarding the seeds of related umbellifers provided with membranous, compressed, round, or oblong seeds, see the General Table of seed images, numbers 20, 20, 21, 21, 22, 22.

<sig>23.</sig>

<summary>This page critiques the botanical classifications of Caspar Bauhin and Lobelius, specifically regarding Skirret and Burnet Saxifrage. The author argues that plants like Tordylium are often mislabeled as Seseli despite having distinct, shield-shaped, flat seeds rather than the striated seeds characteristic of true Seseli.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's detailed taxonomic critique of Caspar Bauhin’s botanical classifications, specifically focusing on the families of umbellifers (plants with umbrella-like flower clusters). The author argues that Bauhin frequently misidentifies plants by relying on names given by previous authors rather than examining the physical evidence of seeds and flowers—the "Book of Nature."</meta>

<header>OF BAUHIN AND OTHER AUTHORS.</header>

23. 21. 24. 24. 25. 31. 26. 16, 27. 27. 28. 28. 29. 29. 30. 30. 31. 31. 32. 32. 33. 33. 33ᵃ. 33ᵇ In the same place, you will observe—according to the intention of nature—the arrangement of all parts agreeing among themselves in form and figure, and indeed in the consistency of the seeds.

Page 862, column 1, plant 11. *Milky Marsh-Parsley* <note>original: *Seseli palustre lactescens*</note>, referenced in Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax* and *Prodromos*. Also called *Marsh Seseli* by Joachim Camerarius in his *Hortus*. This plant has finely dissected leaves, like *meadow seseli*, and is distinguished from it solely by the milky sap <note>original: *succo lacteo*</note> that flows from any broken part of the plant, and by its compressed, membranous, oblong or somewhat round seed. Therefore, taking the seed into account, we have judged in our *Tables of Affinity and Relationship* that it should not be placed here with the species of *Seseli*, but rather we have moved it to its congeners <note>Plants of the same genus or kind</note>—those provided with similar compressed, membranous, and somewhat round seeds and leaves divided in many ways—at the end of the 7th chapter.

<column-break/>

# H E M L O C K.

->*Koneion* <note>original: Κώνειον</note> mentioned by Theophrastus in *History of Plants*, Book 1, Chapters 8 and 6; and Book 2. Also Dioscorides, Book 4, Chapter 79. The *Etymologicus* <note>An ancient Greek grammatical encyclopedia</note> records it was so named from the word *konasthai* <note>original: ἀπὸ τοῦ κωνᾶσθαι - to whirl around</note>, due to the dizziness it causes in those who drink it. For this reason, Galen says in his third book *On the Soul's Manners* that because hemlock produces stupidity when drunk, it is called *koneion*, a name taken from that effect because the body itself is twisted. Pliny, in Book 25, Chapter 13, writes that the green stem of hemlock is eaten by many; but truly, those who are wise should abstain from eating this, warned by the example of Socrates.<-

Page 161, column 1, plant 4. *Third Broad-leaved Cow-Parsley* <note>original: *cicutaria latifolia tertia*</note> in Bauhin’s *Pinax*; *Greatest Cow-Parsley of Brantione* by Lobelius. Lobelius is not consistent here (as in many other places); he asks whether this is our greatest foul-smelling cow-parsley, or the *Third Thapsia* <note>The Deadly Carrot</note> of the people of Salamanca, commonly called *canbeia*. This is the *Third Thapsia* of Clusius, and it has a stem equaling three or four fingers in thickness, like the stem of a giant fennel, and indeed sometimes exceeding a man’s height; it also has yellow flowers and mature seeds that are broad, chaff-like, or surrounded by a leaf-like wing. From the flowers and seed, anyone taught from the "book of nature" could conclude it is a species of *Thapsia* along with Clusius (whom Lobelius himself cites). But because it was shared with him by Brantione under the name of *Cicuta* (hemlock), he blindly bypassed the authority of Clusius. Indeed, Caspar Bauhin is also to be blamed for not more carefully weighing the flawed and imperfect description by Lobelius and his childish reasoning in his observations. From these, one must clearly and manifesty conclude that this is not the hemlock of Brantione, but Clusius’s *Third Thapsia* sent under the name of hemlock.

Page 161, column 1 and 2, plants 5 and 6. *White and Red Broad-leaved Marsh Cow-Parsley* in Bauhin’s *Pinax* and the *History of Lyon*. Since, according to the description by Daléchamps <note>original: *Dalpii*</note> in the *History of Lyon*, the seeds are long and black, and according to Camerarius

<column-break/>

they have the oblong seed of *Chervil*, they are poorly classified as species of hemlock or cow-parsley. In fact, these latter plants are gifted with a larger, striated seed, while the aforementioned marsh cow-parsleys come closer to *Wild Chervil* with their smooth, black seeds. Therefore, they are poorly arranged by Caspar Bauhin because they were poorly named by Daléchamps in his *History of Lyon*.

Same page, column 2, plant 9. *Bulbous Cow-Parsley* in Bauhin’s *Pinax*; *Pannonian Cow-Parsley* of Clusius; *Hemlock-leaved Earthnut* <note>original: *Bulbocastanum coniophyllon*</note> of Thal. Since this umbrella-plant likewise has a beaked and striated seed, it belongs to the class of *Myrrhis* <note>Sweet Cicely</note>, not to the order of hemlock, which is provided with a larger, short, striated seed. Therefore, it is poorly arranged by Caspar Bauhin among the cow-parsleys just because the majority of authors writing before him gave it that name and called it *cicutaria*. More correctly, according to the book of nature, it is called *tuberous, knotted, hemlock-leaved Myrrhis*. A defective figure was provided by Tabernaemontanus; a better one by Jean Bauhin in Volume 3, Part 2. Let those learned in the art of Botany judge these matters mentioned above and those to be mentioned below. If they flee with me to the Lydian stone <note>A touchstone used to test the purity of gold; here, a metaphor for truth</note>—that is, the book of nature—both here and elsewhere, they will without doubt find it necessary to come to my opinion. With nature showing the way, it will not be easy for a philosophical man to go astray, if these words of mine are applied to the things themselves.

---

# W A T E R  D R O P W O R T.

->*Oenanthe* <note>original: Οἰνάνθη</note> in Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 135. As if "wine-flower," because in smell and to some extent in color, it is not unlike the vine; or rather, because it blooms at the same time as the vine. It is also called *Leukanthos* <note>original: λευκάνθου</note> from the whiteness of the flowers it produces; its root is large, swelling into many round little heads. Pliny, in Book 21, Chapter 24, mentions *oenanthe* with a large, numerous root, etc.<-

Page 162, column 1, plant 1. *Celery-leaved Dropwort* in Bauhin’s *Pinax*; *Third Dropwort* of Matthiolus and the *History of Lyon*. The images of this are confusedly presented by Caspar Bauhin. The *Narrow-leaved Dropwort* of Lobelius has fleshy, somewhat thick, spindle-shaped roots; but the *Second Dropwort* in the *History of Lyon* has roots like *Filipendula* <note>Dropwort</note>, just like the *Fine-leaved Filipendula* of Tabernaemontanus. Furthermore, Caspar Bauhin adds a synonym to the preceding: the *Bulbous Cumin* of Pliny and Columna, which is a species of *Bulbocastanum* <note>Earthnut/Pignut</note>, as the most learned Andrea Caesalpinus himself says; and therefore it must be excluded from the species of *Oenanthe*.

Same column, plant 3. *Wild-parsnip-leaved Dropwort with Orache-like seed* in Bauhin’s *Pinax*; *Another species of Dropwort* of Daléchamps in the *History of Lyon*. Up to now, no botanist traveling through the fields of Montpellier, besides Daléchamps, has happened to see this. I am at least very suspicious of its "Orache-like" seed; perhaps Molinaeus <note>Jean Des Moulins, editor of the History of Lyon</note>, deceived by the old papers of Daléchamps, imposed this upon the world as he did with many other things.

Same page, column 2, plant 4. *Water Dropwort* in Bauhin’s *Pinax*; *Marsh Skirret* <note>original: *Siser palustre*</note> in the *History of Lyon*. Caspar Bauhin wrongly places this latter synonym with the *Fragrant Water-Rush* of Dodoens; for these two are species differing by the whole of heaven <note>A phrase meaning they are completely different</note>, as is apparent to anyone looking at them and considering them in wet meadows, marshes, and the banks of rivers. For the *Marsh Skirret* of the *History of Lyon* very well resembles the *hemlock-leaved* (or rather *celery-leaved*) *dropwort* with its poisonous smell. Consult our Table above regarding *Oenanthe* and its explanation. Furthermore, Lobelius provides two figures of *Water Dropwort*: one with nodules, the other without tubercles and with a creeping root. Tabernaemontanus

<column-break/>

likewise assigns two images for one and the same plant, his *Aquatic Filipendula*, but neither mentions a distinction in their descriptions. They are merely variations according to the season of the year.

Page 163, column 1, plant 5. *Ivy-leaved Dropwort* in Bauhin’s *Pinax*; *Myconus's Dropwort* in the *History of Lyon*. This is not an umbrella-plant, much less a species of *Oenanthe*; it produces flowers, seeds, and even roots in the manner of a *Ranunculus* <note>Buttercup</note>. Indeed, it has a caustic power like most buttercups. Therefore, it is wrongly named *Oenanthe* by the most learned Caspar Bauhin, following Daléchamps in the *History of Lyon*. By better right, it is called *Meadow-rue-leaved Buttercup with Asphodel-roots* by John Parkinson.

Same place, column 2, plants 7 and 8. *Greater and Lesser Common Dropwort* <note>original: *filipendula vulgaris*</note> in Bauhin’s *Pinax*. These two are not appropriately associated here with the umbrella-bearing *Oenanthe* by C. Bauhin. They are not umbrellas, as we have sufficiently demonstrated above, much less should they be considered species of *Oenanthe*. The same must be said of *Goat’s Beard*. See our teaching regarding "improperly named umbrellas."

Same page, column 2, plants 9 and 10. *Lousewort* or *Cockscomb* <note>original: *Pedicularis seu alectorolophos*</note> and its species are poorly placed here by Caspar Bauhin and are separated from the rest of their class below, to be placed elsewhere. Concerning these, we have proposed to publish more extensively and clearly in their own place and proper section of the *Galeatae* <note>Helmet-flowered plants, like the Mint family or Louseworts</note> (if God permits) next year, following the order and method given by nature. Provided there are patrons <note>original: *Maecenates*</note>, there will be no lack of "Matthioluses"—or rather, someone who, raised on the shoulders not only of Matthiolus but of all botanists, will observe from a distance more clearly and perceptively than everyone has provided thus far.

---

# S E A  S A M P H I R E.

->*Krithmon* or *kerthramon* <note>original: Κρίθμον sive κέρθραμον</note> in Dioscorides, Book 2, Chapter 157, with thick, whitish leaves, resembling purslane in thickness but not at all in form, being thicker and longer, with a salty taste and white flowers, etc. It is preserved in brine and eaten raw or cooked. Pliny, in Book 21, Chapter 15, calls it *Batis*, it seems—*Sea Batis* and *Garden Batis*—which some call *French Asparagus*, placing it before us without any notes. He also mentions *Batis* in Book 21, Chapter 27, which softens the bowels, and says no more.<-

<summary>This page critiques Caspar Bauhin's botanical classifications of Hemlock (Cicuta), Water Dropwort (Oenanthe), and Sea Samphire (Crithmum). The author argues that many plants are miscategorized as umbellifers due to superficial similarities or historical naming errors, rather than scientific analysis of seeds and flowers.</summary>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic critique of Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax Theatri Botanici*. Having previously discussed Hemlock, the author now moves to Sea Fennel (*Crithmum*) and Parsley (*Apium*), arguing that plants should be categorized by their reproductive structures—flowers and seeds—rather than the superficial appearance of their leaves.</meta>

<header>THE ERRORS OF CASPAR BAUHIN.</header>

Page 288, column 1, plants 1 & 2. **Great Sea Fennel or Sea Samphire with the scent of smallage** <note>original: *Crithmum sive fœniculum marinum majus odore apii*</note>, C.B.P. <note>Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*</note>. **Small Sea Fennel or Sea Samphire** <note>original: *Crithmum, sive fœniculum maritimum minus*</note>, C.B.P. Since these two are perfect and true umbellifers <note>plants with flower clusters shaped like an umbrella</note> in every respect, having five-petaled white flowers and seeds joined in pairs and striated <note>marked with parallel grooves</note>, they are entirely wrongly placed here on page 280 among the thick-leaved plants—namely, purslane and saltwort. Because of the similarity of their thick leaves and succulent density, Caspar Bauhin cast them out into the woods like "spurious offspring," separating them from their kindred umbellifers. They are better arranged by us among their related umbellifers which possess larger, striated seeds and leaves divided in many ways; or (if one prefers) they can be placed among the umbellifers characterized by fennel-like leaves and larger striated seeds. Whether they are placed among their relatives matters little to us. However, it is more fitting and better to establish a tenth sub-genus from this plant and the prickly parsnip <note>original: *pastinaca echinophora*</note> of Columna, as has been said elsewhere.

In the same page, column 2, plant 3. **Sea Samphire with the flower of the Athenian Starwort** <note>original: *crithmum maritimum flore asteris Attici*</note>, C.B.P. On account of the thickness and succulent density of the leaves, this is badly joined here with the umbel-bearing Sea Fennels. Since the Sea Samphire with the Athenian Starwort flower is a "stellate pappescent" plant <note>a daisy-like flower that produces fluffy or downy seeds</note> (as we once observed on the rocks of the Armorican coast <note>the coast of Brittany, France</note> not far from the town of Le Pouliguen, a few miles from

<column-break/>

the mouth of the Loire), it ought to be sent away to its own kind: namely the asters, fleabanes, elecampanes, leopard's-banes, and others of that sort. The thick leaves, similar to sea fennel, serve only to determine the species within the genus under which it serves.

In the same place, column 2, plant 4. **Prickly Sea Fennel**, C.B.P., or **Spiny Samphire**, Dodonaeus. It should be noted (among all other umbellifers) as something peculiar to this plant, that it produces two oblong striated seeds within a cartilaginous or membrane-like oblong seed-case <note>original: *pericarpio*</note>, and at the top it produces six spines—sometimes more, sometimes fewer. The same is seen in the prickly parsnip of Puglia, described by Columna, which is (as we shall say below in the "Errors") considered by Caspar Bauhin to be a species of fine-leaved parsnip. Perhaps also the *Secacul* of the Arabs, or the Sea Parsnip growing around Aleppo, of which we spoke in the "Errors of C. Bauhin" concerning the skirret above. Whoever constitutes the tenth sub-genus of "properly so-called" umbellifers from these species—adding it immediately after our spiny sea fennel, which likewise has a striated seed enclosed in a cartilaginous seed-case—I will not oppose them. For they have a cartilaginous covering, just as all the *Cachrys* <note>a genus of umbellifers with corky seeds</note> or the "berry-bearing" frankincense-herbs have their seeds wrapped in a spongy, soft seed-case. The seeds of all remaining umbellifers are clothed in only a double coat: a harder outer one and a softer inner one, without this kind of cartilaginous or soft spongy seed-case.

---

## PARSLEY.

-><note>original: *Apium*</note><-

*Dioscorides in Book 3 proposed six chapters of **Selinum** <note>the Greek name for the parsley/celery family</note>, namely chapter 74 and following: the "garden" type; the "marsh" type in wet places; the "mountain" type in hilly areas; the "stone" type in Macedonia; the "horse" type; and finally the "Smyrna" type growing in Cilicia. Theophrastus in History [of Plants] Book 1 treated the Selinum, of which he writes there are several kinds (Book 6, chapter 22): these (Book 7, chapter 4) differ among themselves in both leaves and stalks; for one has a thick, heavy, and dense leaf; another a thinner, broader leaf and a larger stalk. Of these, again, one has a white stalk, another a purple or multicolored one. Pliny in Book 20, chapter 11, treated Parsley, Horse-parsley, Marsh-parsley, and Mountain-parsley; and in the following chapter, Stone-parsley and Cow-parsley.*

---

Page 153, column 1, plant 4. **Mountain parsley with larger leaves** <note>original: *apium montanum folio ampliori*</note>, C.B.P. **Mountain Parsley** <note>original: *Oreoselinum*</note> or **Veelguta** (that is, "many-good"), Dodonaeus. Plant 7. **Wild parsley swelling with milky sap**, C.B.P. **The Thysselinum of Pliny**, Lobelius. Plant 8. **Black mountain parsley**, C.B.P. **The Mountain Parsley of the Parisians**, *Historia Lugdunensis*. Plant 9. **Foreign parsley with the appearance of hemlock**, C.B.P. **Second foreign Selinum of Clusius**, *History*. True and genuine Parsley <note>original: *Apium*</note>, according to our new doctrine proposed in the previous treatise and drawn from the "Book of Nature," is that with a smaller, somewhat round, striated seed. But these four species of parsley cited above and so named by Caspar Bauhin and other modern authors are by no means genuine Parsleys; for all these umbellifers are characterized by a membranous, flattened, round, or somewhat round seed. They must therefore be sent elsewhere; most fittingly to the seventh sub-genus or 7th chapter of our doctrine delivered above, and if any others of this "flour" <note>of this same sort</note> are discovered, they should be sent to the same place. We have published our own views; now we seize upon the lazy negligence and inexcusable carelessness of authors who transcribe everywhere. As the common saying goes, "the hurrying dog births blind pups"; likewise many authors among botanists, with a certain itch for writing or rather transcribing without a due examination of the things themselves, err with the erring and say what they are told. We have observed this happen many times among writers of the botanical art, because many, while doing other things and practicing medicine, wish to be seen as botanists only superficially and "as if through a lattice window." Just so, many in this later century, by transcribing and compiling the works of others writing before them in their studies, stitch together huge and monstrous volumes and sell them as their own. They bring forth almost nothing from their own inspection or (so to speak) their own brain. Instead, catching a long series of words from other authors and inserting them into their own papers, the idea of drawing the things themselves from the "Book of Nature" or hunting them down with serious investigation has not yet entered their minds. From this kind of neglect, little benefit comes to the botanical "Republic of Letters" from their writings; rather, a great confusion and a tedious, rambling reading of words appears in the monuments of many. Let what we say here be without injury to any honest and talented writer, since these things are true and rely upon reason, visual inspection, and other senses called to our aid. For what we bring into the light, we have reduced to intermediate genera from a diligent examination and exact consideration of the flowers, seed capsules, and seeds, without regard for the names given by various authors. For if the genera are indistinct, the species must necessarily be confused in many ways. But once the genera are known, the species are easily drawn out from the leaves, stalks, root, color, taste, and smell—all of which primarily required <term>αὐτοψίαν</term> <note>autopsian: personal observation or "seeing for oneself"</note>, and a diligent one at that, besides the labors and late-night studies endured over many years. We have founded this section thus, and have also composed the others, which we have decided to set before the public shortly. But lest this digression become too long as my pen runs on, let us return to the "hallucinations" of Caspar Bauhin and the other authors from whom Caspar Bauhin drew the collection of names of plants

<column-break/>

contained in his *Pinax*. He established two distinct species of the aforementioned: namely, Mountain Parsley with a larger leaf, and the Mountain Parsley of the Parisians (or Black Parsley), which are in fact only one and the same species, differing only by their place of birth. On Mount Valérien <note>a hill west of Paris</note> near the River Seine, overlooking the village of Suresnes, we found this very plant proposed by Dalechampius in the *Historia Lugdunensis* growing in abundance. We saw it elsewhere in our travels through France under the auspices of the Most Serene Royal Duke of Orléans, specifically within the enclosures of Royal Chambord. We collected its seed in both places—on the aforementioned mountain twenty-five years ago, with my most worthy teacher Vespasian Robin leading me there. We provide a figure or image of the seed collected there in the General Table of Seed Icons. See number 31: it is not oblong, black, sharp, and fragrant as are the seeds of Myrrh-seed, nor does it approach the seed of Cumin; much less is it similar to the seed of Parsley, as Dalechampius claims in the *Historia Lugdunensis*. From all the authors cited by Caspar Bauhin, a true drawing of the seed of the mountain parsley overlooking the village of Suresnes cannot be elicited. It is familiar to many authors either to omit the description of the seed or to falsely invent one when they have never observed it. Such is Dalechampius in this place: while he asserts its seed is like cumin, the words he adds there about the seed are transcribed from Dioscorides. Yet the words of Dioscorides cannot agree with any umbellifer other than Chervil, and they should not be adapted to the Mountain Parsley or the "Mountain Parsley of the Parisians." Their origins betray Dalechampius himself; he himself never handled the seeds of the Parisian Mountain Parsley, but cites the words of Dioscorides, and all the rest transfer them from the *Historia Lugdunensis*. Therefore, since the Parisian Mountain Parsley has a membranous, compressed, oblong seed like Sulphur-wort or Milk-parsley, provided with three flatter oval stripes on the hunched part, and of a similarly reddish color, these (and all the aforementioned species of Parsley noted by us) are to be placed among their congeners. For plants should be sorted by the closeness of their seeds, and named accordingly. From similar seeds, similar powers and properties are discovered, which we have observed for the most part, not only in umbellifers but in the whole cohort of plants, in which the flowers and seed capsules should likewise be considered.

In the same place, plant 10. **Foreign parsley with a hemlock leaf**, C.B.P. **Second foreign Selinum of Clusius.** **Another Mountain Parsley**, *Historia Lugdunensis*. The seed of the "Second foreign Selinum of Clusius," according to Charles de l'Écluse, is broad, yet smaller than that of Giant Fennel. But the "Other Mountain Parsley" of Dalechampius in the *Historia Lugdunensis* has a white flower in an umbel, with a seed that is fragrant like parsley and bitter; its leaves are those of hemlock. Indeed, this too is the "Other Mountain Parsley" on the Valerian mountains, by the testimony of Dalechampius himself in that same *Historia Lugdunensis*. Therefore, Caspar Bauhin used this synonym twice in vain by following Dalechampius, since from Clusius's words the seed of the "Second foreign Selinum" is broad (though smaller than Giant Fennel), while from Dalechampius the "Other Mountain Parsley" on the Valerian mountains (as said) has a seed fragrant like parsley and bitter. Thus they disagree regarding the description of the seeds, and it is repeated twice by Dalechampius in the *Historia Lugdunensis*. See "Mountain Parsley," "Mountain Parsley of the Parisians," and "Other Mountain Parsley" in the *Historia Lugdunensis*.

<summary>The author critiques Caspar Bauhin for misclassifying Sea Fennel and Mountain Parsley based on leaf shape rather than seed morphology. He emphasizes the importance of "autopsia" (personal observation) and details his own field research on Mount Valérien to prove that multiple "species" in earlier texts are actually the same plant.</summary>

Since this is a perfect umbellifer <note>a plant with umbrella-shaped flower clusters</note>, and is in every way genuine and true according to the definition of an umbellifer assigned by us at the beginning of this tract—matching in flower, seed, scent, and taste the species of Bishop’s Weed—it is therefore wrongly placed among the spurious thistles (according to our doctrine), namely the Eryngiums, by Caspar

# ANISE.

*Anise <note>original: "Ἄνισον"</note>, Dioscorides book 3, chapter 65. Certain authors, according to Pliny book 20, chapter 17, called it "Anicetus" <note>original: "ἀνίκητον," meaning unconquerable</note> because it provides an appetite for food; and perhaps it is so named because it releases internal flatulence <note>original: "ὅτι ἀνίησι τὰς ἐμπνευματώσεις"</note>, for it remits and relaxes both internal and external gassy winds.*

Page 159, column 2, plant 2. **Foreign Anise** <note>original: *Anisum peregrinum*</note>, C.B.P. **Anise of the Philippine Islands** <note>original: *anisum Philippinarum insularum*</note>, Clusius, *History*. This plant <note>Star Anise</note> is not an umbellifer; therefore, it is much less an Anise, for it is a plant of its own unique kind. Scent does not serve here to establish the genus, as it is not found among its kindred plants. The generic name must be taken from the form of the flowers and seeds, both here and elsewhere according to our doctrine. Scent serves only secondarily among kindred plants to determine the species, as we have frequently observed elsewhere throughout the *General History* constructed by us. For example, among all authors, **Garlic Mustard** <note>original: *alliaria*</note> signifies a plant smelling like garlic; however, having considered the "identifying mark" <note>original: *tesseræ*</note> appropriate to its kindred, since it is a four-petaled pod-bearing plant, we have placed it among the four-petaled pod-bearers <note>the Brassicaceae or Mustard family</note>. In my opinion, it would be more aptly named "Garlic-scented Rocket" <note>original: *hesperis*</note>. Thus I assign the scent to determine that specific species of the aforementioned genus. Similarly, **Water Germander** <note>original: *scordium*</note> indicates a plant like Garlic <note>original: "Σκόροδον"</note>, also smelling of garlic; but since it is a whorled, hooded plant <note>Lamiaceae or Mint family</note>, agreeing in all its parts with the Germander <note>original: *chamædryos*</note>—

# PELLITORY.
<note>Pellitory of Spain, a root used historically to treat toothaches.</note>

*Pyrethrum <note>original: "Πύρεθρον"</note>, Dioscorides book 3, chapter 86, takes its name from fire <note>Greek "pyr"</note>, on account of the fiery heat of its root. It is called "Salivaris" by the Romans because, when chewed, it draws out saliva; in Pliny book 28, chapter 9, only the name Pyrethrum is used.*

Page 148, column 1, plant 1. **Pellitory with the flower of a daisy** <note>original: *pyrethrum flore bellidis*</note>, C.B.P. Because of its very burning taste, this **corymb-bearing Pellitory** <note>daisy-like</note> is joined in this place with the following **umbel-bearing Pellitory**. But it is nonetheless wrongly arranged among the umbels by Caspar Bauhin and other modern authors who follow him. Since "corymb-bearing Pellitory" is a star-flowered plant <note>Asteraceae</note>, it should be placed among the star-flowered corymb-bearers (if it produces solid seeds without downy bristles, which is likely). If otherwise, if it emits seeds that turn into down <note>pappus</note>, it should be sent to the downy star-flowered plants. Since we have not yet been permitted to see or handle the plant itself, much less contemplate the seeds, we leave it in uncertainty as to which of these two genera it should be reduced. This we know for certain: it ought not be reduced to the umbellifers simply because of its burning taste or the similar qualities it possesses. Indeed, from the book of Nature, it is easily reduced (as said) to one of the two aforementioned genera, especially to that one whose identifying mark it is observed to carry. Hence, from what has been said, anyone will be able to restore this star-flowering plant to its distinct class with little or no trouble, assigning its intense heat as the specific mark that sets it apart from others of its kind,

<column-break/>

Bauhin, and certain other recent authors. From what has been said, it must be weeded out from the number of Eryngiums and arranged here among its kindred plants, just as we have done with Daléchamps. Let the truth of this assertion rest upon examination; let the knowledge of my words rest upon the judgment of those who duly examine them—they are not to be simply believed, but truly examined; yet I present them to be examined.

Let us return to the **Anise of the Philippine Islands** <note>Star Anise</note>; it is not an umbellifer, therefore it is wrongly arranged first by Charles Clusius, and then by Bauhin and others of this later century who transcribe from them. I observe this everywhere: botanical authors for the most part err with the first person who errs and wrongly applies a name to a plant; they do not examine the things themselves, but transcribe the writings and dictates of others. They very rarely inquire whether the words match the things. Let learned men in the botanical art (to whom I write) weigh these things I say; their judgment and esteem will prevail. It is not my intention to seek popular favor, but to be of use to botanists by proposing a method for learning about vegetables, so that people eager to learn botany, being thus instructed, may lead botany out of a most confused art into a science.

In the same place, plant 2. **Umbel-bearing Pellitory** <note>original: *Pyrethrum umbelliferum*</note>, C.B.P. **True Pellitory** <note>original: *Pyrethrum verum*</note>, Mattioli, *History of Plants of Lyon*. This umbel varies (as Bauhin asserts in his *Pinax*); it is commonly white, but in the garden at Padua it was yellow and gathered by him there with longer leaves. Caspar Bauhin is far mistaken when he calls this merely a "variation"; for one and the same species is by no means seen varying between white and yellow flowers among the umbellifers—I mean a perfectly yellow color; if he means a pale, yellowish-white <note>original: *ochroleucum*</note>, this I admit.

Cesalpino, in his description on page 294, shows the seeds of his umbel-bearing Pellitory as compressed, round, black, burning, and shaped like a lentil; he likewise assigns it leaves thinner than those of a wild carrot. But Gesner, with other moderns, attributes to his Pellitory leaves similar to those of dill. By others, it is said to have a striated seed, like those of anise. Thus this true, umbel-bearing Pellitory is presented so confusedly by everyone—some transcribing from others—that they do not even seem to agree with themselves in their descriptions, both regarding the different leaves and the very different seeds. However, by this certain and infallible mark it is handed down by all that the seed is of a very burning taste, just like the root of Pellitory; what we have placed here is of a round seed, striated like anise, but not

The author argues that classification must be based on the structural characteristics of flowers and seeds (morphology) rather than scent or taste, which are only secondary identifying marks.</summary>

<header>CASPAR [BAUHIN]’S OVERSIGHTS</header>
<columns>2</columns>

...also compressed in the form of a lens; we have arranged this elsewhere among its kindred species, characterized by membranous, compressed, and somewhat round seeds, and leaves divided into many parts, at the end of chapter 7. This includes *Thysselium* <note>a type of marsh parsley</note> and four types of mountain parsley, or *Oreoselinum*, which were poorly placed among the parsley species, as we noted above.

In the same place, column 2, plant 2: **Pellitory with dill-like leaves** <note>original: *Pyrethrum folio anethi*</note>, Caspar Bauhin's *Pinax*. This is the pellitory of Gesner and the *History of Lyon*. Matthiolus and Guilandinus gave the name "pellitory" to the plant described here above; it has roots and leaves like stinking chamomile, with umbrella-shaped flower clusters <term>umbels</term> joined at the center by many stalks in the manner of Venus’s comb or dill, with small white flowers and a hot, bitter taste. It does not have as powerful medicinal properties as the pellitory of the shops <note>Pellitory of Spain, used for toothaches</note>. These are the words of Daléchamps, but in the usual manner of him and other authors, he makes no mention of the seeds. Whether the seed is compressed like dill, or more like caraway or anise, it could easily be restored to its own specific genus. Indeed, all the described umbellifers (as has often been said) can easily be restored to their proper kindred groups according to this, our doctrine of umbellifers.

---

# UMBELLIFERS ENDOWED WITH
## peculiar leaves.
### PERFOLIATE PLANTS.

*Perfoliata* <note>Thorough-wax</note> is so called because the stem appears to penetrate through each individual leaf. Some suspect this is the *Cacalia* of Dioscorides (book 4, chapter 123); others would declare it his *Cotyledon* (book 4, chapter 92), if the root did not prevent it; others judge it to be his Ethiopian *Seseli* (book 4, chapter 61).

Page 277, column 2, plant 9: **Perfoliate plant with grass-like leaves** or **Alpine narrow-leaved Bupleurum**, Caspar Bauhin's *Pinax*. Also called **Rock Stonecrop with Bupleurum leaves**, or **Rock Bupleurum with grass-like leaves** by Pona. This plant is wrongly placed among the umbellifers and, consequently, among the perfoliate species. According to the "book of nature," it is a perfect species of Stonecrop (*Sedum*), as appears from the description by Johannes Pona and the most learned and hardworking Johannes Bauhin. Anyone can gather this by looking at the illustration and from <term>personal observation <note>original: "αὐτοψίᾳ"</note></term> of this and other plants. We have seen the plant itself flowering and bearing seeds; these seeds, collected from their seed capsules, I currently keep stored in our seed repository (along with 2500 other diverse types). Since it shows itself to be a Stonecrop in all its essential parts, it is wrongly arranged here by Caspar Bauhin solely by taking its name from the leaves, which adhere closely to the stems with a wider base. If he had better observed the shape of the grass-like leaves, he would have more aptly named it, with Johannes Pona, "Bupleurum with grass-like leaves," or at least

<column-break/>

arranged it among the umbellifers. Leaves—by their shape, thickness, size (large or small), notches or divisions, and color—serve only to determine species, both in this section on umbellifers and in others. Let the genera of plants be perfectly known, and the species are easily discerned by distinguishing them by root, stem, leaves, scent, taste, and color, etc. Note, finally, that Caspar Bauhin and other authors publishing general histories and composing them without a method—transcribing word-for-word from one another—have separated the perfoliate plant and *Bupleurum* from the umbellifers because they did not properly observe the "token" <note>the identifying characteristic</note> of the umbellifers. C. Bauhin in his *Pinax*, page 278, placed it between elecampane and lesser centaury; the rest writing after him did the same (except for our most keen-eyed leader of botanists, Johannes Bauhin). But since they are perfect umbellifers endowed with striated seeds, we have restored them to their kindred in the Table, just as we observed elsewhere in our *Botanical Preludes*, page 399.

### BUPLEURUM.
<note>Hare’s Ear.</note>

*Bupleurum* <note>original: "Βούπλευρον"</note> is mentioned by Hippocrates and Nicander in his *Theriaca*. Pliny (book 22) mentions *Bupleurum*, where some think the common *Ammi* should be understood, though Pliny’s *Bupleurum* seems more rightly attributed to field valerian (which is also called lamb's lettuce). For this reason, our *Bupleurum* is called *bo-pytis* <note>original: "βώπητι", likely ox-eye</note> by others, which Theophrastus mentions among the pot-herbs (History, book 7, chapter 8). And as for the *Buprestis* of Pliny (book 22), there are those who refer it to Dioscorides' *Chironian Panax*, and others to *Elaphoboscum* <note>wild parsnip</note>.

---

# CHAPTER III.
## UMBELLIFERS WITH THE SEED ENCIRCLED
### by leafy wings.
### LASERPITIUM.
<note>Laserwort.</note>

*Laserpitium* is the *Silphion* <note>original: "Σίλφιον"</note> of Dioscorides (book 3, chapter 94), whose giant-fennel-like stem is called *maspeton* <note>original: "μάσπετον"</note>. The leaves are like parsley, but the seed is broad. From its stems and root, when cut, <term>juice <note>original: "ὀπὸς"</note></term> is collected. By some, the stem is called *silphion*, the root *magydaris* <note>original: "μαγύδαρις"</note>, and the leaves *maspeta*. Thus Theophrastus (History, book 6, chapter 3): *silphium*, whose leaf they call *maspeton*. And Pliny (book 19, chapter 3): *laserpitium*, which the Greeks call *silphion*, found in the province of Cyrenaica, the juice of which they call *laser*. In Avicenna, the laser-bearing plant is called *althit*; some translators render this as *Assa* <note>Asafoetida</note>, perhaps a word corrupted from *laser*.

The botanical reader may consult our tables of kinship and affinity and the explanation we have made to see how imperfectly this chapter on *Laserpitium* was delivered by Caspar Bauhin. Our work is shown under the third intermediate genus and is uprooted from the "book of nature," not from confused authors. Here, having no regard for the names given by authors under the genus of *Libanotis* <note>Moon-carrot</note> in Bauhin’s chapter on that plant, and as we observed in our first chapter here on the Oversights of C. Bauhin, we have restored it among its kindred. The species of *Libanotis* noted by us above should be recalled here because of the "token"

<column-break/>

common and fitting to them: namely, the seeds encircled by leafy wings, like the paddles <note>pinnas</note> of a river water-mill wheel. If any other species of this sort are discovered beyond those said and noted by us there, they should be recalled here. We leave this to be performed everywhere in all the 9 or 10 sub-genera of umbellifers. I have leveled and trodden the path for others (nature showing me the way by <term>personal observation <note>original: "αὐτοψίᾳ"</note></term> and ocular inspection). Let them follow in my footsteps, and they will detect more things that could not be observed by me until now. Consider the *Laserpitiums* in our Table of Kinship of Laserpitiums, as well as in the first chapter on the Oversights...

<summary>This page critiques Caspar Bauhin's classification of several plants, specifically moving the grass-leaved Bupleurum from umbellifers to Stonecrops and defining Chapter 3's focus on plants with "leafy-winged" seeds, such as Laserpitium.</summary>
<keywords>Caspar Bauhin, Johannes Bauhin, Pellitory, Perfoliata, Bupleurum, Laserpitium, Silphium, seed classification, botanical errors, Stonecrop, Asafoetida</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic revision of Caspar Bauhin’s botanical classifications, moving from the previous discussion of Pellitory and Perfoliate plants into the fourth and fifth categories of umbellifers—those with winged seeds and those with hairy or prickly seeds.</meta>

<header>OF BAUHIN, and other Authors.</header>

...exhibited by Caspar Bauhin concerning *libanotis* <note>original: "libanotidem"; likely rosemary or a frankincense-scented herb</note>, which through the carelessness and negligence (not to say laziness) of authors have not yet become known. We have changed the names of some, and added certain others anew, of which no mention was made by previous authors; the rest, I understand, are to be joined and added to their kindred species.

# CHAPTER IV.

## UMBELLIFERS ENDOWED WITH SEEDS ENCIRCLED BY A LEAFY WING.

### Fourth Subordinate Genus.

## THAPSIA.

*Thapsia* <note>Deadly Carrot</note> is so named because it was first found on the island of Thapsus, according to Dioscorides, Book 4, Chapter 157; its whole nature is similar to the giant fennel. Theophrastus mentions *Thapsia* in Book 9, Chapter 8 of his *History of Plants*, as does Pliny in Book 13, Chapter 22.

## TURPETHUM.

*Turbith* <note>original: "Turbith"; a medicinal root used as a purgative</note> is an Arabic word used by Avicenna and others, although some derive it from "disturbing" <note>original: "turbando"</note> because, due to its great violence, it disturbs the body by purging it. It is of two kinds: one Arabic, the other Indian. According to Mesue, in his second book on simples, the Arabic kind is the root of a herb bearing leaves smaller than giant fennel and swelling with a milky sap; this is surely the reason why some have claimed it is the root of *Pityusa* <note>spurge</note>, others *Alypum*, and some the *Tripolium* of the Greeks. Mesue describes various kinds: domestic, wild, large, small, white, black, and yellow. The Indian kind does not have a large or long root; its stalk is spread across the ground like ivy, a finger's thickness, with leaves and flowers like the marshmallow. Only the lower part of the stalk near the root is gummy and useful, as Acosta, Fragoso, and Dodonaeus advise, following Garcia de Orta. Therefore, this is far different from that which is commonly used in the apothecaries' shops, as Clusius also notes in his commentary on Garcia; for this reason, they maintain that the *Turbith* of Avicenna and *Turbedh* (or *Tarbadh*) differ from one another.

# CHAPTER V.

## UMBELLIFERS WITH HAIRY OR PRICKLY SEEDS.

### Fifth Subordinate Genus.

## FINE-LEAVED PARSNIP.

*Staphylinos* <note>original Greek: Σταφυλῖνος</note>. Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 39. It is perhaps named from the color of a black grape <note>original: "uvæ nigricantis"</note>, as the root is of that single color. There is a wild variety, in whose white flower cluster <term>umbel</term> a small bit of purple color is seen in the center; he also mentions a cultivated variety. Theophrastus (whose word *Daukos* <note>original Greek: Δαῦκος</note> Gaza translates as "parsnip") in *History of Plants* Book 9, Chapter 15, says *Daucus* <note>Wild Carrot</note> grows among the Arcadians with leaves like laurel and the color of saffron; that in the field of Patras is more excellent than the others. This has a heating power and a black root. In Chapter 22, he reports the most praised *Daucus* is from the Spartan field of Achaia; it is heating by nature with a black root.

Pliny shows in Book 19, Chapter 5, that four plants are signified by the name *Pastinaca* <note>Parsnip</note> among the Latins (so called from "feeding" <note>original: "pascendo"</note>, since it nourishes the body). One kind, he says, is wild and grows spontaneously, called *Staphylinos* by the Greeks. Another is sown by root or seed. There is also a fourth kind similar to the parsnip which our people call "Gallic," but the Greeks call *Daucon*. However, in Book 20, Chapter 6, they call *Staphylinos* the "erratic parsnip."

The parsnip is of two kinds, fine-leaved and broad-leaved. We speak now of the fine-leaved variety, both cultivated and wild; we spoke above of the broad-leaved variety under the seventh subordinate genus. Since nothing worthy of observation remains to be said regarding hallucinations <note>errors in classification</note>, we shall refrain from saying more below than bringing the name used by the ancients into the discussion.
Page 151, column 1, plant 2: **Wild parsnip with a larger flower cluster** <note>original: "pastinaca sylvestris umbella majore"</note>, Caspar Bauhin's *Pinax*. The wild carrot-like parsnip of Apulia described by Columna. This has leaves larger than the wild parsnip, divided into fewer, longer, and hooked lobes; it also bears flower clusters <term>umbels</term> cut by leaf-like segments at the base as is common, round in circuit, flat on the surface, and large. The flowers are purplish-white with a saffron center. The seed is oblong and narrow, not prickly but covered with a certain rough, minute, whitish hairiness. It closes up so that it represents a sort of bird's nest, as appears from the metric Latin translation of Nicander in these words: "and the *daucus* mimicking a nest." This description cited from Columna matches our **Hairy-seeded Myrrhis** <note>original: "myrrhide nostra semine villoso"</note>, which is oblong and striated, and so named by us in the tables of kinship and affinity. Be that as it may, it is not a *Daucus* if we argue from the book of nature, since it has the beaked seed of a *Myrrhis*; furthermore, the taste and smell also manifest this, which taken together lead us to conclude that it belongs closer to the *Myrrhis* class than to the series of *Daucus* or fine-leaved parsnip.
Same page, column 1, plant 4: **Narrow-leaved wild parsnip with prickly fruit**, Caspar Bauhin's *Pinax*. The Apulian sea-parsnip of Columna. Its flowers are yellow; the leaves are midway between the sea and land varieties, slightly sharp, lightly hairy, and slightly white because of the hairiness. In

<column-break/>

the tips of the branches, the seed-vessels <term>pericarps</term> remain prickly and hard, divided by six rays, on the summits of which are as many dense tips, which were the flowers before. Inside they nourish a double seed that is striated, oval to round in shape, oblong, and endowed with a yellowish outer bark, which has a marrow inside of aromatic taste. From these words of Fabio Columna and from the inspection of the illustration exhibited by him (even if it has not yet been permitted for us to see the plant itself), it is easy to demonstrate that it is not a fine-leaved parsnip. Following the rule given by nature and observed by us, it does not have a hairy or bristly seed as required by the essential trait of this fifth class proposed by us in our tables above. It must therefore be relegated elsewhere, and nowhere more fitly than to the **Spiny Sea Samphire** <note>original: "Crithmum spinosum marinum"</note>, of which (by the seed and its seed capsule) it is an entirely perfect species, endowed with sharp, short, slightly hairy leaves. Since it has seeds covered by a cartilaginous seed-vessel, joined in pairs, with six spines reaching upward, it matches the spiny samphire exactly, and consequently must be arranged under the same genus. Its sharp, short, lightly hairy, and manifoldly divided leaves constitute a distinct species from the spiny sea samphire, whose leaves are longer, smooth, thick, and ending in a piercing point like spines. Consult the "hallucinations" of Caspar Bauhin in his *Pinax* regarding samphire <note>original: "crithmo"</note> and *Sifarum*, from which, together with this parsnip, I extract and establish a tenth genus, entirely distinct from the other nine aforementioned genera, based on its seed and its cartilaginous seed-vessel.

<summary>This page critiques the classification of umbelliferous plants by earlier botanists like Bauhin, specifically redefining "Thapsia," "Turbith," and "Pastinaca" (parsnips/carrots) based on seed characteristics. The author argues that certain plants classified as parsnips are actually species of Myrrhis or Sea Samphire due to their specific seed pod structures.</summary>
<keywords>Thapsia, Turbith, parsnip, Daucus, Caspar Bauhin, Fabio Columna, botanical classification, umbellifers, Myrrhis, Sea Samphire, seed morphology</keywords>
