<meta>This page continues the author's systematic critique of Caspar Bauhin’s botanical classifications. Having previously discussed winged-seeded umbellifers, the author now turns to the genus *Daucus* (Carrots and their relatives), specifically identifying errors in how Bauhin grouped these plants in his *Pinax Theatri Botanici*.</meta>

<header>THE ERRORS OF CASPAR BAUHIN.</header>

# DAUCUS.

*Daukos* <note>original Greek: "Δαῦκος"; refers to various umbelliferous plants, most commonly the carrot</note> according to Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 83. Called *Daucus*, *Daucum*, or *Daucium* by the Latins. Dioscorides identifies three kinds: The first is Cretan with fennel-like leaves; the second is similar to wild celery; the third has coriander-like leaves. However, Diocles, in Book 3 of *On Health*, added a fourth kind, namely the *staphylinus* <note>The wild carrot, *Daucus carota*</note>. Pliny, in Book 25, Chapter 9, says: "There are four kinds of *Daucus*; Petronius Diodotus identified them, though there are really two distinct types. The most esteemed is from Crete, resembling fennel; the second has coriander-like leaves. If one wishes to make a third genus, it is similar to the *staphylinus*, which they call the 'wandering parsnip.'" Likewise, in Book 19, Chapter 5, he mentions a fourth kind of parsnip which our people call "Gallic," but the Greeks call *daucon*, of which they also made four kinds. Regarding the *Daucus* of Theophrastus—which is the same as the fine-leaved parsnip—it was discussed above; let us now examine the *Daucus* of Dioscorides.

<margin>Page 150, column 1, plant 1.</margin> *Daucus* with very fine fennel-like leaves, according to Caspar Bauhin's *Pinax*. This is the "True Cretan Daucus of Dioscorides" in Lobelius' *Adversaria*. This plant has fennel-like leaves and a seed like cumin—sharp, long, and hairy; therefore, according to our system, it ought to be reduced to the class of *myrrhis* <note>original: "myrrhidum"; plants in the chervil or cicely family</note>.

<margin>Ibid., plant 2.</margin> Alpine *Daucus* with a long, many-cleft leaf, or Mountain *Daucus* with a white umbel, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. Camerarius, in his *Epitome*, assigns it hairy, long cumin-like seeds. But the *Daucus* of Caesalpinus—a synonym for this—has a seed similar to the Cretan type, but by no means downy; it is sharp and very fragrant. It consists of leaves not like fennel, but like the *staphylinus* <note>wild carrot</note>, with a long stalk at the leafy attachment; the umbels close up like the *staphylinus*. The stalk is a cubit high and striated. Each of these *Daucus* types is a species of *myrrhis*: the former has fennel-like leaves and a long, hairy seed; the latter is also a *myrrhis* according to the descriptions of all authors (having fine parsnip-like leaves and hairy seeds) except Andrea Caesalpinus, who attributes to his *Daucus* seeds similar to the Cretan kind but not downy. Be that as it may, they are (I say) according to the descriptions of all authors, characterized by a long, beaked seed—whether covered in hairs or deprived of them—and consequently they ought to be reduced to the class of *myrrhis* according to our system, notwithstanding the authority of Dioscorides and all his followers.

<margin>Ibid., plants 3 and 4.</margin> Namely, Mountain *Daucus* with a short, many-cleft leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. But to us, it is the longer-leaved type, or the Pannonian Saxifrage of Clusius’ *History*. Also, the Mountain *Daucus* with a many-cleft leaf and a tiny seed, in Bauhin's *Pinax*, or the Dwarf Mountain *Selinum* of Clusius’ *History*. For these two umbels have a smaller striated seed and finely divided leaves. Therefore, they are aptly reduced to the first member of the first chapter of our preceding treatise, among the *ferulaceous* <note>fennel-like</note> plants with smaller, striated seeds. See Chapter 2 above regarding Pannonian Saxifrage and Dwarf Mountain *Selinum* in Clusius’ *History*.

<margin>Ibid., plant 5.</margin> Larger Mountain *Daucus* with a celery leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. The second *Daucus* of Tragus (to whom it is also the second *Seseli*). Its florets, says Tragus, end in a somewhat black, broad seed, similar to dill or bedbugs. The third genus of *Daucus* by Fuchs is compared to coriander leaves and has the head and seed of dill, in which the umbel appears similar to the "wandering parsnip," etc. Another *Libanotis*, the "black buck-parsley" of certain authors, according to John Bauhin. Its seed is broad (says John Bauhin) and approaching the seed of the broad-leaved parsnip. From all the descriptions of these authors, this umbel (since it has a membranous, compressed, oblong seed) ought to be referred to our seventh genus. However, the other synonyms brought forward by Caspar Bauhin do not generically agree with the aforementioned umbel. For the "Venetian Saxifrage," or *Daucus selinoides* in Lobelius’ *Adversaria*, bears an oblong, angular seed in its umbels, in the manner of fennel or Mountain *Seseli*. In Clusius’ *History*, the top of its stem and the furthest branches are adorned with umbels of pale florets, which are succeeded by seeds similar to those of *Siler montanum* <note>Laserwort</note>. Therefore, from the synonyms and seed descriptions of Clusius and Lobelius, the Venetian Saxifrage (or *Daucus selinoides* of Lobel and the second Mountain *Seseli* of Clusius) ought to be sent away to the second chapter of our new system as a different umbellifer. They are, therefore, wrongly placed here as one and the same synonymous umbelliferous plant and associated by Caspar Bauhin, since they serve under different signs in the Book of Nature.

<margin>Ibid., column 2, plant 6.</margin> Smaller Mountain *Daucus* with a celery leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. A variety of the second *Daucus selinoides* according to Lobel; through the summer this bears white florets, which are succeeded by oblong seeds that turn a dusky red. This is merely a smaller variety of the former, which has a compressed, membranous, oblong seed.

<margin>Ibid., plant 7.</margin> Whitish Mountain *Daucus* with a celery leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax*; the second Pannonian Mountain *Seseli* of Clusius’ *History* description. This same synonym is offered above with the Venetian Saxifrage of Lobel; it is repeated both here and above twice. I wonder how Caspar Bauhin could have forgotten himself so; at any rate, it has a striated seed and thus should be referred to our second genus.

<margin>Ibid., plant 8.</margin> Mountain *Daucus* with a celery leaf and a yellow flower, in Bauhin's *Pinax* and

<column-break/>

<margin>Prodromus.</margin> According to Bauhin in his *Prodromus*, the stem of this plant supports small umbels with yellow flowers similar to *elaphoboscum* <note>Parsnip-like plant</note>, but Caspar Bauhin makes no mention of the seeds in the cited place; therefore, we leave it in uncertainty as to where it should be reduced.

<margin>Ibid., plant 9.</margin> Meadow *Daucus* with a celery leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax* and *Prodromus*. Upon its furthest branches sit white umbels of tiny florets, which are succeeded by a somewhat long, blackish, aromatic seed. From these words of Caspar Bauhin, because of this sterile and lopsided description, we still remain doubtful as to where it should be relegated.

<margin>Ibid., plant 10.</margin> Field *Daucus* with a celery leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. The second kind of *Daucus* in Daléchamps’ *History of Lyon*. According to the *History of Lyon*, it has an umbel like celery with a white flower and abundant, minute, oblong, sharp, fragrant seeds. Because of the lopsided description of this seed, we leave its classification in ambiguity as well.

<margin>Ibid., plant 11.</margin> *Daucus* with a parsley or coriander leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. The *Bunium* <note>Earthnut</note> of Daléchamps’ *History of Lyon*. It has the flower and umbels of dill, with a fragrant seed smaller than that of henbane. How imperfectly this and the superior umbel are described in the *History of Lyon*, let fair-minded and learned botanists judge.

<margin>Ibid., plant 12.</margin> Nobby Cretan *Daucus* with a yellow umbel, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. The third Cretan *Daucus* of Honorius Bellus, Letter 1 to Carolus Clusius. It bears yellow flowers in umbels like dill, and a seed like cumin; therefore, it ought to be referred to the second genus of our system.

<margin>Ibid., plant 13.</margin> Meadow *Daucus* with a marsh-milfoil leaf, in Bauhin's *Pinax*. The Meadow *Daucus* of Daléchamps’ *History of Lyon*. We have reduced this umbel to the class of *oenanthe* <note>Water Dropworts</note>, as can be seen in Chapter 2.

Alsatian *Daucus* of Caspar Bauhin in his *Prodromus*. The furthest branches of this are adorned with sufficiently large umbels of yellowish florets, which are succeeded by flat seeds. Caspar Bauhin forgot this *Daucus* in his *Pinax*, and not without cause in my judgment, seeing as it is prominent for its membranous, compressed, round seed, and the whole plant abounds with a milky sap. It is *Thysselinum* <note>Milk-parsley</note> growing in wet, boggy, and shaded alder groves, notwithstanding the height which Caspar Bauhin assigns to it—greater than *Thysselinum*. We have encountered the same plant, often exceeding the height of a man, within the fences of Chambord in those damp, shaded alder groves, alongside the river Cosson, a mile distant from the Royal Palace of Chambord.

Thus, the 1st and 2nd plants belong to the class of *myrrhis*, and are therefore reduced to our eighth subordinate genus because they have a beaked seed. The 3rd and 4th belong to our second genus because they have a smaller striated seed and fennel-like leaves. The 5th belongs to our seventh genus because it has a membranous, compressed, somewhat round or oblong seed. But the Venetian Saxifrage, or *Daucus selinoides* of Lobel (the synonym poorly adapted to the former by Caspar Bauhin), ought to belong to our second genus since it has a striated seed. The 6th is only a variety of the 5th, with a compressed, membranous, oblong seed. The 7th is the same as the fifth, as appears from the double repetition made by Caspar Bauhin of Clusius’ synonym, namely the second Pannonian Mountain *Seseli*. The 8th has the seed of *elaphoboscum* or broad-leaved parsnip, therefore it ought to be sent to our 7th genus. The 9th should perhaps be relegated to the same place. The 10th, 11th, 12th, and 13th—all these four most recently placed umbels—ought to be referred to our second chapter according to our system, because they have a smaller striated seed; and all the rest contained in this chapter on *Daucus* which have a striated and short seed should be relegated to our second chapter. The Alsatian *Daucus*, as said above, is the same as *Thysselinum*, and therefore belongs to our seventh subordinate chapter of umbels. Since none of these *Daucus* types belong to the *Daucus* of Theophrastus, each one can very easily be reduced to the individual chapters of our aforementioned system according to its own characteristics, just as has been done by us here.

<summary>This page provides a systematic re-classification of various plants grouped under the genus 'Daucus' by Caspar Bauhin, arguing that many are misidentified based on their seed morphology. The author uses personal observations from Chambord to identify the 'Alsatian Daucus' as Milk-parsley (Thysselinum) and assigns the other species to his own taxonomic categories.</summary>

<header>WORKS OF BAUHIN AND OTHER AUTHORS.</header>

# CHAPTER VI.

## UMBELLIFERS ENDOWED WITH PRICKLY SEEDS,
### or hedgehog-like seeds.

#### The Sixth Subordinate Genus.

# CAUCALIS.
<note>Commonly known today as Bur-parsley.</note>

*Caucalis* <note>original Greek: "Kαυκαλὶς"</note> in Dioscorides, Book 2, Chapter 169, follows *scandix* <note>chervil</note>; some name it "wild carrot." Pliny, in Book 21, Chapter 15, following Dioscorides, classified it among the vegetables.

---

<margin>Page 152, column 1, plant 1. Great Caucalis with prickly seeds like cow-parsnip, according to Bauhin's *Pinax*.</margin> *Greater Caucalis* of Clusius’s *History*. This plant is wrongly named by Carolus Clusius first, and by Caspar Bauhin and other authors who followed Clusius and Bauhin in their General Histories written in this later century. I say that everyone has blindly followed the hallucinating Clusius, who first named it poorly. They have in no way considered the essential characteristic belonging to this genus—partly because they did not know it, and partly because, numbed by a lazy negligence, they did not deign to investigate the "Book of Nature" further. Instead, by transcribing from the books of their predecessors, they chose to err along with those already in error, paying no regard to the generic mark prescribed by nature for every genus. This umbellifer is not a *Caucalis*, since its seed is not prickly (in the manner of *Caucalis*) nor covered on all sides with hedgehog-like ridges. Prickly seeds are the generic and essential mark belonging to all species of *Caucalis*, as we taught above in the chapter on that genus. Anyone can perceive this from our General Table of seed illustrations; see figures 14-14, 15-15, 16-16, 17-17, 18-18, 19-19, and 19a. You have there the seeds of the *Caucalis* genus exhibited with the greatest skill by our most expert engraver, drawn and sculpted from personal observation <note>original: "αὐτοψίᾳ" (autopsia); meaning first-hand visual study</note>. We ourselves provided the seeds from our own seed-store and displayed them publicly alongside all the seeds of the umbelliferous plants in the University's public garden. Therefore, based on what has been said, this umbellifer must be reassigned elsewhere. I reckon it cannot be more aptly referred than to *Tordylium* <note>Hartwort</note> (following the learned Jacques Plateau, who sent its seed to Carolus Clusius). This will be evident to anyone looking at the Book of Nature and contemplating the seeds themselves. See Table 9 of the illustrations, where umbellifers with membranous, compressed, round, or roundish seeds are presented: namely *Peucedanum* <note>Hog’s Fennel</note>, *Sphondylium* <note>Cow-parsnip</note>, *Pastinaca latifolia* <note>Broad-leaved parsnip</note>, and *Tordylium*. Under this latter genus is included the "Greater Caucalis" of Clusius, which was wrongly so named. Consult also the Table of seed illustrations, figures 25-25 through 30-30. According to the Book of Nature, all these umbellifers march under the same banner because of their seeds.

<column-break/>

<margin>Ibidem, plant 2. Field Caucalis with prickly seeds and large flowers, according to Bauhin's *Pinax*.</margin> Prickly-seeded <note>original: "echinophora"</note> thick-fruited <note>original Greek: "πυνκνόκαρπος" (pyknokarpos)</note> or perhaps the *Gingidium* of Dioscorides, according to Columna. The thick-fruited *Echinophora* is wrongly added as a synonym for this by C. Bauhin, since the broad-fruited <note>original Greek: "πλατύκαρπος" (platykarpos)</note> *Echinophora* of Columna is the true synonym. The thick-fruited variety is actually a synonym for the large-fruited *Caucalis* of Montpellier. Let there be a transformation of the names that were wrongly assigned to the illustrations by Fabio Columna, and the mistake of both Columna and Caspar Bauhin will be removed. Observe the Table of seed illustrations, 15-15 and 16-16, and consequently examine the seeds and the plants themselves when you have them at hand. By comparing the Book of Nature with the error of Columna and C. Bauhin in assigning these synonyms, it will be easy to detect the aforementioned hallucination.

<margin>Page 153, column 2, plant 9. Caucalis with Peucedanum-like leaves, according to Bauhin's *Pinax*.</margin> *English Caucalis* of de l'Obel. Since this umbellifer is endowed with a smaller, striated seed (in the manner of fine-leaved saxifrage), both in its leaves and its seeds, it must be cast out from here for the reason stated before. It should be sent to join its own kin, and by no means reduced to the genus *Caucalis*, for it does not bear its symbol—namely, the hedgehog-like or prickly seed.

---

# CHAPTER VII.

## UMBELLIFERS WITH MEMBRANOUS,
### compressed, roundish seeds, and fennel-like leaves.

#### The Seventh Subordinate Genus.

# FERULA.
<note>Giant Fennel.</note>

*Narthex* <note>original Greek: "Νάρθηξ"</note> in Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 91. *Narthex* and *narthekion* in Theophrastus, *History of Plants* 6.2, which is (according to the translator Gaza) *ferula* and *ferulago*. Pliny, Book 13, Chapter 22, calls it *ferula*.

Pliny describes two kinds of *ferula*: "The Greeks call the one that rises to a great height *nartheca*, and the one that is always low *narthecia*. And *Thapsia* <note>Deadly carrot</note> is undoubtedly a *ferula*, but of its own kind," etc. In Book 20, Chapter 23: "The *ferula* that divides from a single stalk at the top is considered the female."

<sig>Y</sig>
<meta>catchword: PANAX</meta>

<summary>In this page, the author critiques the classification of Bur-parsley (Caucalis) by Bauhin and Clusius, arguing that they ignored seed morphology—specifically the requirement for prickly seeds—in favor of repeating older errors. He also begins a new chapter on the genus Ferula (Giant Fennel), citing classical authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Caspar Bauhin’s botanical classifications. Following the previous discussion on prickly-seeded plants like Caucalis, the author now moves to other genera such as Panax, Anethum (Dill), and Peucedanum (Hog’s Fennel), identifying what he considers Bauhin's "hallucinations" or errors in categorization based on seed morphology.</meta>

<header>THE HALLUCINATIONS OF CASPAR BAUHIN.</header>

### PANAX ASCLEPIAS,
<note>Also known as Asclepius’s All-heal.</note>
->and Frankincense-herb with Fennel-leaf and seed.<-

*Asclepias* <note>original Greek: "Ἀσκληπιὰς"</note> is of two kinds according to Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 87. The first has a seed that is round, angular, pungent, and burns the tongue; it is called *kachry* <note>original Greek: "κάγχρυ"</note> and has fennel-like leaves. The second is very similar, but with a broad seed that does not burn at all. A sterile version of these also exists, which resembles them but produces neither stem, nor flower, nor seed. Notwithstanding the authority of Dioscorides, I conclude that this is a paradox <note>original Greek: "παράδοξον"; meaning an inconsistency or biological impossibility</note>.

<columns>2</columns>
Regarding the true and genuine, or "hair-bearing" Frankincense-herb <note>Libanotis</note>, we spoke in the first chapter of the preceding treatise, and in Chapter 1 among the "hallucinations" of Caspar Bauhin. The second, third, and fourth species of Frankincense-herb contained in Bauhin’s chapter on "Frankincense-herb with Fennel-leaf" ought to be placed here with the <term>Ferula|A genus of tall, sturdy herbs in the carrot family, including Giant Fennel</term>, because they are gifted with a membranous, compressed, and oblong seed, as was stated above in the hallucinations regarding the Frankincense-herb. As for the "Asclepius’s All-heal with leafy seeds" of Bauhin’s *Pinax*, and the "Apulian Asclepius’s All-heal" of Columna—since it is a species of *Thapsia* <note>Deadly Carrot</note>—let it be referred to the fourth chapter of our doctrine delivered above. See the rest above in Chapter 1 concerning the Frankincense-herb with fennel-leaf.
<column-break/>
<columns>1</columns>

### ANETHUM.
<note>Commonly known as Dill.</note>

*Anethon* <note>original Greek: "Ἄνηθον"</note> in Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 67, is named from *ano thein* <note>original Greek: "παρὰ τὸ ἄνω θεῖν"</note>, because it grows quickly. Theophrastus in his *History of Plants*, Book 1, Chapter 18, and Book 7, Chapter 4, says there are several kinds of it, though he does not list them. Pliny discusses Dill in Book 2, Chapter 18.

<columns>2</columns>
On page 147, column 2, plant 2: "Greater Wild Dill," according to Bauhin’s *Pinax*. "Wild Dill larger than the cultivated, with fennel leaves," according to Caesalpinus. This <term>umbel|A flower cluster that spreads from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</term> is not Dill, according to the words of Caesalpinus, who asserts the seed is tiny, almost like *Ammi* <note>Bishop's Weed</note>, and somewhat pungent. Since the seed of *Ammi* is oblong and striated, whereas the seed of Dill is roundish, membranous, compressed, and provided with three shallower ridges in the middle of the outer or convex surface (as are all the aforementioned, and many others of which we shall speak below in this 7th chapter), it is poorly placed here as a Dill.
Ibidem, column 2, plant 2: "Lesser Wild Dill," of Bauhin’s *Pinax* and *Prodromos*.
<column-break/>
It is an umbel composed of eight or nine rays, each an inch long, with small yellow flowers and a small, oblong seed, according to Bauhin in his *Prodromos*. Therefore, it does not have a membranous, compressed, round seed like Dill. Because of the brevity of authors in their descriptions, as they do not properly observe the seeds, it is impossible to conclude with certainty to which genus certain umbels should be reduced. But from this method exhibited by us, it will be very easy to reduce all umbels to one of the aforementioned subordinate genera assigned by us, provided that the seeds are present, or that they are correctly and exactly described by authors.
<columns>1</columns>

### PEUCEDANUM.
<note>Commonly known as Hog’s Fennel or Sulfur-wort.</note>

*Peukedanon* <note>original Greek: "Πευκέδανον"</note> in Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 92; and Theophrastus, *History of Plants*, Book 9, Chapter 14. Although there are those who wish the name to be derived from its bitterness, it seems to be named from *peuke* <note>original Greek: "πεύκη"</note>, which is "pine" to the Latins, since its leaves have a certain similarity to pine needles. Whence it is also called *pinastellum* <note>Little Pine</note> by some. Pliny mentions it in Book 25, Chapter 9.

<columns>2</columns>
On page 149, column 2, plant 3: "Lesser Hog’s Fennel," according to Bauhin’s *Pinax*. "A tiny plant with the appearance of Hog’s Fennel," according to Lobelius’s *Adversaria* and illustrations. Hog’s Fennel is an umbel with grass-like leaves and is endowed with a membranous, compressed, roundish seed. It is therefore little consistent with reason that this tiny umbel-bearer is Hog’s Fennel, since the "tiny plant with the appearance of Hog’s Fennel" (as Lobelius has it) has a seed shorter and thinner than anise, with a pleasant, somewhat pungent taste, and a root that is bushy at the top, tasting like *Oreoselinum* <note>Mountain Parsley</note>, etc. Here, as in many other places, Lobelius is not sufficiently consistent with himself; he had ignored the characteristic mark familiar to Hog’s Fennel, nor did others observe it well, but rather erred with him as he wandered (as most often happens). Nor should the "Pannonian Hog’s Fennel" of Clusius be called Hog’s Fennel, because from Clusius's own words, it has a seed almost like *Selinum* <note>Celery/Parsley</note>, short and somewhat thick—a mark that does not fit Hog’s Fennel
<column-break/>
species, since the seed of Hog’s Fennel is compressed, membranous, and roundish. Furthermore, the "Third Saxifrage" of Matthiolus and the *History of Lyon* is not an umbel-bearer, much less a Hog’s Fennel. This clearly appears from the words of Daléchamps: "it bears dense white flowers and pale calyxes, in the manner of *Ocymoides* <note>Campion-like</note>, with notched edges in the shape of a crown, in which is a red seed smaller than a poppy." Since this is a description of a *Lychnis* <note>Campion</note>, Caspar Bauhin is far mistaken when he placed the "Third Saxifrage" of Matthiolus as a synonym for Hog’s Fennel. Indeed, even Johann Bauhin asserts it has a seed like *Selinum*, therefore it is not Hog’s Fennel. Unless my mind deceives me greatly, this "tiny plant with the appearance of Hog’s Fennel" would be more fitly designated as a species of dwarf mountain parsley, or the Mountain Parsley of Clusius; this can be drawn out from the descriptions of Clusius and Lobelius compared with one another.
<columns>1</columns>

---

->UMBELLIFERS WITH COMPRESSED, MEMBRANOUS,<-
->roundish seeds, and provided with lobed leaves.<-

### SPHONDYLIUM.
<note>Commonly known as Cow Parsnip.</note>

*Sphondylion* <note>original Greek: "Σφονδύλιον"</note> has leaves similar to the plane tree, approaching the appearance of All-heal <note>Panax</note> leaves, according to Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 90. Pliny mentions it in Book 12, Chapter 26, and Book 24, Chapter 6. It is so named perhaps on account of the heaviness of its scent, from the *sphondyle*, a type of insect with a heavy odor, as Pena has recorded.

### PASTINACA LATIFOLIA.
<note>Broad-leaved Parsnip.</note>

*Elaphoboskon* <note>original Greek: "Ἐλαφόβοσκον"</note> in Dioscorides, Book 3, Chapter 80, and Pliny, Book [22], Chapter 22; that is, "deer-fodder," for the story goes that deer resist snakes by eating this fodder, on the authority of Dioscorides and Pliny. Whether the *Baucia* or Broad-leaved Parsnip of the Arabs is the *Elaphoboscon* of Lobelius’s *Adversaria* is debated. Since nothing occurs here, nor above in *Sphondylium*, that needs noting, we refrain from adding more about it and the parsnip, just as we do elsewhere in these "hallucinations."

<meta>catchword: TOR-</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the misclassification of several umbelliferous plants—Panax, Dill, and Hog’s Fennel—by previous botanists like Bauhin and Lobelius, emphasizing that seed shape is the only reliable way to distinguish these genera. The author also introduces the next category of plants: those with flat, roundish seeds and lobed leaves, including Cow Parsnip and Broad-leaved Parsnip.</summary>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic reclassification of umbelliferous plants (the carrot and parsley family). Having previously discussed plants with prickly or elongated seeds, he now focuses on those with flat, round, shield-like seeds, such as Tordylium and certain types of "All-heal" (Panax), while continuing to correct the perceived errors of his predecessors Caspar Bauhin and Matthias de L'Obel.</meta>

<header>BAUHIN'S classification, and that of other Authors.</header>

## TORDYLIUM.
<note>Also known as Hartwort.</note>

*Tordylium* <note>original Greek: "Τορδύλιον"</note> is as if it were *Tordyle*, a word which (unless I am mistaken) signifies a shield; and that name fits aptly enough for all the umbelliferous plants contained in this third section, which are endowed with compressed, membranous, round seeds and lobed leaves. Indeed, their seeds are round and compressed, much like a buckler or a military shield.

See the "hallucinations" of Caspar Bauhin in Chapter 2, where the species of *Seseli* <note>Meadow-saxifrage</note> are examined by us, and in that same place four species of *Seseli* are cut off and cleared away from that genus; likewise, the Spanish *Caucalis* <note>Bur-parsley</note> is separated from the number of *Caucalides* for the reasons stated above in the specific chapters on *Seseli* and *Caucalis*. These were moved here to be reunited with their kin, the *Sphondylium* <note>Hogweed</note> and the *Pastinaca* <note>Parsnip</note>, with which they agree regarding the form and figure of the seeds and the arrangement of the leaves. How appropriately they are recalled to this place, I leave to be judged by those who look upon and examine with me the "book of nature." Furthermore, by their scent and flavor, they declare themselves to be umbels, though they have been poorly placed by authors until now. For they are merely wild species of broad-leaved parsnips (growing spontaneously in regions

<column-break/>

and places suitable to them) provided with a fibrous root, and are not edible like those with a fleshy root. From the similar form of the seeds, I have reduced all other umbelliferous plants from the "book of nature" back to their own proper and genuine classes, with Nature herself showing me the way and producing seeds similar to one another. Moreover, similar seeds produce equally similar powers and virtues, at least according to a greater or lesser degree. See the rest in Chapter 2 of this treatise, and Chapter 2 regarding the hallucinations of Caspar Bauhin and other authors concerning the arrangement and naming of *Seseli*; likewise the chapter on *Libanotis* <note>Frankincense-herb</note> and *Daucus* <note>Wild Carrot</note>, where umbels with membranous, compressed, and oblong seeds, which were poorly placed, are noted in the cited passages.

---

## PANAX OF HERCULES, OR HERCULANEUM.
<note>Commonly called Hercules' All-heal.</note>

*Panax* <note>original Greek: "Πάναξ"</note> is as if it were *pan-achos* <note>original Greek: "πᾶν ἄχος"</note>, "bringing a remedy for all things." Of this, Theophrastus in *History of Plants*, Book 9, Chapter 10, lists four kinds: 1. Syrian; 2. Chironian; 3. Aesculapian; 4. Herculanean. The Chironian has a leaf like a dock; the Aesculapian has a leaf like *Thapsia* <note>Deadly Carrot</note>; the Herculanean has a large and wide leaf, so that it measures three palms in every direction. Dioscorides, in Book 3, Chapters 55, 56, and 57, lists three kinds: the Hercules' All-heal, from which *opopanax* <note>a medicinal resin</note> is collected, with fig-like leaves divided into five parts; the Aesculapius' All-heal, with fennel-like leaves; and the Chiron's All-heal, with a leaf like marjoram.

Although these species of Hercules' All-heal mostly have membranous, compressed, round, or nearly round seeds, and pour forth a "tears of incense" <note>resin</note> when the roots are cut, and thus obtain the name *Panax*, we have nevertheless placed them here after their relatives and similar plants because of their leaves, which are divided more broadly, like *Sphondylium* or broad-leaved parsnip. They bear the same common "token"—a seed of the same or entirely similar form—as the Aesculapius' All-heal; but, I say, because of the leaves being dissimilar to it, they shall be separated from the *Panax* with the *Ferula* <note>Giant Fennel</note> leaf contained in the first section of this 7th chapter, and will be most aptly arranged here in this second section. For they agree in their generic mark—namely, the form of the seeds—and therefore both Aesculapius' All-heal and Hercules' All-heal, and their species, are brought back to this 7th chapter. However, because of the thinness of the leaves, Aesculapius' All-heal and its species will be placed in the first section with the *Ferula*, while Hercules' All-heal and its species will likewise be placed in the second section with its kin: *Sphondylium*, broad-leaved parsnip, and *Tordylium*, on account of the lobed leaves, as said above.

Page 157, column 1, plant 2. "Panax with a Hogweed leaf, or Heracleum," according to Caspar Bauhin in his *Pinax*. "Heracleum" of Matthiolus and the *History of Lyon*. "Hercules' All-heal with a parsnip leaf or foliage," according to Adriaan L'Obel. L'Obel says the flowers of this plant are yellow in an umbel in the gardens of the Franciscans in Venice, but white in the green fields of Belgium. Concerning the color of the flowers, one small doubt remains for me; L'Obel speaks here of one and the same species of Hercules' All-heal, but this cannot be, because no single and same species of umbel is seen gifted with both yellow and white flowers. I do not deny the variety of flower colors, but I contend that the umbels themselves are different in species, even if L'Obel did not observe the distinction or diversity in other parts of the same plant. If the Hercules' All-heal

<column-break/>

observed by L'Obel in the Franciscan garden at Venice produced yellow flowers, that same species of Hercules' All-heal in the fields of Belgium did not produce white flowers; therefore, L'Obel observed poorly.

Page 157, column 2, plant 4. "Hungarian Panax similar to the Heracleum," Caspar Bauhin, *Pinax*. "Hungarian Tataria," Clusius, *History*. The flowers of this are followed by very thick seeds, not unlike those large, fennel-like seeds of the berry-bearing Frankincense-herb <note>Libanotis cachryfera</note>. The flowers are not fertile, for they rarely produce seeds. I tended the roots of this plant for two years before they produced a stem, flowers, or seeds; then they rotted and exhaled such a foul odor that they had to be cast out from our garden in Vienna. From these words of Clusius, I conclude that this umbel—since it produced a large, striated seed in the manner of the berry-bearing Frankincense-herb—was some species of *Cachrys*. Therefore, according to our doctrine, it cannot be joined here with its fellow umbels contained under the name of *Panax*, because it does not have a membranous, compressed, round, or nearly round seed. However, since the Hungarians use its root as a substitute for bread, and the Tartars drink a cooled decoction of the leaves in water like wine (according to Clusius), the roots do not seem to me to be of such an earthy or foul odor, unless this happened through the common rule: "the corruption of the best is the worst." Furthermore, all species of *Cachrys* do not produce seeds after their formation except in the 4th or 5th year, nor do they send out stems except in the 2nd or 3rd year. These facts easily concur to prove this is a species of berry-bearing Frankincense-herb; therefore, it is poorly placed here with the Hercules' All-heal, whose "token" it does not bear—namely, the membranous, compressed, round seed—as the "book of nature" demonstrates Caspar Bauhin’s error.

---

## UMBELS WITH LEAVES DIVIDED IN MANY WAYS,
### with a membranous, compressed, nearly round seed.

We have gathered this third section of umbels—those gifted with a membranous, compressed, nearly round seed and leaves divided in many ways—into this place from various classes, having taken into account the "token" delivered above in the doctrine of Chapter 7 of the previous treatise. From Caspar Bauhin’s chapter on *Apium* <note>Parsley/Celery</note>, we shall transfer four umbelliferous plants here: namely, the Mountain-parsley of Clusius; the *Thysselinum* <note>Milk-parsley</note>; the Mountain-parsley of the Parisians from the *History of Lyon*; and the second Foreign-parsley of Clusius;

<column-break/>

that is, *Apium* 4, 7, 8, and 10 of Caspar Bauhin. Likewise, the "Other Frankincense-herb," the "Black Hart-wort" of Johannes Bauhin, and if any others of this kind are discovered, under whatever name they may go, they ought likewise to be referred to this place. The same should be said of the Asiatic Carrot. Consult the doctrine delivered above in Chapter 2 regarding *Apium* and *Seseli*, and Chapter 9 of the "hallucinations" of Caspar Bauhin regarding the Carrot.

# CHAPTER VIII.
## UMBELS ENDOWED WITH BEAKED SEEDS.
### The Eighth Subordinate Genus.
## MYRRHIS.
<note>Sweet Cicely.</note>

*Myrrhis* <note>original Greek: "Μύρρις"</note> or *Myrrha* according to Dioscorides, Book 4, Chapter 116. *Myrrhis*, which is called *Smirrhiza* <note>Myrrh-root</note> by others and *Myrrha* by some. Pliny, Book 24, Chapter 16.

<meta>catchword: Page</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the classification of umbelliferous plants like Tordylium and Panax based on their shield-shaped seeds, while critiquing the botanical errors of Bauhin and L'Obel regarding flower color and species identification. The author emphasizes "the book of nature"—direct observation—over traditional authorities to group plants by seed and leaf morphology.</summary>
<keywords>Tordylium, Panax, botanical taxonomy, Caspar Bauhin, Clusius, Matthias de L'Obel, seed morphology, umbelliferae, Hercules' All-heal, Hogweed, Parsnip, Myrrhis</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critical examination of the "umbelliferous" family (plants with umbrella-like flower clusters). The author is specifically correcting the influential botanist Caspar Bauhin, arguing that classification should be based on the physical structure of seeds—the "Book of Nature"—rather than simply copying the descriptions of ancient authors like Pliny and Dioscorides.</meta>

<header>THE ERRORS OF CASPAR BAUHIN</header>

<columns>2</columns>

<margin>C. B. P. Myrrhis, Fuchs, Lyon.</margin>
Page 160, column 2, entry 3. **Wild Myrrhis with smooth seeds** <note>original: "Myrrhis sylvestris feminibus lævibus"</note> according to Caspar Bauhin's *Pinax*, Fuchs, and the *History of Lyon*. This beaked umbel <term>umbella rostrata: a flower cluster where the seeds end in a long, beak-like point</term> represents for us the wild, perennial, larger chervil with smooth, black seeds. This is because its seeds are smooth and black, just like those of cultivated chervil, though they are beaked and twice as thick as chervil seeds. Indeed, the plant itself is in every way larger than cultivated chervil. Therefore, by its smooth black seeds, it reveals itself to be a chervil, and not a *Myrrhis*. The seed of a true *Myrrhis* <note>Sweet Cicely or similar species</note> is always produced with striations <term>striatum: marked with parallel grooves or ridges</term>, whether it be hairy, rough, or smooth-faced. This can be seen in Pliny’s *Anthriscus*, and in the tuberous, knotted *Myrrhis* with hemlock-like leaves. The seeds of these two are covered in striations—just like the petals of crocus flowers or variegated tulips are striped—without the high ridges, deep depressions, or "keels" and furrows seen in other species of true *Myrrhis*. Therefore, Caspar Bauhin and other authors are wrong to list Pliny’s *Anthriscus* as a synonym for wild chervil. It is not a chervil, as we stated above in our teaching on *Myrrhis* and chervil. There, we showed that chervil is primarily distinguished from *Myrrhis* because the former has smooth seeds, while the latter has striated seeds. Consult our new teaching above in Chapter 8, concerning *Myrrhis* and chervil as beaked plants, and their differences derived from the seeds. Consult the General Table of Seed Illustrations, numbers 37 and 41, which represent the seeds of wild *Myrrhis* (or Pliny’s *Anthriscus* from the *History of Lyon*) and the wild chervil with smooth seeds. These are presented by C. Bauhin and other authors with the names swapped and the facts themselves confused. Regarding beaked seeds, both of *Myrrhis* and chervil, consult the seed illustration tables 34 through 41.

# CHERVIL
<note>Latin: Chærophyllum</note>

->*Columella has "Chærophyllum." Whether the "Chærophyllum" in Pliny (Book 19, Chapter 8), which the Greeks call "pæderota," is the same, cannot be asserted due to the brevity of his description. It is more likely his "anthriscus" (Book 22). Perhaps it is the "enthasicum" <note>original Greek: "εὐθανοὺν"</note> of Theophrastus (History of Plants, Book 7), which Gaza translates as "Enthusicum." To some, it appears to be the "Mountain Parsley" <note>original Greek: "Ορεοσέλινον"</note> of Dioscorides.*<-

Page 152, column 1, entry 2. **Wild chervil** according to Bauhin's *Pinax*; **Pliny’s Anthriscus** in the *History of Lyon*. We have learned from the "Book of Nature" <note>meaning direct observation of the plants themselves</note> that chervil and *Myrrhis* are very closely related and allied umbels, primarily because they are both beaked. However, a distinction exists—as is apparent to anyone who observes the shape and form of the seeds of both and looks at them more attentively—which is that chervil is endowed with beaked seeds that are smooth and black, not striated like all *Myrrhis*. Having established this foundation laid by nature, Pliny's *Anthriscus* should not be placed with chervil. According to Dalechamps' description in his *History of Lyon*, it fits the species of *Myrrhis* perfectly. Consequently, Caspar Bauhin wrongly made Pliny’s *Anthriscus* a synonym for wild chervil; conversely, he joins the wild chervil with smooth seeds to the *Myrrhis* genus, calling it "Wild Myrrhis." Let a mutual swap of these two names assigned by C. Bauhin be made, based on the aforementioned reasoning of the seed structure. This will remove the slip made by the erring Bauhin and others who wrote before him, or those who transcribed after him and compiled vast volumes. All these men failed to recognize the essential characteristic of these two genera of beaked umbels—perhaps because they did not inspect the "Book of Nature" in this section or in others, but instead transcribed or translated the books and volumes of authors who wrote before them. From reading them, I observe that the first one who erred or named things poorly, and consequently arranged them wrongly, dragged all who followed him over a precipice. This happens just like in a flock of sheep: some of the bolder and stronger ones, noticing a recently mown meadow offering soft grass,

<column-break/>

stray from the common path where such animals are usually driven. They cross a ditch or overcome a bank, and once the ditch is crossed, all the others try to cross it by jumping, following the example of the strongest. Yet very few reach the intended goal; instead, they are forced to search for a path in the ditch and in many places near the banks. In the same way, very few botanical authors have reached their goal. Instead, by "chewing" what has already been chewed by others <note>i.e., recycling old information</note>, they take up the same things to be digested again; they transcribe and translate the writings of others and publish them gathered into immense volumes. They have turned the pages of authors' books, but they have held the Book of Nature in neglect. Thus, they have erred with the erring, and thrust the faulty writings of their predecessors upon the public, being little concerned with order and method—which is (as the Philosopher says) the soul of all learning and discipline. But we shall deal with these matters more at length elsewhere.

Same page, column 2, entry 3. **Something like chervil** <note>original: "Chærophyllo nonnihil similis"</note> according to Bauhin’s *Pinax*; **Parsley Piert** <note>original: "perchepier"</note> (that is, the Stone-breaker) of the English, according to Lobel. According to the Book of Nature, a chervil is an umbel with leaves divided in many ways and a long, beaked, nearly smooth seed, according to the doctrine we handed down above. Therefore, the "Parsley Piert" of the English is *nothing* like a chervil, rather than "something like" it, as Caspar Bauhin would have it. It is not an umbel; therefore, this plant should be kept far away from the chervil class, as we said elsewhere in our *Botanical Preludes*, page 368. Since its essential characteristics—taken from the flower and the seed—agree in all things and through all things with *Alchimilla* <note>Lady's Mantle</note>, it should be referred to the *Alchimilla* class (along with the most learned Fabio Colonna, who calls it the smallest mountain *Alchimilla*). It should not be placed among the umbels, as Caspar Bauhin has done here; nor, by the aforementioned reasoning, is it *Scandix*, as Tabernaemontanus wishes.

---

# SCANDIX
<note>Shepherd's Needle</note>

->*Dioscorides (Book 2, Chapter 168) says "Scandix" is a wild vegetable, which he places after "Gingidium." Theophrastus (History, Book 7, Chapter 8); Pliny (Book 22).*<-

Observation on the teaching of Caspar Bauhin and Dalechamps regarding *Scandix*. Page 152, column 1, entry 1. **Scandix with an umbel joined from the tips** according to Bauhin's *Pinax*; **Scandix** in the *History of Lyon*. Its root is similar to wild thin-leaved parsnip, bitter and somewhat sharp; it has almost the same flavor found in the root of *Smyrnium* <note>Alexanders</note>. Its leaves are broader than thin-leaved parsnip, white, and very hairy. They are attached to the stems by a bark-like membrane at the base of the leaf-stalks. The seed-pods are thorny. The stem is a cubit high <note>about 18 inches</note>. The flowers in the umbel are white—not constructed with stalks or rays in a circular fashion like other umbels—but with several tips from

<column-break/>

greenish-white, gathered as if into a small head and joined together in a peculiar composition not appearing in any other umbels; indeed, it has not been seen in any other plant. The author Dalechamps himself, in the *History of Lyon*, makes no mention of the shape of the seeds. Whether this is *Scandix*, or an abortive umbel approaching *Scandix*, we refrain from passing judgment because of the imperfect description and the omission of the seed—a failure that is all too common for this and other authors when describing their umbelliferous plants.

# CHAPTER IX.
## UMBELS WITH ROUND OR TESTICULATED SEEDS.
### Ninth Subordinate Genus.
### CORIANDER

->*"Corion" and "Coriannon" in Dioscorides (Book 3, Chapter 71); they think it is so named because the leaves and stems smell of bed-bugs <note>original Greek: "κόρις" (koris) means bug</note>. The "Coriannon" of Theophrastus (History, Book 7; Causes of Plants, Book 4); he says there are several kinds (History, Book 4, Chapter 7). "Coriandrum" in Pliny (Book 20), who says it is not found in the wild.*<-

<meta>catchword: Page</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the botanical classifications of Caspar Bauhin, arguing that he and others often misidentified plants like Chervil and Myrrhis by ignoring seed characteristics and blindly following ancient texts. The author insists on using "The Book of Nature" (direct observation) to correctly categorize plants, specifically moving Parsley Piert out of the umbel family and into the Lady's Mantle group.</summary>

<keywords>botany, seed classification, Caspar Bauhin, Chervil, Myrrhis, Scandix, Coriander, Parsley Piert, taxonomy, historical botany, Pliny, Dioscorides</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's detailed botanical critique of Caspar Bauhin’s 1623 work, the *Pinax Theatri Botanici*. The author focuses on the families of Coriander and Valerian, using the physical characteristics of seeds and flowers—rather than just leaf shape—to correct previous classification errors. He relies heavily on personal observation (autopsy) and the work of Fabio Columna to challenge the established authorities of the time.</meta>

<header>OF BAUHIN, and other Authors.</header>

Observation on the chapter by Caspar Bauhin regarding coriander. *Page 158, column 2, lines 2 & 3. Smaller testiculate coriander <note>original: "Coriandrum minus testiculatum"</note>, C.B.P. Another smaller, less odorous coriander from the History of Lyon, and the wild coriander of Myconus, from the History of Lyon.* Since two illustrations of wild coriander are presented in the *History of Lyon* <note>The *Historia Generalis Plantarum* (1586), edited by Daléchamps</note>—drawn in different ways by Daléchamps alone—and since others later borrowed these same drawings from him, and since they have round seeds joined in pairs on both sides, I suspect, not without reason, that these are figures of one and the same plant grown in different soils. I make this conjecture because I do not find different essential characteristics

<column-break/>

assigned to them in the description in the *History of Lyon*. If anyone can produce those two plants with distinct essential and specific markers, I shall by no means refuse to agree with them. But since it is a habit of Des Moulins <note>original: "Molinæo"</note>, the composer of the aforementioned *History of Lyon*, to repeat a plant two or three times and represent the same thing with different illustrations from different authors—and sometimes to provide three or even four illustrations for one and the same plant—it occurs to me to judge him guilty of the same fault in this instance. See Caspar Bauhin’s own observations on that same *History of Lyon*.

---

# CHAPTER I.
## IMPROPERLY NAMED UMBELS.
### First sub-genus.
## V A L E R I A N.

*Valerian, according to Dioscorides, Book 1, Chapter 10.* *Phu* <note>original: "φῦ"</note> or wild nard, *because its roots rival the scent of nard, though with a certain heaviness of odor; indeed, they are similar to nard in their medicinal powers, according to the authors Galen and Aegineta. Pliny, Book 12, Chapter 12: Cretan Nard, which some call wild, others call "Phu" with leaves like Italian parsley, etc. And Book 21, Chapter 20: Nard, which we have called "Phu," is known as Valerian in the apothecaries and herbals, named for the many "valencies" or powers by which it is strong <note>The name Valeriana is derived from the Latin "valere," meaning to be strong or healthy</note>; for which reason it is also called Theriacaria <note>A "theriac" was a universal medicine or antidote against poison</note>. Dioscorides seems to have proposed two kinds in a single description: for the Italian parsley leaves belong to the greater Valerian, the deer-fodder leaves to the middle Valerian; the roots to the former, and the color of the flower to the middle kind.*

Page 164, column 1, line 3. *Foreign purple or white Valerian, C.B.P. Indian.* This is not a Valerian properly speaking, since its seed is not equipped with a *pappus* <term>pappus: a feathery or hairy attachment on a seed, like that of a dandelion, which allows it to fly</term> on its upper part. It bears a seed that is somewhat soft like cartilage and spongy, oblong, compressed, and narrow. It is always wrapped in two spongy and softer oblong coverings, as if it were a wedge driven into a small socket between two coverings that firmly embrace the base of the seed. It has an elegantly red flower, which is, so to speak, hooded <term>galeatum: shaped like a helmet or hood</term> on the outside and top, and formed below into a long, thin tube. This distinguishes it from the flowers of the other "little valerians" <note>Valerianella</note> listed below. Because of this double distinction—the different shape of the seeds and the varying structure of the flower—it is more fittingly separated by us from the Valerians and will be sent to the following class of *Valerianella*.

Page 264, column 2, line 7. *Greek Valerian, blue and white, C.B.P.* blue and white flower, *Garden of Eichstätt*. This is not a Valerian by its seed capsule, nor its flowers, and much less by its seeds. However, because of an analogy or similarity in its lobed or pinnate leaves <term>pinnate: leaves arranged like the feathers of a wing on either side of a stem</term> with

<column-break/>

wild Valerian, we have placed it here. Yet in its other parts, it has nothing in common or related to Valerian. Nevertheless, because of the aforementioned similarity in the leaves alone, we have placed it here in the "Table of relationships and affinities" immediately after Sea Valerian (which is a true Valerian, being gifted with a *pappus* on the seeds). See the "improperly named umbels" in Chapter 1 of the previous treatise.

Page [blank], column 1, lines 17 & 18. *Red narrow-leaved Valerian, C.B.P.* Although this Valerian comes very close to the true Valerians in its essential parts—the flowers and the seed—it has very little relationship to them regarding the leaves. At first glance, the leaves agree closely with *ocymastrum* <note>a type of wild campion</note> or bladder campion. It has, I say, the flowers of a true Valerian and seeds entirely matching them. In the flowers, however, this difference is observed: each one is gifted with a spur <term>calcar: a slender, hollow projection at the base of a flower petal</term> at the bottom. This observation (having been omitted by Caspar Bauhin and other authors until now) has led us to place all the *Valerianellas* by themselves in the following chapter of "improperly named umbels."

---

# CHAPTER II.
## IMPROPERLY NAMED UMBELS.
### Second sub-genus.
## V A L E R I A N E L L A.

All the VALERIANELLAS agree with the Valerians mentioned above in this: they produce flowers divided at the edge into five segments, or five-petaled, with the exception of the one hooded kind mentioned in the previous chapter. But they are all very different in their seeds, as can be seen in the General Table of seed illustrations, numbers 47 through 61. Consult also the 7th Table of relationship and affinity, and its explanation above. One may see the different illustrations of the seeds of 10 different *Valerianellas* in the General Table of seed illustrations at the place cited.

Page 165, column 1, line 19. *Greater odorless field Valerian, C.B.P.* We observe that Caspar Bauhin erred here when he hesitantly asserted that the greater odorless field Valerian (or Lamb's Lettuce <note>original: "lactucam agninam"</note> of Gerard and Tabernaemontanus) was the same as the "other Valerianella with a naked, umbilicated, and short seed" <note>original: "valerianella altera nudo umbilicato, & brevi semine"</note> described by Fabio Columna. I marvel at Caspar Bauhin’s negligence, since field Valerian is well enough

<column-break/>

known to any villager, peasant, or townsperson who practices a manual craft; its seed is scattered everywhere in vegetable gardens by Nature herself, who produces it anew every year. Furthermore, Fabio Columna drew the umbilicated <term>umbilicatum: having a central depression or "navel" on the seed</term>, naked, short seed of the other variety with his own hand. Therefore, no room for doubt should have remained for Caspar Bauhin. From this, it must be concluded that either he never saw the seed of either one, or at least (if he did see them) he did not observe them well, nor did he read Fabio Columna correctly or attentively. We have shown the perfect and distinct variety of all the seeds of these umbels in the explanation of Chapter 2 on improperly named umbels in our "New Teaching," and we have presented seeds engraved from personal observation <note>original: "αὐτοψίᾳ"</note> in the General Table cited above. Compare those different seeds themselves, and you will very easily perceive this distinction of ours of all these species. Consult the 7th Table of relationship and affinity, and the unwritten umbels near the end of the preceding treatise.

The author highlights the importance of personal observation and accurate illustration in botany, correcting errors regarding Lamb's Lettuce and Greek Valerian.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic critique of Caspar Bauhin’s botanical classifications. Having previously discussed true umbelliferous plants (plants like carrot or parsley with umbrella-like flower clusters), the author now moves to plants that Bauhin incorrectly grouped with them or placed near them based on superficial similarities, specifically focusing on leaf shape and root structure rather than the essential nature of the flowers and seeds.</meta>

<header>THE ERRORS OF CASPAR BAUHIN</header>

<columns>2</columns>

# CHAPTER III.

## UMBELS IMPROPERLY SO-CALLED.

### Third subordinate Genus.

## SALAD BURNET.
<note>original: "PIMPINELLA SANGUISORBA"</note>

**PIMPINELLA** and *Bipinella* were named by more recent authors after the two rows of leaves arranged like wings or feathers <note>original: "pinnatim, seu plumatim"</note>; they are thought to have been unnamed by the ancients, though they are also called *Bipennula* and *Pimpinella*. This plant is of two kinds: the Burnet-saxifrage <note>original: "pimpinella saxifraga"</note>, or stone-breaker, so named for its ability to expel stones from the kidneys. This *pimpinella* (which we mentioned above) we placed among the umbels with smaller striated seeds and lobed leaves in Chapter 2 of the previous treatise. Consult Table 2 of its relationships and affinities there, along with its explanation, and Table 2 of its affinities and its catalog. The other is Salad burnet <note>original: "pimpinella sanguisorba"</note>, or *sorbastrella*, so called because it absorbs or stops bloody fluxes. It is also called the balsam-scented plant <note>original: "myripne"</note> by Myrepsus, and 

<column-break/>

by some is considered the 2nd *sideritis* <note>A medicinal herb used for treating wounds</note> of Dioscorides, such as by Fabio Columna. We call this the spiked pimpinella <note>original: "pimpinella spicata"</note> to distinguish it from the umbel-bearing *pimpinella* called saxifrage from its effect, which we treated previously in the cited place. There, we observed that Caspar Bauhin wrongly joined Salad burnet with Burnet-saxifrage. We ourselves have separated them and, more appropriately, have recalled Salad burnet here at the end of the umbels among those "improperly so-called" because of the similarity of the lobed leaves attached to the stems in a winged fashion—namely, in the rite and manner of the Burnet-saxifrage, or what we call the umbel-bearing type.

---

# CHAPTER IV.

## UMBELS IMPROPERLY SO-CALLED.

### Fourth subordinate Genus.

## DROPWORT.
<note>original: "FILIPENDULA"</note>

<margin>Page 163. column ii. plants vii & viii. common dropwort <note>original: "filipendula vulgaris"</note> in Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*, and the smaller in his *Prodromos*.</margin>
These two are wrongly placed here by Caspar Bauhin among the species of *Oenanthe* <note>Water-dropworts</note>, which are true and genuine umbels. Dropwort is not an umbel properly so-called, and no more shares the tuberous nature of the root than the *Oenanthe* of Myconus—an error for which we rebuked C. Bauhin above in Chapter 2 concerning *Oenanthe*. Dropwort <note>original: "Filipendula"</note> earned its name from the olive-shaped kernels, some longer and some more swollen, which (in the manner of an Asphodel) are 

<column-break/>

reddish-black on the outside and whitening on the inside, hanging and connected by the filaments of the fibers <note>The name literally means "hanging by a thread" (*filum* and *pendulus*)</note>. We have arranged it here among the umbels improperly so-called because of some similarity in the arrangement of the leaves with true umbels, but we have separated it from the umbel-bearing *Oenanthe* for the reasons brought forward above in Chapter 2 and here.

---

# CHAPTER V.

## UMBELS IMPROPERLY SO-CALLED.

### Fifth subordinate Genus.

## GOAT’S BEARD.
<note>original: "BARBA CAPRÆ"; likely Aruncus dioicus</note>

<margin>Pages 163 & 164.</margin>
*Goat’s beard obtained its name from the shape of the flowers, which in some way resembles a goat's beard. The first or larger kind is considered the Melandryon of Pliny (Book 26, Chapter 7), whose description is as follows: Melandryon growing in cornfields and meadows with a white, fragrant flower. Another kind from the same author (Book 24, Chapter 19) is called Rhodora; he says this herb, which the Gauls call Rhodora, has a jointed stem like a fig branch; the leaves are like a nettle, whitish in the middle, and as time passes, they become entirely red: it bears a silver-colored flower.*

The leaves of Goat’s beard are oblong, pointed like a chestnut leaf, and serrated; many are attached to the middle rib with no small leaves coming between them, unlike in Meadowsweet <note>original: "ulmaria"</note>. Furthermore, the flowers of this plant differ greatly from Meadowsweet (specifically in the petals) as they are arranged in clusters <note>original: "racematim"</note> and are very tiny and white. However, the leaves of Meadowsweet shine with a broad greenness; indeed, seven are connected to the spine or middle rib (with many smaller ones coming between them), serrated and somewhat wavy, folded with a curled texture, and approaching the appearance of elm leaves. The stems are burdened with compact, 

<column-break/>

sweet-smelling flowers, appearing everywhere in damp meadows (whence it is called Queen of the Meadow because of its sweet scent). It has the form of common dropwort, but of a whitish or sulfurous color, which is aptly enough called *erodaxos* <note>a Greek botanical term used by Clusius</note> by Charles de l'Écluse. It should have been placed after Valerian in the *Pinax* <note>Caspar Bauhin’s 1623 botanical index</note>, because it possesses less affinity with true umbels than Valerian does, as we have clearly enough shown in the chapter on Valerian above.

<summary>This page critiques Caspar Bauhin’s classification of Salad Burnet, Dropwort, and Goat's Beard, arguing they are not true umbel-bearing plants. The author reclassifies them based on detailed observations of their roots, flowers, and leaf arrangements, correcting Bauhin's tendency to group plants by superficial similarities.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the author's critique of botanical classifications, specifically focusing on Meadow-rue (Thalictrum). It follows the previous discussion on plants that resemble umbellifers (like carrots or parsley) in their foliage but differ in their reproductive structures. The author justifies moving this genus away from Caspar Bauhin’s original placement near Rue, concluding the primary text before providing a list of errata for the entire work.</meta>

<header>BAUHIN’S [ERRORS], and those of other Authors.</header>

# CHAPTER VI.

## UMBELS IMPROPERLY SO-CALLED.

#### Sixth Subordinate Genus.

## MEADOW-RUE.
<note>original: "THALICTRUM"</note>

*Page 336. Meadow-rue <note>original Greek: "Θάλικτρον"</note> has leaves like coriander, but thicker: it produces a small stem of the thickness of rue, growing in branches, on which leaves are arranged in various or multiple ways by lobes. According to Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> in Book 4, Chapter 98. It is perhaps so named from the word "to flourish" <note>original Greek: "θάλλειν," meaning to bloom or be green</note>, because when it pushes out its buds, it flourishes elegantly. Some suspect this is the "Phellandryum" mentioned by Pliny.*

We have brought Meadow-rue <note>original: "Thalictrum"</note> here to the end of the "umbels properly so-called" because of its leaves, which are divided into many parts in the manner of many umbels. Just as we have better and more appropriately arranged the other plants mentioned above—Salad burnet, Dropwort, and Goat’s-beard—than Caspar Bauhin did, who placed them after Rue. Rue is a plant with a four-part seed pod <note>original: "quadricapsularis"</note>, gifted with a yellow four-petaled flower and adorned with many small stamens or tufts in the center; consequently, it has four capsules following each flower which contain tiny seeds, far different from Meadow-rue. Indeed, the seeds of Meadow-rue are all individual, resting on individual stalks or small rays, and are stri-

<column-break/>

-ated (except for the Canadian variety described by Jacques Cornut <note>A French physician and botanist who described North American plants</note>, which is gifted with a three-sided or triangular capsule or pod). They are angular, or provided with ridges and furrows like the seeds of the umbel-bearing plants; however, the flowers are mossy, consisting of several downy tufts, by which they differ from the true umbels. In their leaves (as said above), all species of Meadow-rue resemble the many umbels, for the leaves are arranged in three parts and are thrice-divided, resting on their own little stalks. Because of this similarity to the umbel-bearers, we have placed Meadow-rue at the conclusion of the "umbels properly so-called," among those "improperly so-called."

TABLE I.

# LIST OF UMBELLIFERS IN TABLE I.
->A general list of those plants contained herein, which are provided with a ribbed seed enclosed in a spongy substance, organized by kinship and affinity.<-

### First Genus.

Cachrys <note>A genus of the carrot family</note> with a smooth spongy seed and Giant Fennel-like leaves. **English:** *Frankincense with a spongy smooth seed and fennel-like leaves.* **French:** True Cachrys, or Galen’s Libanotis <note>An ancient name for several medicinal plants</note> with a smooth, spongy seed and leaves cut like Fennel or Mattioli’s Giant Fennel.

Cachrys with a furrowed, rough, spongy seed, and somewhat broad Hog’s Fennel-like leaves <note>original: "foliis Peucedani"</note>. **English:** *Frankincense with a furrowed rough seed and somewhat broad Sow-fennel leaves.* **French:** Cachrys, or Libanotis, with a furrowed and rough spongy seed and a broader Hog’s Fennel leaf.

<column-break/>

Cachrys with a flat, furrowed, spongy seed, and thinner Hog’s Fennel-like leaves. **English:** *Frankincense with a spongy, furrowed, smooth seed, with somewhat narrow Sow-fennel leaves.* **French:** Cachrys, or Libanotis, with a furrowed and smooth spongy seed and a narrower or smaller Hog’s Fennel leaf.

See the General Table above on the page opposite the preceding one.

<summary>This page presents a detailed botanical index of umbelliferous plants, categorized by seed texture (spongy or ribbed) and leaf shape (fennel-like or lobed). It provides scientific Latin names alongside their common English and French equivalents, including medicinal and edible herbs like Angelica, Fennel, and Skirret.</summary>

## Subordinate Genus II: Umbellifers of Ribbed Kinship or Affinity, Table 2.

->**Top Register: Umbellifers with ribbed seeds and fennel-like leaves** <note>original: "Umbellæ Sem: striato fœniculaceæ". These plants are characterized by fine, needle-like foliage similar to fennel and seeds marked with distinct longitudinal ridges.</note><-

**Group 1: Fennel-like leaves with smaller ribbed seeds**
*   **Spurious Spignel** <note>original: "meum spurium"</note>
*   **Earthnut** <note>original: "bulbocastanum"; also known as the Great Pignut.</note>
*   **Clusius’s dwarf mountain celery** <note>original: "Selinum montanum pumilum clus."; named after the Flemish botanist Carolus Clusius.</note>
*   **Toothpick-weed or Gingidium** <note>original: "visnaga S. gingidium". "S." is an abbreviation for "seu," meaning "or."</note>
*   **Clusius’s Pannonian saxifrage** <note>original: "Saxifraga pannonica clus."</note>

**Group 2: Fennel-like leaves with larger ribbed seeds**
*   **Fennel** <note>original: "fœniculum"</note>
    *   Common Fennel
    *   Sweet Fennel
*   **Cumin**
*   **Athamantic Spignel** <note>original: "meum athamanticum"; a highly aromatic perennial herb.</note>

---

->**Middle Register: Larger lobed-leaf Umbellifers with larger ribbed seeds** <note>original: "Umbellæ lobatæ majores. Sem. Striato majore". These plants have broader, divided leaves (lobes) rather than the fine threads of the fennel types.</note><-

*   **Masterwort** <note>original: "imperatoria"</note>
    *   Great Masterwort
    *   Lesser Masterwort
*   **Great Astrantia** <note>original: "astrantia majus"; often called Great Masterwort in English as well.</note>
*   **Small Alexanders** <note>original: "Smyrnium minus"</note>
*   **Lovage** <note>original: "levisticum majus"</note>
*   **Mountain Laserwort** <note>original: "Siler montanum"</note>
*   **Columbine-leaved Laserwort** <note>original: "Siler aquilegiæ folijs"</note>
    *   Small variety
*   **Angelica**
    *   Great Angelica
    *   Small Angelica
    *   **Cultivated Angelica** <note>original: "Sativa"</note>
    *   **Wild Angelica** <note>original: "Sylvestris"</note>
        *   Greatest mountain wild angelica <note>original: "maxima montana C.B.P."; "C.B.P." refers to Caspar Bauhin’s botanical catalog, the *Pinax*.</note>
        *   Shining Canadian angelica <note>original: "lucida canadensis corn."; "corn." refers to Jacques-Philippe Cornut, who described North American plants.</note>
    *   Lesser wild or "wandering" angelica <note>original: "Sylvestris minor S. erratica B.P."</note>

---

->**Bottom Register: Smaller lobed-leaf Umbellifers with smaller ribbed seeds** <note>original: "Umbellæ lobatæ minores. Sem. striato minore"</note><-

*   **Water-parsnip** <note>original: "Sium"</note>
    *   Land or Corn Water-parsnip <note>original: "terrestre S. Segetale"</note>
    *   Broad-leaved Water-parsnip
    *   Narrow-leaved Water-parsnip
    *   Flowering at the leaf-axils <note>original: "ad alas floridum"</note>
    *   Aquatic Water-parsnip
    *   With wrinkled, three-parted, and toothed leaves
    *   Dwarf variety with fennel-like leaves
*   **Skirret** <term>Skirret: a root vegetable (Sium sisarum) related to the carrot, widely cultivated in Europe before the potato.</term>
*   **Burnet-saxifrage** <note>original: "pimpinella Saxifraga"</note>
    *   Great
    *   Great degenerate <note>original: "major degener"; likely referring to a common variant or wild type.</note>
    *   Small
    *   Small curled-leaf <note>original: "minor crispa"</note>
*   **Honewort** <note>original: "Sison"</note>

# TABLE III.

## LIST OF TABLE III.
->Of kinship and affinity.<-

### UMBELLIFERS with larger ribbed seeds and leaves divided in many directions.

**Ethiopian Seseli, a shrub.** *English: Woody Hart-wort.* French: Woody Ethiopian Seseli. <note>original French: "Seseli d’Æthiopie ligneux"</note>
**Meadow Seseli.** *English: Meadow Hart-wort.* French: Meadow Seseli. <note>original French: "Seseli des prez"</note>
**Mountain Seseli, or Clusius’s First Hungarian Seseli.** *English: Mountain, or Hungarian Hart-wort.* French: Mountain or Hungarian Seseli of de l'Écluse. <note>Charles de l'Écluse, or Clusius, was a pioneering 16th-century botanist.</note>
**Broad-leaved, very foul-smelling Hemlock.** *English: Broad-leaved stinking Hemlock.* French: Stinking broad-leaved Hemlock.
**Greater Hemlock.** *English: Great common Hemlock.* French: Common Hemlock.
**Lesser Hemlock.** *English: Foolish Hemlock, or counterfeit Parsley.* French: Lesser Hemlock.
**Narrow-leaved Marsh Hemlock.** *English: Small water Hemlock.* French: Water Hemlock, or Pliny’s Phellandryon. <note>Phellandryon is an ancient Greek botanical name used by Pliny the Elder.</note>
**Largest Water-Dropwort with a poisonous smell.** *English: Water Drop-wort, or Oenanthe of a high virulent smell.* French: Poisonous and very large Oenanthe, resembling Hemlock.
**Parsley-leaved Water-Dropwort.** *English: Dropwort with Parsley leaves.* French: Oenanthe with Parsley leaves.
**Narrow-leaved Water-Dropwort.** *English: Dropwort with long narrow leaves.* French: Oenanthe with narrow and long leaves.
**Water-Dropwort with water-yarrow leaves.** *English: Dropwort with leaves like water Yarrow.* French: Oenanthe with leaves like water Yarrow.
**Sea Samphire.** *English: Samphire, or sea Purslane, or rather sea Parsley.* French: Sea Fennel, Crithmon, Bacille, and Pliny’s Batis.
**Prickly Samphire.** *English: Prickly Samphire, or sea Parsley rather than sea Parsnip.* French: Sea Fennel, or prickly Crithmon; according to some, Sea Parsnip.

### UMBELLIFERS with smaller ribbed seeds and leaves divided in many directions.

**Smooth garden Parsley.** *English: Plain garden Parsley.* French: Smooth garden Parsley.
**Curled garden Parsley.** *English: Curled Parsley.* French: Curled garden Parsley.
**Garden or mountain narrow-leaved Parsley.** *English: Mountain Parsley with narrow leaves.* French: Mountain Parsley with narrow leaves.
**Degenerate broad-leaved Parsley with cut leaves.** *English: Garden Parsley with cut leaves.* French: Large garden Parsley with cut leaves.

<column-break/>

**Largest Garden Parsley.** *English: Greatest Garden Parsley.* French: The largest Garden Parsley.
**Yellow-umbelled Parsley.** *English: Yellow tufted Parsley.* French: Parsley with yellow flowers.
**Italian Marsh Parsley, or Celery.** *English: Italian Smallage, or Celery.* French: Italian Marsh Parsley, or Garden Celery.
**Our Marsh Parsley.** *English: Smallage, or water or marsh Parsley.* French: Marsh Parsley, or common Smallage.
**Macedonian Parsley with white hoary seed.** *English: Macedonian Parsley with white hoary seed.* French: Macedonian Parsley with white and hairy seed.
**Parsley with dark hairy seed; Clusius’s first foreign Parsley.** *English: Strange Parsley with winged leaves and brown hoary seed.* French: Foreign Parsley, Clusius’s first, with brown and hairy seed.
**Common annual Bishopsweed.** *English: Bishopsweed.* French: Ammi of Dalechamps, according to some. <note>Jacques Daléchamps was a 16th-century physician and botanist.</note>
**Perennial Bishopsweed.** Our designation. <note>original: "Nobis." This indicates the author is proposing this specific name or classification.</note> Fuchs’s Ammi in the *History of Lyon*. *English: Long Lasting, or perennial Bishopsweed.* French: Perennial Ammi, or Fuchs’s Ammi.
**Caraway.** *English: Caraways.* French: Matthioli’s Caraway.
**Anise.** *English: Anise.* French: Anise.
**Pellitory of Spain.** *English: Pellitory of Spain.* French: True Pellitory.

### UMBELLIFERS with ribbed seeds and leaves displaying some unique feature.

**Perennial broad-leaved Hare's-ear.** *English: Large Hare's-ear Perennial.* French: Perennial Bupleuron with broad leaves.
**Perennial narrow-leaved Hare's-ear.** *English: Narrow, perennial Hare's-ear.* French: Perennial Bupleuron with narrow leaves.
**Smallest annual Hare's-ear.** According to Columna. *English: Lesser Hare's-ear annual.* French: Colonna’s small annual Bupleuron. <note>Fabio Colonna (Columna) was an influential Italian botanist.</note>
**Great narrow-leaved Alpine perennial Thorough-wax.** *English: Great narrow leaved Thorough-wax perennial.* French: Great perennial and narrow-leaved Alpine Thorough-wax. <note>Thorough-wax (Perfoliata) is named for the way the stem appears to grow "through" the leaves.</note>
**Most common annual Thorough-wax.** *English: Common great Thorough-wax annual.* French: Large annual Thorough-wax.
**Smallest annual Thorough-wax with Hare's-ear leaves.** According to Columna. *English: The least Thorough-wax annual with leaves of Bupleuron.* French: Very small annual Thorough-wax, with Bupleuron leaves.

# SUBORDINATE GENUS II.
## RIBBED-SEED UMBELLIFERS
->Table 3: Of Kinship or Affinity<-

### I. Umbellifers with leaves divided in many directions; with larger ribbed seeds.

**Water-Dropwort** <note>original: "œnanthe". These plants often grow in wet areas and have tuberous roots.</note>
- Largest Water-Dropwort with a poisonous juice and the appearance of Hemlock, according to Lobel. <note>Matthias de l'Obel was a 16th-century Flemish physician and botanist.</note>
- Parsley-leaved Water-Dropwort.
- Narrow-leaved Water-Dropwort.

**Samphire** <note>original: "crithmū". Famous for growing on sea cliffs; often pickled.</note>
- Samphire with Water-Yarrow leaves.
- Sea Samphire, according to Dodoens. <note>Rembert Dodoens was a major 16th-century herbalist.</note>
- Prickly Samphire, according to Dodoens.

<column-break/>

**Seseli** <note>original: "seseli". Also known as Hart-wort; used historically in medicinal tonics.</note>
- Ethiopian Shrubby Seseli. <note>original: "frutex; æthiopicum"</note>
- Meadow Seseli.
- Mountain Seseli, or the first Hungarian variety of Clusius. <note>Charles de l'Écluse, or Clusius, was a pioneering botanist who described many Eastern European plants.</note>

**Hemlock** <note>original: "cicuta". A genus containing some of the most famously toxic plants in Europe.</note>
- Broad-leaved, very foul-smelling Hemlock.
- Greater Hemlock.
- Lesser Hemlock, or Fool’s Parsley. <note>original: "minor S. fatua". This plant is often mistaken for edible parsley, hence the name "fool's parsley."</note>
- Narrow-leaved Marsh Hemlock, from Bauhin's *Pinax*. <note>Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax* (1623) was the standard botanical reference of the era.</note>

***

### II. Umbellifers with leaves divided in many directions; with smaller ribbed seeds.

**Bishop's Weed** <note>original: "ammi". Traditionally used for digestive issues.</note>
- Common annual Bishop's Weed, according to Dodoens.
- Perennial Bishop's Weed of certain authors, from Dalechamps’s *History of Lyon*. <note>Jacques Daléchamps was a 16th-century doctor and botanist.</note>

**Caraway** <note>original: "Carum".</note>
**Anise** <note>original: "anisum".</note>
**Pellitory** <note>original: "pyrethrū". Known as Pellitory of Spain, often used to soothe toothaches.</note>

<column-break/>

**Parsley or Celery** <note>original: "apium". This category includes both garden herbs and wild marsh varieties.</note>
- **Garden or Cultivated:**
    - Flat-leaved.
    - Curled.
    - Narrow-leaved.
    - Degenerate with long leaves.
    - Greatest variety, from Bauhin's *Prodromus*.
- **Marsh:**
    - With a yellow flower cluster.
    - Italian, or Scelleri's. <note>Likely referring to "Celery" (Seleri).</note>
    - Our native kind.
- **With hairy and hoary seeds:**
    - Macedonian, from Bauhin's *Pinax*.
    - Foreign, according to Clusius.

***

### III. Umbellifers with leaves of a peculiar shape; with ribbed seeds.

**Thorough-wax** <note>original: "perfoliata". So named because the stem appears to pass through the center of the leaf.</note>
- **Perennial:**
    - Great narrow-leaved Alpine variety.
- **Annual:**
    - The most common variety.
    - Smallest variety with Hare’s Ear leaves.

<column-break/>

**Hare's Ear** <note>original: "Bupleurum". Named for the shape of its leaves.</note>
- **Perennial:**
    - Broad-leaved.
    - Narrow-leaved.
- **Smallest annual**, according to Fabio Colonna. <note>Fabio Colonna was a 17th-century Italian botanist who helped pioneer botanical illustration.</note>
