# INDEX OF TABLE 4.
## Of kinship and affinity.

---

### UMBELLIFERS with seed surrounded by leafy wings.
->Genus 3.<-

**Broad-leaved Laserwort** <note>original: "Laserpitium". A genus of plants in the carrot family, historically valued for their medicinal resins.</note>, Ethiopian Seseli, herb, according to Dodoens. Commonly called Libanotis by authors.
*English: Broad leaved Laserwort with plaine seed-filmes.*
*French: Laserwort named Ethiopian Seseli, herb, by Dodoens, and Libanotis by the majority of authors.*

**Broad-leaved Laserwort**, similar to Libanotis, with curled seed, or Bitter Costus <note>original: "Costus amara". Costus was a name often applied to various aromatic roots.</note>, according to Jean Bauhin. 
*English: Broad leaved Laserwort with curled rough seed-filmes.* 
*French: Broad-leaved Laserwort, having wavy or curled and rough seeds.*

**Laserwort with narrower, dark green, and shining leaves**, with curled seed. 
*English: Laserwort with narrow sad-green shining leaves and curled seed-filmes.* 
*French: Laserwort, having narrow leaves of a dark green and shining color, and with curled grain.*

**Laserwort with narrower, light green leaves**, divided in many ways. 
*English: Laserwort with narrow-cut light-green leaves.* 
*French: Laserwort with narrow, cut leaves of a washed-green color.*

**Laserwort with longer, light green leaves**, arranged in pairs. 
*English: Laserwort with long light-green leaves, set opposed.* 
*French: Laserwort with long narrow leaves of a washed-green color, directly opposite one another.*

### UMBELLIFERS with seed surrounded by a leafy wing.
->Genus 4.<-

**Broadest-leaved Thapsia** <note>original: "Thapsia". Also known as "deadly carrot," these plants were known for their skin-irritating properties.</note>, from Bauhin’s *Pinax* and the third variety of Clusius. 
*English: Thapsia, or scorching fennel Giant.* 
*French: Large Thapsia.*

**Hairy broad-leaved Thapsia**, from Bauhin’s *Pinax* and the first variety of Clusius. 
*English: Thapsia, or scorching Fennell with large hoarie leaves.* 
*French: Broad-leaved hairy Thapsia.*

**Lesser Thapsia with the appearance of a carrot**, according to Lobel. 
*English: Little scorching Fennell with the leaves like Carrots.* 
*French: Small Thapsia with carrot-like leaves.*

### UMBELLIFERS with hairy or shaggy seeds.
->Genus 5.<-

**Fine-leaved Parsnip** <note>original: "Pastinaca". In this period, "Pastinaca" was used for both carrots and parsnips; the English "A." translation here identifies it as a carrot.</note>, cultivated, white. 
*English: Carrott with a white root.* 
*French: Common carrot with a white root.*

<column-break/>

**Fine-leaved Parsnip**, cultivated, yellow. 
*English: Carrott with a yellow root.* 
*French: Common carrot with a yellow root.*

**Fine-leaved Parsnip**, cultivated, red. 
*English: Common Carrott with a red root.* 
*French: Common carrot with a red root.*

**Fine-leaved Wild Parsnip**, the genuine variety of Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical authority for 1,500 years.</note>. 
*English: Wilde Carrott.* 
*French: Wild carrot, or the true one of Dioscorides.*

**Fine-leaved Wild Parsnip**, with shining leaves. 
*English: Wilde Carrott with shining leaves.* 
*French: Carrot with shining leaves.*

**Fine-leaved Wild Parsnip**, with a yellow root and flower cluster. 
*English: Carott with a yellow root, & umbell.* 
*French: Wild carrot with a yellow root and flower cluster.*

**Fine-leaved Cretan Parsnip**, with the stalks of the flower cluster longer than those of Gingidium <note>Gingidium refers to a type of wild chervil or toothpick weed.</note>. 
*English: Carott with the stemmes of the umbell longer then those of Gingidium.* 
*French: Carrot with the rays of the flower cluster longer than those of Gingidium.*

**Cretan Parsnip with spherical heads**. Our name. Wild Cumin, according to Tabernaemontanus. 
*English: Carrott of Candie with winged leaves & round tufts in it's umbell.* 
*French: Carrot of Crete or Candie with winged leaves, and having the heads of the flower clusters round, in the manner of balls.*

### UMBELLIFERS with prickly or thorny seeds.
->Genus 6.<-

**Greater Bur-parsley** <note>original: "Caucalis". These plants are known for their "burred" or hooked seeds that catch on clothing.</note> with broad leaves, purple and white. 
*English: Great bastard Carrott with large leaves and red, or white flowers.* 
*French: Large Bur-parsley with broad leaves, and with red or white flowers.*

**Greater fine-leaved Bur-parsley**, from Montpellier, with large fruit, according to Bauhin’s *Pinax*. 
*English: Bastard Carott with narrow leaves & great thicke seed.* 
*French: The large Bur-parsley with cut leaves and large seeds.*

**Greater fine-leaved Bur-parsley**, prickly, with a large flower, according to Clusius. 
*English: Bastard Carott with narrow leaves and a bigg white flower.* 
*French: The large Bur-parsley with cut leaves, with the large white flower.*

**Lesser field Bur-parsley**, with small flowers and fruit, according to Bauhin’s *Pinax*. 
*English: The lesser feild Caucalis, or bastard Carrott with little flowers, & seeds.* 
*French: The small field Bur-parsley, with small flowers and seeds.*

**Knotted Bur-parsley**, with prickly seeds, according to Bauhin’s *Pinax*. 
*English: Lesser bastard Carott with lesser seed, growing together in knotts.* 
*French: Bur-parsley, with many bristly seeds joined together in the manner of a cluster.*

**Carrot-like Bur-parsley of Tangier**. 
*English: Bastard Carott of Tangier with leaves of the true carott.* 
*French: Bur-parsley of Tangier, with carrot leaves.*

*This was smaller when I first saw it, but now in this year that I write these things, it has come forth much larger and grander in every way in the garden of Edward Morgan <note>Edward Morgan (c. 1610–1689) was a famous Welsh botanist who maintained a celebrated garden at Westminster; he was a key source for many 17th-century herbalists.</note> behind the place called Westminster Monastery.*

<header>Umbellifers with seeds surrounded by leafy wings, etc. Kinship or Affinity, Table 4.</header>

### Top Section: Umbellifers with seeds surrounded by leafy wings.

*   **Umbellifer with seed surrounded by leafy wings.** <note>original: "Umbella semine alis foliaceis cincto". These are plants where the seed has a flat, papery border that helps it catch the wind.</note>
    *   **Deadly Carrot** <note>original: "Thapsia". Despite the name, these were often used in ancient medicine, though they can be quite toxic.</note> **with seed surrounded by a leafy wing.** Subordinate Genus IV.
        *   Largest with very broad leaf. <note>original: "maxima latissimo folio". Cited from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax* (B.P.) and Clusius.</note>
        *   Broad-leaved and hairy. <note>original: "latifolia villosa". As described by Clusius.</note>
        *   Smaller, with the appearance of a carrot. <note>original: "minor carotæ effigie". As described by Lobelius.</note>
    *   **Laserwort** <note>original: "Laserpitium". A genus named for 'laser,' a resinous juice once highly prized as a spice and medicine.</note> **with seed surrounded by leafy wings.** Subordinate Genus III.
        *   With broader lobes:
            *   Ethiopian Seseli, herb. <note>original: "Seseli æthiopicum herba". As described by Dodoens.</note>
            *   Similar to Frankincense-herb <note>original: "libanotidi simile". "Libanotis" refers to plants with an incense-like aroma.</note> with curled seed. <note>original: "sem. crispo". Cited from Jean Bauhin.</note>
        *   With narrower lobes:
            *   Deeply green and shining, with curled seed.
            *   Pale green, divided in many directions.
            *   Pale green, arranged in pairs.

---

### Bottom Section: Umbellifers with hairy or shaggy seeds; and seeds equipped with prickles or thorns.

*   **Umbellifer with hairy or shaggy seed; and seed equipped with prickles or thorns.** <note>original: "Umbella semine villoso s. hispido; & sem Echinato, s. aculeato donata". This group includes plants like the wild carrot and bur-parsley, which use hooks or hairs to cling to animal fur.</note>
    *   **Bur-parsley** <note>original: "Caucalis". Known for its "burs" or prickly seeds.</note> **with prickly or thorny seed.** Genus VI.
        *   Larger:
            *   Broad-leaved <note>original: "platyphyllon". From the Greek for "wide leaf."</note>:
                *   Purple. <note>original: "purpurea". Described by Fabio Columna.</note>
                *   White.
            *   Narrow-leaved <note>original: "leptophyllon". From the Greek for "fine leaf."</note>:
                *   Montpellier variety, with large fruit.
                *   Prickly, with a large flower. <note>original: "Echinata, magno flore". Described by Clusius.</note>
            *   Carrot-like variety from Tangier. <note>original: "Daucoides Tingitana".</note>
        *   Smaller:
            *   Field variety with small flower and fruit.
            *   The knotted variety with prickly seed.
    *   **Narrow-leaved Parsnip, Daucus, or Carrot with hairy or shaggy seed.** Genus V.
        *   Cultivated:
            *   White.
            *   Yellow.
            *   Red.
        *   Wild:
            *   The genuine variety of Dioscorides. <note>original: "Genuina dioscor." Referring to the description in the famous 1st-century herbal of Dioscorides.</note>
            *   Shining.
            *   With a yellow root and flower-cluster.
        *   Cretan:
            *   Flower-cluster with rays longer than the Toothpick-chervil. <note>original: "gingidii". Referring to *Visnaga daucoides*.</note>
            *   With round heads. <note>original: "Capitulis Globosis". Identified as Wild Cumin by Tabernaemontanus.</note>

<meta>This page continues the botanical index of the Umbelliferae family, moving from Table IV to Table V. While the previous page focused on plants with "leafy wings" on their seeds, this section organizes plants that have "membranous" (thin, papery) seeds that are flattened and somewhat round. The author continues to rely on the classification systems of Renaissance botanists like Mattioli, Dodoens, Lobelius, and Caspar Bauhin.</meta>

# INDEX OF TABLE V.
## Of kinship and affinity.

### UMBELLIFERS <term>Umbellifer: A plant characterized by flower clusters that spring from a single point, forming a flat or rounded "umbrella" shape, such as carrots, fennel, or parsley.</term> with membranous <note>original: "membranaceo," referring to a thin, papery texture</note>, somewhat round, flattened seeds, and provided with fennel-like leaves.
->Genus 7.<-

Mattioli’s Giant Fennel, <note>referenced from Dodoens</note>. **English:** Giant Fennel. **French:** Mattioli’s Ferula. <note>original: "Ferule de Matthiol"</note>
Galbanum-bearing Giant Fennel, <note>referenced from Lobelius</note>. **English:** Giant Fennel bearing Galbanum <note>Galbanum is a fragrant gum resin used in incense and medicine.</note>. **French:** Ferula bearing Galbanum.
Foreign All-heal, or Panax, <note>referenced from Dodoens</note>. **English:** Woundwort, or All-heal. **French:** Mattioli’s Asclepian All-heal.
Dill. **English:** Dill. **French:** Dill. <note>original: "Anet"</note>
Greater Italian Hog’s-fennel, <note>referenced from Lobelius</note>. **English:** Sow-fennel, or Sulphurwort. **French:** Great Italian Peucedanum.
Lesser German Hog’s-fennel. **English:** Lesser Sow-fennel, or Sulphurwort of Germany. **French:** Small Peucedanum, or German Hog’s Fennel.
Lesser Hog’s-fennel, with leaves arranged in pairs. **English:** Sow-fennel, or Sulphurwort with two leaves set opposite upon the chief rib. **French:** Small Peucedanum, with a pair of leaves opposite to one another.

---

### UMBELLIFERS with membranous, somewhat round, flattened seeds, and provided with lobed or feathered leaves.

Common hairy Cow-parsnip, <note>referenced from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*</note>. **English:** Cow-parsnip. **French:** Mattioli’s Sphondylium.
Hairy Cow-parsnip with narrower leaves, <note>referenced from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax* and *Prodromus*</note>. **English:** Cow-parsnip with narrow cut leaves. **French:** Shaggy narrow-leaved Sphondylium.
Cultivated broad-leaved Parsnip. **English:** Garden Parsnip. **French:** Parsnip, or cultivated Parsnip; also called Elaphoboscon or Baucia by Dodoens.
Wild broad-leaved Parsnip. **English:** Wild Parsnip. **French:** Wild Parsnip.

<column-break/>

Common greater wild Bucklerwort, with a nearly smooth edge. **English:** Common greater wild Bucklerwort. **French:** Field Parsnip, producing seeds without a rimmed border, bearing white flowers.
Lesser Syrian Bucklerwort, with a granulated edge. **English:** Syrian Bucklerwort. **French:** Tordylium or Small Syrian Parsnip with larger seeds, bearing a bead-like rim on the edge.
Lesser Bucklerwort with a granulated edge, or the smallest kind from Apulia, <note>referenced from Columna</note>. **English:** Bucklerwort of Apulia in Naples. **French:** Tordylium or Small Apulian Parsnip with smaller seeds, bearing a bead-like rim on the edge.
All-heal with a parsnip leaf, perhaps the Syrian variety, <note>referenced from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*</note>. Foreign All-heal, <note>referenced from Dodoens</note>. **English:** All-heal with parsnip leaves. **French:** Dodoens’ Chironian All-heal.
All-heal, or Costus-like Panax, or Costus, <note>referenced from Mattioli</note>. **English:** All-heal with leaves of Cow-parsnip. **French:** Mattioli’s bastard Costus, or Great Wild Parsnip.

> This umbellifer and others contained here in Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*, since they belong to the family of those previously mentioned—having the same membranous, somewhat round, and flattened seeds—ought to be joined with the others of their Genus, just as I leave it to be performed in all other subordinate Generies.

### UMBELLIFERS with membranous, somewhat round, flattened seeds, and provided with leaves divided in many ways.

Mountain-parsley, <note>referenced from Clusius</note>. **English:** Mountain parsley with flat seed. **French:** Mountain Parsley with flat seed.
Theophrastus’s Frankincense-herb, a second kind of Frankincense-herb <note>referenced from Dodoens’ French edition</note>. Stone-break of the Venetians, <note>referenced from Lobelius’s *Adversaria*</note>. **English:** Breakstone of the Venetians. **French:** Stone-break of the Venetians.
Milk-parsley, <note>referenced from Dodoens</note>. **English:** Milky Parsley. **French:** Marsh Parsley yielding milk.
Milky marsh Seseli, <note>referenced from Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax* and *Prodromus*</note>. **English:** Marsh, or meadow Hart-wort. **French:** Meadow Seseli yielding milk, or rather a milky juice.

## Umbellifers <term>Umbellifer: A plant in the parsley family (Apiaceae), characterized by flower clusters that spring from a single point, forming a flat or rounded "umbrella" shape.</term> provided with membranous <note>original: "membranaceo," referring to a thin, papery texture</note>, somewhat round, flattened seeds, and fennel-like leaves.

**Left Branching:**
* **Hog's-fennel** <note>original: "peucedanum"</note>
    * Greater Italian variety
    * Lesser German variety
    * With leaves arranged in pairs <note>original: "foliis conjugatim positis"</note>

**Right Branching:**
* **Giant Fennel** <note>original: "ferula"</note>
    * Referenced from Mattioli and Dodoens <note>Mattioli and Dodoens were influential 16th-century botanists.</note>
    * Galbanum-bearing <note>referenced from Lobelius; Galbanum is a fragrant gum resin used in incense and medicine.</note>
* **All-heal** <note>original: "panax" or "panaces"</note>, also known as Asclepian All-heal <note>referenced from Dodoens</note>
* **Dill** <note>original: "anethum"</note>

***

## Umbellifers provided with membranous, somewhat round, flattened seeds, and lobed leaves.

**Left Branching:**
* **Syrian** variety
* **Apulian** variety, the smallest <note>referenced from Fabius Columna</note>
* **Hartwort** <note>original: "Tordylium"</note>
    * Greater common Hartwort, with a nearly smooth border <note>The author notes "nobis," indicating this is his own classification.</note>
    * Lesser Hartwort, with a granulated border <note>original: "limbo Granulato"</note>
* **Foreign All-heal** <note>referenced from Dodoens</note>

**Right Branching:**
* **Hogweed** <note>original: "Sphondylium," also known as Cow Parsnip</note>
    * Common hairy variety
    * Hairy variety with narrower leaves <note>referenced from Bauhin’s *Prodromus*</note>
* **Broad-leaved Parsnip** <note>original: "pastinaca latifolia"</note>
    * Cultivated <note>original: "Sativa"</note>
    * Wild <note>original: "Sylvestris"</note>

# I N D E X  O F  T A B L E  V I.
## On kinship and affinity.

### U M B E L L I F E R S <term>Umbellifer: A plant in the parsley family (Apiaceae), characterized by flower clusters that spring from a single point, forming a flat or rounded "umbrella" shape.</term> with beaked seeds. <note>original: "Semine rostrato," referring to seeds that end in a long, beak-like point.</note>
#### Genus 8.

Perennial Myrrhis <note>A group of aromatic plants including Sweet Cicely</note> with furrowed seeds, white, larger, and fragrant. *English: Sweet Chervil, or Cicely. French: Myrrhis or Matthiolus’s hemlock-chervil.*

Perennial Myrrhis with furrowed seeds, white, smaller, and hairy leaves. *English: Lesser mountain Chervil, or Cicely with hairy leaves. French: Small mountain myrrhis with hairy leaves.*

Perennial Myrrhis with furrowed seeds, white, smaller, and more hairy leaves. *English: Lesser mountain chervil with white hairy leaves. French: Small mountain Myrrhis with more hairy leaves.*

Perennial Myrrhis with furrowed seeds, white, smaller, and very hairy leaves. *English: Lesser mountain chervil with white flowers and most hairy leaves. French: Small mountain Myrrhis with very hairy leaves.*

Perennial Myrrhis with furrowed seeds, yellow, and carrot-like. *English: Mountain Chervil, or Myrrhis with a yellow tuft or umbell, and leaves like carrots. French: Perennial myrrhis with a yellow flower and carrot-like leaves.*

Annual Myrrhis with furrowed, rough, long seeds, and knotted joints. *English: Annual Chervil with rough, long, furrowed seed, and knotty joints in its stalk. French: Annual myrrhis, with a knotty stem, and its seeds long, rough, and channeled.*

Annual Myrrhis with furrowed, rough, short seeds, according to our classification <note>original: "Nobis," meaning the author's own description</note>. The New [plant] of the Equicoli, described by Columna. *English: Wild annual Chervil with short, black, rough furrowed seeds. French: Small Myrrhis with short, black, rough, and channeled seed.*

<column-break/>

Annual Myrrhis with knots, and smooth furrowed seeds, according to our classification. The *Anthriscus* of Pliny, in the History of Lyon <note>original: "Hist. Lug.," referring to the 'Historia Generalis Plantarum' published in Lyon</note>. *English: Annual Cicely with furrowed smooth seeds. French: Dalechamp’s Anthriscus, Myrrhis with a knotty stem, and channeled, smooth seed.*

Annual Myrrhis with furrowed, shaggy seeds. *English: Annual Cicely with hoary furrowed seed. French: Myrrhis with channeled and shaggy seed.*

Cultivated Chervil <note>original: "Cerefolium sativum"</note> as described by Tragus. *English: Chervil. French: Common Chervil.*

Wild Chervil with smooth, black seeds, according to our classification. Perhaps the white Cow-parsley <note>original: "Cicutaria alba"</note> of the History of Lyon. *English: Wild Chervil with black smooth seed. French: Wild Chervil with black and smooth seed.*

*Scandix*, or Shepherd's needle <note>original: "pecten Veneris," literally Venus's comb</note>. *English: Shepherd's needle. French: Shepherd's needle, or the Scandix of some authors.*

---

### U M B E L L I F E R S with round or twin-lobed seeds. <note>original: "testiculato," a botanical term of the era describing seeds that appear in pairs of rounded globes, like coriander.</note>
#### Genus 9.

Cultivated or Greater Coriander. *English: Common or Garden Coriander. French: Cultivated coriander of Mattioli.*

Wild and very stinking Coriander. Described by Caspar Bauhin <note>original: "C. B. P.," referring to his 'Pinax Theatri Botanici'</note>. *English: Wild and most stinking Coriander. French: Very stinking wild coriander.*

## Beaked Umbellifers <term>Umbellifer: A plant in the parsley family characterized by flower clusters that spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella.</term>

### Umbellifers with Beaked Seeds. <note>original: "Semine rostrato," referring to seeds that terminate in a long, beak-like point.</note> Genus VIII.

*   **Annual Myrrhis <note>A genus of aromatic plants, often referred to as Chervil or Sweet Cicely</note> with furrowed seeds.**
    *   **With rough seeds**
        *   Oblong and knotted <note>Referring to the characteristic swollen joints or nodes on the stem</note>
        *   Short, the new [variety] of Fabio Colonna. <note>original: "æquicolorum colum." likely referring to the work of the Italian naturalist Fabius Columna</note>
    *   **With smooth seeds**
        *   Anthriscus of Pliny, as described in the History of Lyons <note>original: "hist. lugd." refers to the *Historia Generalis Plantarum* published in Lyon, attributed to Jacques Daléchamps</note>
    *   **With shaggy seeds**

*   **Perennial Myrrhis with furrowed seeds.**
    *   **White**
        *   **Larger**
            *   Fragrant <note>Commonly known today as Sweet Cicely (*Myrrhis odorata*)</note>
        *   **Smaller**
            *   With hairy leaves
            *   With more hairy leaves
            *   With the most hairy leaves
    *   **Yellow and carrot-like** <note>original: "daucoïdes," meaning it resembles the foliage or form of a carrot (Daucus)</note>

---

### Umbellifers with Beaked Seeds

*   **Scandix, or Venus’s Comb** <note>Known for its exceptionally long, needle-like seed beaks that resemble the teeth of a comb</note>
*   **Chervil**
    *   Cultivated, according to Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock, a pioneering German botanist who used the name Tragus</note>
    *   Wild, with smooth black seeds.

---

### Umbellifers with Round or Twin Seeds. <term>Testiculated: A botanical term describing seeds that grow in pairs of rounded, ball-like lobes, resembling testicles.</term> Genus IX.

*   Wild coriander, most foul-smelling, according to Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax*. <note>original: "C. B. P." refers to the *Pinax Theatri Botanici*, a highly influential botanical index of the 17th century</note>
*   Cultivated coriander, or the larger variety.

### UMBELS Improperly so called. <note>The author uses "improperly" to categorize plants that look like Umbellifers (having flat-topped flower clusters) but belong to different botanical families—in this case, the Valerianaceae rather than the Apiaceae.</note>

# Genus 1. Valerian <note>original Latin: "Valeriana". Often called "Setwall" in older English herbals.</note>

**Great Valerian**
*   **Garden variety** or **Setwall**. <note>original: "hortensis"; also known as "Phu major."</note>
*   **Wild variety**, which branches into:
    *   **With broader leaves**.
    *   **With narrower leaves**.

**Lesser Valerian**
*   **Annual** or **Summer Valerian** as described by Clusius. <note>Charles de l'Écluse (Clusius) was a Flemish doctor and pioneering botanist.</note>
*   **Perennial marsh Valerian**.

<column-break/>

**Sea Valerian**
*   **Broad-leaved** or **Greater Sea Valerian**, which includes:
    *   **Red** flowers.
    *   **White** flowers.
*   **Narrow-leaved** or **Lesser Sea Valerian**, which includes:
    *   **Red** flowers as noted in Bauhin’s *Prodromus*. <note>Refers to Gaspard Bauhin, a Swiss botanist who categorized thousands of plants.</note>
    *   **White** flowers.

**Mountain Valerian**
*   **With smooth, sky-blue leaves**. <note>original: "folio lævi Glauco."</note>
*   **With rough leaves**, which branches into:
    *   **With roots shaped like olives**.
    *   **With fibrous roots**.

**Canadian Valerian** with leaves like nettles, as described by Cornut. <note>Jacques-Philippe Cornut was a French physician who wrote one of the first books on North American plants.</note>

# Genus 2. Lamb’s Lettuce or Corn Salad <note>original: "Valerianella". These are small, edible herbs often found in grain fields.</note>

**With navel-like seeds** <note>original: "Semine umbilicato." This refers to a depression in the seed resembling a belly button.</note>
*   **Naked seeds**, either **round** or **oblong**.
*   **Hairy seeds**, either **larger** or **smaller**.

**Field variety**
*   **Early-flowering**, which is **lowly** with **compressed seeds**.
*   **Late-flowering**, which is **taller** with **swollen seeds**.

<column-break/>

**Shaped like a horn of plenty** <note>original: "cornucopoides."</note>
*   **Red Indian variety** from Clusius.
*   **White prickly variety** from Columna. <note>Fabio Colonna was an Italian botanist known for his detailed copperplate illustrations.</note>

**With seeds like Scabious**
*   **Greater Portuguese variety**.
*   **Lesser thin-leaved variety** from Columna.

# Genus 3. Greek Valerian <note>Now known as Jacob's Ladder (*Polemonium caeruleum*). It is not a true Valerian but was grouped here due to the shape of its leaves.</note>

*   **White** flowers.
*   **Violet** flowers.

### UMBELS Improperly so called. <note>The author uses "improperly" to categorize plants that visually resemble true Umbellifers (like carrots or parsley) but belong to different plant families—primarily the Rose family (Rosaceae) and the Buttercup family (Ranunculaceae) in this section.</note>
#### Genus 4. <note>This genus covers the Burnets, known in Latin as *Pimpinella sanguisorba*.</note>

**Great Burnet of Canada**, with a longer spike and white flowers. <note>original Latin: "Pimpinella sanguisorba major, spica longiori, Canadensis, alba."</note>

**Great Burnet of Canada**, with a longer spike and red flowers.

**Common Great Burnet**, with a shorter head (or clustered) and red flowers; our native variety. <note>original Latin: "nostras" refers to the variety common to Europe.</note>

**Great Burnet of Canada**, with a shorter head and red flowers.

**Great Burnet**, with a shorter head (or clustered) and leaves like Agrimony; **our own classification.** <note>The author uses "Nobis" (To us) to indicate his own unique naming or categorization of this plant.</note>

**Lesser Burnet**, with hairy leaves.

**Lesser Burnet**, with smooth leaves.

**Lesser Prickly Burnet**, with evergreen leaves.

<summary>This page lists several genera of "Improper Umbellifers," specifically identifying various species of Burnet, Dropwort, Meadowsweet, and Meadow Rue. It distinguishes these plants based on their physical traits, such as flower color, leaf texture, and seed pod shape, while noting their similarities to true Umbellifers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical classification from Table 7. It focuses on the "Improperly Named Umbels," plants that look like the carrot family (Umbelliferae) due to their flower structure but belong to other families like the Rosaceae (Rose family) and Ranunculaceae (Buttercup family). This table specifically catalogs Burnets, Dropworts, Goat's Beard, and Meadow Rues.</meta>

-># TABLE 8.<-

->## UMBELS IMPROPERLY SO-CALLED.<-
->### On kinship and affinity. <note>This refers to plants grouped by their physical similarities and botanical "relationships" rather than strict modern genetic families.</note><-

**Genus 4. Burnet.** <note>original: *pimpinella Sanguisorba*. The name "Sanguisorba" comes from the Latin for "blood-absorber," reflecting its historical use as a medicinal herb to stop bleeding.</note>

- **Lesser Burnet** <note>original: *minor*.</note>
    - Hairy
    - Not hairy
    - Prickly, evergreen

- **Greater Burnet** <note>original: *major*.</note>
    - **With a longer spike**
        - Canadian, white.
        - Canadian, red.
    - **With a shorter spike, or clustered** <note>original: *Glomerata*.</note>
        - Our native variety. <note>original: *nostras*.</note>
        - Canadian.
        - Resembling Agrimony. <note>original: *agrimonoides*.</note>

---

**Genus 5. Dropwort.** <note>original: *filipendula*. The name refers to the "hanging" (pendula) "threads" (fili) of its tuberous roots.</note>

---

<column-break/>

**Genus 6. Goat’s Beard.** <note>original: *Barba capræ*. Today typically identified as *Aruncus dioicus*, known for its large, feathery white flower plumes.</note>

- With compact flowers.
- With oblong flowers.

---

**Genus 7. Meadow Rue.** <note>original: *Thalictrum*. These plants have delicate, fern-like leaves and decorative tufts of stamens that resemble the flower heads of true umbellifers.</note>

- **With a three-sided seed pod** <term>siliqua triquetra: a seed vessel with three distinct edges or angles</term>, Canadian variety, as described by Cornut. <note>original: *corn.* refers to Jacques-Philippe Cornut, author of an early 1635 work on Canadian plants.</note>

- **With a striated seed pod.**
    - **Greater Meadow Rue**
        - With **smooth leaves**
            - With tufted purple flowers. <note>original: *muscariis purpureis*. The term "muscariis" refers to the brush-like or tufted appearance of the flowers, similar to a Grape Hyacinth.</note>
            - With tufted or yellow flowers.
        - With **wrinkled, three-cleft leaves**. <note>original: *trifidis*.</note>
    - **Lesser Meadow Rue**
        - As described by Dodoens. <note>original: *dodai*. Refers to Rembert Dodoens, a 16th-century Flemish physician and botanist.</note>
        - The most foul-smelling variety, as described by Bauhin in his *Introduction*. <note>original: *fætidissimu B. in Prod.* refers to Caspar Bauhin’s 1620 *Prodromos Theatri Botanici*.</note>

## EXPLANATION OF THE FIRST GENERAL PLATE OF SEED ILLUSTRATIONS.

**Note 1:** Nine intermediate genera are contained between the ⊕ <note>a symbol likely marking a primary group</note> and ☉ <note>original: *sol* or sun symbol, marking another group</note> symbols; the fertile intermediate genera are distinguished by an asterisk (*) and their varieties are further distinguished by an asterisk (*).

**Note 2:** The capital letters in both the alphabet and the figures designate seeds that are still green and clinging together, so that they appear as one; whereas the lowercase letters show the same seeds when they are dry and shriveled, gaping open and hanging from fibers.

⊕
**A. a.** Indicates the seeds of *Cachrys* <note>A genus of the carrot family known for corky or spongy seeds.</note> with leaves like Giant Fennel, with a spongy seed enclosed in a smooth seed-case.

### ANALYSIS OF THE ILLUSTRATION
This plate is organized into twelve horizontal rows, meticulously detailing the variations in seed structure.

*   **Rows 1-3 (Alphabetical):** Labeled A through Z, followed by symbols (&, †) and combinations (ac, ab, d), these rows cover primary types of <term>Umbellifer</term> <note>Plants with flower clusters that spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella.</note> seeds.
*   **Rows 4-11 (Numerical):** Numbered 1 through 96, this section provides a comprehensive catalog of specific varieties.
*   **Rows 12-13 (Greek):** Labeled α through ω, representing secondary or related groups.

The diagrams at the center (labeled A, B, C, D, E, G) illustrate the internal anatomy of the flowers and the specific orientation of seeds as they transition from fresh to dry states. The bottom right features a heraldic coat of arms with three crosses, belonging to the patron, Peter Mews.

<meta>This page continues the detailed visual and anatomical explanation of the Umbelliferae (parsley family), focusing on the three-dimensional shapes of the seed heads (flat, convex, or concave) and the specific structures of the flowers and leaves used for classification.</meta>

# GENERAL TABLE OF THE ILLUSTRATIONS OF UMBELLIFERS.

->REGARDING THE THREEFOLD DIFFERENT POSITION<-
->of the surfaces, and the threefold variety of the flowers of the Umbellifers.<-

---

### PLATE II.
->Explanation of the illustrations of Umbellifers.<-

**FENNEL** <term>Foeniculum</term> pregnant with seed. Here we have shown, as a representative of all others that exhibit a flat surface, the umbel <note>The umbrella-like flower head characteristic of this family.</note> of fennel alone. It displays a flat surface, laden with striated <note>Grooved or striped.</note> seeds joined in pairs. **A.** shows an umbellet <note>The smaller individual clusters that make up the larger umbel.</note> of the same plant with the seeds still tightly joined in pairs, and in some dried and gaping specimens beginning to fall away; two small fibers remain, by the benefit of which the seeds (while they were still green) received nourishment from the small rays attached to the top of the stalk. This is common to almost all seeds of the umbellifers, and these fibers are no less necessary for the nutrition of the seeds than the umbilical vessels are for the nutrition of the offspring of living creatures.

**GIANT FENNEL** <term>Ferula</term> pregnant with seed. Here we have brought forward the umbel of the galbanum-bearing Giant Fennel <note>original: *ferulæ galbaniferæ*</note>, as a representative of all other umbellifers offering a convex surface; it forms a convex surface in the center. In addition to the representation of the convex surface, one may see scattered seeds that are compressed, dried, and gaping; and after some have fallen, the two fibers appear exposed. **B.** shows an umbellet of the same plant laden with several membrane-like, oblong, compressed seeds joined in pairs, with some even open and beginning to fall into the earth as if into their own womb; indeed, one may also observe the fibers remaining after the seeds have fallen.

**DAUCUS**, or Carrot, pregnant with seed. It appears clearly to anyone looking that this is the umbel of the carrot, or the cultivated fine-leaved parsnip, which forms a concave surface like a bird's nest <note>original: *nidi avis*</note>. Its outer rays are much longer than the internal ones, and both are laden with bristly or hairy seeds. Note that the carrot when flowering forms a convex surface, but gradually, as it turns to seed, the longer outer rays are drawn together by the benefit of the narrower leaves surrounding the base of the rays, and they form a cavity like a bird's nest. We have also observed this in certain other umbellifers, such as in *Gingidium* (picktooth), or *Visnaga*; Syrian *Tordylium*; and the Tangier Carrot-like *Caucalis*; Cretan Carrot; and Azores Fennel; and others worthy of observation. Note that all seeds of the *Daucus* properly so called are striated and covered with hairs on the ridges or stripes, and those which do not produce similar seeds are relegated to other classes according to the doctrine delivered above, as we observed in our *Botanical Preludes* <note>original: *Prælud. noſtr. Bot.*</note>, pages 355 and 356. **C.** shows an umbellet of the same *Daucus* or carrot distinctly laden with hairy or bristly seeds, some still tightly adhering to each other, others gaping, and others falling, with the useless fibers remaining.

**HOGWEED** <term>Sphondylium</term> flowering. Here one may perceive the umbel of Hogweed, pregnant with flowers having five petals that are split in two at the middle. **E.** shows a single flower of the same umbel more distinctly, whose individual petals clearly appear bifid <note>Split into two parts.</note>; this is also seen in many flowers of other umbellifers likewise worthy of note. Indeed, unless my mind deceives me, the greatest part of umbellifers endowed with white flowers have petals that are either split, heart-shaped, or at least suffering some sort of indentation.

**FENNEL** flowering. Here one may clearly see the umbel of fennel, and its individual rays, each supporting individual yellow flowers, which consist of five reflexed <note>Bent backward or downward.</note> petals. This happens to almost all umbellifers gifted with yellow flowers. **D.** shows a particular umbellet of the same with flowers and reflexed petals standing more distinctly on their rays.

**DAUCUS** flowering. Here the *Daucus* or carrot offers itself to anyone looking, bearing its umbel laden with flowers, with five petals that are almost flat and slightly split, which anyone can see from the first glance at this illustration. While the carrot is still flowering, it forms a convex surface, but truly when it is pregnant with seed, it becomes concave, as is shown by the figures themselves. **F.** offers the umbel of the aforesaid carrot laden with flowers and the beginnings of seeds; **G.** offers the leaves embracing the base or the very top of the stalk.

<column-break/>

### PLATE III.
->Explanation of the illustrations of the "Hair-bearing" Umbellifers.<-

In this third plate of illustrations, a threefold diversity of *Cachrys*, or hair-bearing *Libanotis* <note>Historically identified with "Rosemary of Lebanon," though here referring to a specific umbellifer group.</note>, is displayed. The 1st illustration shows a *Cachrys* with a smooth, spongy seed. I do not mean here that the seeds themselves are spongy, for their covering consists only of a spongy pericarp <note>The seed case or fruit wall.</note>, or a spongy substance, which, regarding the two types of external appearance, is either smooth or furrowed; this is likewise twofold, either rough or flat. However, the seeds hidden within the coverings of all these species are moderately striated, and being covered by the pericarps, they are joined in pairs. In the first illustration to the left, the seeds appear while they are still green, tightly adhering to one another; some, being dried, begin to fall; some have already fallen, leaving behind the two fibers, which are shown clearly outlined and engraved. In almost all seeds of all umbellifers, they are seen joined in pairs while still green, and they are like the umbilical vessels in living creatures, being as it were ducts or channels carrying nourishment from the roots, stalks, and leaves for the nutrition of the seeds and their perfection; and after the two seeds are dried and brought to maturity, these fibers are left behind as if useless. All these things appear clearly at a single glance in the illustrations of the plate provided, engraved with the highest skill and very cleverly by the engraver <note>original: *chalcographo*, literally a copper-engraver.</note> imitating nature to a tee <note>original: *ad unguem*, literally "to the fingernail."</note>. The *Cachrys* with a smooth spongy seed-case and giant-fennel-like leaves holds the first place in the Plate; the *Cachrys* with a rough furrowed spongy seed-case and somewhat broad hog's-fennel leaves possesses the second place; the *Cachrys* with a larger, flat, furrowed spongy seed-case and narrower hog's-fennel leaves has the third place in this third Plate. The leaf of the first *Cachrys* is opposite the umbel laden with smooth seeds, dissected like Giant Fennel; the leaf of the second is of the hog's fennel with slightly broader leaves following; the leaf of the third *Cachrys* is also of hog's fennel but slightly narrower than the leaves of the preceding one; the leaves in both are grass-like and divided into threes, or sometimes into twos in the manner of hog's fennel, and resting on their leaf-stalks. At the very top of the stalks, the seeds of all three of these umbels exhibit a convex surface.

---

### PLATE IV.
->Explanation of the illustrations.<-

**COMMON FENNEL** <term>Foeniculum vulgare</term>. Here are represented the leaf-stalk and leaves of fennel as a paradigm <note>An archetypal example.</note> for umbellifers with fennel-like leaves, or leaves finely dissected in the manner of fennel, and endowed with a larger striated seed. To this are related cumin, Spignel <note>original: *meum athamanticum*</note>, and any others.

**SPURIOUS SPIGNEL** <term>Meum spurium</term>. Here are offered the leaf-stalk and finely dissected leaves of Spurious Spignel as a paradigm for umbellifers with fennel-like leaves and endowed with a smaller striated seed. To this are related the following umbels: Earthnut <note>original: *Bulbocastanum*</note>, the small mountain parsley of Clusius, the Pannonian saxifrage of Clusius, picktooth <note>original: *visnaga*</note>, and others to be named below under the name of *Daucus* or saxifrage.

**LOVAGE** <term>Levisticum</term>. Here is shown the leaf-stalk with the lobed leaves of lovage as a paradigm for the larger lobed umbellifers endowed with a larger striated seed. To this are related mountain *Siler*, and *Siler* with columbine-like leaves, angelica and its species, Masterwort <term>Imperatoria</term>, Alexanders <term>Smyrnium</term>, and Masterwort <term>Astrantia</term>; even though the latter does not have its leaf lobes arranged in the manner of other umbels of this subordinate genus, but bears five, six, or sometimes seven lobes meeting in one center at the end of the leaf-stalk, and not in pairs or threes in the manner of its relatives. We have therefore taken care to have it drawn and engraved anew, especially because it is poorly placed by all authors writing "Theaters," "General Histories," and "Compendiums," and is presented as black hellebore or *Veratrum*; see above the errors of C.B. <note>Refers to Caspar Bauhin (1560–1624), a famous botanist.</note> and other authors. **A.** indicates the upper part of the stalk of *Astrantia* cut off, with the umbels laden with striated, rough, oblong seeds joined in pairs. Since, I say, this umbel has been poorly named and arranged by all botanists writing even to the present day, I call it an umbel since it possesses all the conditions for constituting a perfect umbel.

<summary>This page provides a systematic visual guide to the morphology of umbellifer plants, categorizing them by the shape of their seed heads (flat, convex, or concave) and the structure of their petals and leaves. It uses mechanical and biological analogies, like the umbilical cord, to explain how seeds receive nutrients through fibers.</summary>
<keywords>Fennel, Giant Fennel, Carrot, Hogweed, Cachrys, Lovage, Astrantia, umbel, seed morphology, botanical classification, Caspar Bauhin, plant anatomy</keywords>

# EXPLANATION OF THE PLATES

...making them distinct) and it is incorrectly called "black hellebore," as it has no resemblance whatsoever to hellebore, and much less can it be called <term>Sanicula</term> <note>Self-heal or Sanicle</note> for reasons we have provided extensively elsewhere. See the figure itself shown in the Plate. Consult our *Botanical Preludes* <note>original: *Prælud. nostr. Botan.*</note> pages 370 and 377. There is no one, even moderately skilled in botany, who—upon reading these writings of ours—would not conclude that we, relying on reason and experience, have better placed this plant here among its relatives from the book of nature, and have aptly enough named it **Black Masterwort** <note>original: *imperatoriam nigram*</note> following Tabernaemontanus. Concerning the seeds of these four aforementioned umbels, consult the General Table of Seed Illustrations under the letters D, d, G, G, M, m, R, r.

### PLATE V.
->Explanation of the illustrations.<-

**BURNET SAXIFRAGE**, the greater <note>original: *Pimpinella saxifraga major*</note>. Here is indicated the stalk with lobed leaves attached in pairs opposite one another along the stalk, or petiole, of the aforementioned greater Burnet Saxifrage.

**BURNET SAXIFRAGE**, the greater variant <note>original: *degener*</note>. Here is also presented a stalk with leaves also arranged in lobes, but these are divided and fringed <note>original: *laciniatis*</note>. Being a variant from the seed of the former, it is produced by nature in this way, just as very often happens in many other plants.

**BURNET SAXIFRAGE**, the lesser <note>original: *Pimpinella saxifraga minor*</note>. Here likewise are shown the lobed, notched leaves of the Burnet, or the lesser **Goat-parsley** <note>original: *tragofelini minoris*</note> of Tabernaemontanus.

**BURNET SAXIFRAGE**, the lesser variant, or "curled" <note>original: *crispa*</note>. Offered to the sight are lobed leaves, deeply divided, of the same aforementioned Burnet Saxifrage, similarly varying (as I believe) from the sowing.

**WATER PARSNIP**, the narrow-leaved aquatic <note>original: *Sium aquaticum angustifolium*</note>. Here are represented the stalk and the lobed, saw-toothed leaves of the narrow-leaved water parsnip, attached opposite one another on the stalk, together with an odd leaf occupying the end of the stalk, after the manner of most umbellifers.

**WATER PARSNIP** with wrinkled, three-forked, and toothed leaves. Here also are offered to the sight the wrinkled leaves of the aforementioned parsnip, toothed at the edge, emerging in threes from a single stalk. Other species of parsnip produce leaves in pairs attached to the middle rib, as can be seen in the **Skirret** <note>original: *sifaro*</note> and **Stone-parsley** <note>original: *Sifone*</note>. And we have displayed these above-mentioned umbels in this plate as representatives of all the smaller lobed umbels provided with smaller, striated seeds, just as in the preceding Plate 4 we presented **Lovage** <note>original: *levisticum*</note> and **Masterwort** <note>original: *astrantiam*</note> (or Black Masterwort) as representatives of all the larger lobed umbels gifted with larger striated seeds.

---

### PLATE VI.
->Explanation of the illustrations.<-

**HEMLOCK**, the foulest broad-leaved <note>original: *Cicuta latifolia fœtidissima*</note>. Here is shown the stalk with the "wings" <note>leaflets</note> laden with leaves divided in many ways of the aforementioned hemlock.

**HEMLOCK**, the common greater <note>original: *Cicuta maj. vulgaris*</note>. Here is represented the stalk with wings and lobes arranged opposite one another, also divided in many ways, of the common greater hemlock, in the same manner and fashion as the aforementioned foulest broad-leaved kind.

**WATER DROPWORT**, narrow-leaved <note>original: *Oenanthe angustifolia*</note>. Here are offered to the sight the spindle-shaped roots, the stems, and the stalks supporting the narrow leaves divided in many ways (in the manner of mountain Parsley) of the aforementioned narrow-leaved water dropwort.

**WATER DROPWORT** with leaves like Marsh Yarrow <note>original: *Oenanthe millefolii palustris folio*</note>. Here are displayed the tuberous roots, the stems, and the umbels together with leaves similar to those of the marsh yarrow and very finely divided. It is wrongly called "Meadow Carrot" <note>original: *Daucus pratensis*</note> by Dalechamps in his *History of Lyon* and other moderns following him, as we taught in our *Botanical Preludes*, page 366. Even if this umbel does not have leaves divided into lobes in many ways (like the others), but in a peculiar fashion like the leaves of marsh yarrow, we have nonetheless placed it here with its relatives because of its smell and taste, as well as the structure of the seed and root. Concerning the seeds of the aforementioned umbels, consult the General Table of seed illustrations under **x. x y. y. and †. †.**

---

### PLATE VII.
->Explanation of the illustrations.<-

**BISHOP’S WEED** of certain authors <note>original: *Ammi quorundam*</note> from Dalechamps’ *History of Lyon*. Here are shown the stalk of the aforementioned Bishop's weed, and the oblong, saw-toothed leaves, both two-forked and three-forked.

**PARSLEY**, flat garden type <note>original: *Apium hortense planum*</note>. Here is indicated the stalk of the flat garden parsley with leaves divided in many ways into two or three curved and angular lobes.

**PARSLEY**, a variant with long divided leaves. Here are offered the stalk and the curved or fringed-divided leaves attached opposite each other on the petiole or stalk of garden parsley, varying from the common seed, which has not been shown until now.

<column-break/>

**MACEDONIAN PARSLEY**. Here are shown the stalk and the saw-toothed leaves of Macedonian parsley, divided in many ways.

**FOREIGN PARSLEY** of Clusius <note>original: *Apium peregrinum*</note>. Here are indicated the stalk and the lobed, somewhat round, saw-toothed leaves of the aforementioned foreign parsley. Both this and the Macedonian kind produce seeds that are striated, smaller, and hairy <note>original: *villosa*</note>; and because of the similarity of smell and taste, we have taken care to have the images of both engraved here (at least the leaves), even if the arrangement of the leaves of the foreign parsley is not identical to the other species of parsley. Concerning the seeds of the aforementioned umbellifers contained here, consult the Table of Seed Illustrations under **z. z. 1. 1. 2. 2. 4. 4. 5. 5.**

---

### PLATE VIII.
->Explanation of the illustrations.<-

**CARAWAY** <note>original: *Carum*</note>. Here are represented the stalk and the leaves divided in many ways of caraway, which is provided with a smaller striated seed.

**ANISE** <note>original: *Anisum*</note>. Here is offered the stem with stalks laden with anise leaves, and with a small umbel laden with flowers.

**THOROUGHWAX** <note>original: *Perfoliata*</note>. Here are shown the stem and leaves of the common thoroughwax, gifted with a striated seed.

**HARE'S EAR** <note>original: *Bupleurum*</note>. Here one may see the stem and leaves of the greater Hare’s ear, with a small umbel laden with seed.

**DILL** <note>original: *Anethum*</note>. Here are shown the stalk and fennel-like leaves of dill. Concerning the seeds of the Thoroughwax and Hare's ear, see the General Table of Seed Illustrations 6. 6. 7. 7. 7a 7b, and concerning the seed of dill, consult the same at 22. 22. Note that the seeds of caraway and anise so closely approach the seeds of parsley in form and shape that we judged it would be superfluous to engrave them; for the same reason, we have omitted the seeds of skirret, burnet, water parsnip, and stone-parsley. Dill should have been engraved in the last place toward the right of the Plate, because of its compressed, membrane-like, round seed, by which it agrees with **Hog's Fennel** <note>original: *peucedano*</note> at the beginning of Plate IX.

### PLATE IX.
->Explanation of the illustrations.<-

**HOG'S FENNEL**, the greater Italian <note>original: *Peucedanum majus Italicum*</note>. Here is presented the stalk of the greater hog's fennel with grass-like leaves standing in threes upon the petioles.

**COW PARSNIP** <note>original: *Sphondylium*</note>. Here is offered to the sight the stalk with lobed leaves hanging in pairs opposite one another from the petioles of the common hairy cow parsnip.

**PARSNIP**, broad-leaved cultivated <note>original: *Pastinaca latifolia sativa*</note>. Here likewise is offered the stalk of the broad-leaved parsnip with lobed leaves arranged opposite one another, with even smaller lobes attached to the larger ones, and one closing the end of the stalk.

**HARTWORT** of Syria <note>original: *Tordylium Syriacum*</note>. Here one may perceive the stem divided into two small branches, and the lobed, somewhat round leaves, curved at the edge, and a larger concave umbel with long small leaves surrounding it toward the base, laden with membrane-like, compressed, round seeds that are granulated or fringed at the border. In the same place is seen a small flowering umbel, likewise with longer narrow leaflets surrounding the base of the rays, and with the flowers standing far above. Concerning the seeds of the umbellifers contained in this aforementioned plate, consult the General Table of Seed Illustrations 23 23. 24 24. 25 25. 26 26. 27 27.

Concerning the membrane-like, compressed, and somewhat round seeds of these two aforementioned umbellifers, consult the General Table of Seed Illustrations 31. 31. 32. 32.

<header>ILLUSTRATIONS OF THE UMBELS.</header>

**CANADIAN BURNET** <note>original: *Pimpinella Canadensis*</note> with a longer red spike, according to our classification. Shown here are the stalk and the lobed leaves—oblong, scalloped <note>original: *crenata*</note>, and arranged opposite one another—along with two small leaflets (near the attachment of the larger leaf-stalks) and a very long spike (viewed from the side) blooming toward the bottom, while the upper part of the spike still holds unopened flowers.

### PLATE XII.
->*Explanation of the illustrations.*<-

**AGRIMONY-LIKE BURNET** <note>original: *Pimpinella agrimonoides*</note>. Shown here is the stem of the agrimony-like burnet with lobed leaves that are rough and hairy, attached to their stalks, along

<column-break/>

with short, clustered spikes; the flowers are still unopened in the upper part of the spike, but are open in the lower part toward the base.

**MEADOW-RUE** <note>original: *Thalictrum*</note> with wrinkled, three-cleft leaves. Here one may see the stems along with the lobed, wrinkled, and three-cleft leaves, attached opposite one another to the stalks, with one odd leaflet always closing the end of the stalk <note>This refers to a pinnate leaf ending in a single terminal leaflet</note>. At the tips of the small stem-branches grow flowers, or yellow "fly-whisks" <note>original: *muscarii*</note>, composed as if from many tufts or fine stamens; these are open on the highest and largest stem, but remain unopened on the smaller branches.

## EXPLANATION OF THE AUTHORS' NAMES
->*cited in the treatise on umbels.*<-

<columns>2</columns>

Ad. & Adv. Lob. — Matthias de l'Obel <note>Lobelius</note> in his *Adversaria* <note>Botanical notebooks</note>.
C. B. P. & C. B. Pin. & C. B. — Caspar Bauhin in his *Pinax* <note>A landmark 1623 "register" of plant names</note>.
C. B. Prod. — Caspar Bauhin in his *Prodromus* <note>The "precursor" to his main work</note>.
Cæs. & Cæsalp. — Andrea Cesalpino.
Cam. Ep. & Camer Ep. — Joachim Camerarius the Younger in his *Epitome of Mattioli*.
Cam. Hort. — Camerarius in his *Gardens*.
Cluf. & Clus. Hist. — Charles de l'Écluse <note>Clusius</note> in his *History of Rare Plants*.
Col. & Colum. — Fabio Colonna in his *History of Lesser-Known Plants*.
Cord. & Cord. Hist. — Valerius Cordus in his *History*.
Corn. & Cornut. — Jacques-Philippe Cornut on *Canadian Plants*.
Dal. & Dalech. & Dalp.ii — Jacques Daléchamps in his *History of Lyons*.
Diosc. & Dioscor. — Dioscorides <note>The ancient Greek physician</note>.
Dod. — Rembert Dodoens in his *History of the Pemptades*, Latin folio edition.
Dod. Gall. — Dodoens in the French edition.
Dod. Desc. — Dodoens, from the Latin description in folio.
Desc. & Descr. — These denote a plant described by the cited author but not illustrated; for example, "Lob. Dod. Clus. Desc." means the plant was described by l'Obel, Dodoens, and Clusius, but not pictured.
Eyst. & Eystet. Hort. — The *Hortus Eystettensis* <note>Garden of Eichstätt</note> published by Besler.
Fuch. & Fuchs. — Leonhart Fuchs in the Latin folio of his *History*.
Ger. — John Gerard, the "Emaculate" <note>The "purified" or corrected 1633 edition</note> by Johnson.
Ger. non Emac. — John Gerard, the uncorrected edition.
Ges. & Gesn. — Conrad Gessner in the *Corollary* added to the History by Cordus, and in his booklet on the collection of plants.
Hist. Lugd. & Lugdun. — The *History of Lyons* published in folio.
Hist. Lugd. App. — The *History of Lyons* in the Latin folio Appendix.
Hort. Eyst. — The *Hortus Eystettensis*.
Icon. Lob. — Matthias de l'Obel in his *Illustrations* <note>Icones</note>, the oblong volume.
Joh. Bauh. & J. B. — Jean Bauhin in his *Universal History* arranged in three volumes.

<column-break/>

Lob. icon. Obs. & Ob. ad & adver. — Matthias de l'Obel in his *Illustrations* (oblong format), his *Observations*, and his *Adversaria* on plants, in folio.
Lugd. & Lugdun. Hist. — The *History of Lyons*, printed at Lyons in the Latin language, in two folio volumes.
Matth. — Pietro Andrea Mattioli; Matth. Ed. Bauh. refers to the Bauhin edition of Mattioli.
Obs. & icon. Lob. — Matthias de l'Obel in his *Observations* and *Illustrations*.
Obs. Lob. & ad. Lob. — Matthias de l'Obel in his *Observations* and *Adversaria*.
Park. — John Parkinson in his *Theatrum Botanicum* <note>Botanical Theater</note>.
Plin. — Pliny the Elder in his *Natural History*.
Pon. & Ponæ. — Giovanni Pona in his Latin edition.
Tab. & Tabern. — Jacobus Theodorus Tabernaemontanus in his *Illustrations* in oblong format.
Thal. — Johannes Thalius in his *Harz Forest* <note>Sylva Hercynia</note>.
Theoph. & Theophr. — Theophrastus <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Botany"</note>.
Trag. — Hieronymus Bock <note>Tragus</note> in his *History of Plants*.
Col. & colum. — Designates the column in Caspar Bauhin’s *Pinax* where he discusses errors <note>Hallucinationibus</note>.
i. & ii. iii. pl. — Designates the first, second, or third plant in that same place, following the specified page for said plant.
Anyone who desires more information regarding these shortened names of authors should consult Caspar Bauhin at the beginning of his *Pinax*; there he teaches at sufficient length who all the authors were who wrote on botanical matters before his century, in what volume, and when their works were published.
Note, moreover, that when the names of Theophrastus, Dioscorides, Pliny, Mattioli, Daléchamps, and other such authors are cited by others, they are written out in full, as seen above; those citing them bring them into the records of botanical writers in a shorthand manner, as has been practiced by us and others writing before us.

It serves as a bridge between the visual evidence of the plates and the established scientific literature of the early modern period.</summary>

# Plate 3 of Illustrations

### *Cachrys* with a smooth, spongy seed
<note>original: *Cachrys Semine fungoso levi*</note>

<image-desc>An illustration of a seed cluster (umbel) with smooth, rounded, egg-shaped seeds attached to a central stalk.</image-desc>

### *Cachrys* with a rough, furrowed, spongy seed
<note>original: *Cachrys Semine fungoso Sulcato aspero*</note>
<note>The term "fungous" (spongy) refers to the thick, corky outer layer of the seed, which helps it float or protects it from drying out. "Sulcate" means the seed has deep, longitudinal grooves.</note>

<image-desc>An illustration of a seed cluster where the seeds appear darker and more textured, with visible furrows or ridges (sulcate) and a rough (asper) surface.</image-desc>

### *Cachrys* with a flat, furrowed, spongy seed
<note>original: *Cachrys Semine fungoso Sulcato plano*</note>

<image-desc>An illustration of a seed cluster where the seeds are flattened and clearly marked with longitudinal furrows.</image-desc>

# Illustration Plate 5

<image-desc>
This botanical engraving displays six detailed illustrations of leaf structures from two different plant genera: Pimpinella and Sium. The plate is divided into an upper and lower section.

Top row (left to right):
1. A large pinnate leaf with broad, ovate leaflets and serrated edges.
2. A tall leaf with finely divided, bipinnatifid leaflets.
3. A smaller, simple pinnate leaf with rounded leaflets.
4. A small leaf with curled or "crisped" leaflets.

Middle row (left to right):
1. A pinnate leaf with elongated, lanceolate, and sharply serrated leaflets.
2. A pinnate leaf where the leaflets are deeply lobed into three parts (trifid) with jagged edges.

Bottom center:
Between the two lower leaf illustrations, there is a small heraldic coat of arms showing a shield with two rows of three stars (or crosses) and three stylized human figures (likely monks or saints). Below the shield is a decorative cartouche or banner containing a Latin dedication.
</image-desc>

**Greater Burnet-saxifrage**
<note>original: *Pimpinella saxifraga maj[or]*; despite the name, this is not a true saxifrage, but an aromatic herb of the carrot family used in traditional medicine.</note>

**Deviant Greater Burnet-saxifrage**
<note>original: *Pimp[inella] saxifr[aga] maj[or] degener*; the term "degenerate" was used by early botanists to describe a variant, mutant, or "monstrous" form that differs from the standard type.</note>

**Lesser Burnet-saxifrage**
<note>original: *Pimp[inella] saxifr[aga] minor*.</note>

**Curled Lesser Burnet-saxifrage**
<note>original: *Pimp[inella] saxifr[aga] minor crispa*; a variety with ruffled or "crisped" leaf margins.</note>

**Narrow-leaved Water-parsnip**
<note>original: *Sium aquaticum angustifolium*; a wetland plant often found in ditches and streams.</note>

**Water-parsnip with wrinkled, three-parted, and toothed leaves**
<note>original: *Sium aquat[icum] fol[iis] rugosis trifidis & dentatis*.</note>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog of the Apiaceae (parsley family). # Illustration: Plate 6

<image-desc>A botanical plate featuring four detailed scientific illustrations of plants from the Apiaceae family (cicutas and water dropworts), each with its Latin binomial label. In the top left, "Cicuta latifol. fœtidissima" shows a plant with broad, deeply serrated compound leaves. In the top right, "Cicuta maj. vulgaris" displays finer, fern-like bipinnate foliage. The bottom left, "Oenanthe millefol: palustris folio," depicts a plant with tall stems ending in umbels and whorls of fine, needle-like leaves. The bottom right, "Oenanthe angustifolia," shows narrow leaves and a characteristic cluster of tuberous roots. Between the bottom plants is a small heraldic crest featuring three helmets, situated above a rectangular dedication cartouche.</image-desc>

**Foul-smelling Broad-leaved Hemlock**
<note>original: *Cicuta latifol[ia] fœtidissima*; likely referring to a species of *Cicuta* or potentially *Conium*. The term "fœtidissima" highlights the unpleasant, mousy odor characteristic of many poisonous hemlocks.</note>

**Common Great Hemlock**
<note>original: *Cicuta maj[or] vulgaris*; this is the classic Poison Hemlock (*Conium maculatum*), famous in history as the plant used to execute the philosopher Socrates.</note>

**Marsh Water-dropwort with Yarrow-like Leaves**
<note>original: *Oenanthe millefol[ii] palustris folio*; "millefolium" or "milfoil" refers to the yarrow plant, which has very finely divided leaves similar to those shown here.</note>

**Narrow-leaved Water-dropwort**
<note>original: *Oenanthe angustifolia*; the *Oenanthe* genus consists of highly toxic wetland plants, often called "dead man's fingers" due to the appearance of their tuberous roots, which are visible in this illustration.</note>

<insert>
Under the auspices of the most honorable and illustrious man, Lord Henry Compton, Doctor of Sacred Theology, Master of the Hospital of the Holy Cross, and Canon of Christ Church, Oxford.
<note>original: *Auspiciis honoratiss: & Cl. viri D.ni Hen: Compton. S.S. Theol: D. Cœnob: S. Crucis Magistri. Eccliæ Christi Ox: Canon:*. Henry Compton (1632–1713) was a significant figure in the Church of England and a keen botanist who later became the Bishop of London. He was known for collecting exotic plants at Fulham Palace.</note>
</insert>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates various species of Hemlock and Water-dropwort, identifying them by their leaf structures and root systems. It includes a formal dedication to Henry Compton, a prominent 17th-century clergyman and botanical patron.</summary>

<keywords>botany, Hemlock, Water-dropwort, Henry Compton, Apiaceae, toxicology, medicinal plants, 17th-century science</keywords>

### Illustration: Plate 7

**Bishopsweed** of certain authors from the *History of Lyon*
<note>original: *Ammi quorundam Hist. lugd.* This refers to species in the genus *Ammi*, likely described in the *Historia Generalis Plantarum* (1586) published in Lyon (Lugdunum). These plants were often used to treat digestive issues.</note>

**Flat-leaf garden celery**
<note>original: *Apium hortense planum*. A common garden variety of celery or parsley.</note>

**Degenerate celery** with long, divided leaves
<note>original: *Apium Degener fol: dissectis longis*. The term "degenerate" was used by early botanists to describe a variety that had "strayed" from the appearance of the standard species.</note>

**Macedonian parsley**
<note>original: *Apium macedonicum*. Likely referring to *Athamanta macedonica*, a plant whose seeds were a famous ingredient in historical medicinal tonics.</note>

**Foreign parsley**
<note>original: *Apium peregrinum*. A general term for exotic or non-native species of the parsley family brought from abroad.</note>

# Illustration: Plate 8

### Hare's ear
<note>original: *Bupleurum*. The name is derived from the Greek words for "ox" and "rib," describing the prominent veins on its long, grass-like leaves.</note>

### Dill
<note>original: *Anethum*. A staple of ancient gardens, dill was prized both for its feathery foliage and its seeds, which were used to soothe digestive troubles.</note>

### Caraway
<note>original: *Carum*. Best known for its aromatic seeds, caraway is shown here with its finely divided leaves and a detailed view of its flowering head, or umbel.</note>

### Anise
<note>original: *Anisum*. This plant is notable for its "heterophylly"—having differently shaped leaves on the same plant, with rounded leaves at the base and feathery ones near the top.</note>

### Thorough-wax
<note>original: *Perfoliata*. This plant earns its name because the stem appears to grow "thorough" (through) the middle of the leaf, a feature known as being perfoliate.</note>

# Illustration: Plate 9
<note>original: *Icon: Tab: 9.* The word "Tabula" (abbreviated here as Tab.) refers to a prepared engraving or plate.</note>

### **Italian Sulfurwort**
<note>original: *Peucedanum italicum*. This plant belongs to a genus of hardy perennials. The name "sulfurwort" comes from the yellow resin found in the roots of some species, which was historically used in medicine.</note>

### **Hogweed**
<note>original: *Sphondylium*. Known today as *Heracleum sphondylium* or Cow Parsnip. In the 17th century, it was valued for both its medicinal properties and, in some regions, as a food source for livestock.</note>

### **Cultivated Broad-leaved Parsnip**
<note>original: *Pastinaca latifolia sativa*. This is a direct ancestor or variant of the common garden parsnip. The term <term>sativa</term> indicates it is a cultivated or "sown" variety rather than a wild one.</note>

### **Syrian Hartwort**
<note>original: *Tordylium Syriacum*. This Mediterranean plant is notable for its unique, flattened seeds with bristly or "hairy" edges, which are clearly depicted in the botanical drawing.</note>

**John Lamphire**, Doctor of Medicine,

at the **University of Oxford**.
<note>original: *Aula Cervinæ* refers to Hart Hall, a medieval academic hall at Oxford that later became Hertford College. John Lamphire (1614–1688) was a prominent physician and the Camden Professor of Ancient History.</note>

# Illustration: Plate 10
<note>original: *Icon: Tab: 10*. The abbreviation "Tab." stands for *Tabula*, the Latin term for a prepared engraving or plate used in printing.</note>

->**Mountain Parsley**<-
<note>original: *Oreoselinum*. The name comes from the Greek *oros* (mountain) and *selinon* (parsley). This plant was often distinguished from garden parsley by its habitat and more robust, bitter flavor.</note>

->**Milk-parsley**<-
<note>original: *Thysselium*. Also known as marsh hog’s-fennel, this plant is typically found in fens and wet meadows. It is named for the milky sap found in its stems.</note>

**A**
<note>A large, simple leaf illustrating the primary vein structure and the broad, oval shape of certain specimens in this group.</note>

**B**
<note>A <term>pinnate</term> leaf, meaning it has leaflets arranged on either side of a central stalk, like a feather.</note>

**C**
<note>A leaf with deeply incised or "cut" lobes, showing the transition toward the more delicate, feathery appearance of many wild parsleys.</note>

**E**
<note>A botanical detail showing the <term>sheathing</term> of the leaves, where the base of the leaf stalk wraps around the main stem.</note>

**D**
<note>A complete depiction of a small, young plant, showing the cluster of heart-shaped leaves and the initial root structure.</note>

# Illustration: Plate 11
<note>original: *icon. Tab II*. Although the transcription reads "II," the sequence within the volume and the previous page's metadata indicate this is Plate 11. Early modern numbering can often be inconsistent or suffer from printing errors.</note>

<unclear>Jean Bauhin, Volume 3</unclear> <note>original: *J. B. 3.* This refers to the third volume of the *Historia Plantarum Universalis* by the Swiss botanist Jean Bauhin (1541–1613), a standard reference work for 17th-century botanists.</unclear>

*Broad-leaved red sea valerian*
<note>original: *Valeriana marina latifolia, rubra*. This is the **Red Valerian** (*Centranthus ruber*). Though called "sea valerian" in older texts, it is a hardy perennial often seen growing in the crevices of limestone cliffs and old stone walls.</note>

*Great red Canadian burnet*
<note>original: *Pimpinella sanguisorba maj. Canadens rubra*. This species is the **Canadian Burnet** (*Sanguisorba canadensis*). The name *Sanguisorba* comes from the Latin *sanguis* (blood) and *sorbere* (to soak up), referring to its traditional use as a <term>styptic</term> to stop external bleeding.</note>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalogue from Plate 11. Following the valerians and the Canadian burnet, we move to further varieties of burnet and the introduction of meadow-rue. # Illustration: Plate 12
<note>original: *Icon. Tab. 12*. This plate provides detailed anatomical views of two specific medicinal and wild plants, likely for the purpose of identification by apothecaries and scholars.</note>

**Great agrimony-like salad burnet**
<note>original: *Pimpinella sanguisorba maj. agrimonoides*. This species is a variety of the **Great Burnet** (*Sanguisorba officinalis*). The name *agrimonoides* suggests that its leaves resemble those of the Agrimony plant, which was also highly valued in traditional herbalism.</note>

**Great meadow-rue with wrinkled, three-lobed leaves**
<note>original: *Thalictrum maj. fol. rugosis trifidis*. This is likely the **Greater Meadow-rue** (*Thalictrum flavum*). The Latin description specifically notes the "rugose" (wrinkled) texture of the foliage and the "trifid" (cleft into three) shape of the leaflets.</note>

<insert>
->Under the auspices of the most distinguished man,<-
->Mr. Peter Eliot, Doctor of Medicine at Oxford<-
<note>original: *Auspicijs Clariss: viri D.ni Petri Eliot Med: Doctoris Oxon*. Peter Eliot (died 1682) was a well-known physician in Oxford who practiced during the English Civil War and the Restoration. Botanical plates were often "sponsored" by such figures, who provided financial support for the engraving in exchange for a dedication.</note>
</insert>

<summary>This plate depicts the Great Burnet and the Greater Meadow-rue, focusing on the specific textures and shapes of their leaves. The page is dedicated to Peter Eliot, an Oxford physician, illustrating the historical link between botanical study and medical practice.</summary>
